->**MARSILIO FICINO**<-
<note>original: "Marsilii Ficini"; Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was one of the most influential humanist philosophers of the Italian Renaissance, known for translating the works of Plato into Latin.</note>

->**ON LIFE**<-
<note>original: "De Vita"; This work, often titled *De vita libri tres* (Three Books on Life), discusses health, psychology, and how scholars can maintain their well-being through medicine and astrology.</note>

->**PLATONIC THEOLOGY**<-
<note>original: "Platonica Theologia"; This is Ficino's most significant philosophical work, in which he argues that the teachings of Plato are compatible with and supportive of Christian faith.</note>

->**ON THE IMMORTALITY OF SOULS**<-
<note>original: "De Immortalitate Animorum"; This subtitle refers to the core focus of the *Platonic Theology*, where Ficino provides rational proofs for the eternal nature of the human soul.</note>

The front cover of this antique volume is bound in a traditional style characteristic of early modern bookbinding. The boards are decorated with a hand-marbled paper, featuring an intricate, swirling pattern of gold and brown hues layered over darker tones. This marbling effect was achieved by floating pigments on a liquid surface and carefully transferring the design to the paper. 

A vertical strip of reddish-brown leather runs along the right edge, where the cover meets the spine, providing both durability and a classic aesthetic. The edges of the cover show minor signs of wear, telling the story of its long history and use over the centuries.

# CHAPTERS OF THE FIRST BOOK, WHICH TREATS OF THE HEALTHY LIFE.

Preface, in which it is argued that the health of the mind is to be placed before the health of the body.
The nine guides of scholars. chapter 1.
How diligent care must be taken of the brain, the heart, the stomach, and the spirit. chapter 2. <note>In Ficino's medical philosophy, the "spirit" is a subtle vapor that acts as a bridge between the physical body and the soul.</note>
Scholars are prone to phlegm and black bile. chapter 3. <note>original: *pituitae & atrae bili*. According to the ancient theory of the four humors, an imbalance of these fluids caused illness and influenced personality.</note>
How many causes there are by which scholars are or become melancholic. chapter 4.
Why melancholics are ingenious: and what sort of melancholics are of this kind, and what sort are the opposite. chapter 5. <note>Ficino famously revived the Aristotelian idea that all great men of genius are melancholic.</note>
In what way black bile contributes to genius. chapter 6.
The five principal enemies of scholars: phlegm, black bile, sexual intercourse, satiety, and morning sleep. chapter 7. <note>The OCR reads *sonus* (sound), but the context and Ficino's other writings confirm this is a typo for *somnus* (sleep).</note>
Which hour is more opportune for beginning studies, and what the manner of continuing them should be. chapter 8.
How phlegm is to be avoided. chapter 9.
By what means black bile is to be avoided. chapter 10.
Care of the stomach. chapter 11.
Concerning those things which nourish the principal limbs and the powers of the spirit. chapter 12.
Medicines against phlegm. chapter 13.
Catarrh <term>destillatio: literally a "dripping," referring to the downward flow of excess humors from the head</term> and its cure. chapter 14.
Headache and its cure. chapter 15.
Concerning the care of vision. chapter 16.
Concerning the restoration of the sense of taste. chapter 17.
Concerning the precise cure of black bile. chapter 18.
Concerning syrups. chapter 19.

<column-break/>

Concerning pills. chapter 20.
Concerning liquid medicine. chapter 21.
Concerning bloodletting. chapter 22.
Concerning electuaries. <term>electuary: a medicinal paste or conserve made by mixing powders with honey, sugar, or syrup</term> chapter 23.
Concerning excessive wakefulness. chapter 24.
Concerning dullness and forgetfulness. chapter 25.
Indeed, care for the corporeal spirit; cultivate the incorporeal; and finally, venerate the truth. Medicine provides the first. Moral discipline provides the second. Religion provides the third. chapter 26.

## Preface to the Second Book: On the Long Life.
### Chapters of the same book.
A long life is necessary for the perfection of knowledge: which diligence also provides. chapter 1.
Vital heat is nourished by moisture: when it is lacking, dissolution occurs; when it is excessive, suffocation occurs. chapter 2.
How heat is to be tempered by moisture, and vice versa, by a certain counsel of Minerva. <note>Minerva is the Roman goddess of wisdom; Ficino uses her here to signify a wise or intellectual balance in health.</note> chapter 3.
For what reasons natural moisture dries up, or foreign moisture overflows. And how necessary perfect digestion is for life. chapter 4.
Blood and moisture suited for life ought to be airy, tempered in quality, and intermediate and tenacious in substance. chapter 5.
The common rule of eating and drinking, and the quality of feasts. chapter 6.
Do not use foods that rot quickly, nor [live] in regions of that kind... <note>The list continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page lists the table of contents for Marsilio Ficino’s manual on health, focusing on the specific vulnerabilities of scholars and the management of "black bile" or melancholy. It outlines a regimen that includes diet, herbal remedies like syrups and electuaries, and the spiritual-moral balance required for a long, productive life.</summary>

where you live: Choose **wine** and **wheat** above all others: flee putrefaction and dissolution. chapter 7.
**Diet**: food as the medicine of the elderly. chapter 8.
The natures of **spices** and **cordials** that are necessary: and again, what the food of the elderly should be. chapter 9.
Concerning **gold** and golden foods: and the restoration of the elderly. chapter 10. <note>Ficino believed that since gold was a "solar" metal that did not decay, its properties could be transferred to the human body through medicinal preparations.</note>
Concerning the use of **milk** and **human blood** for the life of the elderly. chapter 11. <note>In a passage that later became famous, Ficino suggested that the elderly might rejuvenate themselves by drinking the blood of a willing, healthy young person—a practice he likened to a "fountain of youth."</note>
Diet, habitation, and the customs of the elderly. chapter 12.
What assistance the elderly receive from the **planets** for nourishing all their limbs. Where you should remember that **raisins** fatten and strengthen the liver, which is the greatest support of life. chapter 13.
The conversation of the elderly under **Venus** through green meadows. chapter 14.
**Mercury** addresses the elderly and advises them regarding pleasure, scents, songs, and medicines. chapter 15.
Confirmation of higher things: and that we must avoid constant contemplation and sexual intercourse. chapter 16.
Concerning the medicines of the elderly, and again regarding habitation and diet. chapter 17.
Concerning the nourishment of the **spirit** and the preservation of life through scents, where in this chapter the use of **honey** is discussed. Remember that rose-honey, both solid and liquid, is most suitable for preserving the elderly. chapter 18.
The medicine of the **Magi** for the elderly. chapter 19. <note>The "Magi" refers to the ancient Persian wise men; Ficino often integrated what he believed to be ancient Zoroastrian or Hermetic wisdom into his medical advice.</note>

<column-break/>

Concerning the dangers to be avoided arising from every **seven-year cycle** of life. chapter 20. <note>In ancient and Renaissance medicine, "climacteric" years—especially those divisible by seven—were thought to be times of great physical risk.</note>

# PROEM TO THE THIRD BOOK: ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS
### Chapters of the same book.

In what, according to **Plotinus**, the power of attracting favor from the heavens consists: namely, that the **soul of the world** and the souls of the stars and demons are easily enticed by appropriate forms of bodies. chapter 1. <note>Plotinus was a 3rd-century philosopher; Ficino's work is heavily influenced by his idea that the entire universe is a living being with a soul.</note>
Concerning the harmony of the world. Concerning the nature of man according to the stars. How attraction is made from each star. chapter 2.
Between the soul of the world and its body, its **spirit** is manifest, in whose power are the four elements. We, however, can draw this in through our own spirit. chapter 3.
Our spirit draws in the spirit of the world through the rays of the **Sun** and **Jupiter**, provided it is itself solar and jovial. chapter 4. <note>original: *solaris & iovialis*. A person's "spirit" could be tuned to the frequency of specific planets through diet and lifestyle.</note>
The **Three Graces** are Jupiter, the Sun, and Venus. Jupiter is the grace in the middle of the two and is most suited to us. chapter 5.
Concerning the natural, vital, and animal power within us: and by which planets they are assisted: and how by the aspect of the **Moon** to the Sun and Venus, but especially to Jupiter. chapter 6.
How our limbs are nourished by the relationship of the Moon to the zodiac signs and fixed stars. chapter 7.
Concerning the powers and use of the **fixed stars**. chapter 8.
The dignities of the planets in the signs for use...

...to be observed in medicines. chapter 9.
How we ought to use the **planets** in medicines. chapter 10.
In what ways our **spirit** <note>Ficino defines "spirit" as a subtle, vaporous substance in the blood that acts as a bridge, connecting the physical body to the immaterial soul.</note> can draw most from the **spirit and life of the world**: And which planet creates and restores that spirit: and what things pertain to each planet. chapter 11.
Natural and even artificial things possess hidden powers from the **stars**: through which they expose our spirit to those same stars. chapter 12.
Concerning the power of **images** <note>original: "imaginum." In this context, these are astrological talismans or symbols used to capture stellar influence.</note> according to the ancients / and of medicines acquired from the heavens. chapter 13.
The orders of things depending on the stars, such as **Solar** things and the like: and how the spirit becomes Solar. chapter 14.
Concerning the power of images according to the ancients and of medicines: and how **medicines** are far more powerful than images. chapter 15.
Concerning the power of the heaven. Concerning the forces of **rays** / from which images are thought to obtain their power. chapter 16.
What power **figures** have in the heaven / and under the heaven. chapter 17.
What kind of celestial figures the ancients used to impress upon images / and concerning the use of images. chapter 18.
Concerning the construction of a **figure of the universe**. <note>Ficino describes building a mechanical model of the heavens—an orrery—to help the mind harmonize with the cosmos.</note> chapter 19.
How much power images are thought to have over the spirit / and the spirit over them: and concerning the disposition of the one who desires and the practitioner.

<column-break/>

chapter 20.
Concerning the power of **words** / and of **song** for capturing celestial benefit. And concerning the seven steps leading to celestial things. chapter 21.
How we can adapt ourselves to celestial things in seven ways: and to whom **Saturn** is harmful: to whom he is favorable: whom **Jupiter** defends from Saturn: how the heaven acts upon the spirit / and body / and soul. chapter 22.
That you may live and act prosperously: first, know your **nature** <note>original: "ingenium." One’s innate character or natural talent, determined by their birth star.</note>: your star: your guardian spirit: and a place suitable for them. Dwell here. Follow your natural profession. chapter 23.
By what reasoning the learned may know their own nature: and follow a diet agreeable to the spirit. chapter 24.
**Astronomical diligence** in procreating children. In preparing feasts. In buildings / and habitation / and clothing. And how much it is permitted to care for such things. chapter 25.
How through lower things exposed to higher things, the **higher influences** are drawn down. And through worldly materials, especially worldly gifts. chapter 26.

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory dedication of Marsilio Ficino’s *De vita libri tres* (Three Books on Life) to Lorenzo de' Medici. It follows the table of contents and explains the philosophical and personal connection between the author, his patron, and the unity of body and soul through his writings.</meta>

...to live. To these people, therefore, I next gave the book on the long life. But they were distrustful of earthly medicines and remedies in such a great matter. I added a book concerning a life that is both healthy and long, to be obtained from the heavens: so that from the living body of the world itself, a certain more vigorous life might be propagated into our body—which is like a member of the world—just as if from a vine. 

I pray, most indulgent Lorenzo, that you forgive these books of medicine: if, while I wish to be a physician, I am somehow also a poet, even against my will, though perhaps not a good one. For Phoebus <note>The Greek god Apollo, who presided over both healing and the arts.</note> is the author of medicine and the master of poetry; he bestows his life upon us not so much through herbs as through the lyre and song. Moreover, among astrologers, Venus herself produces the musician as much as the physician. 

But thus far, while I have been carefully consulting the life of scholars and those like them, I have neglected the health of my own books, so long as I allowed them to be separated from one another. Therefore, showing them duty <note>original: "pius." Ficino uses the language of familial duty or "piety" to describe reuniting his separate treatises.</note> for the first time, I now join them into a single body. Now that its limbs have been joined into one form, let life be present first. 

However, this physical work—which is, as it were, my body—cannot receive any life except my own. And such a life depends solely upon my soul. But this soul has long lived with you, magnanimous Lorenzo, my patron: especially in that part of your most grand palace where, along with Plato, that work of ours on the immortality of souls <note>Referring to Ficino's major philosophical work, *Theologia Platonica* (Platonic Theology), which he had previously dedicated to Lorenzo.</note> is kept—which was dedicated to your name long ago. 

Yet this soul of mine, even if it leads its life with you in a sort of blessed homeland, is nevertheless—as theologians maintain—restless in the meantime, until it may receive this physical work as its own body. Receive, therefore, best Lorenzo, after those books on souls, these books on the body as well: and with the same breath with which you long ago inspired those, now breathe favorably upon these. For in this way, this body shall live under your spirit through its own soul; and our soul, in turn, shall find rest in your household along with this, its now-joined body.

### GENERAL INSCRIPTION.

->The Book on Life. Divided into Three Books. The First on Healthy Life.<-
->The Second on Long Life. The Third on Life from the Heavens.<-

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening dedication for the first book of Marsilio Ficino’s *Three Books on Life*. It follows the general preface to Lorenzo de' Medici, narrowing the focus to the specific care of scholars' health and introducing his circle of humanist friends in Florence.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<sig>a ii</sig>

# ON THE CARE OF THE HEALTH OF THOSE
## WHO DEVOTE THEMSELVES TO THE STUDY
### OF LETTERS.

Marsilio Ficino of Florence <note>original: "Florentinus"</note> sends greetings <note>The Latin "S. D." stands for "Salutem Dicit," a standard formal greeting meaning "wishes health" or "sends greetings."</note> to Giorgio Antonio Vespucci and Giovanni Battista Boninsegni, men distinguished for their integrity and learning. Recently, while walking about in the manner of the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle, who famously conducted their philosophical discussions while walking in the "peripatos" or covered walkway of the Lyceum in Athens.</note>, we discussed many things concerning the restoration of health for those who diligently apply themselves to the study of letters. These things I have decided to commend especially to you, summarized in a brief compendium. I will not approve of this little work myself, until I have learned that it has been approved by you especially—as my most esteemed friends—nor will I allow it to undergo the most elegant judgment of our Lorenzo de' Medici. Indeed, this work will consult Lorenzo's prosperous health above all, if necessary. For the well-being of scholars—especially our own—will hardly ever be provided for unless their patron, a Maecenas <note>Gaius Maecenas was a wealthy Roman counselor to Augustus and a famous patron of poets like Virgil and Horace; his name became a title for any generous supporter of the arts.</note>, has prospered first. 

Therefore, read diligently, and take the utmost care of your health. For if health is taken away, the sublime doors of the Muses <note>The nine Greek goddesses who presided over the arts and sciences.</note>—unless Almighty God leads us and opens them by some miraculous power—are either never reached by us or are certainly knocked upon in vain. 

Indeed, we wish this physical discussion of ours to look toward this primary argument: namely, if for the sake of attaining wisdom, the health of the body must be so greatly sought, then even more so must the health of the mind be sought, through which alone wisdom can be understood. Otherwise, those who strive to grasp wisdom with an unsound mind seek knowledge in a completely ignorant way. Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine."</note> promises health of the body, while Socrates <note>The Athenian philosopher who emphasized the "care of the soul."</note> promises health of the mind. But only He provides true health of both, who cries out: "Come to me, all you who labor and are burdened, and I will refresh you" <note>original: "Venite ad me omnes/ qui laboratis/ & onerati estis: atq; ego uos reficiam." A quotation from the Gospel of Matthew 11:28.</note>. "I am the way, the truth, and the life" <note>original: "Ego sum uia/ ueritasq; & uita." A quotation from the Gospel of John 14:6.</note>.

<image-desc>Circular library stamp of the National Central Library of Florence (Biblioteca Nazionale Centrale di Firenze). The seal features a central shield with the Roman numeral "XV", surmounted by a crown and two crossed keys, with the library's name encircling the device.</image-desc>

<summary>In this dedication, Marsilio Ficino introduces his treatise on the health of scholars, arguing that physical well-being is a necessary prerequisite for the pursuit of wisdom and the service of the Muses. He emphasizes that while medical and philosophical traditions (Hippocrates and Socrates) offer health, ultimate restoration comes from a divine source.</summary>

<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, health of scholars, Lorenzo de' Medici, patronage, Peripatetic philosophy, Hippocrates, Socrates, relationship between body and mind, medical humanism</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page begins the first chapter of Marsilio Ficino’s *On Caring for the Health of Scholars*, following the dedication to his friends. It establishes the theoretical framework of "nine guides" necessary for the intellectual life, positioning the physician as the final, missing piece of the puzzle.</meta>

### The Nine Guides of Scholars. Chapter 1

¶ Whoever enters upon that rough, steep, and long path which—only after constant labor—finally leads to the high temple of the nine Muses <note>In Greek mythology, the Muses are the nine goddesses of the arts and sciences. Ficino uses the "Temple of the Muses" as a metaphor for reaching the heights of intellectual and artistic achievement.</note>, seems to stand in need of exactly nine guides for this journey. Of these, the first three lead us in heaven. The next three lead us in the mind. The last three lead us on earth. In the beginning, in heaven, **Mercury** either impels or encourages us to undertake the path of the Muses by investigating. Indeed, the duty of all investigation is attributed to Mercury. Then **Phoebus** <note>Another name for Apollo, the god of the sun, music, and prophecy.</note> himself so illuminates both the searching minds and the things being sought with his most abundant splendor, that what we were seeking is clearly found by us. Then comes most graceful **Venus**, the mother of the Graces; with her entirely nurturing and joyful rays, she so seasons and adorns the whole matter that whatever was sought through the instigation of Mercury, and had already been found through the showing of Phoebus, might always delight and be of use, being bathed in a certain marvelous and healthful beauty <margin>|</margin> of Venus. There follow three guides of this journey <margin>|</margin> in the mind: namely, an **ardent and stable will**, <margin>|</margin> **sharpness of wit**, and a **tenacious memory**. The last three are on earth: the **most prudent head of the household** <term>paterfamilias<gloss>The male head of a Roman household, responsible for the management and economic stability of the family unit.</gloss></term>, the **most proven teacher**, and the **most skilled physician**. Without these nine guides, no one has been able, or will ever be able, to reach the temple of the nine Muses itself. Certainly, God almighty and nature granted us the other guides from the beginning. But our own diligence provides the last three. However, many ancient and wise men have treated the precepts and duties that pertain to the head of the household and to the teacher regarding the study of letters; especially our **Plato**, both elsewhere and most diligently in his books on *The Republic* and *The Laws*. Then **Aristotle** in his *Politics*. **Plutarch** also and **Quintilian** <note>Quintilian was a Roman educator whose work *Institutio Oratoria* was the gold standard for Renaissance education.</note> have done so excellently. But until now, only a certain **physician** has been missing for the students of letters—someone to reach out a hand to those who are traveling, and to help with healthful advice and medicines those whom neither heaven, nor the mind, nor the head of the household, nor the teacher has abandoned. I, therefore, having pitied the lot of those who are most hardworking, who travel the difficult path of Minerva <note>The goddess of wisdom. Ficino uses the phrase "Minerva minuentis neruos," a play on words suggesting that the pursuit of wisdom often "diminishes the nerves" or physical strength.</note> which drains the strength, stand ready as the first physician, as it were, for the weak and the sickly.

<summary>Ficino identifies nine necessary guides for the scholar—three celestial, three mental, and three earthly—and argues that while others have written on education and household management, the role of the physician in protecting the scholar's health has been neglected.</summary>
<keywords>Nine Muses, Mercury, Apollo, Venus, Marsilio Ficino, medical care for scholars, education, Plato, Aristotle, Minerva, health and study</keywords>

...but I wish I had a power as complete as my favorable will. Arise now, eager adolescents, with God as your guide. Arise young men and men: whom the too-ardent study of Minerva <note>Minerva is the Roman goddess of wisdom; here her "study" represents intense intellectual labor.</note> weakens too much. Approach the physician willingly: who, with God showing and favoring the way, will grant you the counsels and healthful remedies for the perfection of your pursuit.

## How diligent a care must be had of the brain, the heart, the stomach, and the spirit.
### Chapter 2.

In the beginning, just as runners are accustomed to take care of their legs, athletes of their arms, and musicians of their voice, at the very least scholars of letters ought to take care of the brain, the heart, the liver, and the stomach. Indeed, they should take so much more care as these organs are more excellent than those; and as scholars use these organs more frequently and for more important things than the others use theirs.

Furthermore, any skilled craftsman cares for his instruments most diligently. The painter his brushes; the blacksmith his hammers and anvils; the soldier his horses and weapons; the hunter his dogs and birds; the lute-player his lute; and each similarly his own. Only the priests of the Muses <note>A poetic term for scholars or intellectuals.</note>, only the hunters of the highest good and truth, are so negligent—alas, the shame!—and so unfortunate, that they seem to utterly neglect that instrument by which they are able, in a certain way, to measure and grasp the whole universe.

An instrument of this kind is the <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "spiritus". In Renaissance medicine, "spirit" was a subtle vapor that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the non-physical soul.</note> itself: which among physicians is defined as a certain vapor of the blood—pure, subtle, hot, and clear. And being procreated by the heat of the heart from the more subtle blood, it flies to the brain; there the soul uses it constantly to exercise both the internal and external senses. For this reason, the blood serves the spirit; the spirit serves the senses; and finally, the senses serve reason. 

Blood, however, is produced by the <term>natural virtue</term> <note>original: "uirtute naturali". This was the physiological power believed to reside in the liver and handle nutrition and growth.</note>, which thrives in the liver and stomach. The thinnest part of the blood flows into the fountain of the heart, where the <term>vital virtue</term> <note>The "vital virtue" in the heart was responsible for life-force and heat.</note> thrives. From there, the created spirits ascend to the brain and—if I may put it this way—the citadels of Pallas <note>Another name for Athena or Minerva, goddess of wisdom; Ficino uses "citadels of Pallas" to refer to the brain as the seat of wisdom.</note>. In these, the <term>animal virtue</term> <note>original: "animalis... uis". From the Latin "anima" (soul), this power was responsible for sensation and movement.</note>—that is, the power of sensing and moving—holds dominion. Therefore, contemplation is almost exactly as the service of the sense itself is. Moreover, the sense is such as the spirit is. And the spirit is such as the blood is,

<summary>Marsilio Ficino argues that scholars must care for their internal organs as tools of their trade, explaining how blood is refined into the "spirit" that powers the brain and enables thought.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, spiritus, physiology, brain health, scholars, Minerva, Pallas, medical theory, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino's discussion on the physiological and astrological basis of a scholar's health. It completes the discussion on the "three powers" from the previous page and introduces the central conflict of the scholar's life: the struggle against phlegm and melancholy (black bile).</meta>

...and the three <term>powers</term> <note>original: "vires". In Renaissance medicine, these are the faculties of the soul that govern bodily functions.</note> which we mentioned: namely the natural, the vital, and the <term>animal</term> <note>original: "animalis". This refers to the "anima" or soul, specifically the faculty governing the brain, senses, and movement, rather than "animalistic" behavior.</note>; by which, through which, and in which the spirits themselves are conceived, born, and nourished.

### Scholars are prone to phlegm and black bile. Chapter 4.

Not only must those who are desirous of learning care most diligently for those limbs, powers, and spirits; they are also commanded to avoid <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "pituita". One of the four bodily humors, traditionally viewed as cold and moist.</note> and <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "atra bilis". The humor responsible for the melancholic temperament.</note> no differently than sailors avoid Scylla and Charybdis <note>Two legendary sea monsters from Greek mythology residing on opposite sides of a narrow strait; a metaphor for two equally dangerous threats.</note>. For as much as they are idle in the rest of their body, so much are they busy in their brain and mind. Because of this, they are forced to produce phlegm (which the Greeks call <gloss>phlegma</gloss>) and black bile (which the same people call <gloss>melancholia</gloss>). The former often dulls and suffocates the intellect. The latter, however, if it should abound too much or become inflamed, vexes the soul with constant care and frequent delusions, and disturbs judgment. So it may be said not without merit that scholars would be remarkably healthy, were it not that phlegm is troublesome; and they would be the most joyful and wisest of all, were they not compelled by the defect of black bile to often grieve or sometimes act foolishly.

### The reasons why scholars are, or become, melancholic. Chapter 5.

That scholars are melancholic happens because of three primary types of causes. The first is celestial; the second is natural; the third is human. The celestial cause is because Mercury—who invites us to investigate doctrines—and Saturn—who brings it about that we persevere in investigating and preserve what is discovered—are said by astronomers to be in a certain way cold and dry. Or if by chance Mercury is not cold, he nevertheless often becomes very dry due to his proximity to the Sun. Such is the nature of <term>melancholy</term> according to physicians; and Mercury and Saturn themselves impart this same nature to their followers, the students of letters, from the beginning; and they preserve and increase it daily. The natural cause, however, appears to be this: that to pursue sciences, especially difficult ones, it is necessary for the mind to [withdraw] from external [matters]...

<summary>Ficino explains that scholars are naturally prone to phlegm and black bile (melancholy) due to their sedentary but mentally taxing lifestyle. He attributes this melancholic temperament to the influence of the planets Mercury and Saturn, which share the cold and dry qualities of the humor itself.</summary>
<keywords>melancholy, phlegm, black bile, Mercury, Saturn, humors, Renaissance medicine, scholar's health, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s exploration of why scholars are prone to melancholy. Having previously introduced the "three powers" and the humors of phlegm and black bile, Ficino now explains the physiological and astrological reasons why deep contemplation leads to a melancholic state.</meta>

...to withdraw into internal things as if from a certain circumference to a center. And while the mind contemplates in the very center of the man (so to speak), it remains there most stably. To gather oneself from the circumference to the center, and to be fixed in the center, is a property most characteristic of the earth itself; to which indeed <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "atra bilis". One of the four humors, associated with the element of earth, dryness, and coldness.</note> is very similar. Therefore, black bile provokes the soul to collect itself into one, to stand still in one place, and to contemplate assiduously. Furthermore, being like the center of the world itself, it forces one to investigate toward the center of individual things.

It carries the mind up to the comprehension of the highest things, since it agrees most with <term>Saturn</term> <note>Saturn was considered the highest and slowest-moving planet in the Renaissance cosmos, traditionally associated with deep thought, old age, and melancholy.</note>, the highest of the planets. Contemplation itself, in turn, through a certain constant collection and a kind of compression, contracts a nature very similar to black bile.

There is also a human cause—that is, one arising from within us. Since the frequent agitation of the mind violently dries out the brain. Therefore, when the moisture—which is the "fuel" <note>original: "pabulum", literally "fodder" or "nourishment".</note> of the natural heat—is largely consumed, the heat itself is also usually extinguished. From this, the nature of the brain becomes dry and cold. This is indeed called a "terrestrial" and <term>melancholic</term> <note>meaning "of the nature of black bile," from the Greek <gloss>melas</gloss> (black) and <gloss>chole</gloss> (bile).</note> quality.

Furthermore, because of the constant motion of inquiry, the <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "spiritus". In Renaissance medicine, these were subtle fluids that carried life and thought through the body.</note> also, being continually moved, are dissolved. It is necessary for these dissolved spirits to be restored from the more subtle parts of the blood. Wherefore, since the clearer and more subtle parts of the blood are often consumed, the remaining blood is necessarily rendered thick, dry, and black.

Added to this is the fact that nature, being entirely intent upon the brain and heart during contemplation, deserts the stomach and the liver. Therefore, when the food (especially if it is either too rich or too hard) is poorly digested, the blood produced from it becomes cold, thick, and black. Finally, because of the excessive stillness of the limbs, neither are the waste products excreted, nor do the thick and dark vapors evaporate. All these things tend to produce a melancholic spirit and a sorrowful and fearful soul.

For indeed, internal darkness, much more than external darkness, fills the soul with sorrow and terrifies it. Most of all, however, of all men of letters, those are oppressed by black bile who, being devoted to the diligent study of philosophy, withdraw the mind from the body and bodily things, and join it to incorporeal things. This is both because the work is quite difficult and requires a greater intensity of the mind, and because, insofar as they [seek] the truth with the mind and in the body...

<summary>Ficino explains how the act of deep study mimics the properties of the element earth and the planet Saturn, leading to a physiological buildup of "black bile" (melancholy) in the scholar’s body.</summary>
<keywords>black bile, Saturn, contemplation, brain health, spirits, digestion, melancholy, philosophy, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical and philosophical defense of the "scholarly temperament." Having explained how the humor of black bile (melancholy) physically mimics the soul's withdrawal into deep thought, he now transitions into how this intense mental focus can actually alienate the soul from the body, leading to the famous "divine frenzy" associated with genius.</meta>

...they join, and are compelled to separate from the body to that extent. From this, their body is sometimes rendered as if half-alive and melancholic. This is indeed what our Plato signifies in the *Timaeus* <note>One of Plato's most influential dialogues, dealing with the creation of the world and the nature of the soul and body.</note>, saying that the soul, contemplating divine things most frequently and intensely, grows so powerful on such nourishment that it exceeds its own body beyond what the nature of the body can endure; and by its more violent agitations, it sometimes either escapes the body in a certain way, or sometimes seems as if it might dissolve it.

### Why melancholics are gifted, and what kind of melancholics are like this, and what kind are the opposite. Chapter 5.

<margin>—</margin>So far, let it suffice to have shown why the <term>priests of the Muses</term> <note>original: "musarum sacerdotes". A poetic way of describing scholars, poets, and philosophers dedicated to learning.</note> are either melancholic from the start, or become so through study, by first celestial, second natural, and third human reasons. Aristotle confirms this indeed in his book of *Problems*. For he says that all men who have been outstanding in any field were melancholics. In this matter, he confirmed that Platonic saying written in the book *On Knowledge* <note>original: "libro de scientia". Ficino likely refers to the *Theaetetus*, where Plato discusses the nature of genius and the philosopher's "dizziness."</note>: namely, that the gifted are usually very agitated and prone to frenzy. Democritus also says that *no men can ever be great in genius except those who are stirred by a certain frenzy.* Our Plato seems to prove this indeed in the *Phaedrus*, saying that the gates of poetry are knocked upon in vain without frenzy. And even if he perhaps wants <term>divine frenzy</term> <note>original: "divinum furorem". A state of inspired "madness" where the soul is moved by a higher power, which Ficino believed was the source of all great art and philosophy.</note> to be understood here, nevertheless, among physicians, such frenzy is never incited in any others except melancholics. Next, however, we must assign the reasons why Democritus, Plato, and Aristotle assert that some melancholics sometimes so excel everyone in genius that they seem not human but rather divine. Democritus, Plato, and Aristotle undoubtedly assert this. Yet they do not seem to explain the reason for such a great matter sufficiently. Nevertheless, with God showing the way, we must dare to investigate the causes. Melancholy, that is...

<summary>Ficino explains how intense contemplation can cause the soul to "outgrow" or separate from the body, and cites Aristotle, Plato, and Democritus to argue that all truly great men possess a melancholic temperament associated with divine frenzy.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Aristotle, Democritus, melancholy, divine frenzy, Timaeus, Phaedrus, genius, scholarly temperament</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical-philosophical exploration of melancholy. Having established in the previous page that scholars are prone to this temperament, Ficino now begins a technical classification of "black bile," distinguishing between the healthy, "natural" kind that fosters genius and the "burned" kind that leads to madness or lethargy.</meta>

<term>Black bile</term> <note>original: "atra bilis". Also known as melancholy, one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids believed in ancient medicine to govern health and temperament.</note> is of two kinds. One is indeed called "natural" by physicians. The other, however, occurs through <term>combustion</term> <note>original: "adustione". In medieval medicine, "adustion" or "burning" of a humor changed its properties, often making it toxic or volatile.</note>. That natural kind is nothing other than a certain denser and drier part of the blood. The burned kind is distributed into four species. For it is conceived either by the burning of natural melancholy, or of purer blood, or of <term>yellow bile</term> <note>original: "bilis". In this context, yellow bile (choler) as opposed to black bile.</note>, or of salty <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "pituita". The cold, moist humor associated with lethargy.</note>. 

Whatever is born from combustion harms judgment and wisdom. Truly, while that humor is ignited and glows, it usually makes men agitated and raving. This is what the Greeks call <term>mania</term>, but we call <term>fury</term>. 

But when it is finally extinguished—the finer and clearer parts having been dissolved, and only a foul soot remaining—it renders men dull and stupid. This condition they properly call melancholy, and madness, and senselessness. 

Therefore, only that black bile which we called natural is conducive to our judgment and wisdom. Yet not always. To be sure, if it exists alone, by its too dark and dense mass, it darkens the <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "spiritus". In Renaissance medicine, these were subtle vapors that carried signals between the soul and the body.</note>, terrifies the soul, and blunts the intellect. 

But if it is mixed with simple phlegm, when cold blood stands still around the heart, it brings on sluggishness and torpor through a certain thick coldness. And as is the nature of every very dense matter, when melancholy of this kind grows cold, it is stretched to the peak of coldness. In which state nothing is hoped for; all things are feared; it is a weariness to look upon the vault of heaven. 

If black bile—whether simple or mixed—putrefies, it generates a <term>quartan fever</term> <note>A fever that recurs every third day (on a four-day cycle), historically associated with the spleen and melancholy.</note>, tumors of the spleen, and many things of the same kind. Where it overflows too much, whether it be alone or joined with phlegm, it makes the spirits thicker and colder. It continuously affects the soul with weariness. It dulls the edge of the mind; nor does the "blood leap in Arcadian fashion" <note>original: "falit arcadico... fanguis". A reference to the Roman satirist Persius, implying a lack of wit or a "thick" mind, as Arcadians were stereotyped as rustic or dull.</note> around the heart. 

However, it is necessary that black bile be neither so scant that the blood, yellow bile, and spirit lack a "bridle," as it were—from which it happens that the intellect is unstable and the memory is fleeting—nor so much that, weighed down by too much weight, we seem to drowse and stand in need of spurs. 

Furthermore, it is entirely necessary for it to be as refined as its nature allows. For if it has been thinned out as much as possible according to its nature, it will perhaps be able without [harm]...

<summary>Ficino distinguishes between "natural" black bile, which acts as a stabilizing "bridle" for the mind, and "burned" black bile, which causes mania when hot and profound depression or stupidity when cold.</summary>
<keywords>black bile, melancholy, humoral theory, mania, adustion, natural melancholy, medical spirits, quartan fever</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical-philosophical exploration of melancholy. Having previously distinguished between "natural" melancholy (good for the soul) and "burned" melancholy (dangerous to the mind), he now describes the perfect physical recipe for the melancholic genius, focusing on the proportions of the four humors.</meta>

...it also needs to be plentiful. And even so much, that it seems to equal at least the weight of <term>yellow bile</term> <note>original: "bilem". In this system, yellow bile (choler) is hot and dry, while black bile is cold and dry.</note>. Let <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "atra bilis"</note>, therefore, be abundant, but very thin. Let it not lack the surrounding moisture of a finer <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "pituitæ". Phlegm is cold and moist; here it acts as a lubricant to prevent the black bile from becoming too brittle.</note>, lest it dry out completely and become extremely hard. 

It should not, however, be mixed entirely with phlegm, especially if it is cold or plentiful, lest the mixture grow cold. But let it be mixed with yellow bile and blood to such an extent that one body is fashioned out of the three: composed of a double proportion of blood compared to the other two. In this mixture, there should exist eight parts of <term>blood</term>, two parts of yellow bile, and again two portions of black bile. 

Let the black bile be ignited somewhat by those two humors, and once ignited, let it glow—not burn. Otherwise, as tends to happen with harder matter when it boils too much, it might burn and agitate the person too violently. For when it cools down, it similarly cools to the extreme. 

Indeed, black bile is like iron: when it is directed toward the cold, it is extremely cold; when, on the contrary, it turns strongly toward heat, it is extremely hot. Nor should it seem strange that black bile can be easily ignited and, once ignited, burn more intensely. Since we see <term>lime</term> <note>original: "calcem". Quicklime reacts violently with water, generating heat; Ficino uses this as a chemical analogy for how dry humors react.</note>, which is similar to it, immediately boil and burn up when water is poured over it. 

Melancholy has such power toward either extreme because of the unity of its stable and fixed nature. Such extremes do not happen to the other humors. When it is supremely hot, it produces the greatest boldness—indeed, ferocity. When it is extremely cold, however, it produces extreme fear and cowardice. 

But when it is affected in various ways by the middle degrees between cold and heat, it produces various dispositions. This is not unlike how strong, undiluted wine is accustomed to bring on various moods in those drinking to intoxication, or even those drinking just a little too freely. 

Therefore, it is necessary that black bile be appropriately <term>tempered</term> <note>original: "temperata". In medieval medicine, "tempering" meant balancing the qualities of hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>. When it is moderated thus, as we said, and mixed with yellow bile and blood—because it is dry by nature and, as much as its nature allows, very thin—it is easily ignited by them. Because it is solid and the most tenacious of the humors, once ignited, it blazes for a very long time. Because it is most powerful in the unity of its tenacious dryness, it grows hot more violently. Just as wood, compared to straw, if both are ignited, remains hot and glows more and for longer. And from that long-lasting and violent heat, a great brilliance and a violent and long-lasting movement proceed. To this point returns that saying of <term>Heraclitus</term> <note>Heraclitus was a pre-Socratic philosopher known for his focus on fire and change. The quote beginning here is "A dry light is the wisest soul."</note>: Light

<summary>Ficino defines the ideal "recipe" for a genius's temperament: a specific 8:2:2 ratio of blood, yellow bile, and black bile. He explains that while black bile is dangerous in extremes (like iron or lime), when properly balanced, it provides a long-lasting intellectual "glow" that straw-like humors cannot sustain.</summary>
<keywords>humoral theory, black bile, blood, yellow bile, phlegm, temperament, medical ratios, Heraclitus, quicklime analogy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical-philosophical exploration of melancholy. Having previously discussed the ideal proportions of the humors—specifically the blend of blood, yellow bile, and black bile—he now describes the physical appearance and the psychological effects of this "perfect" melancholic humor.</meta>

A dry soul is the wisest. <note>original: "sicca anima sapientissima." A famous aphorism attributed to the Greek philosopher Heraclitus, suggesting that a soul free from the "moisture" of base passions is more intellectually capable.</note>

### In what way black bile contributes to genius.

¶ Someone might ask what kind of body that humor is—the one fused from those three humors in the proportion we mentioned. Its color is almost like that of the gold we see, but it leans somewhat toward purple. And when it is ignited, whether by natural heat or by the movement of the body or soul, it glows and shines much like glowing, red gold mixed with purple. And like a rainbow <note>original: "Iris"</note>, it draws forth various colors while the heart is burning. Someone will ask again in what way such a humor contributes to <term>genius</term> <note>original: "ingenio". In the Renaissance, this referred to a person's natural talent or innate intellectual capacity.</note>. Truly, the <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "spiritus". In Ficino's medical theory, these are thin, vaporous substances that act as the bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note> created from this humor are, first of all, subtle—no different than that "water of life" <note>original: "aqua vitae". Referring to distilled alcohol or brandy, which was a relatively recent and wondrous discovery in medieval and Renaissance chemistry.</note> which they also call "water of the vine" or "burning water," as often as it is expressed from thicker wine by a certain distillation over fire, as is usually done. For the spirits, compressed within the narrower channels of such <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "atrx bilis"</note>, are thinned out to the highest degree by a heat that is more intense because of its concentration; and once expressed through these narrower channels, they break forth as more subtle.

Second, they are similarly hotter, and for the same reason, brighter. Third, they are agile in motion and most powerful in action. Fourth, constantly emanating from a solid and stable humor, they serve the mind's action for a very long time. Relying on such service, our soul investigates intensely. It perseveres longer in its searching. It easily finds whatever it has investigated; it perceives clearly; it judges sincerely; and it retains what has been judged for a long time.

Add to this that, as we indicated in the previous sections, the soul—using such an instrument or stimulus that in a certain way corresponds to the center of the world and (so to speak) collects the soul into its own center—always seeks the centers of all things and penetrates to their innermost secrets. Moreover, it corresponds with <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Saturn</term>. The latter of these, the highest of all the planets, carries the investigator to the highest things. Hence unique philosophers emerge. Especially since the soul is thus called away from external motions and from its own body, and is as close as possible to divine things, and of divine...

<sig>a vi</sig>

<summary>Ficino explains how a specific "golden" blend of humors creates refined 'spirits' through a process like distillation, enabling the soul to achieve deep concentration and philosophical insight. He links this physical state to the influence of the planets Mercury and Saturn, which drive the mind toward the centers and heights of knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>black bile, genius, spirits, Mercury, Saturn, aqua vitae, distillation, philosophy, humors, concentration</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion on the nature of black bile and its effects on the mind. Having previously established that a specific, well-tempered version of this humor creates the "spirits" necessary for deep thought, he now transitions into the dangers that scholars face when this balance is disrupted.</meta>

...becomes an instrument. Hence, filled with divine influences and oracles from on high, he always devises new and unusual things and predicts the future. This is affirmed not only by Democritus and Plato, but also by Aristotle in his book of *Problems*, and Avicenna in his *Book of Divine Things* and his *Book on the Soul*. Why so many words about the humor of black bile? So that we might remember that just as this "bright bile" <note>original: "candida bilis". Ficino uses this term to distinguish the healthy, "shining" version of the melancholic humor—which promotes genius—from the toxic, "burnt" version.</note> is to be sought and nourished as the best, its opposite—as we have said—is to be avoided as the worst. For it is such a dreadful thing that Serapio <note>A reference to the 12th-century physician Ibn Sarabi.</note> said its impulse is incited by an evil demon, and the wise Avicenna <note>The influential Persian polymath Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037).</note> did not deny it.

->There are five principal enemies of scholars: Phlegm, Black<-
->Bile, Sexual Intercourse, Over-eating, and Morning Sleep.<-

<margin>Chapter 7</margin>
To return to the point from which we have now wandered for some time: the road that leads to truth and wisdom is very long, full of heavy labors on both land and sea. Therefore, whoever undertakes this journey—as a certain poet might say—is often in danger on land and sea. For if they sail the sea, they are tossed about continuously between two waves—that is, two humors: namely, <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "pituitam". In humoral theory, phlegm was considered cold and moist, often leading to sluggishness.</note> and that harmful melancholy—as if between Scylla and Charybdis <note>Two famous mythological sea monsters from Homer’s Odyssey, representing a choice between two equally dangerous options.</note>. Or if they travel by land (so to speak), three monsters immediately present themselves. First, the earthly Venus and Priapus <note>The gods of sexual desire and fertility, representing carnal temptation.</note> nourish the first monster. Second are Bacchus and Ceres <note>The gods of wine and grain, representing the dangers of over-indulgence in food and drink.</note>. Third, the nocturnal Hecate <note>The goddess of ghosts and the night, here associated with the "fog" of excessive sleep.</note> frequently opposes them. Therefore, Apollo must be called from the heavens, Neptune from the sea, and Hercules from the earth: so that Apollo may pierce these monsters—enemies of <term>Pallas</term> <note>Athena/Pallas, the goddess of wisdom and patron of scholars.</note>—with his arrows, Neptune may tame them with his trident, and Hercules may crush and tear them with his club. The first monster is indeed sexual intercourse, especially if it exceeds one’s strength even a little. For it suddenly exhausts the <term>spirits</term> <note>The vital vapors that Ficino believes act as the link between the body and the soul's intellectual faculties.</note>, particularly the finer ones; it weakens the brain, and shatters the stomach and the vital organs. Nothing can be more hostile to genius than this evil. For why did Hippocrates judge sexual intercourse to be similar to the "falling sickness" <note>original: "comitiali morbo". An ancient term for epilepsy.</note>, if not because it strikes the mind—which is sacred—and causes such great harm?

<summary>Ficino distinguishes between "bright" melancholy (genius) and its demonic, toxic counterpart, before listing the five physical "enemies" that hinder a scholar's progress. He uses mythological allegories to describe how bodily humors and physical indulgences like sex and over-eating threaten the intellectual spirit.</summary>
<keywords>melancholy, Avicenna, five enemies of scholars, phlegm, sexual intercourse, mythology, Hippocrates, vital spirits, epilepsy</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical and philosophical advice for scholars. He is currently listing the "five enemies" of the studious life, having previously discussed Phlegm and Black Bile. He now moves to the third, fourth, and fifth dangers: sexual excess, gluttony, and improper sleep cycles.</meta>

...as Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> said in his *Book of Animals*: if more semen flows out during intercourse than nature can tolerate, it is more harmful than if forty times as much blood had flowed out. *Thus, it was not without reason that the ancients wished the Muses and Minerva <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and strategic warfare.</note> to be virgins. That Platonic saying looks toward this:* When Venus threatened the Muses that she would arm her son <note>Cupid, the god of desire.</note> against them unless they practiced her sacred sexual rites, the Muses replied: "O Venus, threaten Mars with such things; for your Cupid does not fly among us." Finally, nature has separated no sense further from intelligence than the sense of touch <note>Ficino, following Plato, believed that while sight and hearing were "higher" senses that led to the soul, touch was the most "material" and "low" sense, often distracting from intellectual pursuits.</note>.

<margin>The second monster is the fullness of wine and food.</margin>*The second monster is the satiety of wine and food. Indeed, if wine is either too much or too hot and intense,* it will fill the head itself with the worst humors and foul vapors. I pass over the fact that drunkenness makes men insane. But too much food, first of all, calls back all the power of nature to the stomach for the sake of digestion. As a result, one cannot attend to the head and to contemplation at the same time. Furthermore, if food is poorly digested, it blunts the edge of the mind with many thick vapors and humors. Even if it is sufficiently digested, as Galen <note>A 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories on the four humors dominated Western medicine.</note> says, the soul—suffocated by fat and blood—cannot perceive anything celestial. Finally, the third monster is staying awake late into the night, especially after dinner; *which then forces you to sleep after sunrise. Since many scholars err and deceive themselves in this,* I will explain more fully why the fourth thing harms one's genius <note>original: "ingenio". In this context, it refers to the innate intellectual capacity or "spark" of the scholar.</note>, and I will bring forward seven principal reasons: the first from heaven itself; the second from the elements; the third from the humors; the fourth from the order of things; the fifth from the nature of the stomach; the sixth from the spirits <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were subtle substances that acted as the bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note>; and the seventh derived from the imagination.

<margin>}</margin>In the beginning, three planets—just as we said in the previous sections—greatly favor contemplation and eloquence: the Sun, Venus, and Mercury. These, moving together at almost the same pace, flee from us as night approaches. But when day is either approaching or already rising, they rise again and visit us once more. After sunrise, however, they are said to be found in the twelfth region of the sky, which astronomers assign to prison and darkness <note>In traditional astrology, the 12th House is known as the "House of Self-Undoing" or "Imprisonment." Ficino suggests that even if the Sun is up, its position in this specific house makes it unfavorable for intellectual work.</note>. Therefore, it is not those who write either at night when these planets flee us, or during the day after the sun has risen when they enter the house of prison and darkness, but those who write near the time they are seeking their rising, or are already rising, for contemplation...

<summary>Ficino warns scholars against the "monsters" of sexual excess, gluttony, and late-night study, arguing that these activities physically and spiritually block the mind. He begins a detailed astrological and medical defense of waking at dawn, citing the influence of the Sun, Venus, and Mercury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical and philosophical advice from "On Caring for the Health of Scholars." Having discussed the dangers of excess, he now outlines five specific reasons why the morning is the only proper time for intellectual labor, based on the relationship between the human body and the natural world.</meta>

...and while they too rise up, those alone observe most sharply, and write and compose their findings most eloquently. 

The **second reason**, namely from the elements <note>In this context, "elements" refers to the atmospheric conditions of the air surrounding us.</note>, is as follows. When the sun rises, the air is moved, thinned, and becomes bright; but when the sun sets, the opposite occurs. Both the **blood** and the <term>spirits</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" (spiritus) were not ghosts, but a subtle, vaporous substance produced by the blood that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the soul, carrying out functions of thought and movement.</note> are necessarily compelled to follow the motion and quality of the surrounding air and its similar nature. 

The **third reason**, which is drawn from the humors <note>The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which were believed to determine a person's health and temperament.</note>, is of this kind. At dawn, the blood moves and reigns; through this motion, it is thinned, warmed, and brightened. The spirits are accustomed to follow and imitate the blood. But as night approaches, that thicker and colder <term>melancholy</term> <note>Black bile, associated with sadness and sluggishness.</note> and the <term>pituita</term> <note>Phlegm; a cold, damp humor believed to cause dullness of mind.</note> begin to dominate; these undoubtedly render the spirits entirely unfit for contemplation. 

The **fourth reason**, drawn from the order of things, will be this. Day is assigned to wakefulness, and night to sleep. For when the sun either approaches our hemisphere or moves directly over it, it opens the passages of the body with its rays and dilates the humors and spirits from the center of the body toward the surface. This indeed excites and leads us toward wakefulness and action. Conversely, when the sun recedes, everything is constricted. This invites sleep by a certain natural order, especially after the third or fourth part of the night. 

Therefore, whoever slumbers in the morning, when the sun and the world are waking up, but stays awake late into the night, when nature orders us to sleep and rest from our labors, undoubtedly fights against both the order of the universe and his own nature. He is disturbed and distracted by contrary motions. Indeed, while the universe moves toward the extremities <note>The "expansion" caused by the sun's heat during the day.</note>, he moves himself toward his internal parts <note>The inward focus required for study.</note>. And conversely: while he is drawn by the universe toward the internal parts <note>The "contraction" and rest of nighttime.</note>, he meanwhile pulls himself back toward the extremities. Therefore, by this perverse order and these contrary motions, both the whole body and the spirits and intellect are utterly weakened.

In the **fifth place**, we argue from the nature of the **stomach** in this manner. By the long action of the daytime air, the stomach is greatly dilated as the pores are opened; as the spirits fly out, it is finally greatly weakened. Therefore, as night comes on, the stomach demands a new supply of spirits to be nourished. For this reason, whoever begins long and difficult contemplations at that time strives to pull those very spirits back toward the head. These spirits, being thus distracted, satisfy neither the needs of the stomach nor those of the head. Especially...

<summary>Ficino presents five arguments based on astrology and humoral theory to prove that scholars must study in the morning and sleep at night to avoid weakening their minds and bodies. He explains how the sun's movement dictates the expansion and contraction of bodily "spirits," and how working against this natural cycle causes physical and intellectual decay.</summary>

<keywords>circadian rhythm, humoral theory, spirits (pneuma), melancholy, phlegm, stomach health, solar influence, Renaissance medicine, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical advice regarding the optimal times for study. Having previously listed four reasons why the morning is superior to the night, he now concludes with the fifth, sixth, and seventh reasons, focusing on digestion, the "spirits," and the mental faculty of imagination.</meta>

Indeed, it is harmful if, working by lamplight <note>original: *lucubrantes*, refers to the practice of "lucubration," or staying up late to study by candlelight.</note> for a long time after dinner, we apply ourselves too attentively to studies of this kind. For at that time, the stomach requires many <term>spirits</term> <note>In this medical tradition, "spirits" are subtle vapors that facilitate bodily functions like digestion and thought.</note> and much heat to digest food. However, these two things are diverted to the head by such late-night study and labor. As a result, they suffice neither for the brain nor for the stomach. Add to this that the head, because of this movement, is filled with the thicker vapors rising from the food. Meanwhile, the food in the stomach, deprived of heat and spirits, remains raw and rots. From this, the head is again filled with impurities and harmed. 

Finally, in the morning hours when one must rise so that the individual limbs may be purged of all the waste products released during sleep—then, what is worst of all, the same person who had completely interrupted his <term>digestion</term> <note>original: *concoctionem*, literally "cooking," the process by which the stomach's heat transforms food.</note> by working at night is compelled to further delay the expulsion of waste by sleeping late into the morning. All physicians believe this to be extremely harmful to both the intellect and the body. Justly, therefore, those who use the night for the day and the day for the night—acting against nature, like owls—imitate owls in this way even if they do not wish to: just as the owls' eyes are dimmed under the light of the sun, so the sharpness of these people's minds is dimmed under the splendor of truth.

In the **sixth place**, the same point is proven from the spirits. By the fatigue of the day, the spirits—especially the most refined ones—are eventually dispersed. Therefore, at night, only a few thick spirits remain, which are entirely unfit for literary studies; the intellect, relying on these "crippled wings," can no more fly than bats or horned owls. On the contrary, in the morning after sleep, once the spirits are refreshed and the limbs so strengthened that they need very little support from the spirits, many refined spirits are present to serve the brain. These can follow commands more quickly since they are very little occupied with warming and governing the limbs.

Finally, the **seventh reason** is derived from the nature of the <term>phantasia</term> <note>original: *phantasia*, the faculty of the mind that stores and combines images or "phantasms" received from the senses.</note>. The phantasia—whether it should be called imagination, thought, or by any other name—is distracted and disturbed by staying awake among many, long, and conflicting images, thoughts, and cares. This distraction and disturbance is utterly contrary to subsequent contemplation, which demands a completely tranquil and serene mind. Only by the rest of the night is that agitation finally settled and calmed. Therefore, as night approaches, the mind is always disturbed; but as it recedes, the mind is for the most part tranquil for...

<summary>Ficino argues against late-night study ("lucubration"), explaining that it ruins digestion and depletes the refined "spirits" necessary for clear thinking. He concludes that only morning study, following the restorative rest of the night, allows the mind to be truly serene and sharp.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s medical advice regarding the optimal times for study. Having previously criticized the "night owls" who study by candlelight, Ficino now concludes his argument for morning study by citing classical and biblical authorities before providing a specific morning routine for the scholar.</meta>

...we apply ourselves to our studies. Truly, whoever attempts to judge things with an excessively agitated mind is no different from those who suffer from vertigo and think everything is spinning (as Plato says), while they themselves are the ones spinning. Therefore, Aristotle wisely in his *Economics* <note>original: *economicis*, referring to a work on household management attributed to Aristotle.</note> orders one to rise before light; he asserts that it is most beneficial both for bodily health and for the study of philosophy. 

But this must be understood in such a way that, by having a light and moderate dinner, we most diligently avoid morning indigestion <note>original: *cruditatem*, literally "rawness," referring to undigested food in the stomach that creates harmful vapors.</note>. Finally, that holy prophet David, the trumpet of Almighty God, never says he rises in the evening, but always in the morning and at daybreak to sing to his God with the lyre and psalms. Indeed, we ought to rise in mind at that hour entirely, and soon after also in body, if only it can be done conveniently.

## Which hour is more opportune for beginning studies, or what the manner of continuing them should be. Chapter VIII.

From those things which were discussed above, it is now almost sufficiently clear that we should begin our studies opportunely: either immediately as the sun rises, or at least one or at most two hours before the rising of the sun. But before you rise from bed, first rub your whole body for a little while and gently with your palms. Then rub the head with your nails, but do that a little more lightly. 

In this matter, let Hippocrates advise you. For he says that by <term>friction</term> <note>Massage or rubbing was a key part of ancient and Renaissance preventative medicine to move the "humors."</note>, if it is vigorous, the body is hardened; if light, it is softened; if much, it is reduced; if moderate, it is filled out. When you have risen from bed, do not immediately devote yourself to sudden reading and contemplation. Instead, allow at least half an hour for any kind of purging <note>Refers to the expulsion of bodily wastes and clearing the senses.</note>. 

Soon after, gird yourself diligently for contemplation, which you shall prolong for about one hour according to your strength. Then you shall relax the focus of your mind for a little while; and in the meantime, with an ivory comb, you shall diligently and moderately comb your head from the forehead toward the neck, drawing the comb forty times. Then rub the neck with a rougher cloth. Finally, having returned to contemplating, dedicate two additional hours, or at least one hour, to study. Studies can indeed sometimes be extended—though with some occasional intermission having been made—up to the noon hour. Moreover, sometimes...

<summary>Ficino cites Aristotle, Plato, and King David to support rising early, then provides a meticulous morning regimen involving massage, "purging," and combing the hair to prepare the mind for study.</summary>

...since we are rarely forced to take food in the meantime until about two hours after midday. For the Sun is powerful around its rising; it is powerful in the middle of the sky; and also in that region of the sky which immediately follows the middle (the zenith)—which astronomers call the **Ninth House** <note>In traditional astrology, the Ninth House is associated with travel, philosophy, and higher learning.</note> and the **House of Wisdom**—the Sun rejoices most. Since all poets wish **Phoebus** <note>original: *Phębū*, another name for Apollo, the god of light, prophecy, and the arts.</note> to be the leader of the **Muses** and the sciences, it is only right that if something deeper must be thought out, it should be considered especially during these hours. If the Muses are to be sought, let them be sought during these same hours under the leadership of Phoebus. For the remaining hours seem better suited for reading the ancients and the works of others, rather than for thinking out new and original ideas of one’s own.

However, we must always remember that in any given hour, the intense focus of the mind should be relaxed at least once for a little while. For since the **spirits** <note>original: *spūs* (spiritus). In Renaissance medicine, these were subtle vapors that carried commands from the soul to the body; Ficino believed intense study "burned" or exhausted them.</note> are dissipated because of such concentration, you will deservedly become sluggish if you never cease to unbend. While you labor with your mind, meanwhile let the body rest. Fatigue of the body is bad; that of the mind is worse; but fatigue of both together is the worst of all, pulling a man apart with opposing motions and destroying his life. Finally, let meditation not proceed further than the point of pleasure. Indeed, let it stop just short of it.

## How phlegm is to be avoided. Chapter IX.

It seems worthwhile to briefly repeat those things which we said were harmful to the learned, and to apply remedies to each. Therefore, so that **phlegm** <term>pituita: one of the four bodily humors, characterized as cold and moist; Ficino warns that its buildup causes dullness of mind.</term> is not excessively increased, exercise should be used twice daily on an almost empty **stomach** <note>original: *stomachus*, which in this period often referred to the entire digestive system.</note>; yet this exercise should never be laborious, lest the sharp spirits be dissipated. Waste products should be very diligently purged from all passages of the body. Filth should be thoroughly wiped away from the skin of the whole body, and especially the head, both by washing and by rubbing. 

One should avoid foods that are excessively cold; and unless **black bile** <term>atra bilis: the humor associated with Melancholy.</term> prevents it, one should also avoid moist foods, and generally all those that are fatty, virulent, viscous, oily, and sticky, or those which tend to rot easily. If the stomach is cold either by nature or by age, the drinking of water should either be given up entirely or certainly reduced. Food should be moderate, but drink should be more moderate still. The dwelling should be high up, very far removed from heavy and cloudy air. Humidity should be expelled both by the use of fire and of warm scents. Cold must be kept away from the head, especially...

...indeed the neck and the feet: for it is very harmful to the intellect. A moderate use of **spices** <note>original: *aromatū*</note> in colder meals is beneficial, especially **nutmeg**, **cinnamon**, and **saffron**; also preserved **ginger** <note>original: *Zinziberis*</note> in the morning on an empty stomach, which is most helpful for the senses and memory.

### By what method black bile should be avoided. Chapter 10.

Indeed, those things that follow increase that terrible **black bile** <term>atra bilis: also known as melancholy, one of the four humors; an excess was thought to cause depression and madness in scholars</term> which we detested in the preceding sections: thick and turbid wine, especially dark wine. Hard, dry, salty, sharp, pungent, old, burnt, roasted, and fried foods; beef and hare; old cheese; salted meats; legumes, especially beans and lentils; **eggplants** <note>original: *melōgia*</note>; arugula; cabbage; mustard; radishes; garlic; onions; leeks; mulberries; **carrots** <note>original: *carioţę*</note>; and whatever heats or cools while simultaneously drying, and all black things. Anger, fear, pity, grief, idleness, solitude, and whatever offends the sight, smell, and hearing; but of all things, especially darkness. Furthermore, excessive drying of the body—whether arising from long vigils, or from much agitation of the mind, or worry, or frequent sexual intercourse; also the use of things that are very hot and dry; or some immoderate purging and bowel movement, or laborious exercise, or hunger, thirst, heat, a drier wind, or cold. Since black bile is always extremely dry, and also cold (though not equally so), one must certainly resist it with things that are moderately warm and, above all, moist. Use boiled foods regularly, which are easily digested and produce a refined and very clear blood. But in the meantime, so that consideration may be given to the stomach and to **phlegm** <note>original: *pituitæ*</note> just as to the black bile, let the meals be seasoned with cinnamon, saffron, and sandalwood. The seeds of **melons** <note>original: *peponis*</note> and cucumbers are beneficial, as are washed pine nuts. All dairy products are suitable: milk, fresh cheese, and **sweet almonds** <note>original: *amigdalæ dulces*</note>. Suitable too are the meats of birds and young chickens, or suckling four-footed animals; especially soft-boiled eggs; and from the parts of animals, the brain; sweet apples, pears, peaches, melons, **damson plums** <note>original: *pruna damaſcena*</note>, and similar things. **Squash** <note>original: *Cucurbita*</note> properly cooked and moist, non-viscous herbs are good; but cherries, figs, and grapes I praise very little. Indeed, I strongly detest nausea and satiety...
