<meta>This page continues the discussion on countering the effects of "black bile" or melancholy, a condition Ficino believes plagues scholars due to their intense mental efforts. The previous page listed foods and habits to avoid; this page provides the remedies, focusing on diet, aromatics, environment, and music.</meta>

<sig>b ii</sig>

...stors. Nothing, however, is more powerful against this plague than **wine** that is light, clear, and sweet-smelling, which is most suitable for generating **spirits** <term>spiritus: in Renaissance medicine, these were subtle vapors in the body that linked the soul to the physical organs</term> that are clearer than any others. For as it pleases **Plato** and **Aristotle**, this humor <note>black bile</note> is softened, sweetened, and cleared by this wine in the same way as either lupin beans are soaked in water or iron is heated in flames. Truly, as much as its use benefits the spirits and the intellect, so much does its abuse cause harm. Furthermore, it is agreed that it is beneficial to infuse **gold** or **silver**—especially when glowing hot—or their leaves into drinking cups or into broth itself; and to drink from a gold or silver vessel and to take meals from them.

Likewise, it is very useful if the juice of **licorice** is frequently swallowed on an empty stomach; also the juice of a sweet **pomegranate** and a sweet **orange**. Sweet scents are of no small benefit, especially tempered ones: but if cold reigns, they should lean toward the warm; if heat dominates, they should verge toward the cold. They should therefore be tempered from **roses**, **violets**, **myrtle**, **camphor**, **sandalwood**, and **rose water**, which are cold. On the other hand, use **cinnamon**, **citron**, **orange**, **cloves**, **mint**, **lemon balm** <note>original: *melissa*</note>, **saffron**, **aloe wood**, **ambergris**, and **musk**, which are warm. 

Spring flowers are especially helpful, as are the leaves of the citron or orange, and fragrant apples; but most of all, wine. Scents of this kind should be inhaled through the nostrils as suits each person, and applied to the chest and stomach. Truly, we do not approve of scents that are very warm and dry if they are used alone and continuously. A **hyacinth** <note>original: *iacintus*; likely referring to the gemstone jacinth or zircon, which was believed to have medicinal properties when held in the mouth</note> should be held in the mouth, which greatly cheers the soul. **Holy herb** <note>original: *Hierobotanum*</note>—that is, wild **clary sage** <note>original: *sciarea silvestris*</note>—is also beneficial both in food and in scent. Again: **bugloss**, **borage**, **lemon balm**, and the distilled water of these three. Again, **lettuce**, **endive**, **raisins**, and **almond milk** should be very familiar sights at the table. 

One must flee air that is either scorching or too icy, or cloudy. But tempered and serene air should be admitted most freely. **Mercury**, **Pythagoras**, and **Plato** command that a dissonant or mourning soul be composed and at the same time uplifted by the **lyre** and by song that is as steady as it is harmonious. Moreover, **David** the sacred poet used the **psaltery** <note>a stringed instrument similar to a harp</note> and psalms to free **Saul** from madness. Even I—if I may compare the lowest things to the highest—frequently experience at home how much the sweetness of the lyre and song can prevail against the bitterness of **black bile** <note>original: *atrę bilis*</note>. We also praise the frequent sight of clear water, or of green or red colors...

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the melancholic scholar, transitioning from general lifestyle advice—such as environment and exercise—to specific dietary regulations for maintaining a healthy stomach. The previous page concluded with the benefits of aromatic plants like orange and citron.</meta>

<margin>-</margin>...the use of gardens <note>The OCR reads "horrorum" (of horrors), but contextually this is almost certainly "hortorum" (of gardens), a common recommendation for melancholy</note> and groves; we strongly approve of pleasant walks along rivers and through lovely meadows, as well as riding, being carried about, and gentle sailing. But above all, we recommend variety, easy occupations, and various tasks that are not troublesome: and the constant company of agreeable people.

### Care of the Stomach. Chapter 11.

It follows that we must take the most diligent care of the stomach, lest overindulgence ever bring on nausea or indigestion <term>cruditas: a state of undigested food in the stomach, thought to produce harmful vapors</term> and affect the head. Food should be taken twice a day; it should be moderate and light, seasoned moderately with cinnamon, mace, and nutmeg. However, dry food should always outweigh soft foods and drink in weight, unless perhaps we greatly fear the dryness of black bile <note>original: *atræ bilis*</note>. Let food wait for hunger (if this can be done conveniently) and drink wait for thirst. Greed for either should be banished from the table. Loathing and surfeit should be kept far away. One must abstain from those things which, because of excessive moisture or a poisonous, greasy, and viscous substance, weaken the stomach by relaxing it; or those things which are either cold or very hot, or are difficult to digest because of their hardness. Also avoid those things of such a nature that they leave a troublesome aftertaste on the palate long after the meal, or cause gas <note>original: *inflent* - literally "to inflate"</note>, or fill the head with many vapors. Above all, avoid everything that easily rots either outside the bowels or within them. We do not at all approve of sweet or sharp flavors if they are alone. But we want sweet things to be tempered by something sharp, pungent, or dry. 

<margin>-</margin>Mastic <term>mastic: a resin from the mastic tree used to aid digestion and strengthen the stomach</term>, dried mint, fresh sage, raisins, cooked quinces preserved with sugar, chicory, rose, coral, and caper blossoms preserved in vinegar are most friendly to the stomach. Furthermore, pomegranates with a flavor midway between sour and sweet, and generally whatever things are moderately dry and somewhat harsh—which doctors call "styptics" <term>styptica: substances that contract tissues or check secretions</term>—or those that are somewhat sharp, salty, or aromatic. 

<margin>-</margin>Myrobalans <term>myrobalans: a plum-like fruit from India, highly prized in Renaissance medicine for purging bile</term>, however, surpass all these. Red wine is also better than white, with a flavor that is somewhat slightly bitter; and unless heat or a "distillation" <note>original: *destillatio* - likely referring to catarrh or a head cold caused by descending fluids</note> requires otherwise, it will be best drunk undiluted.

<meta>This page continues the specific dietary and lifestyle regimen for scholars, focusing on the mechanics of digestion and the timing of rest. It transitions into Chapter 12, which introduces medicinal remedies from the "doctors' workshop" intended to protect the vital organs and the "spirits" of the mind. The siglum "b iii" indicates this is the third leaf of the second gathering of the printed book.</meta>

...gradually. Generally, more liquid foods should be taken before harder ones. Once food has been taken, coriander is suitable, as well as quince <term>cidonium: the fruit of the Cydonia oblonga, often prepared as a paste or "cotoniata" to aid digestion</term> preserved in sugar: pomegranates, tart pears, medlars <note>original: *nespila* - a small, apple-like fruit that is only edible when very ripe or "bletted"</note>, dried peaches, and the like. One must chew everything most thoroughly before swallowing.

The stomach should be comforted, if necessary from the outside, with mastic <term>mastice: an aromatic resin from the lentisk tree, believed to strengthen the stomach</term>, rose, mint, and coral. Care must be taken so that for two or three hours after a meal, we do not apply ourselves to difficult thinking or diligent reading. Perhaps four hours of leisure will be necessary if the food or drink was too plentiful, or if the food was harder to digest. It is bad to distend the belly with food or drink; it is worst of all to think about difficult matters when the stomach is so stretched. Therefore, either take very light nourishment, or once taken, rest until you have essentially digested it <note>original: *concoxeris* - literally "cooked"; Renaissance medicine viewed digestion as a process of "concoction" where the stomach's heat processed food</note>. Nor should one sleep at midday after a meal, unless the greatest necessity compels it. And even then, not before we have stayed awake for two hours. At night, however, one hour of wakefulness after dinner seems to suffice.

Sexual intercourse is harmful to the stomach, especially if you lie together immediately while full or while starving. The stomach grieves in idleness; it rejoices in exercise—except when it is full of food. Immediately after eating, one should take a moderate walk, but soon after, one should sit down.

### Concerning those things which nourish the primary limbs and the powers of the spirit. Chapter 12.

But now it seems better to bring forward some things from the doctors' workshop: things which might either keep whole or restore the powers of the stomach, heart, brain, and the spirits of the intellect <note>In this period, "spirits" were subtle fluids believed to circulate through the body to facilitate sensation and thought</note>. If phlegm <term>pituita: the cold, moist humor</term> or black bile <term>atra bilis: the humor associated with melancholy</term> should increase, or if nausea threatens, these will drive them far away. Without controversy, all physicians have agreed that nothing is more healthful than theriac <term>tiriaca: a legendary "all-heal" compound containing dozens of ingredients, including herbs and sometimes viper flesh</term> for nourishing and strengthening both individual limbs and powers, as well as the spirits and the intellect. We shall use a half-drachm <term>drachma: a small unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams</term> of this very substance, or at least a third of a drachm, twice every week in winter and autumn; but in summer and spring, once—either by itself, or if preferred during cold and damp times, with a little...

<keywords>digestion, quince, mastic, medicinal sleep, theriac, black bile, mental health, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions for scholars, specifically detailing how to ingest the remedies introduced at the end of Chapter 12. It provides recipes for complex herbal pills and medicinal lozenges ("morsuli") intended to balance the humors and improve cognitive function. The siglum "d ii" marks the second leaf of the fourth gathering.</meta>

with clear, sweet wine; however, in hot and dry weather—especially if one’s own nature or age is of a hotter disposition—it should be taken with two or three ounces of rose water. This should be done on an empty stomach, six or seven hours before eating. If **theriac** <term>theriaca: a famous ancient "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh, used as a general tonic</term> is unavailable, we shall give **mithridate** <term>mitridatum: a complex electuary similar to theriac, named after King Mithridates VI, used to strengthen the body against poison and disease</term>. But when we take theriac or mithridate, one must totally abstain from every "hot" thing <note>In Galenic medicine, "hot" refers to the metabolic quality of foods like spices or heavy meats</note> on that day; and if it is summer or spring, "cold" things must be used. 

In the second place, for the same purpose, **aloe** is approved by everyone, provided it is properly selected and washed. Take two drams of **chebulic myrobalans** <term>mirobalani chebuli: the fruit of the Terminalia chebula tree from India, highly prized in Renaissance medicine for purging phlegm</term>; one dram each of purple roses, red sandalwood, **emblic myrobalans** <note>original: *emblicorum* - also known as Amla or Indian gooseberry</note>, cinnamon, saffron, citron peel, **ben** <note>original: *beem* - likely refers to the root of the Behen plant, used as a cardiac tonic</note>, and lemon balm (which is also called *citraria*); and twelve drams of choice, properly washed aloe. From these, fashion pills using the best pure wine. Use these once any week at dawn, in a weight that suits your specific constitution. In summer, take them with rose water; at other times, take them with wine. 

On the days when you take neither the theriac nor the pills, use a confection of this kind in the morning and evening, two or three hours before food. Take four drams of the finest choice cinnamon; two drams of chebulic myrobalans; and the same amount of emblics; half a dram each of saffron and purple roses; two drams of red sandalwood; and one dram of similar corals; and as much of the whitest sugar as is sufficient. Melt the sugar with rose water and the juice of citrons or lemons in equal portions. Cook it gently. Then add a third of a dram of musk and the same amount of ambergris. Finally, shape them into solid morsels—which the common people call **morsuli** <term>morsuli: "little bites" or medicinal lozenges, often gilded to show status and enhance their "solar" properties</term>—and wrap them in gold leaf. 

We have personally experienced the use of these three things as we have prescribed: the theriac, the aloe tempered in the way we described, and that confection. They benefit every limb, as well as the strengths and the **spirits** <note>original: *spiritibus* - the subtle vapors believed to circulate through the body to facilitate life and thought</note>. They sharpen the senses and the intellect, and they strengthen the memory. Furthermore, **phlegm**, **bile**, and **black bile** <note>The three "troublesome" humors of the four-humor system; black bile was specifically associated with melancholy and scholars</note> are easily either drawn out or corrected by those pills. Moreover, these three remedies are judged to be most suitable for any age or nature.

Note: the siglum b iiii indicates the fourth leaf of the second gathering.</meta>

If we must fight more fiercely against overflowing phlegm, we shall give at dawn pills made of Galen’s **hiera picra** <term>hiera picra: literally "holy bitter," an ancient purgative powder made of aloes mixed with spices like cinnamon and saffron</term>, or those which **Mesue** <note>Mesue the Younger was a legendary Arabic medical authority whose recipes were essential to Renaissance pharmacy</note> calls "elephantine" <term>elefanginas: "elephantine" pills, a potent recipe used to clear heavy, thick humors</term>. We shall give as many and as often as necessary; or, in a stronger constitution, pills composed of equal portions of *hiera* and **agaric troches** <note>agaric: a medicinal fungus (Larch agaric) used as a laxative; troches are small medicinal lozenges</note>. These should always be taken with liquid **honey of roses**, **oxymel** <term>oxymel: a traditional mixture of honey and vinegar used to dissolve thick fluids</term>, and fennel water. This syrup is certainly most useful for digesting phlegm, both before taking the pills and after them. 

If other humors are also disturbed along with the phlegm, we shall purge at the right time with Mesue’s rhubarb pills, or those which later writers call "pills without which" <note>original: *sine quibus*—short for *pilulae sine quibus esse nolo*, or "pills without which I do not wish to be," a famous all-purpose laxative formula</note>. However, we utterly detest every violent and sudden bowel movement and purging. For it weakens the stomach and the heart, exhausts many **spirits** <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were subtle vapors that carried life and thought through the body; scholars were warned not to deplete them</note>, confuses the humors, and darkens the spirits with the dusky fumes of the humors.

### Catarrh. Chapter 14.

When the head is unstable with **catarrhs** <note>original: *deſtillationibus*—literally "distillations," referring to the "dripping" of excess fluid from the brain to the throat or nose</note> because of phlegm, we shall sometimes give some of the pills just described at the hour of sleep. Furthermore, we shall command the person to chew **sumac** <note>original: *rhus*—the berries were used for their astringent properties to "dry up" excess moisture</note> often at that hour and at others. For it wonderfully aids catarrhs, all the senses, and the memory. Again, nutmeg and **theriac** held in the mouth are approved. Also **marjoram**, which they call *amaracus*, or its water applied to or poured into the nostrils. After eating, we shall restrain the fumes of the food with coriander and quinces.

### Headache. Chapter 15.

If the head often aches, weighed down by a cold humor, in addition to those things we have recounted, we shall order the person to hold in the mouth that confection they call **diambra** <term>diambra: a luxury aromatic electuary containing ambergris and musk, used to warm and strengthen the brain</term>, or *diacorum*, or **plisarchoticon** <term>plisarchoticon: a complex Greek remedy intended to cure "cold" ailments of the head and nerves</term>. Indeed, they should often chew **mastic** <term>mastic: a resin from the Pistacia lentiscus tree, chewed to draw out phlegm and sweeten the breath</term>. Furthermore, smear the forehead and temples...

### On the Care of Vision. Chapter 16.

When the eyes grow dim but are not red, nor show any other sign of heat, then truly an eyewash <term>collirium: a liquid medicinal wash for the eyes</term> made from the water of fennel, marjoram, celandine, and rue—with saffron and antimony added—is beneficial. But water of this kind must first be squeezed through a thick cloth. However, you should apply nothing to the eyes unless you have first frequently purged with "pills of light" <note>original: *pilulis lucis*, a common name for medicinal pills believed to clear the "humors" obstructing vision</note>. But if the dimming eyes are red: purge immediately with pills composed of fumitory <note>original: *fumo terrae*, literally "smoke of the earth," a plant used to clear the blood and bile</note>. Soon after, an eyewash of rose water and sugar is helpful. Sometimes, however, adding egg white, tutty <term>tutia: a medicinal powder made of zinc oxide, used as an astringent</term>, and milk brings relief as quickly as possible. In every case, the daily use of fennel <note>original: *maratri*</note> preserves and sharpens the sight. Indeed, one should frequently hold its seeds in the mouth and eat the leaves. The "lesser trifera" <note>original: *Trifera minor*, a specialized electuary or medicinal paste often containing three types of myrobalan fruits</note> described by **Mesue** is best. It is best to take a preserved chebulic myrobalan <term>mirobalanum chebulam: a dried astringent fruit from India, highly prized in the Renaissance for "purging" the brain and sharpening the senses</term> daily on an empty stomach, along with some bread made from sugar and fennel ground into powder. This, furthermore, is wonderfully beneficial for the intellect and for prolonging life. Even the use of eyebright <note>original: *Eufragie*, the herb Euphrasia, traditionally used for eye ailments</note> is a singular protection for the eyes. In every case of either headache or dimness of the eyes, the vapors must be diverted backwards by means of rubbing and cupping glasses <term>cucurbitulis: small glass vessels used to create a vacuum on the skin to draw out "bad" humors</term>. And if heat is the cause and blood is abundant, we shall apply leeches to the neck and shoulders.

### On Restoring the Sense of Taste. Chapter 17.

Among scholars, the stomach often loses almost all sense of taste; this happens through a fault of phlegm, which is indicated by a sour taste in the mouth or by a lot of thick, sticky saliva. After you have moved the bowels with the medicines we described above, use aromatic rose [confection]—specifically, sugar mixed with roses—and also honey of roses with cinnamon. Even with ginger alone...

...preserved [ginger] or mint syrup; but especially theriac <term>theriaca: a complex "universal" antidote and herbal paste used for many ailments</term>. If, however, an abundance of bile <note>original: *bilis*, referring here to yellow bile, associated with heat and bitterness</note> happens to occur—which usually reveals itself by a bitter taste in the mouth—similarly, after a purging with aloes prepared as we have described, or with rhubarb, take either *triasandali* <term>triasandali: a cooling medicinal powder made from three types of sandalwood</term> or *oxysaccharum* <note>original: *oxizachara*, a medicinal syrup made of sugar and vinegar</note> composed of sugar, white vinegar, and the juice of a sour pomegranate. Or take quinces <note>original: *plica*, likely a variant for *cydonia*</note> or pears, either candied or prepared in syrup, as **Mesue** <note>Johannes Mesue, an influential 8th or 9th-century Arab physician whose works were standard in European medical schools</note> teaches; or our own confection of this kind, which is most healthy for the palate.

Take four ounces of rose sugar; two ounces of *diamarenatum* <term>diamarenatum: a preserve made from sour cherries</term>; two ounces of round cakes of *diacytoniton* <term>diacytoniton: a medicinal marmalade made of quinces</term>; half an ounce of Chebulic myrobalans; the same amount of Emblic myrobalans; and half a drachm each of red sandalwood and red coral. Pour over this two or three ounces of julep <term>julep: a sweet medicinal drink, often a sugar syrup mixed with flower or fruit water</term> made from citron or lemon juice. If the stomach is weak and cold, add two drachms of cinnamon. These should be used two hours before food.

*Diacytoniton* often removes nausea arising from both humors <note>referring to bile and phlegm</note>, as does the use of capers with vinegar. Likewise, a small drink of white rose vinegar on an empty stomach is effective, if mixed with twice the weight of sugar. Again, syrup of mint and wormwood [is useful], as is mint, either pure or preserved in vinegar, or diluted with the sour juice of a pomegranate.

### On the Precise Cure of Black Bile. Chapter 18.

But let us now set these lighter matters aside and return to that which is most dangerous: namely, black bile <term>atra bilis: also known as melancholy, one of the four humors, associated with coldness, dryness, and sadness</term>. Whenever it overflows and rages, it weakens not only the whole body but especially the spirit—which is the instrument of the intellect—and the intellect and judgment themselves.

Let the first rule in treating it be as **Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus, the dominant Greek physician of antiquity whose theories of the four humors shaped medicine for 1,500 years</note> taught: that we should not strive to draw it out suddenly. This is lest, perhaps, the thinner and more liquid part being drawn off, the denser and drier residue remains behind. Instead, it should be softened and digested little by little, and then led out. Second, the head and the entire body should be moistened to the highest degree, both with more humid foods and with sweet baths and moderate ointments. This must be done with caution, however, so that a catarrh <note>original: *destillatio*, literally a "dripping" of fluids from the head</note> is not provoked, nor the stomach or liver destroyed, nor the passages of the body blocked. Third...

...mon. Indeed, it is also especially necessary that the heart be continually comforted and strengthened with suitable things, partly taken internally and partly applied externally to the chest or the nostrils. Furthermore, those things which bring delight should be constantly looked at, heard, smelled, and contemplated; conversely, those things which are contrary to delight should be kept far away.

### Concerning Syrups. Chapter 19.

Many remedies have indeed been composed by many authors against this humor <note>Referring to the "melancholic humor" or black bile, thought to cause sadness and sluggishness</note>. However, for the present, I shall present three types of remedies from among the many: the most select and safest of all, first received from the ancients, then confirmed by the moderns, and sometimes even adapted by us for our own use. In the first part is the composition of an excellent syrup. In the second, most proven pills. In the third, most healthful electuaries <term>electuary: a medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</term>. When these three are applied at the right time, the melancholic humor is softened, digested, and dissolved; the spirits <note>In this context, the "animal spirits," which were believed to be the subtle fluids that carried thought and sensation through the nerves</note> are sharpened and brightened; the intellect is nourished; and the memory is strengthened.

The syrup is of this kind. Take borage <term>borago: a cooling herb traditionally used to "gladden the heart"</term>, bugloss, and the flowers of both, lemon balm <term>melissa</term>, maidenhair fern <term>capillus veneris</term>, endive, violets, dodder <term>cuscuta: a parasitic climbing plant</term>, polypody fern, senna, and dodder of thyme <term>epithymum</term>: of each, as much as can be held in the hand <note>original: *quantum manu capitur*, a "handful"</note>. Take twenty damson plums, ten fragrant apples, one ounce of raisins, half an ounce of liquorice, and three drachms <term>drachma: a small unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams</term> each of cinnamon, red sandalwood, and citron peels; and half a drachm of saffron. Let all of these be boiled in water—except for the dodder of thyme and the aromatic spices—until a third of the liquid is consumed. After the decoction <term>decoction: a concentrated liquor resulting from heating or boiling plant substances</term> has been pressed out, let it be boiled again moderately with sugar and the dodder of thyme. Finally, let the spices, namely the cinnamon and saffron, be infused.

At dawn, let three ounces of this syrup be drunk warmed, together with two or three ounces of bugloss water; and along with these, at least two of the following pills should be taken (or more, as suits each individual), specifically in such a way that the bowels are moved a little every day.

### Concerning Pills. Chapter 20.

Regarding pills, which pertain to our fourth purpose, there are two kinds: some are suitable for delicate people, and others for the more robust. The first may be called "golden" or "magical" pills—partly in imitation of the Magi <note>referring to ancient Persian priests or wise men</note> and partly of our own invention. These are composed under the specific astrological influence of Jupiter and Venus <note>In Ficino’s medical astrology, Jupiter and Venus were "benefic" planets that promoted joy and balance</note>. They draw out phlegm, bile, and black bile without causing discomfort; they strengthen every limb, and they sharpen and brighten the spirits. They expand the spirits so that they do not suffer sadness from being constricted, but instead rejoice in this expansion and light. Yet, they also stabilize the spirits so that they do not vanish through excessive stretching.

Take, therefore, twelve grains of gold—ideally from gold leaf, provided it is pure. Take a half-dram each of:
* frankincense
* myrrh
* saffron
* aloeswood <term>lignum aloes: a fragrant, resinous wood used in perfumes and medicine</term>
* cinnamon
* citron peel
* lemon balm <term>melissa</term>
* raw scarlet silk
* mint
* white behen and red behen <note>likely referring to the roots of *Centaurea behen* and *Statice limonium*, common in medieval pharmacy</note>

Take one dram each of:
* purple roses
* red sandalwood
* red coral
* the three types of myrobalans <term>myrobalan: a dried fruit from trees of the genus Terminalia, used as a laxative</term>, specifically the emblic, chebulic, and Indian varieties.

Take an amount of properly washed aloe equal to the weight of all the other ingredients combined. Fashion these into pills using the most select pure wine.

Next follow the pills for dissolving melancholy, which are somewhat stronger, yet not at all violent. Take one dram each of:
* peony
* Arabian stoechas <term>sticados: a type of wild lavender</term>
* lemon balm
* frankincense
* saffron
* the three myrobalans (emblic, chebulic, and Indian)
* roses

Take three drams each of:
* agaric troches <term>trochisci: medicinal lozenges or cakes</term>
* polypody <note>a type of fern</note>
* epithyme <term>epithimum: dodder of thyme, a parasitic plant</term>
* senna
* lapis lazuli <term>lapis lazuli: a deep-blue stone, washed and prepared here for internal use to purge black bile</term>
* Armenian stone <term>lapis armeni: a blue carbonate of copper, similar to azurite</term>, treated in the same manner.

Add two ounces of washed aloe and form the pills with a fine wine. If a manifest heat dominates along with the melancholy, those ingredients in this composition that are "cold" <note>referring to the Galenic quality of the herb</note> should be increased by an additional third of their weight.

I have composed these pills, as is suitable for students of letters <note>meaning scholars and intellectuals whose sedentary lives were thought to cause melancholy</note>, in imitation of the Greek, Latin, and Arabian physicians. However, I did not wish to mix in stronger substances, such as hellebore <note>original: "veratrum"</note>, which Carneades the Fanatic <note>Carneades was a skeptical philosopher said to have taken hellebore to sharpen his mind before a debate</note> used to use. For I advise only men of letters, or those slightly stronger, for whom nothing is more harmful than "violence" <note>meaning harsh, drastic medical treatments like strong purgatives</note>. Therefore, I have omitted the "Indian pills," the common versions of lapis lazuli or Armenian stone, and that composition which they call *hieralogodion* <note>a complex, ancient purgative formula</note>. Finally, if it is appropriate to introduce a simpler composition, which I often...

<summary>Ficino provides recipes for two types of pills to treat melancholy: a gentle "golden" pill aligned with Jupiter and Venus, and a stronger but non-violent purgative for scholars. He emphasizes avoiding harsh substances like hellebore, which could harm the delicate constitution of intellectuals.</summary>
<keywords>pills, melancholy, gold leaf, astrology, Jupiter, Venus, myrobalans, lapis lazuli, Armenian stone, hellebore, Carneades, medical alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific recipe for medicinal pills intended to balance the melancholic humor. It concludes the instructions for the frequency of their use, particularly for scholars, and begins Chapter 21 on liquid medicines.</meta>

...the weight of all the others. There is another method, however, which I use more frequently. Take one ounce of the best washed aloes; two drams each of emblic and chebulic **myrobalans** <term>myrobalan: a dried fruit used in medieval medicine as a gentle laxative to purge bile</term>; two drams of mastic; and two drams of roses, specifically purple ones. Form these into pills using wine.

Furthermore, no one should ever use these pills—or the others we have approved—on their own, lest the body perhaps be dried out too much. Such dryness is the worst possible condition in cases of melancholy. Rather, they should be taken either with the syrup that we described above (following the teachings of **Mesue** <note>Johannes Mesue, an influential 9th-century Arab physician</note> and **Gentile da Foligno** <note>A famous 14th-century Italian physician and commentator</note>), or with one, two, or three ounces of fragrant and light wine, as is suitable for the individual. Alternatively, they may be taken with water of honey, raisins, and liquorice. Or, if heat is dominant in the body, take them with violet julep <term>julep: a sweet medicinal syrup, often used as a base for other medicines</term> and violet water.

I advise all scholars and men of letters who are prone to **black bile** <term>atra bilis: the humor associated with melancholy, thought to cause depression and intellectual intensity</term> to use this purgation twice every year—specifically in the spring and the autumn—for fifteen or twenty consecutive days, taking the pills with the syrup or similar liquids. Those who are slightly less subject to this affliction will find it sufficient to take the first or last pills throughout the year, once every week: in the summer with julep as we have said, and at other times with wine.

### Concerning Liquid Medicine. Chapter 21.

It is necessary to advise that when the greatest danger of over-drying is imminent, yet the necessity of the case compels a purging, it is worthwhile to stop the use of pills. Instead, when purging is required, one should occasionally infuse one ounce (or at least half an ounce) of *diasena*, *diachatholicon*, or *triphera saracenica* <note>These are standard medieval compound electuaries used as laxatives</note> into the syrup itself or into a similar decoction made in bugloss water <term>bugloss: the herb *Anchusa officinalis*, believed to gladden the heart and clarify the blood</term>.

If the body is particularly robust, or if the bowels are more constricted and hard, add one or two drams of the **electuary of hamech** <note>A potent purgative compound specifically used to treat melancholy and skin diseases</note>. Prepared cassia is also useful here, and manna is even more useful. However, these are not suitable for every type of melancholy, but primarily for that which is created by **adustion** <note>original: "adustione"; referring to "burnt" melancholy, a condition where humors become overheated and toxic</note>. But if the melancholy is "natural" <note>Natural melancholy was the cold, dry state of the humor itself, rather than a state caused by heat or "burning"</note>, these remedies are indeed suitable, provided that a double or triple portion of **polypody** <term>polypody: a type of fern whose root was used to purge phlegm and melancholy</term> is added to the syrup; and similarly...

<summary>Ficino provides specific instructions for the use and frequency of medicinal pills for scholars and introduces liquid alternatives to prevent the dangerous over-drying of the melancholic body.</summary>
<keywords>melancholy, black bile, purgation, Mesue, Gentile da Foligno, pharmacy, scholars, seasonal health, electuaries, adustion</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific medicinal recipes for treating melancholy, moving from the ingredients of syrups to the general management of the melancholic "complexion" and the specific dangers and methods of blood-letting for scholars.</meta>

...of liquorice, saffron, and raisins. Likewise, two ounces of liquid honey of roses should be added to the medicine. We have already stated in the preceding sections how often the syrup should be taken. However, the medicine must be taken with it for sixty days <note>original: "ter uiginti diebus" (three times twenty days)</note>.

But if no **melancholic humor** <term>humor: one of the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that determined health and temperament</term> is actually present—but only a "melancholic complexion," which is to say a cold and dry quality of the limbs—remember that it is not at all helpful to purge the bowels or to let blood. Instead, you should only do the remaining things which we have either already mentioned or will mention. This applies especially to whatever pertains to moderately warming and greatly moistening the body, illuminating the **spirits** <term>spiritus: a subtle vaporous substance thought to connect the physical body to the soul and facilitate thought</term> as much as possible, and nourishing the limbs. 

However, when the black bile humor itself overflows, we do not only moisten the body and the humor, but we also loosen the bowels, using the caution we previously described: never do this violently. Indeed, **Plato** <note>The Greek philosopher (c. 427–347 BCE)</note> in the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s dialogue concerning the nature of the physical world and human biology</note> warns us that a long-lasting disease, such as melancholy, should not be provoked with overly powerful medicines and troublesome drugs.

### On the Letting of Blood. Chapter 22

There are some who are rather bold in letting blood, a practice which wise physicians greatly detest. For blood is the tempering agent of **black bile** <term>atra bilis: another name for the melancholic humor; blood was thought to "thin" and balance its thick, dark nature</term>, the fuel of the spirit, and the treasure of life. 

Only in cases where an abundance of blood is indicated—either by excessive laughter and great boldness and confidence, or by a reddish color and swelling of the veins—should we let blood for men of letters. When the situation demands it, we should draw blood from the "spleen vein" <note>original: "uena lienis," likely referring to the salvatella vein on the back of the hand</note> of the left arm, making a somewhat wide incision: four ounces in the morning and as many in the evening. 

Then, after a few days—at least seven, at most fourteen—irritate the **scarifications** <term>scarification: small, superficial incisions made in the skin to draw blood or apply medicine</term>, first with a somewhat rough rubbing, then by applying leeches (which they call blood-suckers), so that three or four ounces of blood may drip from there. Both of these procedures seem appropriate only for the more robust individuals. For the weaker, however, if necessity demands it, only irritate the scarifications as we have taught. But it is permitted neither to loosen the belly with medicines nor to let blood:

<summary>This page discusses the treatment of melancholy through diet and medicine, cautioning against harsh purging and blood-letting unless specifically required. It cites Plato’s advice on gentle treatment and details the specific method for blood-letting in scholars when "blood abundance" is evident.</summary>

<keywords>melancholy, blood-letting, humors, black bile, Plato, Timaeus, medical treatment for scholars, spirits, scarification, leeches</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from general physiological management—specifically the regulation of the bowels and the dangers of blood-letting—to specific medicinal recipes called electuaries. It follows a warning against violent purging for those with a melancholic temperament.</meta>

...you will first have softened the patient with fatty and gentle enemas. And this becomes a common rule for the **melancholic nature** <term>melancholic nature: a temperament dominated by coldness and dryness, associated with the humor of black bile</term>: effort must be made so that, if necessary, through the frequent use of such enemas, the lower belly remains always lubricated and purged.

### On Electuaries. Chapter 24

**Electuaries** <term>electuary: a medicinal paste made by mixing powdered drugs with honey, syrup, or sugar</term> follow: among which I certainly praise that which **Rhazes** <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and physician</note> called "the exhilarant." Furthermore, I praise those which **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the influential author of *The Canon of Medicine*</note> composes in his book *On the Powers of the Heart*. 

But I praise much more what **Mesue** <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh (c. 777–857), a physician famous for his pharmaceutical recipes</note> writes as follows: Take one pound by weight of raw silk, recently dyed with a scarlet color. Submerge it in the juice of sweet and pleasantly fragrant apples, as well as in the juice of **bugloss** <term>bugloss: a plant often used in Renaissance medicine to "gladden the heart" and dispel sadness</term>, and rose water—one pound of each. After twenty-four hours, cook all of this gently until the water turns red. Then extract the silk and, moreover, squeeze it out carefully. Soon pour in one hundred and fifty drachmas of the whitest sugar; cook it again until it thickens like honey. Then remove it from the fire; and while it is still hot, pour in six drachmas of raw **ambergris** <term>ambergris: a rare, waxy substance produced by sperm whales, highly valued as a restorative and fragrance</term> which has been carefully chopped, and allow the ambergris to melt. 

Finally, add to these a powder of this kind: take six drachmas each of raw **wood of aloes** <term>wood of aloes: also known as agarwood, a fragrant resinous wood used to strengthen the heart and brain</term> and cinnamon. Add thirteen drachmas of **lapis lazuli** <note>A blue semi-precious stone; physicians believed that when properly washed, it could specifically purge black bile without being too harsh</note> properly washed. Add two drachmas of "unions"—which is to say, white pearls. Add one drachma of true gold. Add half a drachma's part of choice **musk** <term>musk: a potent aromatic substance used to stimulate the vital spirits</term>. One or two drachmas of this are given with wine, in the morning and evening, three or four hours before food. 

This recipe pleases me greatly above the others. Nevertheless, I also approve of Mesue’s sweet *Diamuscum* <note>A musk-based medicinal confection</note> and his "Composition of Gems," provided they are taken with rose water. I would also wonderfully praise the one composed from vapors by the philosopher **Peter of Abano** <note>Pietro d'Abano (c. 1257–1316), an Italian physician and astrologer</note>, were it not that he himself feared an excessive dilation and exhalation of the **spirits** <term>spiritus: the subtle vapors that carry life and thought through the body</term> from its immoderate use. For this reason, I have devised two other very safe things, which on account of their tempering quality are most suitable for any time, age, or nature; in which the useful is mixed with the sweet. These nourish as much as they soothe and strengthen.

<summary>This page provides recipes for "electuaries"—medicinal pastes—intended to treat melancholy, featuring luxury ingredients like silk, gold, pearls, and ambergris. It highlights the authority of Persian and medieval physicians while introducing the author's own safer alternatives.</summary>
<keywords>electuaries, Rhazes, Avicenna, Mesue, ambergris, lapis lazuli, medicinal gold, Peter of Abano, spiritus, pharmaceutical recipes</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific medicinal recipes for treating the melancholic temperament, moving from the preparation of silk-based syrups to more complex electuaries containing spices, precious metals, and gemstones. It maintains the author's focus on "gladdening the heart" and stabilizing the mind.</meta>

Furthermore, they are as much suited for stabilizing the spirit and the intellect as for sharpening and enlightening them. Take four ounces of rose sugar <term>rose sugar: sugar infused with rose petals, used as a medicinal base</term>. Take two ounces of sugar cooked together with **bugloss** flowers. One ounce of citron fruit peel preserved in sugar. Two ounces of preserved **chebula** <term>chebula: the fruit of the black myrobalan tree, often used in Renaissance medicine as a tonic or mild laxative</term>. One drachm <term>drachm: a unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams</term> of select cinnamon. Red sandalwood and red coral: half a drachm of each. Also, half a drachm each of raw scarlet silk chopped fine, saffron, and pearls. A third of a drachm each of gold and silver. Two grains each of ambergris and musk. Melt all of these together with the juice of a citron or lemon cooked with sugar.

Another confection follows, somewhat healthier and certainly much sweeter. Take four ounces of sweet almonds; pine nuts (specifically those washed for the space of one day); and cucumber seeds—two ounces of each. Take four ounces of the hard sugar that they call "candy" (that is, white candy sugar). Take one and a half pounds of another sugar, but of the whitest sort. Melt all these in rose water and lemon and citron water, in which glowing gold and silver have been extinguished <note>original: "extinctū fuerit ignitū aurū & argentū." This refers to a common pharmaceutical practice where hot metal was plunged into liquid to infuse the water with the metal's "virtue" or medicinal properties.</note>. Cook it all together gently. At last, add one drachm each of cinnamon, **red behen** <term>red behen: a root used as a cardiac tonic, likely *Centaurea behen*</term>, red sandalwood, and similar coral. Add half a drachm each of the most lustrous pearls, saffron, and raw scarlet silk ground very fine. Twelve grains each of gold and silver. A third of a drachm each of **hyacinth, emerald, sapphire, and carbuncle** <note>The use of ground gemstones was a hallmark of "expensive" Renaissance medicine, believed to provide celestial protection and strengthen the vital spirits.</note>. 

However, if anyone lacks gold, silver, ambergris, musk, or precious stones, those confections can still be very beneficial without them. Of these, I choose three above all: namely, the one by **Mesue** <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh (c. 777–857)</note> which I reviewed, and the two of our own which I have just described. We have already sufficiently explained above how they should be used. 

But if anyone desires something simpler, yet suited for all, let him thoroughly crush a perfectly ripe, whole citron fruit; let him cook it with much sugar and much rose juice. Once cooked, let him season it with a little cinnamon and saffron. Or, let him use **aromatic rosate** <term>aromatic rosate: a compound of rose petals and spices used to treat the heart and stomach</term> prepared thus: take one ounce of aromatic rosate, and two ounces each of rose sugar and **diabugloss sugar** <term>diabugloss: a medicinal sugar preparation of bugloss, a plant famous for "expelling sadness"</term>. Or, he may similarly mix in **diamusk** <term>diamusk: a musk-based preparation used to strengthen the vital spirits and the heart</term>. For although these two are not "simples" <term>simples: medicines made from a single herbal ingredient</term>, they are nevertheless easily enough obtained. And if heat <note>Excessive "choleric" heat or feverishness</note> is feared, let **diaprunis** <term>diaprunis: a cooling confection made primarily from plums or prunes</term> and violet sugar be added.

<summary>This page details several complex recipes for electuaries used to stabilize the spirit and intellect, including "luxury" versions with gold and gemstones and simpler alternatives using citron and rose.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medical advice for scholars and those of a melancholic temperament. While the previous page focused on stabilizing the spirit with precious metals and syrups, this section addresses the physical symptoms of mental overexertion—specifically insomnia and the onset of mental "dullness" caused by sedentary study habits.</meta>

### Chapter 24: On Excessive Wakefulness

It often happens to melancholic people, especially those of a scholarly nature, that when the brain is overly dried out, they become exhausted by long periods of wakefulness. Since indeed nothing increases the evils of **black bile** <note>Black bile (melancholia) was one of the four humors; an excess was thought to cause sadness, anxiety, and physical "dryness" in the body.</note> more than long wakefulness, such a great evil must be countered with every effort. 

Therefore, they should eat lettuce after other foods, together with a little bread and a tiny amount of saffron. They should sip pure wine after the lettuce, and they should not work by lamplight <note>original: "lucubrent" — this specifically refers to studying or writing late into the night by candlelight.</note> for more than an hour. And when they go to sleep, let them take a confection of this kind: one containing two ounces of white poppy seed; one ounce of lettuce seed; and half a **drachm** <term>drachm: a unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams</term> each of **amomi** <term>amomi: an aromatic spice, likely cardamom or a related plant from the ginger family</term> and saffron. Also six ounces of sugar. Dissolve and cook all these together in syrup of poppy. 

Let them eat two drachms of this, and at the same time taste a little poppy syrup or wine. You should smear their forehead and temples with oil of violets, and with **water lily oil** <note>original: "nenu fare" refers to the Nenuphar or water lily, which was prized in Renaissance medicine for its "cooling" properties.</note> with camphor added. Likewise with milk and oil of almonds and violets. The scent of saffron, camphor, and the rinds of sweet apples should be brought to the nostrils. Add a little vinegar, but a large amount of rose water. 

You should also strew the bed with the leaves of "cold" plants <note>In Galenic medicine, plants were classified by temperaments; "cold" plants like willow or lettuce were used to counteract the "heat" and "dryness" of insomnia.</note>. You should soothe the ears with low-pitched songs and sounds. <margin>Handwritten bracket in right margin</margin> You will often moisten the head with baths of this kind: namely, water in which pieces of poppy, lettuce, purslane, mallow, roses, vine leaves, willow, and reed leaves have been cooked, with chamomile added. Also, the shins and arms—and often the whole body—should be moistened with sweet baths made from such things. Furthermore, drinking milk mixed with sugar on an empty stomach is very beneficial, provided the stomach tolerates it well. These moistening remedies are wonderfully helpful for melancholic people even if they sleep enough. Remember that almond milk should be a staple of their table.

### Chapter 25: On Dullness of Mind and Forgetfulness

It happens indeed from time to time—either because scholars diligently read or write with their heads bent down, or because they grow sluggish from too much leisure—that a certain thicker phlegm, together with an excessively cold melancholy, [occupies] the head...

<summary>This page provides dietary and topical remedies for insomnia in scholars, emphasizing "cooling" and "moistening" ingredients like poppy, lettuce, and almond milk. It also begins a discussion on how sedentary study habits lead to mental dullness and the accumulation of phlegm.</summary>
<keywords>insomnia, melancholy, scholars, poppy, lettuce, almond milk, black bile, Galenic medicine, mental health, hygiene</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical instructions for scholars, transitioning from the treatment of insomnia to the remedies for mental "dullness" and memory loss caused by an accumulation of phlegm in the head.</meta>

...occupies, burdens, and makes the head dull and forgetful. Therefore, the head must be unburdened by those remedies which we have said elsewhere are suitable for **phlegm** <note>original: "pituitæ" — in the humoral system, phlegm was considered cold and moist; an excess in the brain was thought to cause sluggishness and "foggy" thinking.</note>. If those are not sufficient, you should flee to **Hiera Logodion** <term>Hiera Logodion: a traditional purgative "holy" medicine often containing aloes</term> and **Cocchia pills**. Furthermore, turn to the **Hiera of Colocynth** <note>original: "diacoloqntidos" — a powerful laxative made from bitter apple</note>, or the preparations of Archigenes, Andromachus, or Theodotion. Or use the "Pills of the Jew" which **Mesue** <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, known as Mesue the Elder, a Persian Christian physician whose works were foundational to medieval pharmacy.</note> describes in his chapter on headaches. 

If the person's nature or age is colder, and it is not summer, use that **Anacardine** confection <note>original: "confectione illa anachardina" — a remedy made from the marking nut (*Semecarpus anacardium*), famously used to "sharpen the senses" but requiring careful handling due to its caustic nature.</note> after the purging; Mesue calls this the "Confection of the Sages" in his book of medicines. There is also that Anacardine recipe which he records in the chapter on forgetfulness, following the opinion of the **son of Zachariah** <note>Referring to Al-Razi (Rhazes), a major Islamic physician.</note>. Give one **drachm** <term>drachm: a unit of weight, approximately 3.9 grams</term> in the early morning; but he who takes it must first abstain on that day from anger, sexual intercourse, drunkenness, hard labor, and hot things. These are indeed most powerful against dullness and forgetfulness.

But if you prefer more familiar things, give **ginger** preserved in sugar, but mixed with a little **frankincense** <note>original: "thure"</note>; this greatly benefits the senses and memory, especially if these items are added: namely, honey of cashew, honey of **chebulic myrobalan** <note>original: "chebulanū" — an astringent fruit from India used in digestive and memory tonics</note>, sweet flag, ambergris, and musk. Also beneficial are *diabra*, *plis archoticon*, and *diacori*. But these should be held in the mouth for a longer time, and also infused into the nostrils and ears. The scent of frankincense, marjoram, fennel, nutmeg, rue, and cloves also helps in no small measure. Remember, however, that in these and similar cases, as we said from the beginning, **Theriac** <note>original: "tiriacā" — the most famous "universal" medicine of the ancient and Renaissance world, consisting of dozens of ingredients including viper flesh and opium.</note> should never be placed second to any remedy. 

Furthermore, for those who are dull and forgetful, rub the temples and the back of the neck with an ointment of this kind: Take one ounce of elderberry oil, two ounces of **oil of ben** <term>oil of ben: oil pressed from the seeds of the Moringa tree, highly valued by perfumers and apothecaries for its stability</term>, half an ounce of **euphorbium** <note>a caustic plant resin used as a stimulant</note>, and an equal amount of **castoreum** <note>a yellowish secretion from beaver sacs, used to treat nervous system disorders.</note>. Rub the arms, legs, and neck vigorously. And if it is necessary, you shall apply **cupping glasses** <note>original: "cucurbitulas" — heated glass vessels used to draw blood or humors to the surface of the skin.</note> to the neck. Additionally, you should cover and warm the crown of the head with crushed marjoram, frankincense, and nutmeg.

Care for the **corporeal spirit**; cultivate the incorporeal; finally, venerate the truth. Medicine provides the first; moral discipline the second; and religion provides the third.

### Chapter 26

If men who are eager for the truth ought to care for the corporeal spirit with such great diligence by doctors, lest by chance, if entirely neglected...

<summary>This page provides complex pharmaceutical recipes for treating memory loss and mental sluggishness, emphasizing the "Anacardine" confection and Theriac. It concludes with a philosophical transition, linking physical health (medicine) to moral and religious cultivation.</summary>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page transitions from specific medicinal recipes (like the Anacardine confection) to a broader philosophical conclusion regarding the relationship between the health of the body and the cultivation of the soul.</meta>

...or that it might be an impediment, or serve poorly those seeking the truth: beyond any doubt, it is far more fitting to cultivate the incorporeal spirit—that is, the intellect itself—through the teachings of moral discipline, for it is by this alone that truth itself (being incorporeal) is captured. For it is a sin <note>original: "nefas" — a term implying a violation of divine law or a deep moral wrong.</note> to care only for the servant of the soul—that is, the body—while neglecting the soul, which is the body's master and king. This is especially true since it is the opinion of the **Magi** <note>Ancient Eastern sages often cited by Renaissance philosophers as keepers of "primordial" wisdom.</note> and **Plato** that the whole body depends so much on the soul that the body cannot be healthy unless the soul is well.

Therefore, **Apollo**, the founder of medicine, judged **Socrates** to be the wisest man, rather than **Hippocrates**, even though Hippocrates was born of Apollo's own lineage <note>Hippocrates was traditionally considered an Asclepiad, a descendant of the god of healing.</note>. This is because Hippocrates studied the healing of the body, while Socrates dedicated himself to healing the soul; yet, what those men merely attempted, only **Christ** fully achieved. 

Furthermore, if we are commanded by Socrates to cultivate our minds with the best morals so that we might more easily attain the light and truth sought by our natural instinct with a serene mind, how much more is it right to venerate the divine truth itself, primarily through holy religion? For the mind was created for no other purpose than seeking and grasping this truth, just as the eye was created for looking at the light of the sun. 

And as our **Plato** says, just as the sense of sight perceives nothing visible except in the splendor of the highest visible thing—that is, the sun itself—so the human intellect grasps nothing intelligible except in the light of the highest intelligible being—that is, God—who is always and everywhere present to us. I speak of the light that "enlightens every man coming into this world" <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 1:9.</note>. It is the light of which **David** sings: "In your light we shall see light" <note>Psalm 36:9.</note>. 

Truly, just as when the eyes are cleansed and looking toward the light itself, its splendor is suddenly infused, shining abundantly with the colors and shapes of things, so too as soon as the mind is cleansed of all bodily disturbances through moral discipline and directed toward divine truth—that is, God himself—with a certain religious and most burning love, divine truth suddenly (as the divine Plato says) flows into the mind. It happily explains the true reasons of things which are contained within it and by which all things consist; and in the same measure that it surrounds the mind with light, it simultaneously and blessedly fills the will with joy.

->FINIS<-

<summary>This page concludes the work by arguing that the health of the body is secondary to and dependent upon the health of the soul. It synthesizes Platonic philosophy with Christian scripture to explain how moral discipline allows the human intellect to be illuminated by divine truth.</summary>

<keywords>soul and body, Plato, Socrates, Apollo, Christ, divine light, moral discipline, intellect, Neo-Platonism, health of the soul</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the relationship between the soul's health and the body's health to the formal opening of Ficino's treatise "On Long Life." It begins with a dedicatory letter to Filippo Valori, emphasizing the patronage of the Medici family in reviving Platonic studies.</meta>

## Preface to the Book on Long Life.

MARSILIO Ficino of Florence sends greetings to Filippo Valori, the best and most noble citizen. Although our **Plato** lives by his own genius and will live (as I think) as long as the world itself lives, my own genius always impels me toward this: that after divine worship, I should look out for the life of Plato <note>original: "Platonis uitae" — referring here to the "life" or preservation of Platonic teachings.</note> above all things. Toward this very goal, the **House of Medici** <note>original: "Medica domus" — a clever play on words referring both to the Medici family and to a "medical house" or medical profession.</note> has aspired for us for a long time above others. To the same end you also conspire, my dear Valori, a most devoted friend of the **Medici** and a very great enthusiast for Platonic glory and discipline. Therefore, the life I always wish for Plato, I wish the same for the **Medici** <note>original: "medicibus" — Ficino frequently puns on the name "Medici" and the Latin word for physicians.</note>, and a similar one for you, Valori. Wherefore I exhort and beseech you, my Valori: that with as much effort as you always favor Platonic glory, you may with such great diligence read and observe these our precepts on prolonging life. By which, living long, you may be able to patronize for a longer time the recently reborn discipline of Plato, together with the magnanimous **Lorenzo de' Medici**. Farewell.

---

# THE BOOK ON LONG LIFE
# BY MARSILIO FICINO OF FLORENCE

**A long life is necessary for the perfection of knowledge: which diligence also provides.**

### Chapter One.

NOT SO MUCH the teachability of the intellect or the firmness of memory leads us to perfect art and science, as does the perspicacity of **prudent judgment**. Judgment, however, is so difficult—on account of the ambiguity conceived from the diversity of conjectures—that it must necessarily be confirmed by **experiment** <note>original: "experimento" — in this context, it refers to practical experience or clinical observation over time.</note>. Experiment is also deceptive, both because of the difficulty of judgment itself and because of the fleeting opportunity in the brevity of time for grasping the experiment. For which sound reasons, we rightly conclude with **Hippocrates** <note>Ficino is referencing the famous first aphorism of Hippocrates: "Art is long, life is short."</note> that art is long, and that we cannot [master] it except [through a] long life...

<summary>This page contains the dedication of the treatise to Filippo Valori and the opening of Chapter One, where Ficino argues that a long life is essential for scholars to master complex arts and sciences.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Filippo Valori, Plato, Medici family, Lorenzo de' Medici, Hippocrates, longevity, medical philosophy, experimental knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the first chapter of Ficino’s "On Long Life," moving from the necessity of knowledge to the physiological requirements for a long lifespan. It transitions from the idea that while fate sets a beginning, human diligence can extend the end.</meta>

...to achieve through habit. Indeed, the Fates do not only permit a long life once and for all from the beginning: but our own diligence also provides it. This is something both astrologers admit—when they discuss "elections" <note>original: "electionibus" — the astrological practice of choosing the most auspicious time to begin an undertaking</note> and the use of images—and which the diligent care and experience of physicians confirms. Through such prudence, not only are naturally strong men very often preserved, but even the sickly <note>original: "valitudinarii" — those with delicate or chronic health conditions</note> sometimes achieve a long life. Thus, it is no wonder that a certain **Herodicus** <note>A 5th-century BC Greek physician and teacher of Hippocrates known for using exercise to treat his own frail health</note>, a lover of letters and the most infirm man of his time (as **Plato** and **Aristotle** testify), reached his hundredth year through such prudence. **Plutarch** moreover relates that many who otherwise had a poorly firm body achieved a long life through diligence alone. I will omit for now how many weak people I myself have known who, by the gift of prudence, have surpassed the years of the most robust. 

Therefore, it will be neither useless nor in vain—following the book we composed on caring for the health of scholars—to hand down certain additional precepts for minds thirsty for knowledge that lead to a long life. However, we do not wish these instructions to be shared with the idle and the lazy. For why should we desire for those people to live long? They do not truly live at all; it would be as if we were nourishing drones rather than bees. Nor should they be spread among men lost to the lust for pleasures, who foolishly prefer a brief daily pleasure to a long life. Nor should they be revealed to the wicked and the unjust, whose life is the death of the good. Rather, these are for prudent and temperate men alone, whose skillful mental powers will benefit the human race either privately or publicly.

### Vital heat is nourished by moisture: When it is lacking, dissolution occurs; when it exceeds, suffocation occurs. Chapter 2.

Life, indeed, consists in natural heat, just like a light <note>Ficino uses the metaphor of a lamp flame to represent the "vital heat" of the body.</note>. The fuel for this heat is an airy and fatty moisture, much like oil. If, therefore, such moisture happens to fail, or if it entirely exceeds or becomes tainted, the heat is immediately weakened, and at last, having been weakened, it is extinguished. If the heat is weakened and perishes through a deficiency of moisture, death occurs by **resolution** <note>original: "resolutione" — a process of drying out or "thinning" where the body's substance dissolves away.</note>. If it is instead overwhelmed by an excess or vice of the moisture, life perishes by **suffocation**. This suffocation of any moisture [comes from] its oozing out or putrefaction...

<summary>Ficino argues that diligence and medical prudence can extend life even for the infirm, citing historical figures like Herodicus. He introduces the physiological theory that life is a "vital heat" fueled by bodily moisture, which must be carefully balanced to avoid death by drying out or being overwhelmed by fluids.</summary>

<keywords>vital heat, radical moisture, Herodicus, longevity, medical prudence, Plato, Aristotle, resolution and suffocation, humoral theory</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory argument about the causes of aging and the role of the humors, specifically phlegm, before transitioning into Chapter III. It continues the previous page's discussion on how physicians must protect the life force from two opposing threats: "dissolution" (drying out) and "suffocation" (drowning in excess fluids).</meta>

...happens by a certain internal faction: especially when phlegm <note>original: *pituita* — one of the four humors, associated with cold and moisture</note> either overflows or becomes putrefied in some way; thus it was not without reason that phlegm was named "the seeker of life" <note>original: *pituita petes vitam* — a wordplay suggesting that excess phlegm "seeks" or attacks the life force</note>. Therefore, these are the precepts most necessary for prolonging life. On the one hand, they avoid dissolution <note>original: *resolutio* — the leaking away or evaporation of the body's vital heat and moisture</note>; on the other, they equally avoid suffocation <note>original: *suffocatio* — the extinguishing of the vital spark by an excess of cold, damp fluids</note> and putrefaction.

They do so, I say, by a common principle. For if they happen to treat a man who is naturally warmer or drier, and whose channels <note>original: *meatus* — the pores and pathways in the body</note> are open, then the humors and the subtle spirits <note>original: *spiritus* — the refined "air" that carries life through the nerves and arteries</note> are more prone to dissolution. But if they treat a body affected by the opposite condition, they instead come to the aid of suffocation. They strive especially toward one or the other if the location or the time of year leans in that direction. 

But when providing counsel for the ingenious and the studious <note>scholars, writers, and intellectuals who were Ficino’s primary audience</note>, both precepts are almost equally necessary, and one must labor similarly in both directions. For in these people, a sharp and hot intellect and the constant movement of the imagination threaten dissolution; yet the leisure of the body and indigestion <note>original: *cruditas* — literally "rawness," referring to undigested food that creates blockages</note> seem to threaten suffocation. Nowhere, therefore, do physicians labor more intensely than in the care of such men. And although the entire discussion of the previous book contributes greatly to prolonging life, such a great matter seems to demand its own specific care, which I shall hereafter summarize in a few words as best I can.

### How Heat Must Be Balanced with Moisture, and Vice Versa, by a Certain Counsel of Minerva. Chapter III.

Furthermore, while we treat this rich oil—so necessary for our fiery vigor—with "plain common sense" <note>original: *pingui Minerva* — a Latin idiom meaning "with a thick Minerva," or simple, unrefined wisdom</note>, Minerva herself, the olive-bearer and origin of the vital oil, born from the head of nourishing Jove, smiles. She smiles because while we clearly saw the quantity of her gift, we did not meanwhile sufficiently see its quality. Laughing, therefore, she says: "I have given you oil in abundance, not only enough to nourish the flame, but also pure oil, without the dregs <note>original: *amurca* — the watery, bitter sediment of olive oil</note>, to be poured into the lamp."

Thus she spoke. But we, wandering as we spoke, stumbled; because the lamp was "a word to our feet" <note>A play on Psalm 119:105, "Thy word is a lamp unto my feet"</note>, though its meaning was not yet heard by us. From this lamp, therefore, let us learn that oil must be constantly supplied to the flame with such diligence that we do not overwhelm the light with a sudden flooding. Nor, on the other hand, should we delay giving drink to the thirsty flame. But these two matters we think have been sufficiently treated in the preceding sections. Two things remain for us: one of which I have touched upon lightly, and the other...

<summary>Ficino explains that scholars face a double danger: their intense mental activity dries them out (dissolution), while their sedentary lifestyle leads to a buildup of harmful fluids (suffocation). He introduces a metaphor using Minerva and her olive oil to explain the balance of "fuel" needed for the body's vital lamp.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medical and philosophical advice for scholars, transitioning from the general principles of preserving vital heat and moisture to specific causes of their depletion. It introduces the famous metaphor of the lamp and the wick to explain how life is sustained or extinguished, leading into Chapter IV which details the physical and emotional factors that dry out the body's natural moisture.</meta>

...we seem to have barely touched upon it. Meanwhile, we touch Pallas <note>Minerva, goddess of wisdom and intellect</note> in such a way that she, who is never accustomed to laughing, might smile upon us. What then? First, we shall consider that even a small flame is initially greedy; therefore, that lamp shines longer in which the little flame is so tempered to the wick <note>original: *ellychnium* or *lucinium* — the material that draws the oil</note> that it does not gulp down the oil, but sips it <note>original: *pitisset* — a term derived from the Greek meaning to taste or sip sparingly</note>. 

And so, in our entire regimen <note>original: *dieta* — referring to a complete lifestyle and medical routine, not just food</note>, we will take care lest the innate fire—which is naturally voracious—grows too strong, especially in youth. It will be enough to ward off overflowing moisture on one hand, and penetrating cold on the other. 

Next, we will reflect that the lamp is often extinguished when the oil poured in is not pure, but (as I might say) full of dregs <note>original: *amurcosum* — from *amurca*, the bitter, watery sediment of pressed olives</note>. From these dregs, a little later, a "fungus" <note>the charred, crusty buildup on a lamp wick</note> grows and extinguishes the light. Now, we have received a nourishing oil from Pallas: namely, an airy oil, very pure, and made firm through a certain natural stickiness. Therefore, whatever is supplied <note>through food and drink</note> to replace what is gradually consumed must be not only equal to this oil, but similar to it. To be truly similar, it must not only be airy and fatty, but entirely separated from the dregs—that is, from a certain sediment collected from thick earth and water. 

To avoid this congestion and sediment, let us shun such foods, as well as idleness, indigestion <note>original: *cruditatem* — literally "rawness," the state of food that has not been properly "cooked" by the stomach's heat</note>, and impurities. Meanwhile, let us worship Minerva with such moderation that she may strengthen our head (from which she herself was born), but not weaken our nerves and stomach.

### For what reasons the natural moisture dries up, or foreign moisture leaks out: and how necessary perfect digestion is for life. Chapter IV.

These things quickly dry out the natural moisture <note>original: *humor naturalis* — the fundamental "fuel" for the body's life-fire</note>: A profuse flow of blood. Violent loosening of the bowels. A long-term slippery gut. Excessive sweating. Widely opened pores <note>original: *meatus*</note>. Sexual intercourse performed to the point of weakness. Panting thirst. Tormenting hunger. Long periods of wakefulness. The use of things that are simultaneously hot and dry. Laborious movement of both mind and body. Anxiety. Anger. Grief. Air that is both drier and hotter, especially when heated by fire. A wind that is dry, violent, and long-lasting. 

The opposites of these things increase the moisture beyond measure. Frequent drunkenness <note>original: *ebrietas*</note> does both: for by excessive heat it dries, and then by moisture it suffocates the drunkard. But nothing is more harmful to both <note>drying and suffocating</note> than indigestion. For where food is not cooked <note>original: *concoquitur* — referring to the "digestion as cooking" theory</note>...

<summary>Ficino uses the metaphor of a lamp to explain how the body's vital heat must "sip" rather than "gulp" its fuel and identifies various lifestyle factors—from emotions to environment—that deplete the body's radical moisture.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medical advice for scholars, shifting from the metaphor of the lamp to the practical physiological priority of digestion (concoction). It emphasizes that even the best food is useless if the body cannot process it, and outlines the four stages of digestion recognized in Renaissance medicine.</meta>

On one hand, indeed, that which might irrigate the natural moisture is lacking; on the other hand, a putrefaction remains which, by overflowing, overwhelms the natural heat. For this reason, Avicenna <note>original: *Avicenna* — the Latinized name of Ibn Sina, the Persian "Prince of Physicians"</note> says that blood is corrupted when digestion itself is corrupted. Following Galen <note>original: *Galienus* — the 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years</note>, he calls digestion the "root of life." Therefore, this rule of Galen is the best and, as it were, the only one: to care for the concoction <term>concoction: the internal "cooking" or processing of food by the body's heat</term> of food above all else. For what seems to be the greatest precept—namely, to use healthy foods—will be of no use unless you digest them <note>original: *coquas* — literally "to cook," referring to the stomach's role</note>. For indeed, a noxious humor flows from healthy foods almost as much as from their opposites if they flow into the limbs while still raw <note>undigested</note>. <margin>Handwritten mark in margin</margin> However, even from foods less praised by medical art, often a less harmful nourishment is received if they are more vigorously concocted.

Therefore, let us most diligently avoid "rawness" <note>original: *cruditatem* — indigestion or the state of undigested food in the gut</note> as a cause of both heavy dissolution and suffocation. We do this by choosing a quantity of food and drink suited to us; by quality; by simplicity; by careful preparation; by thorough chewing <note>original: *contritione* — the mechanical grinding of food</note>; by waiting for a true fast to bring back hunger; and if need be, by an external warming poultice for the stomach, or by certain astringent things taken internally after meals.

And let us take diligent care that drink does not exceed the food. Nor should the food be too liquid or too hard; nor should this or that be extremely cold in its effect; nor should the foods be widely diverse; nor should we add raw food to what is already raw in the stomach. These things greatly hinder concoction. <margin>Handwritten mark in margin</margin> Furthermore, let us take the utmost care that we do not impede digestion either by sexual intercourse immediately after eating, or by frequent, unnecessary midday sleep, or by staying awake at night, or by any untimely labor of mind or body, or by any other means.

Indeed, I speak of digestion not only as the first stage, which occurs in the stomach; but also the second, which occurs in the liver; moreover, the third, which occurs in the veins; and finally the fourth, which is effected in the limbs. These require a certain long interval, and if any of these stages is hindered, the humor does not supply the "fuel." Therefore, just as with digestion, it is primarily necessary for life to assist in the purging of excrements <note>original: *excrementorum* — referring to all metabolic waste products</note>.

It is also necessary to wipe away filth from the skin. A movement of the body is necessary—one as continuous, as tempered, and as varied as the movements of the heavens, the air, the fire, and the water—provided that the requirements of concoction and sleep are maintained. However, exhaustion and dissolution must be avoided. Furthermore, in the shade we are covered by mold and decay; under the open sky and in the light, we truly live. This was something my father, Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino's father, Diotifeci d'Agnolo, was a prominent physician</note>, a distinguished physician, frequently had on his lips.

<summary>This page establishes "concoction" (digestion) as the absolute foundation of health, arguing that the quality of one's digestion is more important than the quality of the food itself. It outlines practical dietary habits and identifies the four stages of digestion through which food becomes part of the body's limbs.</summary>
<keywords>digestion, concoction, Galen, Avicenna, humors, hygiene, physical exercise, Ficino, medical stages of digestion</keywords>
