<meta>This page continues the medical advice for scholars, transitioning from the mechanics of digestion (concoction) to the lifestyle and specific qualities of the blood required for a long and healthy life. It emphasizes the importance of a hardy upbringing and defines the ideal "airy" temperament of the vital humors.</meta>

But to carry these things out successfully, it would be worthwhile to have accustomed the body, from a tender age, not so much to city business as to certain rustic exercises and similar activities, and even occasionally to varied types of nutrition; in this way, one maintains a somewhat diverse way of life. That wise man <note>original: *prudens ille* — likely referring to Ficino’s patron, Cosimo de' Medici, or perhaps a classical philosopher like Socrates</note> often used to warn me of this. For those who live at every age with a certain most refined fussiness <note>original: *lautissima quadam curiositate* — referring to a delicate, over-managed, or luxurious lifestyle</note> often live less safely. However, those who did not accustom themselves as youths should at least do so as adults, attempting it nevertheless by cautious degrees.

### The blood and humor suited for life ought to be airy, tempered in quality, and medium and firm in substance. <margin>Chapter 5</margin>

All the Greeks, among the precepts most necessary for a long life, command that we be nourished by **euchymia** <note>original: *euchimis* — from the Greek *euchymia*, meaning "goodness of juices" or healthy humors</note>. We call those healthy foods **euchima** which provide good nutrition—that is, good blood. We call blood "good" when it is not cold, not dry, and not turbulent; rather, it should be warm, moist, and clear. It should be warm, indeed, with a heat that is not sharp. It should be moist, but with a moisture that is not watery. It should also be clear, yet not meanwhile too thin.

For blood that is too fervent <note>too hot or boiling</note> both sharpens the natural heat and dries out the moisture; the moisture it does bring in, or the heat it provides, is made easily dissolvable and fleeting. Furthermore, blood that is too moist and prone to being watery blunts the natural heat; it either weakens the natural moisture, or forces it to liquefy under heat, or even suffocates the heat with its dampness. Altogether, if any portion of natural moisture is drawn from watery blood, it then putrefies easily and is quickly dispersed and dissolved. 

This is what happens to those who eat softer fruits and herbs—unless perhaps they take them rarely as a medicine to soften the bowels—for they soon fill their veins with a raw juice <term>juice: the "succus" or liquid byproduct of the first stage of digestion</term> that is prone to putrefaction. To prevent this, such foods are more safely taken as nourishment if they are either cooked or at least mixed with bread. Therefore, let the blood be neither fiery nor watery, but **airy** <note>In the system of the four humors, blood is linked to the element of Air, which is naturally warm and moist</note>; not like the thickest air, lest it be too inclined toward water; nor like the thinnest air, lest it easily catch fire.

Blood, humor <note>the bodily fluids</note>, good juices, nutrition, long life, warmth, moisture.

<summary>This page argues that a hardy, "rustic" upbringing protects health better than a delicate city life and defines the ideal state of the blood as "airy"—a perfect balance of warmth and moisture.</summary>
<keywords>euchymia, blood quality, humors, rustic exercise, airy temperament, nutrition, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of maintaining the body's vital "spirit" and "humors" through diet and lifestyle, focusing on the ideal consistency of the blood.</meta>

But let its substance hold a middle ground: one in which the middle air <note>In Renaissance medicine, "air" was one of the four humors/elements; the "middle air" refers to a temperate, balanced state.</note> has the greatest influence. Let other nutrients be present only insofar as they accommodate the air. Its substance should not be too refined (or "subtle"), lest it generate an unstable humor and a spirit that is volatile and prone to dissolving. 

Nor should it be too thick; otherwise, it will not serve the intellect at all, and it will hardly be transformed into sufficient humor and spirit. It will block the passages <note>original: "meatus"; the channels or pores in the body through which spirits and fluids flow</note> and provide an occasion for suffocation. Furthermore, a spirit that finally emerges with difficulty from thick matter is itself poorly suited for life; it suffocates the natural heat just as dense smoke suppresses and extinguishes a flame immediately. I will pass over the fact that such a condition is so gloomy that it makes life more miserable than death itself. 

Most of all, however, it seems to me that for a long life it is beneficial to have blood that possesses a very airy substance, yet is not too thick, and contains within itself a certain sticky and tenacious humor. This is much like the combination of refinement and stickiness found in olive oil, or the humor of a fat yet thin eel, or oil drawn from turpentine through a certain process of sublimation <note>A chemical process of purification where a substance is heated into vapor and then condensed back into a liquid.</note>. 

You, therefore, should carefully choose foods and all other things which, according to your strength, might make the blood and humor like this. For such blood and humor is the fuel for the vital heat, just as oil is for a flame; it possesses both refinement and stability. For it is the precept of Rhazes <note>Rhazes (c. 865–925 CE) was a Persian physician whose medical writings were foundational to European universities.</note> for preserving youth to use things that draw the blood toward the heart <note>original: "praecordia"; literally the area around the heart/diaphragm</note>, condensing it there and nourishing the heart. This is approved by Avicenna <note>Avicenna (c. 980–1037 CE) was the author of the *Canon of Medicine*, the most influential medical text of the era.</note>, who teaches that watery and unstable blood must be avoided. 

However, a distinction must be made in this matter according to the different types of bodies. For where the body is dense, one must lean toward making the blood more refined; where the body is porous <note>original: "rarus"; meaning less dense or having more open pores</note>, one must use all remedies to lean toward thickening it. Where there is a medium bodily constitution, the path will similarly be a middle one. We shall attempt to reach this until we hit the limit of the body's natural constitution; otherwise, we would uproot life itself. Furthermore, it is helpful to remember that when we greatly fear the excessive thinness of the blood, and the stomach is not particularly strong by nature, one must approach the thickening of the blood in small, gradual steps; while we strive to nourish a slender man with thicker foods, we must also nourish and support the stomach...

...during sleep. Physical exercise should be increased according to one’s strength, while mental exertion should be decreased, as it very often harms most people. And if the body cannot bear foods that are too viscous <note>sticky or thick in consistency</note>, excessively hard, or quite cold, we can finally achieve a necessary firmness of the blood and humors <note>the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to control health</note> through the use of corals, sandalwood, rose-flavored items, coriander, myrobalans <note>a plum-like dried fruit used as a digestive aid</note>, quinces and quince preserves, rose-sugar, and thin astringents. We can achieve this more safely than by using overly "gluey" substances.

Indeed, it is most convenient to provide nourishment from pine nuts, pistachios, licorice juice, and starch, with the addition of sweet almonds and their oil, quince seeds, and sesame oil, together with the whitest sugar and rose water; this is because the stomach may be unable to digest the viscous limbs of larger animals. To these patients, we will also allow the extremities of chickens or kids <note>young goats</note>, as well as turtles, snails, and testicles. 

We shall give wines that are not white, but red, astringent, and somewhat bitter. Tempering the wine with water infused with iron or mastic <note>a resin from the Mediterranean mastic tree</note>, we will also lightly soothe the skin with oil of mastic and quinces. In the meantime, we will forbid things that make the blood too thin or "boiling" <note>overly agitated or hot</note>, unless perhaps we infuse a little saffron or cinnamon into the meals; these act as "vehicles" so that the food may digest more easily and be carried more quickly through the narrow passages to the limbs. 

For it is difficult to bring viscous or slightly firmer foods from a stomach that is not particularly strong all the way to the third and fourth digestion <note>In ancient medicine, the "third and fourth digestions" occurred in the blood vessels and the tissues/organs themselves</note>, unless they are carried by such means and, moreover, are encouraged by light massages. When you perform these massages, do them with soft hands, and remember to moisten the hands with a fragrant wine in which chamomile, melilot <note>a sweet-smelling clover</note>, and roses have been steeped.

...the distinction between internal decay and injury inflicted from the outside. In this case, there is a danger of decay <note>original: *putrefactione*; in the medical theory of the time, this refers to the "rotting" of humors when they become stagnant or overly hot and moist</note> and suffocation. Although I restrain you, avoiding the extremely strict rules that **Hippocrates** <note>The "Father of Medicine" (c. 460 – c. 370 BCE), known for his moderate approach to health</note> condemns, I do not give you license to be reckless.

Eat moist herbs and fruits on their own. Be more sparing with milk and fish—and consume both with honey. Eat mushrooms most sparingly of all, and only with aromatics and pear seeds. Similarly, drink pure water only in moderation. Season moist or fatty foods with aromatics <note>term: <term>aromatics</term> (spices like cinnamon or ginger used to "dry" or "warm" moist foods)</note>; otherwise, they introduce a great deal of foreign and decaying moisture into the limbs. Even if they provide necessary natural moisture, it is of a kind very prone to corruption. This is no different than watery wine, which quickly becomes cloudy and spoils. This leads to premature graying <note>original: *canities*</note>, paleness, and senile wrinkling. 

If meat is eaten daily, even in small amounts, it quickly causes decay. This is why **Porphyry** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234 – c. 305 CE) who wrote a famous treatise on abstaining from animal food</note>, relying on the authority of the **Pythagoreans** and the ancients, detested the eating of animals. Have we not heard that long-lived men before the Flood <note>A reference to the biblical patriarchs who were believed to have lived hundreds of years on a plant-based diet</note> abstained from animals? However, physicians do not forbid the use of meat so much as its abuse. 

Finally, flee moist things that are prone to decay, remembering that the moist and the fat age more quickly and die. **Hippocrates** says this, and the facts themselves declare it. On the other hand, take very dry things in moderation, or at least balance them with a more liberal amount of drink. Safely choose the middle ground. Nevertheless, **Avicenna** <note>A Persian physician (c. 980 – 1037 CE) whose medical encyclopedia was the standard text in Medieval and Renaissance universities</note> prefers a slightly juicier and softer constitution to avoid gray hair. 

Be most cautious regarding cold or hot things. Seek what is both warm and moist. If the air is hot, let the moisture of the feast exceed the heat. If it is cold, let the heat exceed the moisture. In both cases, the excess should be moderate. In every instance, let the heat and moisture be somewhat "sticky" and astringent <note>original: *stirpticum*; substances that contract body tissues or "bind" the humors</note>, so that the moisture, once distributed to the limbs, may cling more firmly and endure for a long time under the influence of heat. 

Choice wheaten bread has this quality above all. Then, red and astringent wine, as well as sweet wine. Third, pine nuts and things similar to them in warmth and stickiness. Fourth, meats that are not both moist and loose, such as pork and suckling lamb. Yet the ancient physicians, especially **Galen** <note>The most influential physician of the Roman Empire (129 – c. 216 CE), whose humoral theories lasted for 1,500 years</note>, strongly recommend pork and blood because of their similarity to our own bodies. These are best, therefore, for similar bodies, such as those of peasants and robust men who exercise their bodies a great deal, especially if...

But to return to our list, we do not recommend the moister meats, as we have mentioned; nor those that are simultaneously tough and dry, such as the older meat of hares and oxen. Instead, choose a middle ground: such as chickens, pullets, capons, peacocks, pheasants, partridges, and perhaps young pigeons, preferably domestic ones. Nor would it be unreasonable to feed a goose on spelt or clean water, and after it is slaughtered, to preserve the meat for seven days seasoned with salt and coriander prepared in vinegar before you eat it. Similarly with stags, if the stomach is very strong. For it is probable that certain long-lived animals contribute to a long life, provided that the meat of such kinds is eaten while young. 

...The amount of food should be twice the amount of the drink. Bread should be double and a half <note>original: "sexquialter"</note> compared to eggs, triple compared to meat, and quadruple compared to fish, herbs, and moister fruits. Let the meal not begin with a drink, nor should the drink be too copious at once. Always include something <term>astringent</term> <note>original: "stiptici"; substances that shrink or constrict body tissues</note> at the table, either without a drink or with a very small one. 

When the <term>temperament</term> <note>original: "complexio"; the individual's unique balance of the four humors (hot, cold, wet, dry)</note>, age, location, or season leans toward the hot or dry, you too should lean slightly toward the opposite qualities. Where it leans toward the cold or moist, likewise turn toward the opposites. Where there is a balance, maintain that balance. 

Furthermore, physical exercise should be increased or decreased according to the heaviness of the feasts we consume, which may sometimes be necessary for longevity. You should have two meals within the nine hours of the day, both of them moderate. Indeed, let the dinner be the lighter one. There should be roughly two physical exercises after the first digestion, carried out until one almost reaches a sweat. Nighttime sleep, as it is often necessary, always...

### Chapter 7

Animals that come into our care must be fed clean and select foods if we are to eat them. These, and all other things, should be chosen from higher, fragrant pastures. Regarding this, one should always remember a rule approved by the philosopher Arnaldus <note>Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous medieval physician who wrote extensively on hygiene and diet.</note> before all others: one must choose animals, herbs, fruits, grains, and wines from high and fragrant regions, as we have mentioned. These places are tempered by mild winds and warmed by the pleasant rays of the sun. There, the waters do not stagnate, and the fields are not made fertile by manure <term>sterquilinio; dung or refuse used as fertilizer</term>, but by natural moisture. Whatever grows there remains unspoiled for a long time. One should live only in such a place and eat only these things. 

One cannot trust that a body intended to be long-lasting and free from decay can be built from foods that rot quickly. Nor can we hope to live long where the fruits of the earth do not keep for long, or where people rarely reach old age. How much difference geography and diet make is shown by the peach <note>original: *pomum persici* or "Persian apple." In antiquity, it was a common belief that peaches were toxic in Persia but became wholesome when planted in Egypt.</note>, which is poisonous in Persia but a friend to the heart in Egypt. Likewise, hellebore <note>A medicinal but toxic plant; the town of Anticyra was famous for producing the best hellebore for treating madness.</note> is taken without harm in Anticyra, but elsewhere it is a poison. 

Aristotle chooses a dwelling that faces south and east, under thin air that is neither damp nor cold. Plato found long-lived people in the highest and most temperate regions. It is determined that manuring the fields or failing to drain stagnant water from the land is harmful. Everything born there is subject to quick corruption. For this reason, I cannot blame those who criticize the wise Hesiod <note>An ancient Greek poet whose work *Works and Days* serves as a guide to farming.</note> for omitting the use of manure in his agricultural writings. However, he was prudent and cared more for health than for fertility. He believed the field could be sufficiently enriched by turning over the leaves of lupines and beans at the proper time. 

But if we are compelled to live in damper and less clean regions and to consume foods that do not last, we should follow the same diet that doctors prescribe for those living under pestilential <term>diseased or plague-carrying</term> air...

<summary>This page argues that health depends on consuming food from high, well-ventilated, and naturally watered regions rather than manured fields. It cites classical authorities like Aristotle, Plato, and Hesiod to support the idea that environment directly dictates the medicinal or poisonous nature of plants.</summary>
<keywords>Arnald of Villanova, diet, manure, hellebore, peach, Aristotle, Plato, Hesiod, agriculture, geography and health</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues medical advice on hygiene and diet, likely following a section on environmental hazards or general constitutional health.</meta>

...they commanded. On this matter, we have dealt sufficiently in the book *Against the Pestilence* <note>original: "contra pestilentiae"</note>.

In short, we shall use sweet and somewhat warm scents. We should purge ourselves often and gently with well-prepared aloe. We call it "properly prepared" if it has been washed with rose water, or rose juice, or if fresh crushed roses have been perfectly mixed into it. Then, myrobalans <term>dried medicinal fruits used in Galenic medicine as a gentle laxative</term> should be added to it, along with mastic <term>a resin from the mastic tree, used for digestion and breath</term>, and perhaps rose. This medicine is, beyond controversy, wonderful for long preserving a healthy mind in a healthy body <note>A translation of the famous Latin maxim: "mens sana in corpore sano"</note>.

Furthermore, we shall exercise the body. We shall use a suitable hearth. We shall season our meals with this powder: let there be a fourth part of an ounce of emblic myrobalans <term>the Indian gooseberry, prized in ancient medicine</term>; a half part of sandalwood; a whole part of cinnamon; and an eighth part of saffron. With this powder and these practices combined, we may perhaps be able to prevent the corruptions threatening us from foul foods and locations.

Let us remember, however: where many more die from the suffocation of putrefaction <note>the "rotting" of bodily fluids or humors, often associated with infectious disease</note> than from wasting away <term>resolution, or the weakening and dissolution of bodily strength</term>, there we must resist putrefaction and suffocation most strongly. Where it is the opposite, we should instead use aromatic and somewhat astringent seasonings (as we have said) and similar scents, which prevent putrefaction at any time.

Anointing with oil prevents the harm of cold; using baths of water and oil prevents the wasting away that threatens from labor or hot weather. Similarly, rinse the mouth often with water; keep licorice juice <term>derived from the Glycyrrhiza plant</term> or crystal sugar in the mouth. Pour plenty of rose water and a little rose vinegar over the hands and face, and use similar scents.

Every seventh hour, refresh oneself with moderate food; rest both body and mind together, avoiding heat. It matters most of all what kind of wheat and wine we use in abundance. These should be such that they remain wholesome beyond a year, or rather up to three years, if we are to hope for incorruptible nourishment from them. Let the wine, whether red or tawny, be clear, sweet, astringent, fragrant, and such that it requires water. Unless perhaps you find a wine that is light yet long-lasting, which is usually very rare. Regarding that which is stronger, the philosopher Isaac <note>Isaac Israeli ben Solomon (c. 832–932), a highly influential Jewish physician and philosopher whose works on diet were staples of medieval medical education</note> says one type is "vinous," boiled, and cleared of its dregs. He orders this to be tempered with pure spring water some time before we drink it, so that it may be mixed more perfectly. However, watery and weak wine...

<summary>This page provides practical medical recipes and lifestyle advice for avoiding "putrefaction" (disease), focusing on aromatic spices, gentle laxatives like aloe, and the proper selection of wine and wheat.</summary>
<keywords>pestilence, aloe, myrobalans, mastic, spice powder, cinnamon, saffron, Isaac Israeli, dietary health, rose water</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
...or bitter: he warns that it should be avoided, as it quickly becomes acidic within the veins and limbs, or otherwise putrefies. Watery wine is subject to putrefaction; if its substance is preserved and it is boiled, it will at least be useful. Otherwise, it is not praised, because it will create corruptible moisture. However, its sharpness must be countered with specifically chosen water. But the kind of wine we have approved, Isaac <note>original: "staach," likely referring to Isaac Judaeus, a 10th-century physician</note> says—according to the opinion of the ancients—is very similar to the great **theriacs** <note>Theriac: a famous ancient "universal antidote" and herbal medicine used to treat poisons and diseases</note>. This wine (as we have said), being well-balanced, warms a cold bodily constitution, cools a heated one, moistens a dry one, and dries out a moist one. Furthermore (as **Galen** <note>A Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over 1,000 years</note> says), it restores the natural humor, fosters internal heat, and balances both. 

Mixing such wine with water is more necessary for young people, less so for the elderly; for those elderly who are of a cold nature, it is needed least of all. For a cold and hard old age (as **Plato** says) is warmed and softened by undiluted wine, just as iron is by fire or lupins <note>A type of legume often soaked to remove bitterness</note> by water. What we have said regarding the power of wine to balance opposing and clashing states, I know also occurs through **licorice** <note>original: "glycatbizam," a corruption of glycyrrhiza</note>, but more weakly. It also happens through rose oil, but only externally. Therefore, let these things be familiar to you. Do not doubt that whatever is balanced in quality and power can balance other things, just as cold makes other things cold. It is especially helpful to possess this balance, through which things are most healthful. But these matters must be discussed elsewhere.

### Diet: Lifestyle: Medicine for the Elderly
### Chapter 8.

When a person has first completed seven sets of seven years <note>The "climacteric" year of 49</note>, reaching their **fiftieth year**, let them reflect that **Venus** <note>The planet/goddess associated with youth, desire, and reproduction</note> signifies the young, while **Saturn** <note>The planet/god associated with old age, coldness, and melancholy</note> signifies the elderly; among astronomers, these stars are the most hostile to one another of all. Therefore, those of Saturn [the elderly] should flee the works of Venus [sexual activity], which detracts significantly even from the young. For it does not benefit those already born, but those yet to be born; it even dries out plants immediately once the seed is produced. Furthermore, they should consider the cold and the night air to be lethal to them. They should maintain a diet from which they can expect the most blood and the most **spirit** <note>In ancient medicine, "spirit" or pneuma was a vital substance that carried life through the body</note>: specifically from fresh egg yolks and wine that is somewhat sweet and, above all, highly fragrant. For...

<summary>This page discusses the medicinal properties of wine as a balancer of bodily humors and begins a chapter on the lifestyle of the elderly, advising those over fifty to avoid sexual activity and cold air.</summary>
<keywords>wine, theriac, Galen, Plato, geriatrics, astrology, Venus, Saturn, egg yolks, humors</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dietary and lifestyle advice for the elderly, following the previous page's discussion on general health. The author is likely Marsilio Ficino, writing on "The Care of the Aged."</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>

This egg yolk specifically restores the blood of the heart; wine especially refreshes the spirit <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirit" refers to the subtle, vaporous substance that connects the physical body with the soul and mind.</note>. Let them altogether use a diet of the choicest meats that are very easy to cook, as such a diet increases both heat and moisture. Let them constantly refresh the spirit with pleasant odors, especially that of wine. One should always avoid insomnia and hunger, as well as excessive labor of both body and mind, and likewise solitude and sorrow. Let them frequently return to their enjoyments if they have happened to stop; indeed, these should never be interrupted.

Let them recall certain games and habits, as far as is proper, which they enjoyed long ago before the onset of old age. For it is most difficult (so to speak) to become young again in body unless you first become a child again in your mind. Therefore, in every stage of life, it is of great benefit to retain something of youth and to always provide oneself with various delights. However, long and profuse laughter <note>original: "risum"</note> should be avoided; for it dilates the spirit too much toward the extremities of the body, wasting it. 

But let us return to the elderly. Furthermore, if they feel cold, they should seek aromatic poultices <note>original: "fomenta"</note> that are simultaneously hot and moist. Let them remember that it is not a childish thing, that youthful poultice of Abishag <note>A reference to the biblical Abishag the Shunammite, who was brought to keep the elderly King David warm. Ficino treats this as a medical therapy rather than just a story.</note>; it was indeed done for David, though perhaps done too late. 

A wonderful poultice for an old man: The crumb <note>original: "medulla," literally the "marrow" or soft center of the bread.</note> of fresh, still-warm bread, infused with Malmsey wine <note>A sweet, potent Greek wine highly valued in the Renaissance.</note> and powdered mint, applied to the stomach and often held up to the nose to be smelled. For even the crumb of bread alone, in this manner, held back the fleeing spirit of Democritus <note>The ancient Greek "laughing philosopher" who was said to have prolonged his life for several days by smelling hot bread.</note> when he was about to die, for as long as he wished. 

Furthermore, let them use light rubbings or sometimes baths that draw nourishment to the extremities of the body. Above all, let them be familiar with pine nuts—specifically washed ones. For the ancient doctors proved this food to be most suitable for the elderly. For it is hot, moist, and fatty; it softens all roughness, and at the same time—which is wonderful—while it increases the natural moisture of the body, it meanwhile dries out what is superfluous and purges putrid humors. 

There are those who give one drachma <term>drachma: a small unit of weight, approximately 3.5 grams</term> of such pine nuts daily to the elderly to be eaten after meals. I would also give another drachma to the elderly on an empty stomach: either fresh, warm, golden, or shelled. You may also compose an electuary <term>electuary: a medicinal paste or conserve made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</term> in this way: 

Take four ounces of clean sweet almonds; the same amount of those pine nuts; two ounces of pistachios; one ounce of cucumber seeds; one ounce of cleaned hazelnuts; crush them; cook them together with the whitest sugar; to which, however, you will have added one ounce of fresh ginger...

<summary>This page provides practical advice for the elderly, focusing on the psychological importance of "becoming a child in mind" to stay young in body, alongside specific remedies like pine nuts and aromatic poultices to maintain vital heat and moisture.</summary>
<keywords>geriatrics, pine nuts, spirit, Malmsey wine, Democritus, Abishag, electuary, dietary health, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on medicinal preparations for longevity, focusing on specific recipes and the authorities of Arabic medicine like Rhazes and Avicenna.</meta>

...of the preserve, take a drachm <note>A unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams.</note> of saffron, a half-drachm of musk <note>original: "musci"</note>, and an equal amount of ambergris. Dissolve sugar in lemon balm water—that is, citron-flavored water—and rose water. Add many gold leaves to these. For by the daily use of this, the elderly will achieve a stronger and longer life. They may take this at the table <note>original: "mensa"</note>, or several hours before a meal. It will be most effective if they drink some white and fragrant wine along with a confection of this kind. 

In warmer seasons, however, rose sugar with gold leaves and preserved myrobalans <term>myrobalans: an astringent plum-like fruit used in medieval medicine to balance the "humors"</term> will prolong the lives of the elderly. No one doubts that Theriac <term>Theriac: a famous ancient "universal antidote" made of dozens of ingredients</term> is suitable for the same purpose for people and seasons of a "moist" temperament. Enough has been said regarding its use in the preceding book. 

No one will deny that the roots of Elecampane <note>original: "Inulae"</note> and Behen roots <note>original: "beenia"; likely referring to the White and Red Behen, plants used as tonics</note>—both the white and the red varieties—are extremely beneficial to them, especially when fresh. The former is used for nourishment, the latter for spice. Generally, all things that are simultaneously warm, moist, aromatic, styptic <note>original: "striptica"; meaning astringent or "binding"</note>, and oily are beneficial. It is certain that the elderly should regularly use the juice of choice licorice <note>original: "glycirrhiza"</note>. For they say that licorice is very similar to the heat and moisture of the human body; furthermore, it helps with various diseases of the elderly. Almond milk and foods made with starch should also be familiar staples, as well as sugar and raisins.

Rhazes <note>A famous 9th-century Persian physician</note> highly approves of the *Triphera* <note>original: "Triseram"; a type of medicinal paste or electuary</note> made from Indian, Emblic, and Belleric myrobalans. Likewise, he recommends Indian myrobalans preserved in sugar, not only for maintaining health but even for delaying old age. Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina, the influential 11th-century philosopher and physician</note> praises both the "Greater" and "Lesser" Triphera of myrobalans. Furthermore, he praises a confection made of iron scales, but especially one made of gold. He commands that one should chew myrobalans daily, specifically the Chebulic variety preserved in sugar, to ward off the inconveniences of old age.

### The necessary nature of Aromatics and Cordials.
And again, the manner of diet for the elderly.

<summary>This page outlines specific medicinal recipes and dietary recommendations for longevity, emphasizing the use of gold leaf, myrobalans, and licorice. It cites the prominent medical authorities Rhazes and Avicenna to support these geriatric treatments.</summary>
<keywords>geriatrics, longevity, gold leaf, myrobalans, Rhazes, Avicenna, licorice, Theriac, dietetics, medieval medicine</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
f

It is quickly apparent that there are various virtues in myrobalans <note>original: "mirobalanis"; these are astringent, plum-like fruits used in Ayurvedic and medieval European medicine</note>. First, one which wonderfully dries out excess humor, by which it prevents grey hair. Second, one which gathers the natural humor and protects it from both corruption and inflammation at the same time; thus, it extends life for a long duration. Third: one which, through its aromatic and astringent <note>original: "striptica"</note> power, gathers, nourishes, and strengthens the natural and animal virtue and spirit. Because of this, someone might think that the Tree of Life in Paradise was perhaps a myrobalan tree. 

Gold and silver, coral, spodium <note>original: "spodiumq"; medicinal ash or metal slag</note>, and precious stones do something almost similar, although instead of aromatic virtue, they offer the power of illuminating the spirits. 

But you should consider that aromatics benefit our lives most—as we indicated above—when, along with a certain aromatic vigor, they are equally moist and warm, and have a rich stickiness <note>original: "lentoremq; pinguem"</note> suited for growth. Such are, first and foremost, the roots of white and red *behen* <note>original: "beeniæ"; a root used as a tonic, possibly *Limonium* or *Centaurea behen*</note>, especially when fresh. Or at least when, with a certain subtle, fragrant, and sharp virtue, they have a dense substance and a very astringent property. 

Such a composition seems to exist among "cold" cordials <note>medicines intended to strengthen the heart</note>, first in myrobalans and in amber <note>original: "luccino," likely a variant of *succino*</note>. Then in roses, juice, and citrus seeds. Thirdly, in sandalwood and coriander, as well as myrrh and other similar things. 

Among the "hot" cordials, however, are zedoary <note>original: "zedoane"; a root similar to ginger</note>, agarwood, citrus peel, and cloves <note>original: "chariophilis"</note>, nutmeg, mace, frankincense <note>original: "olibano"</note>, mastic, and leopard's bane <note>original: "doronico"</note>. We experience the same quality to be in sage. 

Authorities relate that ambergris <note>original: "ambram"</note> and musk have an astringent virtue. Ginger <note>original: "zinziber"</note>, however, because of its certain moisture—especially when fresh and preserved—is often beneficial to the elderly. But this, along with cloves, should be taken cautiously because of the intensity of their heat. Zedoary also should be taken cautiously, even though it is judged similar to theriac <note>original: "tiriacæ"; a famous ancient poly-pharmaceutical compound used as an antidote and panacea</note>; it has both an astringent and rich nature, making it very suitable for the elderly. 

Ambergris, because of its almost temperate heat, is nearly the most excellent of all. And because of its richness mixed with a subtle astringency, it has a special privilege for preserving life in the limbs <note>original: "meõns," likely *membris*</note> and spirits. Furthermore, if a water is made from it and the skin is washed, it restores the "fourth digestion" <note>in Galenic medicine, the final stage of nutrition occurring within the tissues themselves</note> and cures its diseases...

<summary>This page discusses the medicinal virtues of myrobalans and various "cordial" substances (like ambergris, gold, and spices) believed to strengthen the heart and extend life. It categorizes these substances into "hot" and "cold" according to the humoral theory of medicine.</summary>

<keywords>myrobalans, longevity, cordials, ambergris, zedoary, humoral medicine, Tree of Life, astringency, fourth digestion</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the properties of various medicinal substances and their effects on the body's "humors" (vital fluids), leading into a specific chapter on the use of gold for longevity.</meta>

...expels those ailments occurring from a deficiency. However, aromatics that possess an extremely subtle substance—such as cinnamon and saffron—should be mixed with cold and harder **cordials** <term>heart-strengthening medicines</term>. For if aromatics are only hot and subtle, and are taken on their own, they excite the natural heat too much and dissolve the humors. Nevertheless, they are necessary both for the digestion of colder and more humid foods, and for transporting the "hard" cordials to the **chest region** <term>original: *praecordia*, the area around the heart</term>. 

It must not be overlooked that the very humor necessary for life has its primary seat in the heart and its associated arteries and veins; this is something that Isaac <note>Isaac Judaeus, a prominent 10th-century physician and philosopher</note> clearly teaches. And as Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina, the influential Persian polymath</note> proves, this humor is frequently irrigated and nourished by the natural humor of the other limbs. Therefore, one must take care lest the humor of any limb should dry out by some chance, and even more so that the humor of the heart region is not diminished. 

And so that nutrients, or poultices, and all cordials may be carried most widely through narrow passages to the heart, add **saffron** to them. To ensure they remain there, use **myrobalans** <note>A dried fruit from tropical trees, often used in medieval medicine as a binder</note>. To achieve both effects: among hot remedies, take **musk** and **ambergris**; among cold remedies, take **roses** and **myrrh**. Remember that sweet **fennel** <note>original: *matarruum*, likely a corruption of the Greek *marathron*</note> is beneficial for the elderly. For it diffuses nourishment through the limbs and, with the same faculty as milk, increases the natural humor. Hence Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and author of the most important herbal of the ancient world</note> says that serpents shed their annual old age—their skin—by using fennel. We also approve of **sage**. For by its nature, it warms and strengthens with tempered virtue and repels paralysis. We also approve of the moderate use of preserved **ginger**, for it possesses a richness along with its heat.

### On gold, golden foods, and the restoration of the elderly
### Chapter 10

Everyone approves of **gold** <note>original: *Aurum*, misspelled as *Vrum* in the OCR</note> above all things, as being the most temperate of all substances and the most protected from corruption. It is attributed to the Sun because of its brilliance, and to Jupiter because of its balanced governance. Therefore, it is able to temper the natural heat when mixed with humor, and to keep the humors from corruption...

<summary>This page discusses how aromatics like saffron and cinnamon help deliver medicine to the heart, and introduces gold as a supreme "temperate" medicine for preserving the health of the elderly.</summary>
<keywords>medicine, heart health, humors, saffron, cinnamon, gold, Avicenna, Dioscorides, longevity, fennel</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
...to preserve: to instill a <term>Solar and Jovial</term><note>Refers to the astrological influences of the Sun and Jupiter, which were believed to govern vitality, warmth, and the "vital spirits" of the heart.</note> virtue into the spirits and limbs. But they nevertheless wish to make the very hard substance of gold more <term>subtle</term><note>In alchemy, "subtle" means refined or broken down so finely that it can flow through the body’s smallest channels.</note> and very easy to penetrate. For they know that the <term>cordial</term><note>Heart-strengthening</note> virtue then most greatly refreshes the depths of the heart when nature labors least in absorbing them. So that the body is not fatigued, gold must be administered either after it has been made most subtle, or together with the most subtle substances. 

They think it would be best if "potable gold" <note>original: "aurum potabile" — a legendary alchemical life-extender consisting of gold in liquid form.</note> could be made without any foreign mixture. If that is not possible, they wish it to be taken blended and reduced into leaves <note>Gold leaf.</note>. You will have a nearly "potable gold" (so to speak) if you do the following: 

Collect flowers of borage, bugloss, and melissa (which we call <term>lemon balm</term>); and when the Moon enters the sign of Leo, Aries, or Sagittarius, and looks upon the Sun or Jupiter: Cook them with white sugar dissolved in rose water, and for every ounce, carefully add three gold leaves. Take this while fasting with a certain "golden wine." 

Also, take the water distilled from a capon <note>A castrated rooster, valued in historical medicine for its "temperate" and nourishing meat.</note> over a fire, or otherwise consumed together with a rose <term>julep</term><note>A sweet medicinal syrup.</note>, into which you have previously dissolved certain gold leaves. Furthermore, quench glowing hot gold in the clearest spring water. Grind gold leaves with that same water. Temper "golden wine" with it, and eat a fresh egg yolk along with such a drink. 

Truly, you will easily preserve the moisture in the whole <term>tree of the body</term><note>A common Renaissance metaphor comparing the human nervous and vascular systems to a tree that must be kept "moist" to avoid the "drying out" of old age.</note> if you preserve it in the roots. Therefore, take the heart, liver, stomach, testicles, and brain of hens, chicks, and capons of this kind: 

Cook them in a little water with very little salt. Once cooked, mash the meat and all the juices with sugar; add a fresh egg yolk. Make a cake seasoned with a little cinnamon and saffron, and covered in gold leaf. Eat this, or take it once at least every fourth day. And then eat it alone, though with clear wine added for the drink.

### On the use of milk and human blood for the life of the elderly <page-num>11</page-num> Chapter 11

Often after the tenth stage of life, and sometimes after the ninth <term>septenary</term><note>A cycle of seven years. The 9th septenary is age 63; the 10th is age 70.</note>, the "human tree" wastes away as its moisture gradually dries up. Then, for the first time, this human tree must be irrigated with human and youthful liquid: by which...

<summary>This page provides alchemical and dietary recipes for "potable gold" and restorative foods intended to prolong life and strengthen the heart. It concludes by introducing the controversial use of human milk and blood to "re-hydrate" the elderly body during its final cycles of life.</summary>
<keywords>potable gold, aurum potabile, borage, lemon balm, astrology, capon, tree of the body, longevity, septenary cycles, elderly care</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on medical treatments for old age, likely from Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life" (De Vita Libri Tres). The previous section would have discussed restorative diets.</meta>

...may flourish again. You should therefore choose a healthy, beautiful, cheerful, and temperate girl. Avoid her milk when she is hungry and while the moon is waxing. Immediately after, eat a small amount of powdered sweet fennel <note>original: *maratri dulcis*</note> properly prepared with sugar <note>original: *sacharo*</note>.

Sugar will not allow the milk to curdle or spoil in the stomach, while fennel, because it is both subtle and "friendly" to milk, will help it spread to the limbs. Diligent physicians strive to restore those whom senile hectic fever <term>Hectic fever: a wasting disease or "consumption" believed in the Renaissance to dry out the body's natural moisture during old age.</term> has consumed by using the liquid of human blood which has been distilled over a fire with sublime art. 

Why then do we not occasionally refresh those already worn out by old age with this drink? There is a certain common and ancient opinion that certain old hags, who are called *striges* <note>original: *striges*; vampire-like spirits or witches in Roman folklore believed to suck the blood of infants</note> in the common tongue, suck the blood of infants so that they might grow young again. Why should our old men, who are destitute of all other help, not also suck the blood of a youth? I say the blood of a willing youth—one who is healthy, happy, and temperate—whose blood is of the best quality but perhaps too abundant. Let them suck, in the manner of leeches, from a barely opened vein in the left arm, one or two ounces. 

Immediately afterward, they should take the same amount of sugar and wine; they should do this while hungry and thirsty and when the moon is waxing. If the raw blood is difficult to digest, let it be cooked first with sugar, or let it be distilled moderately with sugar over hot water. Then let it be drunk. 

To warm the stomach at that time, swine’s blood is an immediate help. Let a sponge soaked in warm wine absorb the blood flowing from the pig's vein, and let it be applied warm to the stomach at once. Galen <note>original: *Galienus*; a prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> and Serapion <note>an influential 9th-century physician from Damascus</note> say that the bite of a mad dog is cured by the drinking of dog's blood. They did not choose to assign a reason for this. Therefore, having searched for two days, I am of the opinion that the poisonous saliva of the mad dog, pressed into a man's wounded foot, ascends gradually through the veins to the heart like a poison—unless they are distracted in the meantime. If, therefore, in the meantime he drinks the blood of another dog, that raw blood floats in the stomach for many hours, and finally, it draws the dog's saliva back down to the stomach as if it were a plaything, to be cast out by the other blood. For there is a power in canine blood to attract the saliva of a dog...

<page-num>d iii</page-num>

<summary>This page contains Marsilio Ficino’s controversial medical advice on rejuvenation, suggesting that the elderly might regain vitality by consuming the milk of young women or the blood of healthy young men. He also discusses the sympathetic medicinal use of animal blood, specifically for treating rabies.</summary>
<keywords>rejuvenation, blood-drinking, Ficino, medicine, Galen, Serapion, rabies, sugar, fennel, striges</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino's discussion on rejuvenation and the controversial practice of consuming youthful blood to restore the vital spirits of the elderly.</meta>

...to be avoided; and in turn, there is a <term>power</term> <note>original: "uirtus"; in Renaissance medicine, this refers to an inherent quality or biological force that directs bodily functions</note> in saliva to pursue similar blood. Therefore, the poison absorbed from the heart of the thighs and the blood, while floating in the stomach, is led down through the lower parts together with the blood; and thus it leaves the man unharmed. Why mention these things? Firstly, indeed, so that I might reveal the cause of such a hidden matter that occurred during the discussion. Secondly, to advise that blood can be drunk, and indeed healthfully. And that in human blood there is a power by which it attracts human blood and mutually pursues it. Lest you perhaps doubt that youthful blood, when drunk by an elderly person, can be drawn to the veins and limbs and there be of the greatest benefit.

### Diet, habitation, and habits of the elderly. xii.

It is beneficial for the decrepit to remember: a weak nature must not be fatigued by the weight of nourishment, nor even distracted by an excessive variety of feasts. For even a youthful age quickly becomes elderly from this <note>likely referring to the over-consumption of wine or rich foods</note>. Therefore, let them divide their meals: nor should they refresh their nature with so much frequent nourishment; yet with an interval...

...and let them dwell among vines; or at least receive the vapor and scent of the pine. For it is probable that trees naturally endowed with long life—especially if they remain green in winter—will benefit you toward a long life through their shade, their vapor, their fresh fruit, their wood, and any other timely use. Regarding long-lived animals, we have spoken above. Now indeed, it will be conducive to this same goal if you live as much as possible among those who are healthy, naturally similar to your own healthy state, and who are your friends. And perhaps it is even more effective if they are slightly younger than you. But whether and how frequent association with the young can delay the aging of the senses <note>original: "sensui retardare"; to slow down the decline of the faculties</note> for a little while, the modest Socrates <note>original: "pudicus Socrates"; a reference to the Socratic idea of "Platonic love" or the restorative power of youthful company</note> should be consulted.

### What supports the elderly receive from the planets to comfort all their limbs.

## Chapter 13

But you anxious old men should instead consult Apollo, who judged Socrates the wisest of the Greeks. Consult Jupiter as well, and Venus. Phoebus <note>another name for Apollo, god of the sun and medicine</note> himself, the inventor of the medical art, will give you a crushed nut for comforting the stomach. Jupiter, together with Phoebus, provides mastic and mint. Venus indeed provides coral. 

For comforting the head: Phoebus provides peony, frankincense, marjoram, and myrrh with savory. Jupiter provides spikenard and mace. Venus finally provides sweet fennel and myrtle. 

For comforting the heart <note>The original "uer" is likely a transcription error for "cor" (heart), as the following remedies match Ficino's usual heart tonics</note>, you shall receive from Phoebus balm-mint, saffron, aloe wood, frankincense, amber, musk, leopard's bane <note>original: "doronicum"</note>, a little cinnamon, citron bark, and cassia. From Jupiter, you receive lily, bugloss, basil, and mint, as well as behen roots—both the white and the red varieties. From Venus alone, you receive myrtle, sandalwood, and rose. Together with Saturn, she provides coriander. 

Consult these things diligently: and those which are for the stomach, prepare with oil of quinces into the form of a <term>cerate</term> <note>a stiff, medicinal ointment made of wax and oil</note>. Those for the head: pour over with oil of spikenard and smear the neck, temples, and forehead. Those finally for the heart: sprinkle with golden wine and rose water, and apply them to the <term>praecordia</term> <note>the region of the chest over the heart and diaphragm</note>. 

But the liver—which is primarily necessary for creating blood—I know not how we passed over. To this organ, therefore, Phoebus will always bring help with eupatory and balsam. Jupiter helps with pistachios and raisins. Venus, however...

->**A Conversation of Old Men under Venus through Green Meadows**<-
### Chapter 14. ###

But from these weightier deities, I recall you for a little while, we old men, to the leeks and the greening meadows of Venus. I call you all to nourishing Venus. Not indeed a Venus who plays tricks on you, but one who is joyful. She says this both to you and to me, now that I am old. First, indeed, she pours forth this joyful oracle: "I gave you a man who was unknown to his son, one who died of pleasure. Therefore, I will preserve life for you through a certain pleasure, though not one similar to that." The god <term>Liber</term><note>The Roman god of wine and freedom, often identified with Bacchus</note>—the propagator of life—will also preserve that life with the same liberty and virility. Liber himself always hates slaves; and the life which he promises through wine, he fulfills as a long life only for free men. Formerly, it was profitable to the simulacrum of my life and mind when Saturn was reigning; but now, with a diminished mind, you please me daily. To you, however, a greater <term>mint</term><note>The author is making a pun on the Latin words "menta" (mint) and "mens/mentis" (the mind). He suggests that mint is good for the "mind" of the elderly.</note> is profitable for the mind and for virility. A diminished amount is harmful. Gather laughter from my gardens. Ignore the fig. But when you pluck these violets, consider that you are plucking lilies; and when taking the lily, take the saffron. Jupiter himself, having obtained saffron from Phoebus, propagated it.

### Elysium

I have transformed Lalium <note>Likely a mythological character, though the name may be a variant of a flower-nymph name</note>, who was received by Jove, into these violets which you see here. Therefore, through their scent, their use, and by frequently dwelling among them, that youthful spirit flows into us. We shall find, at last, that the nature of sight <note>original: *visus*</note> is luminous and a friend to light; yet it is volatile and easily dissipated. For that reason, while it expands through light—being a friend to the cool—it is sometimes seized by an excessive abundance of light and entirely dissolved by that vehement expansion. However, it naturally flees from darkness as if from an enemy; therefore...

In green things, the light of the sun—which is implanted within them—still retains a spring-like moisture and a subtle watery essence filled with a certain hidden light. Since the spirit of vision is in some way the primary part of the <term>animal spirit</term><note>In Renaissance medicine, the "animal spirit" (from the Latin *anima*, meaning soul) was a refined substance that flowed through the nerves to facilitate sensation and thought.</note>, green also restores the animal spirit as a whole. 

And we should also remember: if the color green benefits the animal spirit so much—because it is the middle and most gentle degree among the colors—then things that are perfectly balanced in their qualities will be even more helpful to the <term>natural and vital spirits</term><note>The "natural spirit" was believed to govern digestion and growth from the liver, while the "vital spirit" carried life-force and heat from the heart.</note>, and will be greatly beneficial to our lives. <meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of the "spirit" (the bridge between the physical body and the soul) and shifts into a dialogue between the planetary archetypes of Venus and Mercury regarding the stages of human life.</meta>

Just as the body—composed of the thicker parts of the humors <note>In pre-modern medicine, the "humors" were the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile) believed to determine health and temperament.</note>—is directed into a quantitative form, so the spirit, formed from the most subtle portions of those same fluids, possesses a form that is naturally most pervasive and bright, and therefore celestial. It must be preserved in this very form, so that it remains subtle and yet firm, as we have previously stated. Thus, it should be entirely bright, yet in a certain way solid. Furthermore, it should be continually nourished by fragrant, firm, and bright things if we wish to preserve the life that flourishes in the spirit and claim for ourselves celestial gifts. Let us consider that these things have been contemplated thus far by the command of Venus, and that we have heard them from Venus herself.

### Chapter 15

Meanwhile, while Venus herself was conversing among the elders—quite beautifully so far, and perhaps she would have spoken even more at length thereafter—Mercury, the author of words and speech, here interrupts the discourse. 

"What business have you old men with this Venus, who is always a young girl? And again, what has Venus to do with such speeches? Are not these discourses mine as much as yours? My reasoning is the same as yours. Listen to me now, therefore, with the same attention you gave her—and indeed, with much greater attention than you gave to Venus. 

There are five senses: sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch. Learn again, as I might say, five principles <note>original: "rationes"; here referring to the intellectual processing of sensory data.</note>. For while the mind is daily imbued through the five senses and conceives reasonings within itself, meanwhile the notions and habits for judging things shine forth like five principles. Furthermore, just as there are five senses on one hand and, in a certain way, five principles on the other, so the course of life is arranged in five stages regarding sense and reason. From this, five ages of man are counted: 

1. **The first age** is drawn by sense alone. 
2. **The second age** is attracted quite a bit more by sense, but is led by reason. 
3. **The third age** is driven after this by an equal balance of reason and persuasion. 
4. **The fourth age** is led by reason rather than sense. 
5. **The fifth age**, finally, ought to be ruled by reason alone. 

Therefore, let the first and second ages, as they are subject to Venus <note>In astrology and myth, Venus rules youth, desire, and the early stages of life.</note>, listen to Venus speaking if it pleases them. But the remaining ages should listen to Mercury. I, therefore..."

I address all the rest of you: not only for my own sake, but also for this Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and chastity, often associated with moon and maidenhood.</note> here, whom you see on my left. Indeed, since she is speechless <note>original: "elinguis" — literally "tongue-less."</note> while I am "double-tongued" <note>original: "bilinguis" — here used to mean he speaks for both himself and the silent statue.</note>, I speak by right for her, as I possess her tongue. 

Truly, Venus has instilled in you a single and harmful pleasure; one which might indeed harm you, but would benefit those yet to come. She slowly drains you like an open pipe, filling another with your fluids and procreating, leaving you at last on the ground like the exhausted, discarded husk of a cicada <note>original: "spoliu cicadarum" — the dry, empty shell left behind when a cicada molts.</note>. Looking meanwhile at the cicada's renewal... <note>original: "renationem perspiciens" — observing the rebirth or molting process.</note> Do you not see? That which Venus generates from your matter is something fresh and alive, and endowed with sense. I snatch away from you your youth, your life, and your senses; from the whole body, I say, through the pleasure of the whole, so that she may make a whole new being from it. 

I, meanwhile, by showing the quality of that matter which remains from the fourth digestion <term>Fourth Digestion: In ancient and medieval medicine, the final stage of "cooking" or refining nutrients within the body, where blood was thought to be transformed into the actual substance of the organs or into reproductive seeds.</term>, warn you...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and astrological discourse on the influence of the planets on human life, specifically contrasting the dangers and benefits of Saturn and Venus.</meta>

Saturn also often presents himself as an enemy by his countenance; yet he harms more slowly, and he denies no one the time for remedies. Venus alone comes openly as a friend, but secretly she arrives as an enemy. Therefore, accuse her instead—if indeed it is permitted to accuse anyone among the celestial beings. Against her manifold snares, you must both equip your eyes like those of **Argus** <note>original: "argiuos"; referring to Argus Panoptes, the hundred-eyed giant of Greek myth who was always watchful</note> and arm yourselves with the shield of **Pallas** <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom, often depicted with the Aegis shield</note>. As for her charming promises, block your ears against them as if they were the deadly songs of the **Sirens**.

Finally, accept from me this flower of providence, by which you may avoid the sorceries of this **Circe** <note>The enchantress from Homer’s Odyssey who turned men into beasts</note>. She promises you—though she hardly bestows them—two pleasures, and deadly ones at that. I, however, through the favor of the Father and the Brother, promise you five; five I provide: pure, perpetual, and healthful. These range from the lowest in smell, to the higher in hearing, the loftier in sight, the more eminent in imagination, and the most excellent and divine in reason.

The greater the delight perceived in touching and tasting, the more frequently life suffers serious harm. Conversely, the more pleasure you daily derive from smell, hearing, and sight—and likewise from imagination and <term>separation</term> <note>original: "seperatione"; likely referring to the Neoplatonic concept of the soul "separating" from bodily distractions to contemplate higher truths</note>—the longer you extend the threads of your life. 

But just as I warned you to beware the deceitful **Venus** in the charms of touch and taste, so too must you beware **Saturn** in that more secret nymph: the delight of the mind's constant contemplation. For in that state, he frequently "devours his own children" <note>A reference to the myth of Saturn/Cronus eating his offspring; here used as an allegory for how deep intellectual melancholy can consume the person who practices it</note>. For those whom he snatches away with the loftier charms of his contemplations, and whom he recognizes there as his own, he—if they stand there too long—cuts them off from the earth with a certain **scythe** <note>The traditional tool of Saturn/Time</note>, often stealing away the earthly life of the unwary. 

In this, his scythe is actually more indulgent than Venus. For while Venus gives to another the life she takes from you, restoring nothing to you for your loss, Saturn restores a heavenly and eternal life in exchange for the earthly life from which he separates you. In this very way, Venus and Saturn seem similar to each other; for he who rejoices in **Aquarius** also reigns in **Libra** <note>In astrology, Saturn is the traditional ruler of Aquarius and is "exalted" (at his strongest) in Libra, which is the home of Venus</note>. Both he and she trouble humanity with the desire for procreation; he harms and troubles so that he may benefit posterity. But while she makes the body fertile and stimulates the "second" <note>The lower or reproductive organs</note>, he [Saturn] urges the mind, pregnant with his own seed, toward birth. 

You, therefore, mindful of the proverb "nothing in excess" <note>original: "ne quid nimis"; a famous classical maxim of moderation</note>, by constant prudence...

...restrain the lust of both <note>Original Latin: "parrurientis utriufq; libidinem". The text likely refers to the "reins" or "bridle" of the passions.</note>. Although it harms those it weighs down with boredom, lethargy, sorrow, cares, and superstition much more quickly and severely than those it raises above the powers of the body and mortal customs to high things. In all things, however, I advise you to observe what fair Jupiter taught **Pythagoras** and **Plato**: that human life is maintained in a certain equal proportion of the soul itself with the body. Both must be nourished and similarly increased by food and exercise. If someone, through their own education, makes one significantly stronger than the other, they cause a significant loss to life. Therefore, whoever chooses from among those things praised by the medical art those which benefit the body and the mind simultaneously, they claim the greatest help for themselves.

In that number, one should consider wine, mint, <term>myrobalan</term> <note>A dried plum-like fruit used in traditional medicine to balance the "humors."</note>, musk, <term>amber</term> <note>Likely ambergris, a substance used in perfumes and medicine.</note>, fresh ginger, frankincense, aloe, <term>hyacinth</term> <note>Referring to the gemstone "jacinth," which was ground or worn to protect health.</note>, and similar stones or herbs, and those things which are likewise composed by physicians for the utility of both.

But having cut short these long-winded digressions: I, too, approach this as a physician. If flavors taken from things no longer living, and likewise odors from spices that are dry and devoid of life, are thought to contribute much to life, why do you doubt that odors from plants with their roots still clinging and living will accumulate the strengths of life in a wonderful way? 

Finally, if you believe that vapors exhaling from merely vegetable life will greatly benefit you, consider how much more songs will benefit: being "airy" they match the airy spirit; being "harmonic" they match the harmonic spirit; they are still warm, living, and endowed with sensation for the sensitive soul, and conceived by reason for the rational soul. 

Therefore, I hand over to you this lyre I have fashioned, and its **Phebean** <note>original: "phebeum," referring to Phoebus Apollo, the god of music, light, and healing.</note> song—a solace for long labors and a pledge of life. For just as things of a very tempered quality and aromatic nature balance the humors and the <term>natural spirit</term> <note>In ancient medicine, the "natural spirit" governed growth and nutrition.</note> within themselves, so do such odors balance the <term>vital spirit</term>, and similar harmonies balance the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>The "animal spirit" (from "anima" or soul) was believed to carry thought and sensation through the nerves.</note>. While you temper the strings on the lyre and the sounds and tones in your voice, believe that your spirit within is likewise being tempered. 

And lest I myself be more stingy than Venus—who freezes without **Bacchus** <note>A reference to the proverb "Sine Cerere et Libero friget Venus," meaning love grows cold without food and wine.</note>—accept through me this nectar from Father Libero <note>Another name for Bacchus, the god of wine.</note> himself. For those of you who are chilled...

### Confirmation of the things above: and that we ought to avoid constant thinking and sexual intercourse. Chapter XVI.

Astrologers say that **Venus** and **Saturn** are enemies to one another. But since in heaven—where all things are moved by love and where there is no defect—hatred cannot exist, let us interpret "enemies" as being different in their effects. Let us set aside the rest for now. Look, **Saturn** has placed us in the center <note>Focusing on the internal self and deep thought.</note>, while **Venus** has placed pleasure in the circumference <note>Focusing on external sensations and the world.</note>. 

Pleasure is indeed a kind of bait for the **spirits** <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirit" was a subtle vapor in the blood that linked the soul to the body; it was easily exhausted by study or sex.</note>. Therefore, from opposite sides, Venus and Saturn hunt for the flight of our spirit. She, through her pleasure, lures it toward external things. He, meanwhile, through his influence, recalls it to internal things. And so the spirit is pulled apart and dissipated if they move it at nearly the same time. 

For this reason, nothing is more pestilential to the contemplator or the seeker of knowledge than the <term>venereal act<note>original: "Venereus actus" - sexual intercourse.</note></term>; nothing is more foreign to one following this most useful path than [excessive] care and contemplation. We count the **natural philosopher** and the **religious man** in the same rank as the contemplator. And in a similar rank, anyone deeply thoughtful in their business or subject to heavy cares. From this it follows again, that if someone is too occupied by **Saturnian** things—either by contemplation or pressed by care...

<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Myrobalans, Saturn, Venus, Spirit, Renaissance medicine, Contemplation, Alchemy, Dietetics</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of balancing the human "spirit" (spiritus) through astrological and medical tempering, specifically addressing the extremes of Saturnine melancholy and Venusian dissipation.</meta>

Meanwhile, if we should wish to relieve and console someone in a different way—through the acts of Venus, <note>original: "Venereos actus" — referring here to sexual activity or physical pleasures.</note> games, and jokes—you would be attempting it as if using remedies that are far too distant from the problem. We would be striving in vain and even with some loss. Conversely, if we wish to moderate someone who is either lost through the works of Venus or dissolved by games and jokes, we might not easily be able to correct them through Saturnine gravity. <note>The author is arguing that you cannot cure extreme depression (Saturn) with extreme partying (Venus), nor vice versa, because the "distance" between these states is too great for the body to bridge easily.</note>

The best discipline, truly, is this: to use certain studies and similar remedies of Phoebus <note>Phoebus: Another name for Apollo, the god of the Sun, music, and healing.</note> and Jupiter. These two are the "middle" powers situated between Saturn and Venus; they can call back to a middle ground those men who are leaning too far toward one extreme or the other. 

But to speak finally as physicians: just as a flame is extinguished in two common, violent ways—either blown apart by the winds or smothered as if compressed by ashes—so we dissipate the <term>spirit<note>Spirit (spiritus): In Renaissance medicine, this is a thin, warm vapor that flows through the nerves and blood, acting as the bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note> either quickly through the effects of Venus, or we gradually suffocate it through Saturnine influence. Often, we squeeze it out by compression and simultaneously dissolve it. 

The spirit certainly flies frequently toward the outermost parts of the body <note>This refers to the spirit moving to the skin and senses during excitement or physical pleasure.</note>, which leaves the innermost parts either empty or lacking in vital life. But when the spirit is often forced into the innermost depths <note>This refers to the spirit retreating inward during deep, heavy contemplation or depression.</note>, it renders the rest of the surrounding limbs less fit for life.

<summary>The author explains that the "spirit" must be balanced using the moderate influences of Jupiter and the Sun (Phoebus) to avoid the extremes of Saturnine withdrawal and Venusian exhaustion. He compares the vital spirit to a flame that can be either blown out by over-activity or smothered by deep melancholy.</summary>

### On the medicines of the elderly. And again on habitation and diet. Chapter 17.

It is a rule of the Chaldeans <note>Ancient Mesopotamian scholars often cited in Renaissance texts as sources of occult and medical wisdom</note>, perhaps to be tested for recovering youth: to gradually purge the "foreign humors" <note>In the "humoral" theory of medicine, health depended on the balance of four fluids; "foreign" humors were those that had become corrupted or entered from outside</note> absorbed by the body. This is done first internally with appropriate medicines, then externally through rubbings, baths, and the induction of sweat. Meanwhile, the body should be gradually replenished with healthy and lasting nourishment. 

There are some who allow all the old and putrid humors to be drawn out first using certain lozenges made from viper, or with prepared hellebore <note>A potent medicinal plant (genus *Helleborus*) used in antiquity as a drastic purgative</note>. Once these are purged and a very healthy humor is restored through wholesome food, youth is returned. Those who are more cautious feed the hellebore to chickens, and then feed the person the meat of those chickens <note>This "indirect" method was believed to transfer the medicinal properties while tempering the plant's toxicity through the bird's metabolism</note>. 

However, I consider such a treatment to be dangerous in youth and more suited to be tried in old age—lest we perhaps experience that "youth" promised by Medea to the elderly Pelias <note>In Greek mythology, the sorceress Medea tricked the daughters of King Pelias into killing and boiling their father by promising a magical ritual of rejuvenation that she never intended to complete</note>. For Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician of the 5th century BCE</note> asserts that even young people age quickly when they use excessively "exquisite" <term>purging medicines</term> <note>Strong laxatives or emetics intended to clear the body of fluids</note>. 

But when diet is not enough, use an enema, or manna <note>A sweet, gummy sap from the flowering ash tree, used as a mild laxative</note>, or especially aloe, which safely warms the body. If your bowels are more constricted, use manna with capon broth and myrobalan <note>An astringent fruit used in medieval medicine to balance the humors</note>. Otherwise, you will keep yourself young even in old age with this purgation: 

Take one ounce of wood-aloe <note>original: *lorçaloes*; likely a preparation of aloe or agarwood</note>, two drachms of <term>emblic myrobalans</term> <note>The Indian gooseberry, *Phyllanthus emblica*</note>, the same amount of <term>chebulic myrobalans</term> <note>The fruit of *Terminalia chebula*</note>, two of purple roses, and the same of mastic <note>A resin from the Pistacia lentiscus tree</note>. Make these into pills with Malmsey wine <note>A sweet, fortified wine</note>. 

This should be done when the Moon is favorably positioned, enjoying a propitious aspect of Jupiter, especially if either the Moon itself or Jupiter occupies fixed houses <note>In astrology, "fixed signs" like Taurus or Leo were believed to provide stability and endurance to the medicine's effects</note>. For these things contribute greatly to the longevity of life. You can also usefully mix rhubarb here with aloe—specifically, half a part of aloe and half of rhubarb. Whenever needed, take one pill in the morning, up to three or five, and drink a little wine afterward. Where you seek to clear phlegm more effectively, you can conveniently add ground agaric lozenges <note>A medicinal fungus, *Laricifomes officinalis*, known as the "Larch Agaric," used as a purgative</note> to these pills...

...[to take] another part together with two-thirds of aloes and half a part of rhubarb <note>original: "reu barbaro"</note>. We have found it useful for those taking and giving this four hours before a meal to strengthen the **vital power**, and to brighten and firm the spirit. 

It will be especially beneficial if you drink a little golden wine afterward. It will also be helpful to soak warmed bread in pure golden wine and rose water, seasoning it with a bit of cinnamon and plenty of sugar. Frequently also mix these two with almond milk and a little bread. For mixtures of this kind reflect a **Jovial nature** <note>In Renaissance medicine, "Jovial" refers to the planet Jupiter, which was believed to govern health, growth, and a cheerful temperament</note>. 

Besides the things which we explained or indicated above, all city-dwellers must diligently beware of these: heat and frost; any sudden cold after heat, or the night air; misty springs or vapors from marshes and rivers, or those bursting out from narrow places; going to places where the air either moves too violently or does not move at all; and any dwelling that is too damp, foul-smelling, or stagnant. 

**Followers of Mercury** <note>This refers to scholars, writers, and intellectuals, whose patron was the planet Mercury</note> should be more careful, and the elderly most careful of all. Furthermore, after they have lightly rubbed their whole body in the morning, they should soothe it against the discomforts of the air and labor by warming it with oil, or with some slightly bitter wine in which they have first infused myrrh, rose, and balsam. 

Let them frequently keep **sage** in their mouths, which is most friendly to the nerves and teeth. And when, due to the failure of their teeth, they are compelled to return to liquid nourishment like infants, let them avoid the very softest foods. Indeed, they should temper milk with a moderate amount of wine...
