### On the nourishment of the spirit and the preservation of life through odors. Chapter XVIII.

We have observed that in certain hot regions, which are fragrant everywhere with many scents, many people with slender bodies and weak stomachs are nourished as if by odors alone. This is perhaps because the nature of the place itself—as well as the herbs, crops, and fruits—converts almost all their juices into odors. Furthermore, the nature of the place there resolves the humors of human bodies into **spirit** <term>spiritus: a thin, vaporous substance in the body that serves as the bridge between the physical organs and the immaterial soul.</term>. Therefore, since both odor and spirit are a kind of **vapor**, and "like is nourished by like," both the spirit and the "spiritual" man <note>those with a refined or delicate constitution, often associated with scholars</note> certainly receive a great deal of nourishment from odors.

Indeed, this nourishment through odors (or "fomentation," as it may be called) is especially necessary for the elderly and those of a slender build. By this means, we can compensate for a lack of more solid and substantial food. Some people tend to doubt whether the spirit is truly nourished by odors. However, I am of the opinion that the spirit is perhaps nourished *only* in this way: unless thick foods are finally thinned into vapors through digestion, the spirit itself—which we have said is a vapor—receives no nourishment from them. Thus, the scent of wine suddenly refreshes a "full spirit," <note>a spirit already present or active</note> whereas other things can barely restore it after a long time. We call that vapor into which cooked foods are finally transformed "odor" for two reasons: first, because an odor is always a kind of vapor; and second, because this vapor drawn inward from food, unless it pleases the spirit with a certain pleasant scent, provides hardly any nourishment to the spirit...

<page-num>e ii</page-num>

<summary>This page discusses how the "vital spirit"—the vaporous link between body and soul—is nourished by scents and vapors, particularly in the elderly. It argues that even solid food must be converted into a vaporous "odor" through digestion before it can sustain our internal spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the physiological needs of the body and the spirit, likely following a description of physical substances or humors.</meta>

...of bodies. For this reason, we highly approve of our Avicenna <note>Avicenna (c. 980–1037) was a Persian physician and philosopher whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> when he says: the body is nourished by sweetness, but the spirit is nourished by a certain (to use his words) "aromaticity." Since the density of the body cannot grow except by a nature that is itself dense—such as is found in sweetness—the thinness of the spirit, however, can be refreshed by nothing other than a kind of smoke and vapor in which that very aromatic quality flourishes. 

We define the aromatic quality as fragrant, sharp, and in some way <term>styptic</term> <note>original: *stripticam*; a substance that tends to shrink or constrict body tissues, often associated with a "dry" or "puckering" sensation.</note>. Therefore, since the liver provides nourishment to the body through the blood, it is greatly strengthened by sweetness. The heart, however, because it both creates the spirit and generates nourishment for that spirit, rightfully desires aromatic things. It is helpful, nevertheless, for aromatics to be seasoned with sweetness for the sake of the heart, and for sweet things to be mixed with aromatics for the sake of the liver, while avoiding excessive sweetness in the process. 

What more is there to say? Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), an influential Greek physician in the Roman Empire.</note> himself, following Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician of the 5th century BC, often called the "Father of Medicine."</note>, believes the spirit is nourished not only by scent, but by air—air, I say, that is not simple, but rather suitably blended. If we believe these men, we will judge that no choice of food or anything else is more necessary for life than the choice of air suited to us. For the air, always very easily influenced by the qualities of both the lower and the heavenly realms, and surrounding us with an immense (so to speak) adaptability, penetrating us from all sides with perpetual motion, wonderfully draws us back into its own quality. 

This is especially true for the spirit, particularly the <term>vital spirit</term> <note>original: *vitalem*; in Renaissance medicine, this was the life-force produced in the heart and carried by the arteries.</note> flourishing in the heart. Into its innermost chambers, the air flows both continuously and suddenly. Thus, it immediately affects the spirit according to how the air itself has been affected; and through the vital spirit—which is both the material and the origin of the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>original: *animalis*; the "refined" spirit produced in the brain that governed thought and sensation.</note>—it likewise affects the animal spirit. 

The quality of this spirit is of the greatest importance, especially for <term>gifted people</term> <note>original: *ingeniosis*; referring to scholars, artists, and intellectuals who were thought to consume their "spirits" through intense mental labor.</note> who work much with this kind of spirit. Therefore, the choice of pure and luminous air, scents, and music belongs to no one more than to them. For these three are judged to be the primary <term>foments</term> <note>original: *fomenta*; traditionally "poultices," but here meaning things that warm, cherish, or stimulate the spirit.</note> of the animal spirit. Indeed, the choice of air is most powerful for life. For many born in the eighth month in Egypt survive; and some survive in the temperate regions of Greece by the benefit of the healthiest air. Because...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the physiological and metaphysical importance of air and scent, likely from Marsilio Ficino's 'Three Books on Life,' focusing on how the "spirit" (the bridge between body and soul) is nourished.</meta>

Aristotle relates it, and Avicenna confirms it. But truly, just as the body is composed of various parts and must be nourished with various foods—for it is not nourished by the same table—so the <term>spirit (spiritus)</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirit" was a subtle, vaporous substance that linked the physical body to the immaterial soul.</note>, being similarly composed, must be delighted and fostered by a variety of carefully chosen air. It must also be refreshed daily by a similar variety of scents. For air and scent seem to be, as it were, a kind of spirit themselves.

Now indeed, Alexander and Nicolaus the Peripatetics <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias and Nicolaus of Damascus, influential commentators on Aristotle.</note>, together with Galen, conclude that the <term>vital spirit</term> and the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>The vital spirit (in the heart) governed life and heat; the animal spirit (in the brain) governed thought and movement.</note> are nourished by fragrant air because both are mixed and of a similar nature. Both, once inhaled, penetrate into the <term>chest cavity (præcordia)</term>, where they are "cooked" and tempered for life, and then diffused through the arteries. There, having been cooked again, this air nourishes both spirits (as they say), especially the animal spirit. 

They also say that the air benefits the spirit not only by cooling internal heat, but also by nourishing it. For even animals that are naturally very cold still breathe. They add that thicker air suits the <term>natural spirit</term> <note>The spirit responsible for basic nutrition and growth, centered in the liver.</note> as it is more bodily in nature, but subtle, pure, and clear air suits the vital spirit, and most especially the animal spirit. Nor should it seem strange that a spirit so thin and subtle is nourished by thin and subtle things. Since indeed many small fish are nourished by the clearest water, and <term>basil (ocimum)</term> lives, grows, flowers, and smells sweet in similar water. I pass over those elements which some say nourish the Chameleon and the Salamander <note>Ancient folklore claimed chameleons lived on air and salamanders on fire.</note>.

Let us return to our own matters. It certainly matters a great deal what kind of air we breathe and what scents we inhale. For such as these are, so the spirit within us becomes. Indeed, the soul gives us life <note>original: "vegetat"</note> only insofar as the spirit maintains a harmony that sings in tune with the soul. Our spirit lives first and most intensely; it almost lives alone. Does it not often happen, through some sudden accident or emotion, that life, sense, and movement suddenly desert the limbs? This is because the spirit has retreated to the inner chambers of the heart. And often it returns to the limbs immediately when the spirit is drawn back there through rubbing and scents. It is as if life resides in the spirit itself—a volatile thing—rather than in the bodily fluids or the limbs. Otherwise, because of the thick density of these physical parts, life would return far too slowly...

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the preservation of the "vital spirit"—a subtle substance in Renaissance medicine thought to link the soul and the body—focusing here on the use of aromatic substances and a specific medicinal recipe.</meta>

Touching on many things: entice the <note>original: "spiritum"; the vital spirit was believed to be a very fine, vaporous substance that circulated through the nerves and sustained life</note> not so much by odors applied from the outside as by those taken internally; indeed, rather feed and retain it. Above all, choose the smell of wine. For the exhaled odor of wine nourishes the spirit greatly, both because its nature nourishes the body most quickly and because it affects the senses with pleasure. Such wine is excellent, being warm, moist, fragrant, and clear. I would say sugar is similar, if only it gave off a scent. Cinnamon is also similar, as are leopard’s bane, anise, and sweet fennel—if they added a greater sweetness to their sharpness. 

But where nature has not created the perfect balance, you must fashion it for yourself. Whenever you fear the "distraction" <note>This refers to the thinning or scattering of the vital spirits, which was thought to lead to weakness or death</note> of the spirit, apply things that are warmer, sharper, and very subtle, which are able to restrain the flight of the spirit and make it stay. Use substances such as saffron, cloves, cinnamon, toasted bread, rose water, rose vinegar, rose, myrtle, violet, sandalwood, coriander, quince, and citron. I shrink from camphor, however, when one must act against the graying of hair <note>Renaissance doctors often believed camphor was "cold" and could cause premature aging or grayness</note>. I always love fresh mint; it is healthy for the mind and safest for the spirit. Finally, remember that all things which are contrary to poison are very healthful in their power—not only to the taste, but also to the sense of smell—especially <term>theriac</term> <note>A famous ancient "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh</note>. 

These things we have described in the book against the plague, and we shall describe them in the following book as well. Among these, lest anything be hidden from you, we also number wine. For just as hemlock is a poison to man, so wine is a "poison" to hemlock—not if taken together, but if the wine is drunk shortly after the hemlock. And so that you are not enticed here by odors alone, I command that you prepare an <term>electuary</term> <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</note> to be tasted every day in the morning, which is pleasant to the smell and taste, and very healthful in its power. 

Take three ounces of <term>chebulic myrobalans</term> <note>A dried fruit from India, highly prized in medieval and Renaissance medicine for purging and strengthening the body</note>; one ounce of emblic myrobalans; one ounce of belleric myrobalans; a half-ounce of leopard’s bane; two ounces of cinnamon; one drachm of saffron; a third of a drachm of ambergris; and the same amount of musk. Crush them diligently. Add as much rose-sugar as satisfies the taste. Add red sandalwood, as much as is needed for color; likewise, use honey of emblic or chebulic myrobalans in an amount that makes the electuary a soft shape. There should also be as many gold leaves as there are ounces aforementioned. Where this complex composition is too difficult to make, we have found this simple one to be best: namely, made from chebulic myrobalans, sweet fennel, sugar, and rose water.

...once it is melted. You should take it on an empty stomach, or after dinner.

Remember, however, that **preserved myrobalans** <term>Myrobalans are dried, plum-like fruits (Terminalia chebula) highly valued in both Ayurvedic and medieval European medicine for balancing the "humors."</term> are better. If they are dry, soak them for at least a day in sweet almond oil or cow’s butter before you prepare them. **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina, the famous Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> also approves for you a confection made from **emblic** and **Indian myrobalans**, with **cashew honey** <note>original: "melle anacardorum"</note> and cooked butter. Likewise, he suggests **chebulic myrobalans** with ginger and iron scales—or rather, gold scales. **Peter of Abano** <note>An influential 13th-century physician and astrologer.</note> also approves a mixture made of **saffron**, **mace**, and **castoreum** <note>original: "castoreo," a secretion from beavers used in medieval medicine for nervous system disorders.</note> taken in equal parts, blended together and mixed with wine. He claims that by this means, life has been known to be extended even for those who are nearly dying. Finally, **Haly** <note>Likely Haly Abenragel, an Arab astrologer and physician.</note>, the excellent Astrologer and Physician, asserts that by the use of *triphera* <note>original: "tnifera," a term for a medicinal paste or electuary, often "triphera" or "triphala."</note> and similar things, life is made longer. In every *triphera*, the myrobalan is the foundation. But they temper this with certain subtle and soft ingredients, especially when the myrobalan is drier, so that it may penetrate and not block the passages, nor dry the body out too much, nor constrict it. Furthermore, we use it most conveniently with wine, but in moderation, so that it does not become too diluted. As for Peter's mixture which I just mentioned, if it is useful at all, I believe it would be more useful as a **scent** rather than as a drink.

->### Medicine of the Magi for the elderly.<- 
->xviii.<-

The **Magi**, observers of the stars, came to **Christ**, the leader of life, guided by a star, offering a precious treasure of life: **Gold**, **Frankincense**, and **Myrrh**. They dedicated these gifts to the Lord of the stars on behalf of the three ruling planets. **Gold**, indeed, represents the temperament of **Jupiter**, the most balanced of all planets. **Frankincense** represents the **Sun** especially, burning with Phoeban <note>Phoebus is a classical name for the Sun god.</note> heat and scent alike. Finally, **Myrrh**, which firms and preserves the body, represents **Saturn**, the planet it most resembles. Therefore, let all elderly people come here to the wise Magi; they bring gifts to you also that will prolong your life, just as they are recorded to have once venerated the Author of life.

<summary>This page details recipes for life-extending tonics using myrobalans and spices, concluding with an astrological-theological defense of Gold, Frankincense, and Myrrh as medicines corresponding to Jupiter, the Sun, and Saturn.</summary>
<keywords>myrobalans, Avicenna, Peter of Abano, Haly the Astrologer, triphera, Magi, medicinal gold, frankincense, myrrh, planetary medicine, Jupiter, Saturn</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
Come, old men, I say—you who find old age a heavy burden to bear. Come also you, whom the dread of approaching old age troubles. Receive, I pray, these vital gifts with eagerness. Take two ounces of frankincense, one of myrrh, and of gold <note>original: "triſia," likely "trita" meaning ground or powdered</note> two and a half drachms. Grind these three together, mix them, and form them into pills with a certain pure, golden wine. Prepare this at a favorable time, specifically when Diana <note>The Moon</note> enjoys the favorable gaze of Phoebus <note>The Sun</note> or Jupiter. From then on, take a small portion of this great treasure every dawn. Wash it down with a little pure wine, unless perhaps the summer heat has come. In that case, it is better to drink rose water. If any among you fears the heat more at any time, he should add a miraculous <term>chebulic myrobalan</term> <note>original: "balanum chebulam," a fruit used in medieval medicine for its cooling and digestive properties</note> or an <term>emblic myrobalan</term> <note>original: "emblicã," known today as Indian gooseberry</note> in a weight equal to the frankincense, myrrh, and gold combined. This will undoubtedly protect the natural moisture of the body from decay. This will drive away the dissolution of that moisture for a longer time. This will nourish, confirm, and strengthen the three spirits within you: the natural, the vital, and the animal <note>In medieval medicine, the natural spirit governed nutrition (liver), the vital spirit governed heat and life (heart), and the animal spirit governed sensation and movement (brain)</note>. This, in turn, will revive the senses, sharpen the intellect, and preserve the memory.

## On avoiding the dangers that loom from every seven-year cycle of life. Chapter 18.

Since astronomers have assigned the individual hours of the day to the planets in order, and similarly the seven days of the week, and have even distributed the duties of the planets through the months while a child is still in the womb; why should we not also arrange the years of life in the same way? For just as Saturn rules the first month and the Moon the last while the infant is hidden in the mother, so, once born, let the Moon lead his first year in a reversed order. Second, if you like, Mercury; third, Venus; fourth, the Sun; fifth, Mars; sixth, Jupiter; and seventh, indeed, Saturn. And thereafter, a similar order is repeated throughout life. Therefore, in every seventh year of life, there occurs a very great change in the body <note>This refers to the "climacteric years," a belief that the body undergoes a fundamental transformation every seven years</note>.

<keywords>alchemy, anti-aging, frankincense, myrrh, gold, myrobalans, medical astrology, planetary hours, seven-year cycles, climacteric years</keywords>

<meta>The previous page discussed the influence of the planets on human health; this page continues by explaining the "climacteric" years and how astrology can be used alongside medicine to prolong life.</meta>

And therefore these times are most dangerous, since **Saturn** is generally a stranger to us: <note>In traditional astrology, Saturn is associated with coldness, old age, and hardship; being a "stranger" implies its influence is often at odds with human vitality.</note> and from him, then the highest of the planets, the governance returns immediately to the **Moon**, the lowest of the planets. Greek astronomers call these years **climacteric** <note>original: "climatericos"; these are "critical" years in a person's life, usually multiples of seven or nine.</note>. We call them ladder-like, or step-like, or decisive years. 

Perhaps truly in illnesses the planets rule the very movement of the humors <note>The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—thought to determine health.</note> or of nature through the days in the same order. For this reason, every seventh day is called **judicial** <note>In medical astrology, a "judicial day" is a critical point when the course of a disease is decided.</note>. The fourth day is also significant, because it holds the middle position in the group of seven. 

You, therefore, if you desire to prolong your life to old age, uninterrupted by steps of this kind: whenever you approach any seventh year, consult an astrologer diligently. Learn from where danger may threaten you. Then, either go to a physician, or approach with prudence and temperance. For even **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century mathematician and astronomer who wrote the "Tetrabiblos."</note> agrees that the threats of the stars can be prevented by these remedies. He even adds that the promises of the stars can be increased, just as a tiller of fields increases the strength of the earth. 

**Peter of Abano** <note>Petrus Aponensis (c. 1250–1316), a famous Italian physician and astrologer who sought to reconcile Greek and Arabic science.</note> proves with many arguments and the testimony of **Aristotle**, **Galen**, and **Aly** <note>Haly Abenragel (Albohazen), an 11th-century Arab astrologer.</note> that the natural end of life is not determined to a hair's breadth from the beginning, but can be moved further or closer. He asserts this both from the fixed stars <note>original: "ex afſis" (possibly "astris")</note> and also from the nature of the body <note>original: "marena," likely a corruption of "materia" or "manera".</note>. He concludes by these authors and reasons that even a natural death can be postponed, both by the tools of astrology <note>original: "aftrologiç machinis"</note> and the aforementioned remedies of physicians. 

Therefore, we do not labor in these instructions in vain. Nor should you be reluctant to inquire of physicians what your natural diet should be, and from astrologers, which star favors your life. And when this star is well-positioned and the Moon is near it, arrange those things which you have learned to be beneficial. Nor should you be ashamed to often listen to those who seem to have achieved a prosperous old age not so much by luck as by virtue. 

Furthermore, **Ptolemy** and other professors of astronomy promise a prosperous and long life through certain **images** <term>talismans: astrological amulets or figures carved into stones or metals during specific planetary alignments.</term> made from specific stones and metals under a specific star. Truly, concerning these images in part, and for the most part concerning the remaining celestial favor, we are composing a commentary on **Ptolemy**. Which book we [will add] to this work...

<summary>This page explains the "climacteric" years (multiples of seven) as dangerous transition points and argues that death is not a fixed date, but can be postponed through a combination of astrological timing, medical diet, and talismans.</summary>
<keywords>climacteric years, medical astrology, Peter of Abano, Ptolemy, talismans, longevity, humors, Haly Abenragel, Saturn, Moon</keywords>

<meta>The text transitions from the author's previous work on the health of scholars (Book I of De Vita Libri Tres) into a more philosophical and astrological discussion on longevity, invoking the mythological figures of Apollo (Phoebus) and Dionysus (Bacchus).</meta>

We think the following should be added next. We want this to be written after the book we composed on **Caring for the Health of Scholars** <note>This refers to the first part of Ficino's famous work, *Three Books on Life*, which offers medical advice for intellectuals prone to melancholy.</note>. Now, for the sake of a long youth, we shall seek that celestial favor I just mentioned—insofar as a poet is allowed to speak and a physician is allowed to act—from Phoebus <note>The god Apollo, representing the sun, reason, and music.</note> and Bacchus <note>Dionysus, the god of wine and vital energy.</note>.

->The Sun’s youth is eternal for Phoebus and Bacchus,<-
->For long, unshorn hair is fitting for both gods.<- <note>original: "Solis æterna eft Phebo Bacchoq; iuuentus. / Ná decet intonfus crinis utriq; dei." In classical mythology, both Apollo and Dionysus are depicted with long, flowing hair to symbolize perpetual youth.</note>

Phoebus and Bacchus are always inseparable brothers; both are almost the same. Phoebus is the very soul of that celestial sphere. The sphere, however, is Bacchus. Indeed, Phoebus is the entire circuit of that sphere, while Bacchus is that fiery circle within this circuit. Or rather, Phoebus is the nourishing light within this fiery globe, and Bacchus is the health-giving heat arising from that light. Therefore, they are always brothers and companions, almost always the one and the same. 

What of this? If the Sun in the spring is Phoebus, then by his song he awakens the music of the ears, restoring the seasons with his lyre <term>Cithara|An ancient stringed instrument similar to a lyre, used here to symbolize the harmonious "music of the spheres."</term>. In the autumn, however, the same Sun exists as Bacchus, the author of wine. That father, Liber <note>A Roman name for Bacchus, literally meaning "the Free One," highlighting wine's power to release the mind from worry.</note>, who loves the hills, brings us three things to preserve our youth: first, these very sunny hills; second, the sweetest wines from these hills; and third, a perpetual freedom from care through wine. 

Phoebus, the brother of Bacchus, bestows three things with equal kindness: first, the light of day; second, sweet-smelling herbs under the nourishment of that light; and third, the lyre and eternal song in the shade of this light. By these weights, therefore, and by these breaths, Clotho <note>One of the three Fates (Parcae) who spins the thread of human life. Ficino suggests that these solar gifts influence how long she spins our thread.</note> will no longer be stingy toward us but will draw out the long threads of our life. 

Almost all poets sing of the three Fates. We, though not poets, also sing of three things. Indeed, a wise moderation <note>The Latin text puns on *parcitas* (restraint/moderation) and *Parcae* (the Fates).</note> in all food begins a long life for us. Constant moderation in undergoing worries also extends life. However, a life that is "moderate" <note>Here used ironically to mean "neglectful" or "stingy."</note> in enjoying the heavens cuts life short. Pythagoras <note>Ancient Greek philosopher who emphasized harmony, mathematics, and a disciplined lifestyle.</note> celebrates three forms of temperance <term>Temperantia|The virtue of moderation and balance, which Ficino views as a medical necessity for balancing the body's humors.</term> above all else; we also celebrate three at this present time: maintain temperance in your emotions; temperance in every...

<summary>Ficino blends poetry, medicine, and solar theology to explain how the "celestial brothers" Phoebus (light) and Bacchus (heat/wine) provide the vital spirit necessary for a long life. He argues that longevity requires both physical moderation and a joyful engagement with the heavens.</summary>

# Concerning the Extraction of the Universal Spirit

->In the month of September, Anno Domini 1642<-

I have labored long in the laboratory to separate the volatile from the fixed. As the old masters wrote, **the wind carried it in its belly** <note>original Latin: "Portavit eum ventus in ventre suo," a famous line from the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, referring to the elusive "Life Force" or gas.</note>. To begin the work, take the <term>Vitriol of Venus<note>A historical name for copper sulfate, often used in alchemy for its striking blue color and chemical reactivity.</note></term> and grind it until it is as fine as flour upon the marble slab. 

<margin>Use only the purest rainwater collected during the spring equinox.</margin>

Once the substance is prepared, place it within the <gloss>glass vessel</gloss> and apply a gentle heat—what the chemists call a **"Lamp Heat"** or **"Balneum Mariae"** <note>original Latin: "Bath of Mary," a water bath used to provide steady, gentle warmth, similar to a double boiler.</note>. 

| Ingredient | Weight | Condition |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Vitriol | 4 Ounces | Calcined to whiteness |
| Spirit of Wine | 1 Pint | Highly rectified |
| <unclear>Niter</unclear> | 2 Drams | Purified by fire |

Be careful not to increase the fire too quickly, or the glass shall shatter and the <insert>precious</insert> spirit will escape into the air. If the mixture turns a deep blood red, you have found the **True Lion**. As the Greeks said, the **nature of the soul** <note>original Greek: "psyche," here referring to the animating principle of the chemical reaction.</note> is revealed through the struggle with the fire.

<meta>This page serves as the introductory dedication for the third book of Marsilio Ficino's influential medical and astrological treatise, "De Vita Libri Tres" (Three Books on Life). This specific book is titled "De Vita Coelitus Comparanda" (On Obtaining Life from the Heavens). There is no previous page for continuity as this is the "Prohemium" or Preface.</meta>

# Preface to the Book on Life from the Heavens

**MARSILIO Ficino of Florence** to the Most Serene King of **Pannonia**, <note>original: "Pannonie Regi"; Pannonia refers to the Kingdom of Hungary, then ruled by Matthias Corvinus, a great patron of the arts and sciences.</note> always unconquered. 

O King, most fortunate of all, the ancient philosophers diligently investigated the powers of the heavens and the natures of lower things. Since they believed that a man is wise in vain who is not wise for himself, they seem to have rightly directed all their scrutiny primarily toward obtaining a life for themselves from the heavens. They judged, I believe, that both the elements and those things composed of them would be known in vain, and that the motions and <term>influences</term> <note>original: "influxus"; the astrological belief that celestial bodies "flow" their powers into the earthly realm.</note> of the heavens would be observed to excess and without purpose, unless these things—once known and joined together—might eventually lead them to life and happiness. 

Such contemplation profited them, it seems, first regarding this present life. For **Pythagoras**, **Democritus**, and **Apollonius of Tyana**, <note>A 1st-century Neopythagorean philosopher and traveler often credited with miraculous powers.</note> and whoever else studied this goal most intensely, achieved prosperous health and a long life through the use of the things they had learned. It also contributed to their future life: both through the propagation of their glory among posterity, and in the enjoyment of eternity with God. Indeed, from the marvelous order of the entire world, they finally recognized its Ruler, and once known, they loved Him above all things. 

To you, indeed, your magnanimity, magnificence, and perpetual victory promise glory through all future ages. To your piety and justice, divine mercy promises a blessed life with God in eternity. Finally, as far as I can gather from certain signs, the happy stars have decreed for you a prosperous and long life—as much as can satisfy mortals. 

But in order that what the stars promise may be fulfilled with the strongest faith and even extended with a fuller abundance, your own diligence and the care of physicians and astrologers can undoubtedly bring it about. Indeed, all the most learned astrologers and physicians agree that this can be achieved through science and prudence. 

Therefore, since I recently composed a commentary on a book by **Plotinus** <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher of the 3rd century; Ficino spent much of his life translating and commentating on his works.</note> concerning the drawing of favor from the heavens—among the other books of Plotinus destined for the great **Lorenzo de' Medici**—I have decided to select this particular work (with the approval of Lorenzo himself) and dedicate it especially to your Majesty. I hope if...

<summary>In this preface, Marsilio Ficino dedicates his work on astrological health to King Matthias Corvinus of Hungary, arguing that true philosophy must serve to improve human life and longevity. He claims that ancient sages used their knowledge of the stars to maintain their health and that the King can do the same through the "science and prudence" detailed in this text.</summary>

<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Matthias Corvinus, Astrology, Neoplatonism, Lorenzo de' Medici, Longevity, Medical Astrology, Plotinus, Pythagoras, Apollonius of Tyana</keywords>

Indeed, while I provide for your life and prosperity, I shall simultaneously be providing for the life and splendor of our age and of the entire human race. And so that these writings of ours might be of strong benefit to your royal health and prosperity, I thought they should be sent through <note>original: "per Valorem ipſum"; referring to Filippo Valori (1456–1494), the Florentine nobleman who funded the printing of this book.</note> Valori himself. Therefore, most merciful King, I pray you embrace our friend Valori. For your nature, your virtue, and your authority are so great that without you, I do not think even <note>Ficino is making a pun on the name of his friend "Valori" and the Latin word "valor," which means value, strength, or worth.</note> Valor itself could be strong. 

July 10, 1489. Florence.

# Words of Marsilio Ficino to the Reader of the following Book.

Greetings, ingenious guest. Greetings also to whoever you are who is eager for <term>health<note>The Latin word used here is "salus," which carries a double meaning: it is a standard "greeting" but also specifically refers to "health" or "salvation," which is the primary subject of this medical treatise.</note></term> and who approaches our threshold. I pray you see first, eager guest, how hospitable I am. It was certainly the duty of the one entering to offer a greeting immediately upon being welcomed; but I, coming forward with health, have already bid you to be well in anticipation. You who are entering and are as of yet unknown, I very gladly...

<meta>This page appears to be from a treatise on "iatromathematics" (astrological medicine), likely by the Renaissance philosopher Marsilio Ficino, discussing the practical efficacy of preparing medicines according to planetary alignments.</meta>

which things I do not so much prove as relate: allow these indeed to be granted; and also, if you wish, dismiss them with your advisor. At least do not neglect those medicines bolstered by a certain heavenly aid <note>original: "caelesti quodam adminiculo"; this refers to the practice of "electing" a time when the planets are favorably positioned to enhance a medicine's power</note>—unless perhaps you have no regard for life itself. For through frequent study and experience, I have discovered that there is as much difference between medicines of this kind and others made without <term>astrological selection|the practice of timing the harvest and preparation of herbs to coincide with beneficial planetary aspects</term>, as there is between pure wine and water. 

Indeed, I have seen an infant born in the eighth month after conception <note>In ancient and Renaissance medicine, it was a common belief that a baby born in the eighth month was less likely to survive than one born in the seventh, due to the sequence of planetary influences</note>, during the blooming month of March, at night while Saturn was ascending in retrograde <note>In traditional astrology, Saturn is "malefic" (ill-omened) and associated with cold and death; "retrograde" motion was seen as a sign of weakened or contrary energy</note>. The child was half-dead; yet, through this kind of diligence, he appeared to be restored to life by us—or rather by God—instead of merely being saved. He has now completed nearly three years in a state of great health. Now, if I were to relate more stories of this same kind, I would be speaking the truth. I bring these things forward not boastfully (which is entirely foreign to the character of a philosopher) but rather as an encouragement. But enough now, partly indeed... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "concessum" or "conclusum".</note>

<summary>The author argues for the superior efficacy of medicines prepared using astrological timing, providing a case study of a "half-dead" infant born under ill-omened stars who was restored to health through these methods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a philosophical argument regarding the mediation between the intellectual (divine) realm and the physical (material) world, focusing on the role of the World Soul as the connective link.</meta>

...as if [matter] were by itself ineffective and unfit for motion, and far removed from the intellect. But if a <term>soul<note>Latin: anima. Here referring to the animating principle that connects the physical to the spiritual.</note></term> is interposed that conforms to both, an attraction would easily occur from both sides and toward both. First, the soul itself leads all things most easily because it is the "first moved" <note>original: primum mobile</note> and is moved by its own nature and spontaneity. Furthermore, since it is (as I have said) the intermediary of things, it contains all things in its own way and is related to both sides by a close connection. Therefore, it is reconciled to all things—even equally to those things which are distant from one another, provided they are not distant from the soul itself. For besides the fact that the soul conforms on one side to divine things and on the other to <term>perishable things<note>Latin: caducis. Referring to the material world which is subject to decay.</note></term>, and leans toward both with affection, the whole soul is also everywhere at once. 

To this is added the fact that the <term>World Soul<note>Latin: anima mundi. In Neo-Platonic philosophy, this is the vital spirit that permeates and animates the entire universe.</note></term> possesses at least as many <term>seminal reasons<note>Latin: rationes rerum seminales. These are the formative "seeds" or blueprints within the Soul that translate abstract divine Ideas into physical reality.</note></term> from the divine as there are <term>Ideas<note>In this context, "Ideas" refers to the perfect, eternal archetypes in the Divine Mind.</note></term> in the Divine Mind; by these same reasons, the soul constructs as many species in matter. Thus, every single species corresponds closely to an Idea through its own seminal reason. It can easily receive something from that higher realm through this reason, since it was created from there by means of it. Therefore, if a species should ever degenerate from its proper form, it can be formed again through this closest medium, and through that medium, it can be easily reformed. It is as if you were to properly apply many things to a certain species of things, or to an individual of that species—things which are themselves physical, but conform to that same divine Idea. Soon, you will draw a singular gift from that Idea into this appropriately prepared matter, specifically through the seminal reason of the soul. For the soul is not drawn to matter for its own sake.

Therefore, let no one think that certain "divinities" <note>original: numina; likely referring to celestial powers or planetary spirits</note> who are entirely segregated from matter are drawn by specific material substances of the world. Rather, it is the <term>daemons<note>Not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits or guiding intelligences.</note></term> and the gifts of the living world and the living stars that are drawn. Again, let no one wonder that the soul can be enticed, as it were, by material forms. Indeed, the soul herself created baits of this kind for herself, which are suitable for her and by which she might be enticed, and she always inhabits them willingly. Nor is there anything found in the whole living world so deformed that it lacks a soul, or in which the gift of the soul does not reside. Therefore, <term>Zoroaster<note>A legendary ancient sage often cited by Renaissance philosophers as a source of "primeval" wisdom.</note></term> called these harmonies of forms to the reasons of the World Soul "divine lures," which <term>Synesius<note>A 4th-century Neo-Platonic philosopher and bishop known for his writings on dreams and magic.</note></term> also confirmed were "magical allurements." Finally, let no one believe that all gifts are drawn from the soul at just any time or into just any specific type of matter, but rather when the opportunity arises...

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the sympathetic relationship between terrestrial objects and celestial bodies, likely from a Renaissance Neoplatonic or Hermetic text (such as Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life"). It transitions from discussing human-specific influences to the overarching structure of the heavens and the World Soul.</meta>

...gifts only for women <note>The text here refers to "female" things in an astrological sense—objects or beings that share a receptive, lunar, or Venusian nature</note> in whom such a spirit has sprouted, and for like-formed women. Thus, a human, by applying only human things, does not claim for himself the specific properties of fish or birds, but rather human and similar ones. However, by applying things that pertain to such a star and Spirit <note>original: *Daemonem*; in this context, it refers to a guiding celestial spirit or planetary intelligence, not an evil entity</note>, one undergoes the specific influence of that star and Spirit, just as wood prepared with sulfur is ready for a flame that is present everywhere. And this influence occurs not only through the rays of the star and Spirit themselves, but also through the World Soul <term>World Soul (Anima Mundi): a Neoplatonic concept of a spiritual essence that permeates the entire universe, connecting the divine mind to physical matter</term> itself, which is present everywhere. In this Soul, the principle <note>original: *ratio*</note> of every star and Spirit flourishes: partly as a "seed" principle for generating life, and partly as an "exemplary" principle for understanding.

For according to the older Platonists, this Soul, by its own principles, built figures in the heavens beyond the stars; and its parts are such that they too are certain figures, and it impressed specific properties upon all of them. In the stars, the figures, and these properties, all the spirits of lower things are contained, along with their own properties. Indeed, Nature established forty-eight universal figures <note>These are the 48 classical constellations described by Ptolemy</note>: namely, twelve in the zodiac and thirty-six outside of it. Likewise, in the zodiac, there are thirty-six figures corresponding to the number of "faces" <note>The "faces" or decans are 10-degree subdivisions of the zodiac signs, each associated with a specific planetary ruler</note>. Again, there are three hundred and sixty in the same place, according to the number of degrees. For in any given degree, there are several stars from which these images are constructed.

Similarly, it divided the images outside the zodiac into many figures according to the number of their faces and degrees. Finally, it established certain relationships and proportions from these universal images to other universal images, so that those images also exist there. Figures of this kind also have their own continuity arising from the rays of their stars, which are cast upon one another with a certain peculiar property. From these most orderly forms, the forms of lower things depend; they are, evidently, ordered from that celestial source. But even those celestial forms, although distinct among themselves, proceed from the principles of the Soul joined together; and in a way, they are changeable things derived from stable ones. But insofar as these principles do not grasp themselves, they are referred back to forms in a Mind—whether animate or more eminent—which *do* grasp themselves; these forms, though manifold, are ultimately reduced to the most simple unity.

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument regarding how universal principles manifest in the physical world, specifically focusing on the relationship between the World Soul and the human body.</meta>

...one and good, just as celestial figures relate to the pole. But let us return to the soul. Therefore, when the soul generates the specific forms and powers of lower things, it brings them forth through its own intrinsic principles <note>original: "rationes," referring to the 'seminal reasons' or internal blueprints that Neoplatonists believed guided the growth of all things</note>, under the support of the stars and celestial forms. 

Truly, the soul exhibits the singular gifts of individuals—which often seem as wonderful to many as the traits found in whole species—through the likenesses of these internal blueprints. This happens not so much through the general support of celestial forms and shapes as through the specific positioning of the stars, the character of their motions, and the "aspects" <note>the geometric angles between planets that astrologers believe influence their power</note> of the planets, both among themselves and in relation to the stars higher than the planets. 

Our own soul, beyond the specific powers of our individual limbs, brings forth a common power of life everywhere within us. It does this most especially through the heart, which acts as a source of fire closest to the soul. Similarly, the <term>World Soul</term> <note>Anima Mundi: the concept that the entire universe is a living, breathing being with its own soul</note>, flourishing everywhere, unfolds its power of common life in all places, primarily through the sun. For this reason, some people place the soul—both within us and within the world—entirely within every member, but most powerfully in the heart and in the sun. 

Always remember that just as the power of our soul is applied to our limbs through the "spirit" <note>the subtle vapor that connects the physical body to the immaterial soul</note>, the power of the World Soul is applied through the <term>quintessence</term> <note>the 'fifth essence' or ether, a celestial substance that permeates all things</note>, which flourishes everywhere. This spirit within the worldly body, directed by the World Soul, is spread through all things; it most especially infuses this power into those things that have drawn in the most of this kind of spirit. 

This quintessence can be more and more absorbed into our own bodies if you know how to separate it when it is mixed with other elements, or at least if you frequently use those things in which it is abundant and especially pure. Such things include amber-colored wine, sugar, balsam, gold, precious stones, <term>myrobalans</term> <note>a type of dried, plum-like fruit used in Renaissance medicine to balance the humors</note>, and things that smell most sweet or that shine brightly. 

It is most especially found in those things which, in their subtle substance, possess a quality that is warm, moist, and clear. Aside from wine, this includes the whitest sugar, especially if you add gold to it, along with the scent of cinnamon and roses. Furthermore, just as food properly taken into us is not alive by itself, but is transformed by our spirit into the form of our own life; so too our bodies, when properly adapted to the body and spirit of the world...

<summary>This page explains how the World Soul animates the universe through a "quintessence" or spirit, mirroring how the human soul animates the body through the heart. It identifies specific substances like gold, wine, and aromatics that are rich in this celestial spirit and can be consumed for health.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, quintessence, celestial influences, astrology, Renaissance medicine, alchemy, gold, spiritus, seminal reasons</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on natural magic and the medicinal use of sympathetic correspondences, specifically how to draw celestial influences (like those of the Sun and Jupiter) into the human body and spirit.</meta>

That is to say, through worldly things and through our own spirit, we may draw the best out of worldly life. If you wish for nourishment to be directed toward the form of your brain, or your liver and stomach, more than toward other parts: take in as much similar nourishment as you can—that is, the brain, liver, and stomach of animals that are not far removed from human nature. <note>The author refers to the "Doctrine of Signatures" or the principle of sympathy, where parts of animals were eaten to heal the corresponding human organ.</note> If you desire your body and spirit to receive power from a specific member of the world—namely, to receive virtue from the Sun—seek out those things which are primarily Solar: among metals and stones, but even more so among plants. Yet more so among animals, and most of all among humans. For things more similar to you undoubtedly contribute more to your well-being. These things should be applied externally and taken internally as much as your strength allows: especially on the day and in the hour of the Sun, and while the Sun is ruling in the astrological figure of the heavens.

Indeed, "Solar" things include all those stones and flowers which are named <term>heliotropes <note>original: "elitropia"; plants like the sunflower or turnip-weed, and stones like bloodstone, believed to turn toward the sun or reflect its power.</note></term> because they turn toward the sun. Likewise gold, <term>orpiment <note>original: "auripigmentum"; a deep yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy.</note></term>, and golden colors; chrysolite, <term>carbuncle <note>A term used for deep red gemstones, usually garnets or rubies.</note></term>, myrrh, frankincense, musk, ambergris, balsam, yellow honey, aromatic reed, saffron, spikenard, cinnamon, aloe wood, and other aromatics. Among animals, these include the ram, the hawk, the cock, the swan, the lion, the <term>scarab beetle <note>original: "cantarus."</note></term>, and the crocodile. Among people, they are those who are blonde, curly-haired, often bald, and magnanimous. These aforementioned things can be adapted for use partly as foods, partly as ointments and <term>fumigations <note>The practice of burning substances so the patient inhales the medicinal smoke or vapor.</note></term>, and partly for general use. This principle should be frequently pondered and, above all, cherished. And much light must be sought out.

If you suspect the stomach is deprived of the warming support of the liver, draw the faculty of the liver to the stomach, both by rubbing and by warm compresses, using things that agree with the liver: through chicory, endive, <term>spodium <note>original: "ſpodium"; a technical term for the ash or metallic residue produced in furnaces, used in ancient pharmacy.</note></term>, eupatorium, <term>liverwort <note>original: "epaticam."</note></term>, and liver-remedies. Similarly, lest your body be deprived of the influence of Jupiter, move the body on the day and hour and under the reign of Jupiter; and meanwhile, make use of "Jovial" <note>Things associated with the planet Jupiter.</note> things:

Silver, hyacinth <note>A blue or reddish-orange gemstone.</note>, topaz, coral, crystal, beryl, spodium, sapphire, and green and bronze colors. Also wine, <term>sugar <note>original: "fachato," likely a variant of "saccharo."</note></term>, white honey, and thoughts affected [by...]

...and many others. With Jovial <note>original: *Iouialibus*; referring to things or people influenced by the planet Jupiter</note>—that is, constant, fair <note>original: *equis*; meaning balanced or equitable</note>, religious, and law-giving people; and among men of this kind, you will spend time with those who are sanguine <note>original: *sanguineolq*; referring to the 'sanguine' humor: traditionally characterized as optimistic, social, and ruddy-faced</note>, beautiful, and venerable. But remember that to those initial cold things, one must add gold, wine, mint, saffron, cinnamon, and leopard's bane <note>original: *doronicum*; a medicinal herb thought to have protective qualities</note>. Indeed, the Jovial animals are the lamb, the peacock, the eagle, and the young man. But as for how the power <note>original: *uirtus*; meaning the inherent quality or "virtue" of a thing</note> of Venus is attracted by turtledoves, pigeons, wagtails <note>original: *motacillis*</note>, and the rest, modesty does not permit me to show. <note>The author implies that the rituals involving Venus's animals are too erotic or unseemly for polite discussion.</note>

# On the Harmony of the World.

## On the nature of man according to the stars.

### How an attraction is made from each star: Chapter II

Nor should anyone doubt that we and all things around us can, through certain preparations <note>original: *præparamétis*</note>, claim for ourselves celestial influences. For these things were made from heaven <note>original: *cœlitus*</note> and are continually governed from there; and from that source, they were first prepared. And what is most important: the world <note>the cosmos</note> is in itself more of a single living being <note>original: *animal*</note> than any other animal, provided it is the most perfect living being. Therefore, just as in us the quality and movement of any member—especially a principal one—pertains to the others, so the actions of the principal members in the world move all things; and the lower members easily receive from the supreme ones, which are ready to give of their own accord. For the more powerful a cause is, the more ready it is to act; therefore, the more inclined it is to give. Thus, even a small preparation applied by us is sufficient for receiving the gifts of the heavens, provided that...

<summary>This page discusses the specific earthly substances and animals associated with the planet Jupiter and introduces the philosophical concept of 'Sympathy,' where the entire universe is viewed as a single living organism whose parts influence one another.</summary>
<keywords>astrological magic, Jupiter, Venus, Marsilio Ficino, microcosm and macrocosm, planetary influence, celestial harmony, sanguine temperament</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary influences on the human species, transitioning from general archetypes to specific physical and material correspondences, particularly focusing on Mercury.</meta>

Each person should especially adapt themselves to that which they are primarily subject to. But before the property <note>Latin: *proprietatem*. This refers to the essential nature or defining characteristic of a being.</note> of each of us, we must consider the property of the human species. The Arab astrologers agreed that this species is **Solar**. I infer this to be true from the upright and beautiful stature of man, the subtlety of his humors <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to determine health and temperament.</note>, the clarity of his spirit, the transparency of his imagination, and his pursuit of truth and glory.

Moreover, I add to this a **Mercurial** property, because of the vigorous movement of a versatile mind. And since the human race is born naked, unarmed, and in need of everything, it provides all these things for itself through its own industry, which is the hallmark of Mercury. I also add a **Jovian** <note>Of the planet Jupiter.</note> property, because of the tempered physical constitution and laws of humanity. And because we receive life in the second month after conception, when Jupiter rules, and we are born in the ninth month, when he again takes up dominion.

Thus, the human species will finally be able to claim even greater gifts from these three planets especially, if it adapts itself more and more daily to Solar, Mercurial, and Jovian things. But what of the others? **Saturn** does not easily signify the common quality or lot of the human race, but rather a person secluded from others, or one who is either brutish, or blessed, or crushed by extreme misery. **Mars**, the **Moon**, and **Venus** represent affections and actions common to man as well as other animals.

Let us therefore return to the Sun, Jupiter, and Mercury. We have mentioned some Solar and Jovian things; however, I do not know how we overlooked Mercurial ones. They are of this kind: Tin, Silver, <note>In many traditions, tin belongs to Jupiter and silver to the Moon, but the author associates them here with Mercury's versatile nature.</note> porphyry, silver marcasite <note>A metallic mineral, often iron pyrite.</note>, the agate stone, glass, porphyry, and those colors which mix saffron-yellow with green: the emerald and lacca <note>A red resin or pigment, often shellac.</note>. Among animals, those that are wise and clever as well as vigorous: the ape and dogs. Among men, those who are eloquent, sharp, and versatile, with long faces and hands that are not fleshy.

<summary>The author identifies the Sun, Mercury, and Jupiter as the primary planetary influences on the human species, contrasting them with the more extreme or animalistic influences of other planets. He provides a list of minerals, animals, and physical traits associated with Mercury.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Mercury, Jupiter, Sun, human nature, humors, minerals, animal traits, planetary influence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary influences and the specific materials or conditions required to harness their powers, likely following a section on the "superior" planets like Jupiter or the Sun.</meta>

Furthermore, we must seek and practice those things that clearly pertain to a specific planet. This should be done specifically when that planet is ruling (as we have said) on its own day and hour, if possible. It is even better when the planet is in its own house, or its exaltation, or at least in its <term>triplicity</term> <note>A group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element</note>, its <term>term</term> <note>A specific subdivision of a zodiac sign ruled by a planet</note>, or in an <term>angle</term> <note>The most powerful houses in a chart: the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th</note> of the sky. It should be outside of <term>combustion</term> <note>The state of being too close to the Sun, which was thought to "burn up" and weaken a planet's power</note>, moving in direct motion, and ideally <term>oriental</term> <note>Rising before the Sun in the morning</note> if it is a "superior" planet (Saturn, Jupiter, or Mars). Likewise, it should be at its <term>apogee</term> <note>original: "auge"; the point in a planet's orbit furthest from the Earth</note> and be aspected by the Moon. 

If anyone seeks a benefit from the Moon herself or from Venus, they must observe similar timing. From **Venus**, favor is sought through her animals (which we have already mentioned) and through carnelian, sapphire, lapis lazuli, saffron-colored and trumpet-shaped <note>original: "tubeum"</note> objects, coral, and all beautiful, varied, or green colors; also through flowers, musical harmonies, sweet scents, and flavors. 

From the **Moon**, favor is sought through white, moist, and green things; through silver, crystal, <term>pearls</term> <note>original: "uniones"; large, high-quality single pearls</note>, and silver <term>marcasite</term> <note>A metallic mineral, often iron sulfide, used here for its shimmering lunar qualities</note>.

Since **Saturn** indeed rules over social standing and perseverance, while **Mars** rules over the effectiveness of motion, we are sometimes forced to request patronage from them for these reasons, provided the appropriate times are similarly observed. From Saturn, one seeks favor through materials that are somewhat earthy, dark, and leaden, such as dark jasper, magnetite <note>loadstone</note>, <term>chamomile</term> <note>original: "camoimum"; possibly referring to the herb or a similarly named stone like "cammanum"</note>, and chalcedony; and even to some extent through gold and golden marcasite. 

From **Mars**, favor is sought through fiery and red things: all sulfurous substances, iron, and <term>bloodstone</term> <note>original: "lapidem fanguineum"; likely hematite, which produces a blood-red streak</note>. Do not doubt that Saturn has some share in gold, for it is thought to possess it because of its great weight. Furthermore, gold is as similar to the Sun among all metals as the Sun is among all the planets and stars. 

Now, if anyone argues that Saturn and Mars are harmful by nature—a claim I myself would never believe—nevertheless, they must still be utilized, just as physicians sometimes use poisons. **Ptolemy** proves this in his *Centriloquium* <note>A famous collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to the astronomer Claudius Ptolemy</note>. Therefore, the power of Saturn, if taken cautiously, will sometimes be beneficial, just as physicians use substances that are astringent and...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the medicinal and spiritual effects of substances and activities. It focuses on how ancient philosophers like the Pythagoreans used specific music and lifestyles to balance the heavy, potentially "tyrannical" influence of the planet Saturn.</meta>

...dulling ones and those which stupefy: such as opium and <term>mandrake</term> <note>original: "madragora"; a plant root used as a potent sedative and anesthetic in the Renaissance</note>. There are also Martian ones <note>substances associated with the planet Mars, often sharp or caustic</note>, such as <term>euphorbium</term> <note>a caustic plant resin</note> and <term>hellebore</term>. The Magi, the Brahmans, and the Pythagoreans seem to have been most cautious in this matter. Since they feared the tyranny of Saturn <note>Saturn was thought to cause deep melancholy and isolation, particularly in scholars</note> because of their diligent pursuit of philosophy, they were accustomed to using daily <term>Jovial</term> <note>related to Jupiter, the planet of joy and balance</note> or Phoebaean <note>related to Phoebus Apollo, the Sun</note> sounds and songs, and they lived mostly under the open sky.

Always remember that through the affection and study of the soul, and through the very quality of our own spirit, we are most easily and suddenly exposed to the planets that signify that same affection, study, and quality. 

Therefore, through separation from human affairs, through leisure, solitude, and constancy; through theology, secret philosophy, superstition, magic, agriculture, and through sorrow, we are subjected to Saturn. Through civic and ambitious business, through natural and common philosophy, through civic religion, and through laws, we are subjected to Jupiter. To Mars through anger and contests. To the Sun and Mercury through the study of eloquence, song, truth, glory, and skill. To Venus through joy, music, and festivities. To the Moon through a life similar to that of plants <note>referring to the vegetative soul, or basic biological growth and reproduction</note>. 

But keep this difference in mind: the more public and grand exercise of the mind belongs to the Sun; the private and technical exercise belongs rather to Mercury. Then indeed, serious music belongs to Jupiter and the Sun; light music to Venus; and middle music to Mercury. The reasoning regarding the fixed stars is similar. 

This is the general rule for the human species. But the specific rule for each individual would be to investigate which star promised what good to whom in their <term>birth chart</term> <note>original: "genitura"</note>, and to seek favor from that star rather than another. And one should not expect just any gift from each star, but that which is specifically its own—unless perhaps you receive many general gifts from the Sun, as the common leader of the heavens, and similarly from Jupiter; and likewise, you may receive all worldly things from the <term>Soul of the World</term> <note>original: "anima spiritus mundi"; the animating force that Ficino believed connected the physical world to the divine</note>. Platonic reasoning and the testimony of Arabic astrologers in these and similar matters persuade me that this World is more effectively animated than any other living creature. And we, because of another and everywhere valid...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the "spirit of the world" (spiritus mundi) and its role as a vital intermediary between the divine soul and the material body, likely following a discussion on how celestial influences affect human health and spirit.</meta>

...they prove it. They also prove it through a certain application of our own spirit to the <term>spirit of the world (spiritus mundi)</term> <note>In Renaissance philosophy, this is a subtle, invisible substance that connects the physical universe to its divine soul, acting like a bridge.</note> achieved through natural science and focused emotion: by this means, celestial benefits are channeled to our soul and body. This happens on one hand through our own spirit—which acts as the medium within us—being strengthened by the world’s spirit; and on the other hand through the rays of the stars, which act favorably upon our spirit. Because our spirit is similar in nature to those rays, it is very often adapted to these celestial influences.

->Between the soul of the world and its body, its spirit is clearly present; within its power are the four elements. We, however, are able to draw this spirit in through our own spirit.<-

Truly, the body of the world is alive everywhere, as is evident from its motion and its power of generation; reliable philosophers prove this by the fact that the world produces living things from itself in every place. Therefore, it lives through a soul that is present everywhere and perfectly suited to it. Consequently, between the tangible and partially perishable body of the world and the soul itself—whose nature is very different from such a body—there is spirit everywhere, just as there is between the soul and the body within us. This is true as long as life is always communicated by the soul to the "thicker" <note>meaning more dense or material</note> body. For such a spirit is necessarily required as a medium, so that the divine soul may both be present to the thicker body and grant life to it from deep within. Every body that is easily perceptible to your senses is "thicker" and falls far short of the most divine soul. Therefore, there is a need for the support of a more excellent kind of "body" that is almost not a body at all. Thus, we know that all living things, both plants and animals, live and reproduce through a certain spirit similar to this. And among the elements, that which is most spiritual <note>likely referring to air or fire</note> reproduces most quickly and is constantly in motion as if it were alive. But you may ask in the meantime: if the elements and living creatures generate something like themselves through their own spirit, why do stones and metals—which are between the elements and animals—not reproduce...

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the nature of the cosmic spirit and its role as a link between the material world and the world of the soul. This page continues that exploration, specifically focusing on how this spirit can be refined or concentrated, much like an alchemist's elixir, and how the human spirit interacts with it.</meta>

<note>original: "animantes"</note> Living beings are in the middle. This is because the spirit within them is contained by denser matter. If this spirit is ever rightly separated and preserved in secret, it can generate its own likeness just as a <term>seminal power</term><note>The "seminal power" (seminaria uirtus) refers to the Stoic and Neoplatonic idea that the universe is permeated with "seeds" or formative principles that organize matter.</note> does. This is possible provided that matter of the same kind is applied to it, such as the spirit which diligent <term>natural philosophers</term><note>Physici: In the Renaissance, this term referred to those who studied the physical laws of nature, often including what we now call physics, chemistry, and medicine.</note> have produced from gold through a certain sublimation by fire. To this, they apply the power of metals and produce gold.

Such a spirit, rightly drawn from gold or from another substance and preserved, is what the Arabian <term>astrologers</term><note>Astrologi: Historically, this term often encompassed both astronomers and those we would call "natural magicians."</note> call the **elixir**. 

But let us return to the spirit of the world, through which the world generates all things—since all things are also generated through their own specific spirits. We may call this spirit either "the heavens" or the **quintessence** <note>The "fifth essence," a substance distinct from the four earthly elements (earth, water, air, fire), believed to make up the celestial bodies and the cosmic spirit.</note>. It is almost the same within the body of the world as our own spirit is within ours. There is one primary exception: the Soul of the World does not draw this spirit from the four elements as if they were its humors, as our soul draws spirit from ours. Rather, it procreates this spirit directly—to speak in the manner of Plato or Plotinus—from its own generative power, as if swelling with it. Together with this spirit, it procreates the stars, and immediately through it, it gives birth to the four elements, as if all things exist within the power of that spirit.

Indeed, the spirit itself is a most subtle body; it is almost not a body, and yet it is almost a soul. Likewise, it is almost not a soul, and yet it is already a body. Within its power, there is a very small amount of earthy nature, but more of water, more again of air, and most of all, the nature of fire and the stars. According to the measures of these degrees, the very quantities of the stars and the elements have come forth. 

This spirit flourishes everywhere. It is the immediate author of all generation and movement in all things, of which it is said: *The spirit nourishes from within.* <note>original: "Spiritus intus alit" — a famous quote from Virgil’s Aeneid (VI.726), used here to describe the life-force of the universe.</note> It is entirely bright, hot, moist, and life-giving above the level of nature, having obtained such gifts from the higher powers of the soul. 

Iarchas the Indian testified that **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A famous Greek sage and miracle-worker from the 1st century AD, often compared to Pythagoras or Jesus in the Renaissance.</note> had drunk most deeply of this spirit, saying: "No one should wonder, Apollonius, that you have attained the science of divination, since you carry so much **ether** <note>The "bright upper air" or the substance of the heavens.</note> in your soul."

### Our spirit draws the spirit of the world through the rays of the Sun and Jupiter, insofar as it is solar and Jovial. Chapter 3.

Now, therefore, you should strive first to internalize this for yourself. For by this <term>medium|The "spirit" (spiritus), a fine substance that acts as a bridge between the physical and the spiritual</term>, you will receive certain natural benefits: both from the body of the world, from its soul, and even from the stars and the <term>Daemons|In this context, "daemons" refers to guiding spirits or celestial beings, not "demons" in the modern sense</term>. For the spirit itself is the mediator between the dense body of the world and its soul. The stars and the Daemons exist within it and act through it. 

For whether the body of the world and worldly things come directly from the <term>Soul of the World|Anima Mundi: the concept that the universe has an inner soul that gives it life</term>—as <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) and Porphyry (c. 234–305 AD) were influential Neoplatonist philosophers who argued that nature is an overflow of a divine soul.</note> **Plotinus** and **Porphyry** believe—or whether the worldly body and the soul both come directly from God—as is the view of <note>The author likely refers to Christian theologians ("our people") here.</note> **our writers** and perhaps **Timaeus the Pythagorean**—it remains that the world is entirely alive and breathes, and we are permitted to draw in its spirit. 

It is drawn in properly by a human being through their own spirit, which is by its very nature <gloss>predisposed to be</gloss> similar to that cosmic spirit. This is especially true if one makes it truly related to the stars through practice, so that it becomes as celestial as possible. Indeed, the spirit becomes <note>original: "coelestis"</note> **celestial** if it is purged of impurities and of all those things which are inherently unlike it...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how to attract and cultivate the "solar spirit"—a vital, life-giving force—within the human body and soul, following the specific methods previously established.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>

...by the method we have stated. Then, you too should compose exquisite solar preparations <note>original: "solaria"; likely referring to solar-themed medicines, talismans, or dietary regimens</note> everywhere. Then begin to use these solar things, but with this caution: that you carefully avoid drying out under the heat. For the solar spirit cannot easily emerge unless it is abundant; for abundance belongs especially to the Sun. 

It will be most effective through diligence: warming the heart with <term>cordials</term> <note>medicines or foods believed to stimulate the heart and the "vital spirits"</note> both internally and externally; and also through a diet consisting of light foods that are nevertheless very nourishing, easy to digest, and healthful. Frequent and gentle movement is also necessary, along with timely rest, thin and serene air (removed from both extreme heat and frost), and especially a **joyful mind**. 

Furthermore, your spirit will not be "solar" unless it is warm, refined, and clear. You will make it refined and clear if you avoid sad, thick, and dark things. You shall use things that are bright and joyful, both inside and out. You should receive much light by day and by night. You shall expel filth, idleness, and torpor. Above all, you must avoid darkness. 

When you are bringing the spirit toward the natural heat of the Sun, beware lest you lead it to the <term>third degree</term> <note>In ancient medicine, qualities like heat and dryness were measured in four degrees; the third degree was considered dangerously intense</note> of heat and dryness. For the heat of the Sun itself does not naturally dry things out; otherwise, the Sun would not be the lord of greening and regeneration, nor the author of growth. It only happens that its rays cause drying when they are trapped in hollows or in dry, enclosed spaces. 

Therefore, you shall apply and preserve a refined moisture for this heat—the kind of moisture that is solar and especially <term>Jovial</term> <note>pertaining to the planet Jupiter, considered the "Great Benefic" in astrology</note>—within the spirit by using such things. Do this if you wish to make the spirit solar, lest you accidentally lead it toward a <term>Martian</term> <note>pertaining to Mars, a planet associated with harsh, scorching, and destructive heat</note> nature rather than a solar one. 

Indeed, they say that **Mars** is similar to the **Sun** in only a few ways—and those are the obvious ones, not particularly excellent—and that Mars is among the enemies of living things. But we know that **Jupiter** is very similar to the Sun in many excellent gifts (though they are more hidden) and is its greatest friend. Whence **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the influential 2nd-century astronomer and astrologer</note>, where he discusses harmony, says that Jupiter harmonizes most perfectly with the Sun above all others, just as **Venus** harmonizes with the **Moon**. 

All astrologers attribute universal beneficence to the Sun, just as they do to Jupiter. Although the Sun acts more effectively in these matters, Jupiter acts under the power of the Sun. In both, heat flourishes and overcomes moisture. But in Jupiter, it overcomes it moderately; in the Sun, it overcomes it excellently; and in both, it acts beneficially. Since, therefore, they harmonize so well, you can easily make your spirit solar and Jovial at the same time; you may rightly mix solar and Jovial things together.
