Especially if you compose these things together and apply them to the <term>spirit<note>In Renaissance medicine, the 'spirit' (spiritus) was a subtle substance that bridged the physical body and the soul.</note></term> when Jupiter looks at the Sun in a <term>trine<note>original: "trino" — an astrological aspect where planets are 120 degrees apart, considered highly harmonious.</note></term> aspect, or a <term>sextile<note>original: "sextili" — an aspect where planets are 60 degrees apart, also considered favorable.</note></term>, or at least when the Moon moves from the aspect of one to the other. This is most effective when the Moon proceeds from an aspect with the Sun toward a <term>conjunction<note>original: "coitum" — when two celestial bodies appear in the same position in the sky.</note></term> with Jupiter. You will make a purely Solar or Jovial <insert>remedy</insert> separately when you have observed the Moon’s aspect toward the Sun or toward Jupiter. If you have captured the nature of one, you will soon easily capture the nature of the other. Understand a **sextile aspect** to be when two planets are separated by the space of two signs. A **trine**, however, is when they are separated by an interval of four signs. We measure a conjunction or aspect of the Moon toward others within twelve degrees before and just as many after.

->### The Three Graces are Jupiter, the Sun, and Venus. Jupiter is the middle Grace between the two and is most suited to us.<-
->### Chapter 5.<-

**Y**ou will find the compositions and treatments for both Jupiter and the Sun together in our book *On Long Life* and in the book *On the Care of Scholars* <note>original: "de uita lóg̃a" and "de litteratorũ cura" — these are parts of Marsilio Ficino's famous work, *Three Books on Life*.</note>. In those works, we also mixed in many things related to **Venus**. For we who are scholars fear "drying out" <note>Renaissance scholars were thought to suffer from "melancholy," a cold and dry condition; the planet Venus provided helpful moisture and warmth.</note>, which Venus resists. And Venus herself is a most dear friend to Jupiter, just as Jupiter is to the Sun. They are like three Graces, harmonious and joined together. From these three high ones, and from stars of the same nature, astrologers both hope for and diligently seek "graces" <note>Here "graces" refers to beneficial celestial influences or spiritual gifts.</note>. They believe these are transmitted through **Mercury** and the **Moon** as if by messengers; they manage these influences most easily and commonly through the Moon. Indeed, the Moon is thought to be more fortunate when in **conjunction** with Jupiter or Venus than if she viewed them through a sextile or trine aspect. Nevertheless, if she views them through a trine and they "receive" her, it is considered as if they were in conjunction. Similarly, if she is looked upon by the Sun and simultaneously "received" <note>In astrology, 'reception' occurs when a planet is located in a zodiac sign ruled by another planet, creating a hospitable connection.</note>. However, if we wished to run through all the powers and effects of these three stars and similar ones individually, we would be undertaking a long task, difficult to research and most difficult to observe. If we apply ourselves specifically to Venus, we do not easily have the Sun. If specifically to the Sun, we do not easily have Venus. Therefore, so that we may embrace the grace of all three together in one...

<summary>This page explains how to time the creation of astrological remedies using aspects of the Moon, Sun, and Jupiter, and introduces the concept of the 'Three Graces' (Jupiter, Sun, and Venus) as the primary sources of celestial favor for scholars.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Jupiter, Sun, Venus, Three Graces, Marsilio Ficino, planetary aspects, trine, sextile, conjunction, melancholy</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
We shall finally flee to Jupiter: by nature, he is placed as a middle effect between the Sun and Venus, very tempered in quality. In a certain way, he delivers whatever one hopes for from Venus or the Sun. Venus is indeed more magnificent and honorable, but Jupiter <note>The OCR reads "Iol," likely a printer's error for "Iovem" (Jupiter).</note> is more temperate, and in all things, he is most congruent with human nature. We shall therefore make use of Jupiter when both he and the Moon possess dignity—both natural <note>Natural dignity refers to a planet being in its own zodiac sign or "home."</note> and accidental <note>Accidental dignity refers to a planet's favorable position in the sky at a specific moment, such as being high in the heavens.</note>—and when they are together or regard each other fortunately. If ever this cannot be achieved, mix Solar things together with Venereal things <note>Items associated with the Sun (like gold or laurel) and Venus (like copper or roses).</note>, and thus make a "Jovial" <note>An object or medicine imbued with the qualities of the planet Jupiter.</note> composite from both. This should be done when the Moon goes from a conjunction with Venus toward a sextile aspect <note>A harmonious astrological angle of 60 degrees.</note> with the Sun, or vice versa. Remember, indeed, that in compounding things which cherish and strengthen the spirit, it is most beneficial to bring in the Moon; if, along with these gifts, she also has this quality: that she runs through the airy signs <note>The zodiac signs of Gemini, Libra, and Aquarius.</note>, especially through Aquarius, which is the most purely airy; or if she is in her own domicile, or her exaltation, or the house of Jupiter or the Sun. And wherever she may be, let her hold a station <margin>The 28 Lunar Mansions.</margin> out of those twenty-eight that is suitable both to herself and to the work.

->On the natural, vital, and animal power within us. And by which planets they are assisted, and how by the aspect of the Moon toward the Sun and Venus: but especially toward Jupiter.<-
### Chapter VI.

The principal discipline, however, is to rightly understand which spirit, which power, and which thing these planets primarily signify. The Moon and Venus signify the natural and generative power and spirit, and those things which increase it. Jupiter signifies the same, but more effectively, and he also governs the liver and the stomach, and possesses no small power over the vital spirit <note>In Renaissance medicine, the "vital spirit" was believed to be a subtle substance that carried life-force through the arteries.</note> and the suitability of that vital spirit for endurance—indeed, even through his own nature. Otherwise, the heart would not properly receive the vital spirit of Jupiter into itself. Whence the Greeks call Jupiter "Life" <note>original: "uita" — a reference to the Greek word "Zēn," which sounds like "Zēn" (to live) and is a name for Zeus/Jupiter.</note> and "He through whom there is life." They also maintain he has power over the animal spirit <note>The "animal spirit" (from the Latin 'anima', meaning soul) was thought to reside in the brain and nerves to govern thought and movement.</note>. Astrologers agree, saying that Jupiter contributes to philosophy, the finding of truth, and religion. And Plato says that philosophers proceed from Jupiter. This also Homer signified, according to the opinion of the ancients, saying...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the relationship between celestial bodies and human physiology, likely following a section on general planetary influences.</meta>

Such is the mind of men: as the father of men and gods <note>A reference to Jupiter (or Zeus), quoting a famous line from Homer’s *Odyssey* (18.136–137), which was a common philosophical proverb in the Renaissance.</note> brings upon them day by day. Indeed, no other deity is so named except for Jupiter. 

The Sun <note>original: "Sol"</note> signifies above all the vital spirit <term>vital spirit: In pre-modern medicine, this was the "pneuma" or breath of life, thought to be centered in the heart and responsible for life and heat.</term> and the heart. It also holds some influence—indeed, no small amount—in the head, because of sensation and movement, of which it is the master; nor does it lack natural power. 

Mercury rules the brains and the instruments of the senses. Therefore, he signifies the animal spirit <term>animal spirit: Derived from the Latin "anima" (soul/mind), these were spirits thought to be refined in the brain to govern thought, sensation, and movement.</term>.

Consequently, the safest path is to do nothing without the favor of the Moon <note>original: "Lunae"</note>. For she commonly, frequently, and easily sends celestial influences down to the lower realms. She is called "another Sun," creating the four seasons of the year every month. For in her first quarter, as the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy.</note> believe, she is hot and moist. In the second, hot and dry. In the third, cold and dry. In the fourth, cold and moist. 

And her light is, without doubt, the light of the Sun. She rules the humors and generation, and she measures all the changes of the fetus in the womb by her revolutions. Whenever she joins with the Sun, she receives from him a life-giving virtue which she pours into the humor; and there, she receives from Mercury a power that mingles the humors. Mercury brings this power through his transformation into all his forms and his many-layered orbits. There, she also soon receives from Venus a power that leads to the forms suitable for birth. 

It is well worth remembering that the daily course of the Moon is divided into four quarters. In the first, she ascends from the horizon <note>The OCR reads "onere," likely a transcription error for "ortu" (rising/east).</note> to the middle of the sky; meanwhile, she increases the humor and the natural spirit. In the second, she seeks the sunset from the middle of the sky, producing the opposite effect in us. In the third, she goes from the sunset to the point below the middle <note>The "Imum Coeli," or the lowest point of the celestial meridian.</note>; again, she increases that spirit and the humor. In the fourth, she falls from there toward the sunrise and decreases them in turn. 

This is most apparent on the shores of the ocean, where the sea visibly rises and falls according to this course. In the same order, vigor flows in the sick <note>original: "agrotatibus"</note>. It is also probable that the Sun, through his own similar quarters, increases or diminishes natural heat and the vital spirit; and also the animal spirit, insofar as he has Mercury as a companion. Having learned these things, we will be able to keep more opportune times for the sake of restoring the humor, natural heat, and any spirit. 

But enough of the Moon for now. Nor is it right to leave out Jupiter, in whose month we received life in one sense, and in another, we were more commonly and fortunately born. And who between the Sun...

...and Venus: likewise, the path is between the **Sun** and the **Moon**. In quality and effect, [Jupiter] is the middle ground, and therefore it embraces all things. We consider it a sin and dangerous to overlook the **Sun** itself, the lord of heaven—unless perhaps someone says that whoever possesses **Jupiter** already possesses the Sun within Jupiter. For it is there that the Sun is most powerfully received by humans. Indeed, all the virtues of the celestial beings are contained within the Sun; not only do Iamblichus and Julian <note>Emperor Julian "the Apostate" (331–363 AD), who wrote a famous hymn to the Sun as a central deity</note> say this, but everyone affirms it. And Proclus <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher (412–485 AD) who taught that lower things reflect the powers of higher divine things</note> says that at the sight of the Sun, all the virtues of all the heavenly beings are gathered and collected into one. No one will deny that Jupiter is, in a sense, a "Sun" that has been tempered for our benefit.

Do not neglect the **Moon** either, when she is tempered toward **Venus**. For she helps greatly toward a strong and prosperous life, since Venus makes a person fertile and happy. Therefore, you should observe her. Although, if you mix the Moon—which is similar to Venus (being entirely equal in moisture and hardly less warm)—with Jupiter or the Sun, you almost possess Venus already.

What then? So that you may walk the safest and most convenient path, observe the Moon when she gazes upon <term>Aspects|The geometric angles between planets that astrologers believe transmit influence</term> the Sun and joins with Jupiter; or at least when she gazes at Jupiter and the Sun together; or certainly when, after looking at the Sun, she soon moves toward a conjunction or gaze with Jupiter. At that very time, combine them; or apply to yourself **Solar**, **Jovial**, and **Venereal** <note>Terms referring to the influences of the Sun, Jupiter, and Venus</note> things at once.

But if necessity forces you to flee to one of the "Great Ones," or if your business is with Jupiter himself—or rather with the Moon and Jupiter joined together—for that star [the Moon] fosters and strengthens our natural powers (indeed, all of them) even more than Jupiter does. Again, no star promises more prosperous or more numerous things. It is lucky to receive Jupiter's influence anywhere. However, receiving the Sun's influence is perhaps not safe everywhere. For Jupiter always helps, while the Sun often seems to do harm. Venus, on her own, is as if weak. Therefore, Jupiter alone was called the "Helping Father" <note>The name *Iuppiter* is etymologically derived from the archaic Latin *Iovis Pater* or "Helping Father"</note>.

Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose work *Tetrabiblos* defined Western astrology</note> confirms this when he says that a medicinal purge <note>original: *pharmacum*</note> hardly affects one's nature at all when the Moon is joined with Jupiter. He thinks that the nature of the entire body is so properly strengthened by that union. I have also found that when the Moon is joined with Venus, medicine hardly moves the body. However much we may search for a surplus of phlegm <note>original: *pituita*, one of the four bodily "humors" or fluids, associated with cold and moisture</note> in you, let us especially observe the Moon in relation to the Sun. But where we look for bile and dryness of that sort, we look to Venus; yet...

-># The direction of the Moon to Jupiter<-

...and in some way it contributes to all these things: and especially for expelling black bile <note>original: *atram bilem*; in the theory of the four humors, black bile was associated with melancholy and needed to be balanced for good health</note>: as well as for restoring and strengthening the common constitution <term>constitution: the "complexion" or specific balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) within an individual</term> of men. For just as licorice <note>original: *glyciriza*</note> and rose oil warm what is too cold and cool what is too warm—and wine does similarly—so Jupiter moistens dry things and dries those that are too moist. Thus, Jupiter is suited to human heat, just like wine, rose oil, chamomile, and licorice. 

Therefore, when you hear Abu Ma'shar <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD) was a famous Persian astrologer whose work heavily influenced European medieval and Renaissance science</note> saying: "There is no life for living things, besides God, except through the Sun and Moon," understand this insofar as it relates to the common influence <note>original: *influxum*; the "flowing in" of celestial power or energy to the earthly realm</note> upon all things. However, the most specific and suitable influence for man comes from Jupiter. 

There are, moreover, in the nature of the body, powers for attracting, retaining, "cooking" <note>original: *coquendi*; this refers to digestion, which was viewed as a heat-based "concoction" of food into humors</note>, and expelling. Jupiter himself aids all of these. He especially assists the power of cooking—that is, the virtue of digesting—as well as the powers of generating, nourishing, and increasing, all at once. This is because of his airy and abundant moisture, and his ample heat, which moderately governs the humors. 

Indeed, like a precious seed of the sun, the Sun’s own light is most tempered for the health of men. Meanwhile, the ray of Venus also contributes to this constantly, with the Moon likewise transferring these influences. The rays of Venus and the Moon, being moister, require a certain tempering agent; just as the rays of the Sun, being hotter, require the tempering of something moister. 

But the rays of Jupiter require no such tempering. For what else is Jupiter but a certain Sun that was tempered from the beginning for the health of things, especially human ones? Or what else is he but a Moon and Venus made warmer and more powerful? Therefore, astrologers look to Jupiter for a fertile, serene, and healthy year, and from him they hope for remedies against looming evils <note>original: *malorum*; the OCR reads *borum*, but context implies ills or misfortunes</note>. 

And Empedocles <note>An ancient Greek philosopher who proposed the theory of the four elements</note>, when assigning specific gifts to each of the planets, names Jupiter alone as the prince of generation, imitating Orpheus <note>The legendary Greek poet and prophet</note>. Besides Jupiter, they instruct that the Moon must be diligently observed in all works, as she is the competent medium between heavenly and earthly things. Therefore, let the Moon be in a degree, position, and aspect <note>aspect: the angular relationship between planets, thought to change their influence</note> suitable for the desired work. Let her not be in an eclipse, nor under the rays of the Sun within twelve degrees on either side, unless perhaps she be...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the astrological conditions of the Moon, specifically focusing on its proximity to the Sun and its relationship with other planets to determine its strength for medical or personal timing.</meta>

...in the same minute as the Sun. Indeed, most [astrologers] hold that all planets are strong when they exist in "unity" with the Sun <note>This refers to the state of *Cazimi*, where a planet is "in the heart of the Sun" and thus greatly strengthened rather than weakened by the Sun's heat</note>. This unity is measured as thirty-two minutes: sixteen minutes before and sixteen after [the Sun's exact position] are counted together. 

The Moon should not be hindered by Saturn or Mars <note>In traditional astrology, Saturn and Mars are considered "malefics" or planets that bring difficulty</note>; nor should she descend into southern latitude when she moves beyond the twelve stated degrees. She should not be positioned opposite the Sun <note>The Full Moon</note>, nor should her light be waning, nor should her motion be slow—which occurs if she fails to travel twelve degrees in a single day. 

She should not be in the **"Burned Way"** <term>Via Combusta: A section of the sky, usually from the late degrees of Libra to the early degrees of Scorpio, traditionally considered unlucky or unpredictable</term> from the 28th degree of Libra to the 3rd degree of Scorpio; nor should she be in the eighth house, nor in the Ascendant <note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon</note>, nor in the "bounds" of Mars or Saturn. 

Some also advise against having the Moon in the sixth, twelfth, ninth, or fourth [houses]. They approve of her being in the other regions of the heavens. If you cannot satisfy all these conditions, at least wait for Jupiter or Venus to be in the Ascendant or the tenth house. For in this way, they will remedy the Moon's weaknesses. 

It is also worth remembering that as the Moon increases in light, so too do our own moisture, spirit, and vitality increase, and these are expanded toward our outer surfaces. This is especially true in her second quarter <note>The phase between the First Quarter and the Full Moon</note>. However, when she wanes, the opposite occurs, particularly in the final quarter. Her first "trine" aspect to the Sun <note>original: "trinum"; an aspect of 120 degrees, considered very harmonious</note> is superior to the second. This, in turn, is better than the first "sextile" <note>original: "sextili"; an aspect of 60 degrees</note>, and that better than the second sextile. As the Moon fills with light, she is likewise filled with heat. 

Therefore, some do not seem to care as much about how the Moon "views" the Sun; they only require that she have as much light as possible, especially while waxing. Meanwhile, let her be viewed through a trine or sextile aspect by Jupiter or Venus. 

Accordingly, the **fiery** signs assist the **attractive** faculty of the body. The **earthy** signs assist the **retentive** faculty. The **airy** signs assist the **digestive** faculty, and the **watery** signs assist the **expulsive** faculty. If you wish to support all these faculties in yourself, you will strengthen the **attractive** faculty most through fiery signs; specifically, when the Moon is positioned in fiery signs or stations and views Jupiter alone: **Aries**, **Leo**, and **Sagittarius**. 

You will strengthen the **retentive** faculty through earthy signs; most effectively when the Moon gazes upon Jupiter while she is located in earthy signs or stations: **Taurus**, **Virgo**, and **Capricorn**. 

You will successfully aid the **digestive** and generative faculties through the airy signs—**Gemini**, **Libra**, and **Aquarius**—whenever the Moon looks up at or joins Jupiter while under these airy signs. 

Finally, the **expulsive** faculty is aided through the watery signs—**Cancer**, **Pisces**, and **Scorpio**—when the Moon is positioned under these watery signs and is illuminated by the rays of Jupiter.

Most desired of all these things is the following: if Jupiter occupies the same, or similar, or at least not entirely different zodiac signs or <term>mansions</term> <note>original: "manſionef"; these are the 28 divisions of the sky through which the Moon passes</note>.

For those of a medium consistency, use **Cancer** <note>original: "cãctrum"</note>. If you are purging through the <term>lower parts</term> <note>referring to the use of laxatives or enemas</note>, use Pisces and Scorpio. For purging through the <term>upper parts</term> <note>referring to emetics used to induce vomiting</note>, use Cancer.

You shall avoid the harmful <term>aspect</term> <note>the geometric angle or relationship between planets</note> of **Saturn** or **Mars** toward the Moon. For the former [Saturn] disturbs the stomach, while the latter [Mars] causes the intestines to give way. You shall also avoid **Capricorn** and **Taurus**, for they bring about nausea.

You know that a body part must not be provoked <note>meaning treated or operated upon</note> when the Moon occupies the zodiac sign that governs that specific part <note>A core rule of medieval medicine was that you never "touch with iron" the part of the body ruled by the sign the Moon is currently in</note>. This is because the Moon moves the <term>humors</term> <note>the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—believed to dictate health</note>. Instead, that part of the body should be comforted.

Let these remarks suffice regarding the power and the <term>natural spirit</term> <note>original: "ſpiritu naturali"; in Galenic medicine, this spirit was believed to be produced in the liver and responsible for nutrition and growth</note> which dominate especially in the **liver** <note>original: "tecore," a typo for "iecore"</note>, and which are divided into the four functions we have previously described.

->**How the limbs in us are nourished by the comparison of the Moon to the signs and to the fixed stars. Chapter VII.**<-

### On the Virtues and Use of the Fixed Stars. Chapter 8.

The greater astrologers relate that certain stars, reckoned from Mercury, possess very many powers. Such is the one at 22 degrees of Aries, the **Navel of Andromeda** <note>original: *umbilicus Andromade*; the star Mirach</note>, possessing the nature of Mercury and Venus. Likewise, at 18 degrees of Taurus is the **Head of Algol**, possessing the nature of Saturn and Jupiter. They wished for the diamond and mugwort to be subject to it, and for it to provide bold victory. At 22 degrees of the same [sign] are the **Pleiades**, a lunar and martial constellation. To it they subjected crystal, the herb dragonwort, and fennel seed. They think it contributes to sharpening the vision. As for what some say about it being conducive to summoning demons, I judge this to be a fiction.

**Aldebaran**, in the first or third degree of Gemini, is of the nature of Mars and Venus. **The Goat** <note>original: *Hircus*; the star Capella</note>, in the 13th degree of the same, is of Jupiter and Saturn. To this they subjected sapphire, prase <note>a leek-green variety of quartz</note>, mint, mugwort, and mandrake. They trust it helps toward dignity and the favor of princes, unless perhaps their opinion is mistaken. <note>The text here likely transitions to the star Regulus, the 'Little King'</note> They wish for the ruby, spurge <note>original: *ririmallu*</note>, and woodruff <note>original: *matriſiluã*</note> to be under it, to increase riches and glory. In the 6th or 7th degree of Cancer is the **Greater Dog** <note>Sirius</note>, of the nature of Venus. It presides over beryl, laurel, mugwort, and dragonwort. It bestows grace. Likewise, in the 17th of the same is the **Lesser Dog** <note>Procyon</note>, of the nature of Mercury and Mars. To this they wished the agate stone, the herb heliotrope <note>original: *folſequm*; literally 'sun-follower'</note>, and pennyroyal <note>original: *pule*</note> to be subject.

This should be performed when the Moon joins that star, or looks upon it with a "trine" or "sextile" aspect <note>Harmonious astrological angles of 120 or 60 degrees</note>.

And the star strikes in the mid-heaven <note>The "mid-heaven" or *Medium Coeli* is the highest point in an astrological chart, representing peak power.</note> or while ascending. I, however, would compose things pertaining to such stars in the form of **medicine** <note>original: "medicine"; Ficino, being a physician, often preferred ingestible or topical remedies over physical jewelry because they interacted more directly with the body's "spirit."</note> rather than a ring, to be applied internally or externally, once the opportunity of the aforementioned time has been observed. Nevertheless, even the ancients held rings in high esteem. For Damis and Philostratus <note>Damis was the student and companion of Apollonius of Tyana; Philostratus was the 3rd-century biographer who recorded their travels.</note> tell that Iarchas, the prince of the Indian sages, made seven rings in a similar manner, named after the seven stars <note>The "seven stars" refers to the seven classical planets: the Sun, Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>, and gave them to Apollonius of Tyana. He thereafter wore them one by one on their respective days, distinguishing them according to the names of the days. 

Indeed, Iarchas told Apollonius that his grandfather had lived for one hundred and thirty years, perhaps relying on a celestial gift of this kind. Because of this, Apollonius also, even in his hundredth year—as they say—appeared to possess the vigor of a youth. Finally, if rings of this kind have any power from on high, I think it pertains not so much to the soul or the dense physical body as to the <term>spirit</term> <note>In Ficino's philosophy, the "spirit" is a subtle, vaporous substance that acts as the bridge between the immaterial soul and the physical body.</note>. As the ring is gradually warmed [by the body], the spirit is affected in such a way that it becomes firmer or clearer, more vigorous or milder, lighter or more joyful. These affections indeed pass entirely into the body and, in a certain way, into the sensual soul, which for the most part indulges the body. 

As for what they promise regarding rings being useful against <term>Demons</term> <note>In this context, *daemones* refers to low-level spirits or planetary entities, not necessarily "demons" in the modern evil sense.</note> or enemies, or for gaining the favor of princes: it is either a fiction or derived from the fact that they make the internal spirit firm, or perhaps gentle, lovable through obedience, and gracious. 

Now, if I were to say that celestial things contribute something to talent, art, or fortune beyond physical health, I would not be disagreeing with our **Thomas Aquinas** <note>Ficino frequently cites Aquinas, the great Catholic theologian, to prove that his "natural magic" is compatible with Christian doctrine.</note>, who in the third book of *Against the Gentiles* <note>original: "contra gentiles"; referring to Aquinas's *Summa contra Gentiles*.</note> proves that impressions from celestial bodies are made on our body; by this gift, we are often disposed to choose what is better, even if we do not know the reason or the end. In this matter, he calls them "the fortunate," and—following Aristotle—the "well-born." He adds that by celestial power some become effective in certain actions (to use his words), just as a soldier is successful in conquering, a farmer in planting, or a doctor in healing. For he says: Just as herbs and stones have certain wonderful celestial powers beyond their elemental nature...

### The <term>dignities of the planets</term> <note>Dignities are the signs where a planet is most powerful or "at home."</note> in the signs to be observed for medical use. Chapter 9.

In <term>Cancer</term>, Mars has seven; Venus six; Mercury just as many; Jupiter seven; and Saturn four.

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of planetary "bounds" (the specific degrees of a sign ruled by a planet) and begins Chapter 10, which bridges these astrological positions with the preparation of medicines.</meta>

Mercury seven; Venus ten; Jupiter four; Mars seven; Saturn two. In **Libra**: Saturn six; Mercury eight; Jupiter seven; Venus as many <note>original: "totidem"; seven</note>; Mars two. In **Scorpio**: Mars seven; Venus four; Mercury eight; Jupiter five; Saturn six. In **Sagittarius**: Jupiter twelve; Venus five; Mercury four; Saturn five; Mars four. In **Capricorn**: Mercury seven; Jupiter seven; Venus eight; Saturn four; Mars as many. In **Aquarius**: Mercury seven; Venus six; Jupiter seven; Mars five; Saturn as many. In **Pisces**: Venus twelve; Jupiter four; Mercury three; Mars nine; and finally, Saturn two.

The **Sun** and **Moon**, however, have their <term>bounds</term> <note>original: "fines"; segments of a zodiac sign that grant a planet specific influence</note> according to a different logic. For the Sun possesses six signs as its bounds: Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, and Capricorn. The Moon, however, possesses the remaining signs: Aquarius, Pisces, Aries, Taurus, Gemini, and Cancer. Therefore, the Sun and Moon are considered to exercise the same principal influence in these signs as the other planets are thought to have within their specific bounds.

Besides their bounds, the planets also have their own **faces** <note>original: "facies"</note> within the signs, which the Greeks call <term>Decans</term> <note>original: "Decanos"</note>, each occupying ten degrees of a sign. In **Aries**: the first face belongs to Mars; the second to the Sun (who follows Mars in the heavens); and according to the **Chaldean order**, the third belongs to Venus (who succeeds the Sun in the heavens). In **Taurus**: the first face belongs to Mercury (following Venus); the second to the Moon (who succeeds Mercury); and the third to Saturn. For one must return to Saturn once the full number of planets has been cycled through. In **Gemini**: the first face belongs to Jupiter (evidently following Saturn); the second to Mars; the third to the Sun; continuing in the same order and manner thereafter.

### How we should use the planets in medicines, Chapter 10.
->10<-

We have recorded what **dignities** <note>original: "dignitates"; the various ways a planet gains strength or "honor" based on its position in the zodiac</note> the planets hold within the signs, so that whenever we are to prepare medical compositions that pertain to a specific planet, we may know how to position that planet within its own dignities. This is especially important when a planet holds lordship in our <term>rectification</term> <note>original: "redutione"; likely referring to an astrological calculation to determine planetary strength</note> and **genesis** <note>original: "genesi"; a person's birth chart or nativity</note>. This applies even to Saturn and Mars, which we might otherwise need to restrain <note>Saturn and Mars were traditionally viewed as "malefics," or planets with a naturally harsh or challenging influence, and thus required careful management in medical astrology.</note>.

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the practical application of astrology in daily life and medicine, specifically focusing on the influence of planetary "bounds" and the layout of the astrological houses.</meta>

...nevertheless, they should be raised up if they turn out to be the "significators" <note>original: "significatores"; planets that represent specific people or life areas in a birth chart</note> of our birth.

We will find it especially worthwhile from this mention if, when we intend to use the benefits of the Moon, Venus, and Jupiter in preparing medicines, we take care that they are not within the "bounds" <note>term: bounds (Latin: "fines"); specific subdivisions of each zodiac sign ruled by different planets which alter the planet's expression</note> of Saturn or Mars. This is unless we are compelled by necessity—perhaps to use Saturn to restrain a "dissolution" <note>likely referring to diarrhea or excessive fluid loss</note> and repress a feverish heat, or to use Mars to warm the coldest parts and awaken the sluggish. Otherwise, we should choose the bounds of Jupiter and Venus. 

We shall also accept the bounds of Mercury, especially to benefit "Mercurial" people. Nor should it escape our notice that strongly Mercurial people—those who excel in intellect, craftsmanship, and eloquence—are also significantly "Solar" in nature. For Mercury is always full of Apollo <note>Apollo is the Greek and Roman god of the Sun; the author implies that the intellect (Mercury) is powered by the light of the Sun.</note>. 

So that anyone may understand how we map the figures of the heavens into regions: we call the sign that rises from the east the first "house"—this applies to both methods <note>likely referring to different systems of house division</note>. That which follows in the circle is the second, then the third, and so on similarly. Thus, the seventh house is the sign which, now descending in the west, is opposite to the "ascendant" <note>the point on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth</note>. 

The eighth house follows this. The ninth "falls away" from the Midheaven <note>original: "medio cælo"; the highest point of the sky</note>, which constitutes the tenth house. The eleventh follows this. But the twelfth "falls away" from the ascendant. Therefore, for planets to be powerful, they must be located in the "angles"—either the east, the west, or the two mid-points of the sky. Most especially, they are powerful in the angle of the ascendant, or the tenth house (holding the Midheaven directly above one's head), or at least in the regions that immediately follow the angles. 

Although they believe the Sun rejoices in the ninth house (which falls from the Midheaven) and the Moon in the third (also a "falling" house). Among these, astrologers wish to keep two rules in mind: one concerning the sick person, and the other concerning the physician. Truly, where the seventh house of the sick person is made "unfortunate" by Saturn or Mars, or if its "lord" <note>the planet that rules the zodiac sign on that house's threshold</note> is unhappy, you must separate the doctor from the patient—if you believe Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the foundational 2nd-century authority on astronomy and astrology</note>. 

Furthermore, when choosing a doctor, he [Ptolemy] orders one to avoid those of a "Saturnine" or "Martial" nature <note>men characterized by the coldness of Saturn or the aggression of Mars</note>. Instead, one should seek a doctor whose birth chart <note>original: "genitura"</note> shows a sixth house that is "fortunate"—whether through the "aspect" <note>a geometric relationship between planets</note> of the Sun, or through Venus or Jupiter. We call a sign or planet "unfortunate" because of Saturn or Mars, unless these are in their own "dwellings" <note>term: domiciles (Latin: "domicialia"); the signs where a planet is most naturally "at home" and behaves better</note> or kingdoms: namely, when they are either there...

...are either looking there from an opposite or square position. We call an **Opposite** aspect those between things separated by the longest possible interval. We call it **Square**, however, where one is distant from the other by a fourth part of heaven—that is, a space of three signs. Saturn and Mars, being less favorable, are harmful to other planets through conjunction, opposition, or square aspects; this is especially true when they receive those planets as guests within their own <term>domicile (house)</term>, kingdom, or boundaries. In the same way, planets that are naturally fortunate are more helpful when—besides the <term>sextile (sixty-degree)</term> or <term>trine (one-hundred-twenty-degree)</term> aspect and conjunction—they receive others in this same manner. However, planets fear a conjunction with the Sun; they rejoice in its aspect, specifically the trine or the sextile.

Accordingly, it is necessary to remember that **Aries** <note>original: Anetē - a variation of Ariete</note> presides over the head and face; **Taurus** over the neck; **Gemini** over the arms and shoulders; **Cancer** over the chest, lungs, stomach, and upper arms; **Leo** over the heart, stomach, liver, back, and the rear ribs; **Virgo** over the intestines and the base of the stomach; **Libra** over the kidneys, the hips, and the haunches <note>original: naribus - while literally "nostrils," in this anatomical context it refers to the lower back or buttocks area</note>; **Scorpio** over the genitals and the womb; **Sagittarius** over the thighs and the area below the groin; **Capricorn** over the knees; **Aquarius** over the legs and shins; and **Pisces** over the feet.

Mindful of this order, you will take care not to touch a limb with iron <note>meaning surgical tools</note>, or fire <note>cauterization</note>, or <term>cupping glasses (suction therapy)</term> when the moon is passing through that specific sign. For at that time, the moon increases the humors <note>bodily fluids</note> in that limb. This prevents the flowing away of excess fluids and the closing of wounds, and it burdens the limb's natural strength. You will observe, however, that at such a time, applying remedies to the limb—whether externally or internally—will be beneficial and successful. It is also useful to know which sign was rising at the time of your birth <note>the Ascendant</note>. For, in addition to Aries, this sign represents your head; and the moon looks upon this as your head as well. Furthermore, when the Moon enters Aries, it is a favorable time to attempt baths and washings. When it is in Cancer, it is a fortunate time for <term>bloodletting</term> and for taking medicine in the form of an <term>electuary (a medicinal paste mixed with honey or syrup)</term. When it is in Leo, do not induce vomiting. When in Libra, it is suitable for <term>clysters (enemas)</term>. In Scorpio, do not attempt baths. There are some who say that giving laxative medicine is neither prohibited nor helpful in this sign. In Capricorn, it is harmful to take medicine. It is similar in Aquarius. In Pisces, however, it is beneficial. Which limbs each of the planets holds in any given sign, though necessary to know, is a long story to tell. Truly, when it comes to purging the bowels, the precept of **Ptolemy** <note>the famous 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer and astrologer</note> must not be hidden from us. Medi—

...Take purgatives <note>original: "purgatoria"; medicines used to cleanse the body of "humors" or fluids</note> when we find the Moon is in **Cancer**, **Pisces**, or **Scorpio**: especially if the lord of the sign ascending at that time aligns itself with a planet moving beneath the earth. But if the lord of the ascending sign instead joins overhead with a planet holding the mid-heaven <note>the highest point in the sky at a given time</note>, nausea and vomiting will be immediately provoked. Finally, let us conclude with Galen <note>A foundational 2nd-century Greek physician whose works dominated European medicine for centuries</note> that astrology is necessary for the physician. When discussing "critical days" <note>specific days during an illness when a patient was expected to either recover or decline</note>, he says the opinion of the Egyptians is certain: that is, the Moon signifies the daily condition of both the sick and the healthy. This is true to such an extent that if the rays of **Jupiter** and **Venus** mingle with the Moon, both patients feel well; but if those of **Saturn** or **Mars** mingle, the opposite occurs. But having wandered far enough, let us finally return to the spirit, to life, and to the Graces.

->**In what ways our spirit can draw the most from the spirit and life of the world. And which planets create and restore the spirit. And what things pertain to each planet. Chapter 11.**<-

All these things tend toward this: that our <term>spirit</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirit" was a subtle vapor that linked the physical body to the immortal soul</note>, prepared and purified through natural means, may receive as much as possible from the spirit of the world <note>original: "spiritu mundanae"; the life-force of the universe</note> itself through the rays of the stars caught at the right moment. Indeed, the life of the world, implanted in all things, spreads visibly into herbs and trees, like the fur and hair of its own body. Moreover, it swells into stones and metals <note>original: "metallar"</note> like teeth and bones. It also sprouts into living shells of the earth and clings to stones. These things live not so much by their own life as by that common life of the whole. This common life flourishes much more above the earth in subtle bodies, as if they were closer to the soul. Through its inner vigor, water, air, and fire possess their own living things and move them. This life nourishes air and fire even more than earth and water, and agitates them with perpetual motion. And finally, it gives the greatest vitality to the celestial bodies, which are like the head, heart, or eyes of the world. From there, through the stars as if through eyes, it pours out rays everywhere that are not only visible, but "vision-giving." Constructed in this way, as we have said elsewhere, it looks down upon lower things and nourishes them by seeing; indeed, by "touching" them with these rays, it generates, forms them in every way, and moves them.

Therefore, by observing the movement of shining water, as well as the moderately balanced air and fire, and the heavens themselves, you will receive the movement of the **World-Life** <note>original: "mundanae vitae." Ficino refers to the *Anima Mundi* or World Soul, suggesting that humans can synchronize their personal energy with the universe's inherent vitality.</note>. You should also move yourself gently and in a similar fashion to the <term>spirit</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, the 'spirit' (spiritus) was a fine, vaporous substance that linked the physical body to the immaterial soul.</note> itself, tracing circles in purer realms while avoiding dizziness; surveying the celestial bodies with your eyes and contemplating them in your mind.

Likewise, through the frequent use of plants and other living things, you should draw in as much as possible of the pores and the **World-Spirit**. This is especially effective if you are nourished and sustained by things that are still living and fresh, as if they were still clinging to Mother Earth.

You should spend as much time as possible among sweet-smelling plants, or at least those that do not have an unpleasant odor. For all herbs, flowers, trees, and fruits possess a scent, even if you often fail to notice it. By this very scent—which acts like the state and breath of the **World-Life**—nature refreshes and invigorates you from all sides. Your own spirit is very similar in its nature to these kinds of odors; and it is through the spirit, which acts as the intermediary between the body and the soul...

It truly brings pleasure: through which (if I may say so) Venus herself, the friend of pleasure, comes into the <term>spirit</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, 'spirit' (spiritus) was not a ghost, but a very thin, airy vapor generated by the blood that allowed the soul to communicate with the physical body.</note>. And as is her duty: having entered this spirit, she immediately propagates it. In summary, if anyone considers the Garden of Eden <note>original: "paradyfum"</note> and the use of the fruit <note>original: "pomi"</note> in the writings of Moses, and likewise the similar way of life described by Plato in the *Phaedo*, and what Pliny says about the peoples who live only on the scent of aromatics <note>Pliny the Elder described a legendary tribe, the Astomi, who had no mouths and survived solely by smelling fruits and flowers.</note>, he will understand that what we say is true. 

But let us descend to the nature of the spirit. The spirit is indeed of a quality that is, without doubt, **Jovial** <note>associated with the planet Jupiter</note>. It is infused into us during the time of Jupiter. It is also **Solar** <note>associated with the Sun</note>. For Jupiter infuses this spirit insofar as he tempers the vast power of the Sun within himself. 

Likewise, for another deep reason, the spirit is Jovial: since it is hot and moist, and abounds in heat rather than moisture; and it is born from the blood, and is called a certain "bloody vapor." Again, since it glows and is extremely refined <note>original: "subtilis"</note>, as well as shining and being formed in the heart, it is judged without doubt to be Solar. 

It also has, in a way, a **Venereal** <note>associated with the planet Venus</note> virtue within itself. For by the motion of Venus, it overflows, flows forth, is transferred, and propagates offspring; it expands toward the pleasure of any sense and flees from pain. 

In summary, insofar as the spirit leads the body toward life, motion, and propagation, it is considered **Jovial**, **Venereal**, and **Solar**. Insofar as it serves the soul for imagination <note>original: "imaginatiõneq;"</note>, it is reckoned **Solar** and **Mercurial** <note>associated with the planet Mercury</note>; and everywhere it is Mercurial, since it is so mobile, and so easily changed and formed. 

A healthy spirit commonly does not have much of **Saturn**, **Mars**, or the **Moon**. Otherwise, from Saturn it would often be dull, from Mars it would be furious, and from the Moon it would be in some way obtuse. Therefore, Lunar things—being thicker and moister—are quite foreign to the refined and volatile nature of the spirit. Those things which are excessively Saturnine or Martial are like poisons, naturally hostile to the spirit: the former because of extreme cold and dryness, and the latter because of dryness and a devouring heat. 

Therefore, the nature of the spirit is reckoned to be primarily Jovial and Solar; then Mercurial and, in a way, Venereal. By a primary distinction, the <term>natural spirit</term> <note>The 'natural spirit' was believed to be centered in the liver and responsible for nutrition and growth.</note> is properly dedicated to Jupiter; the <term>vital spirit</term> <note>The 'vital spirit' was centered in the heart and responsible for life and heat.</note> to the Sun; and the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>The 'animal' or 'animate' spirit was centered in the brain and responsible for thought and sensation.</note> to Mercury. When, therefore, the situation requires helping one of the three spirits, if its patron [planet] is then unfortunate or weak, it is not easy for the little client...

There is a similar reasoning and distinction regarding smells, for these are the siblings of tastes. It is the same regarding colors. Colors that are watery, white, green, and somewhat saffron-colored—similar to the colors of violets, roses, and lilies—as well as smells of this kind, refer to **Venus**, the **Moon**, and **Mercury**.

The more "burning" saffrons, pure golds, and brighter purples belong to the **Sun**. In metals also, and stones and glass, they are powerful because of their heavenly resemblance.

Such as is found in green ginger, or cinnamon, or sweet <term>fennel<note>original: "marratro" / "marathrum"</note></term>, or <term>leopard's bane<note>original: "doronico"</note></term>, if these are tasted when preserved with much sugar. [Aro]matic <note>original: "matica"; the text begins mid-word, likely completing "aromatica."</note> things: insofar as they bear a pleasing scent, they pertain to Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury, just as they do to the Sun. However, among these, the sharper scents pertain more to the Sun, while the duller ones belong to Venus, or rather Mercury. Nor should you wonder that we attribute so much to colors, odors, and voices. For flavors indeed pertain chiefly to the <term>natural spirit</term> <note>The 'natural spirit' was believed to be produced in the liver and governed nutrition and growth.</note>; odors, however, pertain rather to the <term>vital</term> <note>The 'vital spirit' was centered in the heart and distributed life-force through the arteries.</note> and <term>animal spirits</term>. Colors, shapes, and voices pertain to the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>The 'animal spirit' (from the Latin 'anima' meaning soul) was centered in the brain and governed sensation and movement.</note>. Also, the movement of the soul—whether joyful, sad, or constant—vehemently agitates the spirit into its own likeness: first the animal spirit, through this the vital, and through this finally the natural. 

Finally, because every spirit—due to its somewhat fiery and airy nature—is entirely luminous and mobile, it is similar to light. Therefore, it is easily moved and shaped in either direction by colors, airy voices, odors, and the habits of the soul. And whatever quality the spirit itself becomes, it in turn creates a similar disposition in the soul and generally in the quality of the body. Ultimately, when the spirit is opportunely exposed to the Graces through those things which are proper to the Graces—since it was already naturally in harmony with them—it immediately brings back the wonderful gifts of the Graces through their rays, which are both present everywhere and akin to the spirit itself.

### Natural and even artificial things have hidden virtues from the stars, through which they expose our spirit to those same stars. Chapter 12.

When we say that the spirit is exposed to the Graces through those things which belong to the Graces, we believe it is accommodated not only through qualities that are seen, heard, smelled, and tasted, but also through those that are touched. Remember, therefore, that **Heat** is in the first degree of Jupiter. In the second, of the Sun with Jupiter. In the third, of Mars with the Sun. In the fourth, of Mars. **Cold** is in the first degree of Venus. In the second, of the Moon. In the third, of the Moon with Saturn. In the fourth, of Saturn. **Moisture** in the first...

<meta>The text continues a technical discussion regarding the planetary alignments and their elemental qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) and how these influences interact with the human body and spirit.</meta>

...of Mercury with Jupiter. In the second, of Venus with the Moon. In the third, of the Moon with Venus. In the fourth, of the Moon when it is joined to Venus and Mercury.
Dryness in the first is of Jupiter. In the second, of Mercury with the Sun. In the third, of the Sun with Mars. In the fourth, of Mars with Saturn. In summary, from the qualities of the planets which Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, an influential 2nd-century astronomer and geographer</note> describes in his *Tetrabiblos* <note>original: "quadripartito", referring to Ptolemy's four-part foundational text on astrology</note>, we gather that the harmony forged from all of them together inclines toward heat and moisture. 
For the immense heat of Mars and the Sun, along with the temperate heat of Jupiter, overcomes the vast coldness of Saturn and the slight coldness of Venus and the Moon, so that in that balance, heat exceeds cold. Likewise, the abundant moisture of the Moon and Venus (which are close to us) and the temperate moisture of Jupiter overcome the great dryness of Saturn and Mars and temper the dryness of the Sun. Heat and moisture, therefore, dominate cold and dryness; and similarly, heat dominates moisture. 

Just as in the healthy body of a human being, which is tempered to a celestial consonance <note>a state of being in "tune" or harmony with the stars</note>, a certain complexion <note>a person's physical and mental temperament resulting from the balance of the four humors</note> coalesces from the heat and dryness of the heart, the heat and moisture of the liver, and again from the cold and moisture of the brain, tending toward heat and moisture while heat moderately reigns. For the heat of the heart and liver overcomes the cold of the brain. Likewise, the moisture of the liver and brain exceeds the dryness of the heart. 

Nor indeed would I wish to omit that such a harmony exists from the fixed stars, common to all, just as we have said of the planets. For astrologers consider these fixed stars to be similar in nature to the planets. To what end are these things mentioned? So that you may remember that our spirit <note>in Renaissance medicine, "spirit" was a thin vapor that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the soul</note> and our body—through a certain temperament verging toward heat and moisture—can be adjusted to the celestial qualities, whether by constant nature or by art <note>the "art" of medicine or natural magic</note>, and can claim these celestial benefits for yourself. 

Yet we do not say that our spirit is prepared for the heavens only through the qualities of things known to the senses <note>visible or physical qualities like heat or color</note>. But also, and much more so, it is prepared through certain properties implanted in things from heaven, hidden from our senses, and barely known even to reason. For since properties of this kind and their effects cannot consist of elemental power alone <note>meaning they cannot be explained by just being "hot" or "dry"</note>, it follows that they proceed uniquely from the life and spirit of the world <note>the *Spiritus Mundi*, a universal animating force</note> through the very rays of the stars. Therefore, the spirit is greatly affected by these properties and, through them, is vehemently exposed to celestial influences. For this reason, the emerald, hyacinth, sapphire, topaz, ruby, the horn of a unicorn, and especially the bezoar stone <note>a stone-like mass found in the stomachs of certain animals, highly prized as an antidote to poison</note>, as it is called by the Arabs, act through hidden and pleasing properties...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the celestial and occult properties of natural substances, likely from Marsilio Ficino's "Three Books on Life." The previous page likely established that certain herbs and stones are endowed with "formal" powers from the stars.</meta>

...they are endowed with. And for that reason, not only do they affect the body from within, but even if they merely touch the flesh, they bring forth their power as they are warmed there. From that point, they instill a celestial force into the spirits <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were subtle vapors that connected the soul to the body.</note>, by which the spirits protect themselves against plague and poisons.

That these and similar things act by such a celestial power is proven by the fact that even when taken in a tiny weight, they have a great influence in their action. This is a quality hardly ever granted to a simple "elementary" quality <note>The four elements: earth, air, fire, water. Ficino argues these cures work through a higher "quintessential" or celestial power rather than just physical properties.</note>, except perhaps for a fire that is truly celestial. For a material power requires much matter to act much; but a formal power <note>original: "formalis"—referring to the "form" or essence of a thing rather than its physical bulk.</note> is most effective even with the smallest amount of matter.

With a similar power, especially the Phoebean peony <note>Peonies were traditionally associated with Apollo (Phoebus), the god of healing.</note>, when it touches the flesh, arms the spirits against the falling sickness <note>A historical term for epilepsy.</note> by instilling a vapor into them within. Similarly, coral and chalcedony work against the illusions of black bile <note>Melancholy, believed to cause hallucinations and depression.</note> through the power of Jupiter and especially Venus. Other things work in the same way. 

Through a certain property of this kind, myrobalans <note>A group of astringent, plum-like fruits used in medieval medicine.</note> preserve youth, sharpen the senses, and benefit the intellect and memory; this is especially due to Jupiter—the restorer of Saturn—and Mercury, the friend of the senses. Perhaps someone might even think that such a tree existed in Paradise for the prolongation of life. 

The agate stone is consecrated to Mercury. Hence, natural philosophers agree that it is effective for eloquence and vigor, and against poisons. Serapio <note>likely Ibn Sarabi, a 12th-century physician influential in the West.</note> writes that he who wears a jacinth <note>A gemstone, often identified as zircon or sapphire in old texts.</note> or a seal made of it, is safe from lightning; and he says this power of the stone is very widely known. If it has this power, we believe it receives it from Jupiter. 

The *echites* or eagle-stone <note>A hollow stone containing a loose pebble, believed to be found in eagles' nests.</note> has its power from Lucina <note>The Roman goddess of childbirth.</note>—that is, from Venus and the Moon—so that it may prompt women to bring forth their birth soon and very easily. Rhazes <note>Al-Razi, the famous Persian physician.</note>, confirming Serapio, says he has frequently experienced this himself. 

Perhaps it also has power from Phoebus (who pierced the python) which we call "Cretean" <note>Referring to Dittany of Crete, a herb famously used to draw arrows or iron from wounds.</note>, so that it may both resist poisonous things and draw iron from a wound. Through the power of the Sun, ginger added to feasts keeps away fainting. Gentian <note>original: "Sentianis"—likely a corruption of Gentiana.</note> calms the madness of dogs and puts serpents to flight. Verbena is said to provide prophecy, gladness, expiations, and better sight. Rue and zedoary <note>A root related to ginger.</note> act as a theriac <note>A universal antidote or sovereign remedy.</note> against poisons. Frankincense <note>original: "Thus"</note> supports the vital and animal spirits against dullness, forgetfulness, and fear. 

Sage and mint indeed have the power of Jupiter; the former puts paralysis to flight, while the latter strengthens the mind even by its scent. By that same power, Cinquefoil <note>original: "Pentaphillo"—literally "five-leaved."</note> resists poisons; and one of its leaves drunk twice daily in wine cures a one-day fever; three leaves cure a tertian fever; and four cure a quartan fever; by this...

herbs which ancient priests used in their purifications because of their inherent purity. The <term>Chasteberry</term> <note>original: "Agnus castus". A shrub historically believed to suppress sexual desire.</note>, through the power of Saturn, halts the movements of Venus <note>Meaning it suppresses libido or sexual urges, which were associated with the planet Venus.</note>. Jasper, truly, stops the flow of blood. Wonderful effects usually arise when the elemental property serves the hidden <note>or "occult"</note> property; for example, in the <term>myrobalan</term> <note>A plum-like fruit used in medieval medicine to balance the "humors."</note> used to strengthen the spirit and the body: it is not only that celestial virtue acting, but also a great deal of physical power. Nor is the aromatic virtue of little importance, for it prevents decay and dissolution and strengthens the spirit.

What I say regarding "simples" <note>Medicines made of a single natural ingredient.</note>, I wish to be understood as applying similarly to medicinal compounds. 

Tell me, then: how does <term>Theriac</term> <note>A famous ancient "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh.</note> help us against poison? It does not simply draw the poison out. Indeed, it binds the bowels, and it does not entirely change the nature of the poison so suddenly. For the poison is not so weak as that. Rather, the Theriac strengthens the vital spirit—which is otherwise very tenuous and changeable—with a "spirit" that conforms to it in a certain proportion. It strengthens the vital spirit to such a degree that the spirit itself, now strong and active, uses the Theriac as its instrument: partly overcoming the poison, partly transforming it, and partly driving it away from the heart. 

But by what proportion or virtue does Theriac accomplish this? By a "Jovial" <note>Related to the planet Jupiter, associated with growth and health.</note> and "Phoebeian" <note>Related to Phoebus Apollo, the Sun, associated with life and light.</note> virtue, which it seems to have acquired through the mixing of many ingredients blended together according to a specific proportion. This virtue is, in fact, threefold. 

First, there is that celestial virtue I just mentioned. Second, there is another celestial property inherent in the herbs and aromatics from which the Theriac is properly composed; these support the first virtue toward the same goal. There is, moreover, another virtue in most of its parts that is elemental rather than celestial. Yet, it is of such a nature that it contributes to fortifying the spirit. I am speaking of the <term>styptic</term> <note>original: "stripticam," meaning astringent or contracting.</note> and aromatic qualities. The former firms the spirit, while the latter nourishes it. 

Therefore, Theriac has obtained a wonderful power against approaching old age and against poison, because these various virtues conspire together for that same purpose. One of these is celestial, achieved through skillful mixing; another is celestial as well, but inherent in its individual parts...

...naturally implanted. Another is purely elemental. But that which I spoke of before—acquired from the heavens—would be even much more wonderful: if it were composed not only of Jovian <note>relating to the planet Jupiter, often associated with growth and benevolence</note> and Solar proportions and from things of that sort, but also if an opportune time were chosen for this through the observation of the heavens. For just as the body relates to place and time, so movement and action relate to time. Therefore, just as certain bodies and their forms everywhere coalesce and are preserved in certain places and times, so too do certain specific actions obtain their efficacy from certain specific times.

Socrates indicates this in the *Alcibiades* <note>A dialogue by Plato concerning the nature of man and politics</note> and Proclus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who commented extensively on Plato</note> explains it. Pythagoras, noticing this, named the very good and the perfection of things "Opportunity" <note>original: *opportunitatem*; Pythagoras likely referred to the Greek concept of *Kairos*, the "right or critical moment"</note>. For the First Principle, according to Pythagoras and Plato, is the measure of all things, such that it has distributed different places and times to different bodies and actions. 

Hence, just as certain aspects of happiness or reason are not properly born elsewhere than here, nor at any other time than then, so they do not obtain efficacy and a perfect effect except when the harmony of the heavens resonates from all sides toward that same end. This harmony is thought to possess such great power that it bestows a frequently miraculous virtue <note>virtue here means a specific power or "potency"</note> not only upon the labors of farmers and the crafts of physicians composed through herbs and spices, but even upon the <term>images</term> <note>In this context, "images" refers to astrological talismans or amulets engraved with specific symbols at precise celestial moments</note> which are made by <term>astrologers</term> <note>At this time, astrology and astronomy were a single discipline focused on the influence of stars on earthly life</note> from metals and stones. 

But the subject of images now requires its own chapter. 

As for what pertains to choosing hours for actions and works, this is greatly confirmed by Ptolemy <note>An influential 2nd-century astronomer and geographer</note>, where he says in the *Centiloquium* <note>A collection of 100 aphorisms once attributed to Ptolemy</note>: "he who chooses what is better seems to differ in no way from him who has this by nature." In these words, he seems to confirm the power both of the heavens and of our own judgment and choice. Albertus Magnus <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar and philosopher</note> also says in the *Mirror* <note>original: *Speculum Astronomiae*, a medieval text on the compatibility of astrology and Christianity</note>: "For the freedom of the will is not constrained by the choice of a praiseworthy hour; rather, to despise the choice of an hour in the beginnings of great matters is a rashness of the will, not freedom."

It cites classical and medieval authorities to argue that using astrology to choose the right moment (election) is an exercise of wisdom rather than a restriction of free will.</summary>
