-># On the power of images according to the ancients, and of medicines acquired from heaven.<-
->### Page 14 ###<-

Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer and geographer.</note> says in the *Centiloquy* <note>original: "cœriloquio"; referring to the *Centiloquium*, a collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy.</note> that the forms of lower things are subject to the celestial faces. He says that the ancient sages were accustomed to fashion certain images at the time when planets entered similar faces in the sky, serving as models for the things below. Haly <note>Ali ibn Ridwan, a medieval Islamic physician and astrologer.</note> confirms this in that same place, saying: A useful image of a serpent can be made when the Moon enters the celestial Serpent <note>likely the constellation Serpens or Draco</note> or looks upon it favorably. Similarly, an effective image of a scorpion can be made when the Moon enters the sign of Scorpio, provided this sign holds one of the four <term>angles</term> <note>The four most powerful houses in an astrological chart: the Ascendant, Midheaven, Descendant, and Imum Coeli.</note>. He says this was done in Egypt in his own time, and that he was present: where a scorpion's figure was pressed into a **Bezoar stone** <note>original: "lapide Bezahar"; a mass found in the digestive system of animals, highly prized as a universal antidote to poison.</note>—which is so sacred—and it was given in a drink to one whom a scorpion had stung; and he was suddenly cured. The physician Habamed <note>Ahmad ibn Yusuf, also known as Ametus.</note> affirms that this can be done usefully, as does Serapion <note>Either Serapion the Elder or Younger, influential medical writers in the Arabic tradition.</note>. Furthermore, Haly recounts that a wise man known to him there had industriously made images that moved—an effect of the kind we read about, I know not how, regarding **Archytas** <note>Archytas of Tarentum, a Greek philosopher famed for building a mechanical flying pigeon.</note>.

[Hermes] Trismegistus <note>The legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of Hermeticism and alchemy.</note> also says such things: that the Egyptians were accustomed to make materials out of certain parts of the world, and they used to opportunely insert the souls of **Daemons** <note>In the Neoplatonic sense, these are intermediary spirits, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense.</note> into them; and thus the spirit of **Mercury**, or **Phoebus** [Apollo], or **Jove** [Jupiter], and **Osiris** <note>original: "Olyridiſq;"</note> descended into the statues to be either beneficial to men or even harmful. This is similar to the myth of **Prometheus**: that he stole life and celestial light for a certain figure made of clay. The Magi, followers of **Zoroaster**, to evoke a spirit from **Hecate**, used a certain golden ball marked with celestial characters; a sapphire was inserted into it, and it was turned with a thong made of bull's hide; and meanwhile they chanted. But as for the songs, I gladly omit them. For Psellus the Platonist <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine philosopher.</note> disapproved of and ridiculed them. The Hebrews also, while in Egypt, learned to build a **Golden Calf** <note>original: "uirulũ aureũ"</note> in the presence of the deity; for their astrologers thought they should catch the favor of **Venus** and the **Moon** against the influence of **Scorpio** and **Mars**, which was hostile to the Jews. **Porphyry** <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonic philosopher.</note> also, in his letter to Anebo <note>original: "Anaboneĩ"</note>, testifies that images are effective, and adds that with certain vapors...

<summary>This page discusses the historical and astrological basis for creating "images" or talismans, citing ancient authorities like Ptolemy, Hermes Trismegistus, and Arabic physicians to explain how celestial influences can be captured in physical objects for healing and magic.</summary>
<keywords>astrological magic, talismans, Ptolemy, Hermes Trismegistus, Haly, Bezoar stone, Hecate, Golden Calf, Neoplatonism, planetary influence</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
...who were accustomed to immediately introducing aerial spirits <note>original: "Daemonas." In this Neoplatonic context, "daemons" refers to intermediary spirits or "guides" that inhabit the air, rather than "demons" in the modern sense.</note> exhaling from their own ritual fumigations <note>The practice of burning incense or herbs to attract or communicate with spirits.</note>. Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote extensively on theurgy and ritual magic.</note> confirms that in materials which are naturally suited to higher things, and which have been opportunely and duly collected and fused from all sides, powers and effects can be received—not only celestial ones, but even spirits and divine forces. Proclus and Synesius <note>Two other prominent late antique philosophers who studied the relationship between the material world and the divine.</note> say exactly the same thing.

Indeed, wonderful works for health can be made by physicians who are skilled in astrology through things composed of many ingredients—namely, powders, liquids, ointments, and electuaries <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup.</note>. These seem to have a more probable and better-known rationale than "images" <term>images: In this text, "images" refers to talismans or statues engraved with symbols intended to capture planetary influences.</term>. This is because, firstly, powders, liquids, ointments, and electuaries, when prepared at the right astrological moment, receive celestial influences more easily and quickly than the harder materials from which talismans are usually made. Secondly, they either take in the influence already affected from heaven and convert it into us, or at least when applied externally, they cling more effectively and finally penetrate the body. Furthermore, images are usually constructed from only one or very few things.

Those medicinal preparations, however, can be fused from very many ingredients according to one's will. For example, if you wished to gather a hundred "odors" of the Sun or Jupiter <note>referring to plants or substances governed by these planets in the system of "correspondences."</note> through a hundred plants, animals, and similar things, you could arrange these hundred things together and fashion them into a single form. In such a preparation, you might seem to possess almost the whole power of the Sun and Jupiter. You certainly know that an inferior nature <note>The material world.</note> cannot grasp all the powers of a superior nature <note>The celestial or divine world.</note> in just one single thing. Therefore, those powers are divided among many different natures here with us. It is more convenient for similar qualities to be collected through the help of medicines than through talismans.

Hence, images made of wood perhaps have little power. For wood is perhaps too hard to easily capture celestial influence, and it is less tenacious in retaining it if it does receive it. Overall, once wood has been torn from the bowels of mother earth, it loses almost all the vigor of its mundane life shortly thereafter and easily transforms into another quality <note>e.g., it rots or dries out.</note>. But gemstones and metals, although they seem harder for receiving the celestial gift, nevertheless retain it longer—which Iamblichus confirms—if they do receive it. For they contain their own traces and the worldly gifts for a very long time after being extracted, and they possess a certain "hereditary" property of the earth. Therefore, they judge these materials at least to be most fit for capturing and holding celestial influences. It is also probable, as we said in the previous book, that such beautiful things could not be formed under the earth...

<summary>This page discusses why medicinal compounds (like ointments and powders) are more effective than hard talismans for capturing celestial and spiritual influences. It argues that because planetary power is spread across many earthly things, a complex mixture of many ingredients can "capture" a planet's essence better than a single piece of wood or stone.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Proclus, Synesius, talismans, astrology, medicinal compounds, celestial influence, Sun, Jupiter, gemstones, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion on how celestial influences are captured in material objects, moving into a specific list of "chains" or "series" of things that correspond to specific stars and planets.</meta>

...with a certain supreme effort of the heavens; and that the power <term>virtutem: the inherent "virtue" or spiritual potency of an object</term> once imprinted in them from that effort remains. For the heavens have labored for a very long time in "cooking" and gathering these things. But since you cannot easily compose many things of this kind, you are compelled to diligently seek out which metal among the rest is most powerful in the order of a certain star, or which stone is highest in that order. This is so that you may grasp the remaining powers, as far as possible, in one supreme thing of that entire genus and order. Through such a receptacle, you may borrow celestial things agreeable to it. 

For example, in the **Solar order**—under a "Phoebian" <note>Phoebus is another name for Apollo, the god of the Sun.</note> man—the highest rank among animals is held by the **hawk** or the **rooster**. Among plants, it is **balsam** or **laurel**. Among metals, **gold**. Among stones, the **carbuncle** <note>A glowing red gemstone, like a deep ruby or garnet.</note> or the **pantaura** <note>A legendary stone said to attract other stones, much like a magnet.</note>. Among the elements, it is **glowing air**—for fire itself is considered to belong to **Mars**. When we say that the influence of the **Sun**, **Jupiter**, or **Venus** should be increased, we understand this as a general rule; however, this does not apply to a person in whose birth chart <term>genesi: the natal horoscope</term> any of these appears as a "killer" <note>A technical astrological term for a planet that threatens life in a specific chart.</note>.

->**The orders of things hanging from the stars, such as Solar things and the like; and how a Solar spirit is made. Chapter 14.**<-

Indeed, I have said elsewhere that from each and every star—to speak in a Platonic fashion—a series of things proper to it hangs down from above, all the way to the lowest things. Under the **Heart of the Scorpion** <note>The star Antares.</note>, after spirits <term>Demonas: intermediary spirits, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense</term> and men of that kind, we can place the **scorpion** animal and the herb **Asterion**. This herb is said to shine at night with a star-like shape, and physicians claim it possesses the quality of a **wine-rose** and holds a miraculous power against diseases of the genitals. 

Under the celestial **Serpent** or **Serpent-bearer** <note>The constellations Serpens and Ophiuchus.</note>, they place **Saturn** and, in some manner, **Jupiter**. After these, they place spirits who often take on the forms of serpents. Similar to these are men who resemble serpents, the herb **serpentaria**, the stone **draconites** (which is born in the head of a dragon), and likewise the stone commonly named **serpentine**. 

Beyond those things I shall bring forward in what follows: Under the solar star **Sirius** <note>The Dog Star, often associated with intense heat and the Sun.</note>, they place the **Sun** first; then "Phoebian" spirits, who **Proclus** <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on how earthly things relate to the divine.</note> testifies have sometimes appeared to men under the form of **lions** or **roosters**. Next are men very similar to these, and Solar animals. Then Phoebian plants, Solar metals, small stones, and glowing airy vapor. By a similar reasoning, they believe a web <note>original: "contextum"</note> is woven from any star of the firmament through some planet...

<summary>Ficino explains the Neoplatonic "Chain of Being," listing specific animals, plants, and minerals that correspond to the Sun and various constellations. He argues that by using the "highest" item in a category (like gold for metals), one can capture the spiritual influence of a specific star.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Solar order, Sirius, Proclus, celestial influence, medicinal herbs, gemstones, planetary series, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Solar" influences, specifically how various earthly things—animals, plants, and minerals—belong to the "Chain of the Sun" (the Golden Chain of Homer) and how their properties can be harnessed for natural magic and medicine.</meta>

...to descend step by step through the properties of things. If, therefore—as I was saying—you have appropriately grasped all **Solar** things through every degree of that order: namely, such men, or something belonging to such a man. Likewise, you will thoroughly absorb the power of the Sun through irrational animals, plants, metals, small stones, and those things which pertain to these; and, in a certain way, you will absorb the natural <term>Solar power</term> <note>original: "solanium," referring to the specific spiritual or magical influence of the Sun</note> of the <term>Daemons</term> <note>In the Platonic sense, "Daemons" are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily evil "demons."</note>.

Similar things have been said about the others. Indeed, **Solar men** are of the sort I mentioned before: those born when the sign of **Leo** is ascending or when the **Sun** is occupying or looking upon that sign; and similarly those born under **Aries**. Also Solar is the blood drawn from the left arm of those who are well-disposed. <margin>Solar Animals</margin> The <term>Phoebean</term> <note>of or belonging to Phoebus Apollo, the Sun god</note> animals are the **crocodile**, the **hawk**, the **lion**, the **rooster**, the **swan**, and the **crow**. 

Nor is there any other reason why the lion fears the rooster, except that in the **Solar order**, the rooster is superior to the lion. For the same reason, **Proclus** <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Greek Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the "hierarchies" of things.</note> says that a **Solar Daemon**, which once appeared in the form of a lion, immediately vanished when a rooster was presented to it.

Truly, in these animals, the **heart** is the most Solar part. I believe also that the "morning title" <note>original: "matinum titulum." Likely referring to the star Regulus, the "Heart of the Lion."</note> of the **celestial Lion** dwells within the heart; and for this very reason, whoever wears a naked buckle made from the bone of that same animal is redeemed from the pain of the limbs. For against pain of this kind, astrologers are accustomed to use the influences of that star. By the same logic, perhaps, this skin is said to protect us from lightning.

Among plants, the **palm** is Phoebean, and especially the **laurel**, which by its power repels both poison and lightning. The **ash tree** also, by a similar faculty, keeps poisonous things far away. The **Lotus** <note>original: "Loron"</note> is also Phoebean, as both its round leaves and its fruit testify; its leaves unfold by day and fold back by night. That the **peony** is Phoebean is indicated not only by its power, but also by its name <note>The peony (Paeonia) is named after Paeon, the physician to the Greek gods, often identified with Apollo.</note>. To this same category belong the flowers and herbs which contract as the sun departs and unfold when it returns, and continually turn toward the sun. 

Furthermore, there is **gold**, and the **sunstone** <note>original: "lapis elitis," likely the Heliotrope or Sunstone</note> which imitates the sun with its golden rays. Likewise, the stone which is called the **"Eye of the Sun"** has the shape of a pupil from which light shines forth. In the countryside, the **carbuncle** <note>A red gemstone, often a ruby or garnet.</note> glows at night; and the **panraura** <note>original: "panraura," possibly referring to a *panchros* stone</note> contains within itself all the powers of stones, just as gold does for metals and the Sun for the stars. 

Finally, many things have been signified to us by the higher powers. From these sources—if it is permitted—one may prepare an **electuary** <note>A medicinal paste or lozenge made by mixing powders with honey or syrup.</note> or an **ointment** from the blood of the Sun itself, from the hearts of those animals, from the Solar fruits of the aforementioned trees, and likewise from the flowers, herbs, Solar gold, and even the dust of those small stones.

It explains how these items can be used in medicine and natural magic to repel poison, lightning, and pain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion on "solar" remedies from his 1489 work, *Three Books on Life*. He is describing how to attract the life-giving influence of the Sun through specific ingredients, behaviors, and timing.</meta>

...you will be able [to do this] by adding: saffron, balsam, aromatic reed <note>Also known as sweet calamus, an ancient perfume ingredient.</note>, frankincense, musk, ambergris, wood-aloes, ginger, mastic, spikenard, cinnamon, leopard's-bane, citron peel, zedoary <note>A root similar to ginger.</note>, nutmeg, mace, and cloves. These should be mixed with yellow honey, or with oils of balsam, mastic, laurel, or nard. 

This is used for soothing the heart, stomach, and head—whether applied internally or externally—so that the person may then emerge "solar" <note>In medical astrology, a "solar" person is one filled with vitality, clarity, and health, mirroring the properties of the Sun.</note>. I say that from all these things, or at least from many of them, a compound should be prepared while the Sun is dominant. You should also begin to use it under that same solar dominance, provided that you also dress in solar clothing, live in solar places, and look at, hear, smell <note>original: *obfacias*, likely a typo for *olfacias*</note>, imagine, think about, and desire solar things. Likewise, you should imitate the dignity of the life and the gifts of the Sun. While moving among solar people and plants, you should touch the laurel tree continually. 

Furthermore, for total health, it is beneficial to mix things associated with Jupiter and Venus together with these solar items. Venus especially is the moderator of solar heat through her moisture, such as rose water, rose juice, and violets. 

I have partly composed medicines of this kind myself, partly described those composed by others, and partly tempered them—first in my book *On Caring for the Health of Scholars* <note>Latin: *De Cura Valetudine Eorum Qui Incumbunt Studio Litterarum*.</note>, and then in my book *On Long Life* <note>Latin: *De Vita Longa*.</note>. Likewise, in my book *Against the Plague*, I discussed what wonderful powers a herb nurtured by the Sun has against epidemics and poisons; among these is *Perforata* <note>St. John's Wort (Hypericum perforatum).</note>, which is called the "flee-demon" <note>original: *fuga demonum*. This herb was historically believed to drive away evil spirits and melancholy.</note>. You should not think that it wards off the harmful vapors of evil demons from us by any power other than that of celestial graces. The same seems to be achieved by certain other herbs, or a small stone, or coral. 

Truly, the "Lunar herb" <note>Likely *Moonwort* or *Botrychium*.</note> handed down by Mercury—with its round, sky-blue leaves, producing one leaf daily as the moon waxes and shedding one as it wanes—promises "lunar years" <note>A metaphorical way of promising a life cycle in harmony with natural rhythms.</note> to the one who uses it. But now, let us return to the subject of images <note>In this context, "images" refers to astrological talismans or seals engraved on stones.</note> by a new beginning.

### On the power of images according to the ancients and of medicines; and how medicines are far more powerful than images. <header>Cap. xv.</header>

If you have obtained <note>original: *nacrus*, likely *nactus*</note> the small stones which I described a little earlier as "common," there will be no need to engrave images upon them. You shall instead hang them from the neck, encased in gold with threads of saffron-colored silk, at a time when the Sun is in Aries...

He references his previous works on the health of scholars and the plague, emphasizing the spiritual and physical protection offered by herbs like St. John's Wort.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural magic and the occult properties of stones, moving from general celestial influences to specific minerals like Selenite, Helioselenite, and the Magnet (loadstone).</meta>

...passes through [Leo] or ascends to the mid-heaven and looks upon the Moon. Proclus <note>Proculus or Proclus (c. 412–485 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the "theurgic" or magical properties of nature.</note> tells of stones that are much more powerful during the waning moon <note>original: "i fene Luna," literally "in the old moon."</note>. First indeed is Selenite <term>Selenite: A transparent variety of gypsum, named after Selene, the Greek moon goddess; it was believed to wax and wane in size or brilliance with the lunar cycle</term>, which not only mimics the Moon in its shape, but also in its movement, circling along with the Moon. If you should happen to find this, and having encased it in silver, suspend it from your neck by a silver thread while the Moon is entering Cancer or Taurus <note>In astrology, the Moon is said to be "exalted" in Taurus and "at home" in Cancer, making its influence strongest in these signs.</note> and holding the angles favorable to it, you will then render your own spirit <note>original: "spiritum," referring to the "pneuma" or vital breath that connects the body and soul</note> ultimately lunar. This happens, of course, while the lunar stone itself, warmed by you, continually instills its lunar virtue into your own breath.

He records another small stone called Helioselenite <note>original: "heliofelinon," a composite of the Greek words for Sun (Helios) and Moon (Selene)</note>, which naturally possesses the image of the Sun and Moon joined together. Therefore, if anyone places this—encased in gilded silver—around their neck with similar threads when the Moon is in its own domicile or the Sun's <note>Leo is the Sun's domicile; Cancer is the Moon's.</note>, and meets with the Sun in the same minute and holds its proper angles, he will carry back a spirit that is simultaneously solar and lunar; or at least such a spirit as the Moon radiates when joined to the Sun at the center. Here indeed you see the scattered gifts of Phoebus <note>Phoebus is a title for the Sun god, Apollo.</note>, like the limbs of Osiris, being gathered by his sister Phoebe <note>Phoebe is a title for the Moon goddess, Diana or Artemis. This is a poetic metaphor: the Moon gathering the "scattered" light of the Sun, just as the goddess Isis gathered the severed limbs of her husband Osiris in Egyptian myth.</note> as if she were Isis. 

But if only we could easily find a solar or lunar stone somewhere that was so powerful in that same order! For we already have the magnet and iron under the sequence of the Northern Pole. They say, in fact, that a solar stone was found among the Indians by Apollonius of Tyana <note>A famous Greek Neopythagorean philosopher and reputed miracle-worker from the 1st century AD.</note>, specifically a stone named the Pantaura <term>Pantaura: A legendary "all-shining" stone described in ancient texts as having the power to attract other stones, much as a magnet attracts iron</term>, which glitters like fire. It is born four paces deep under the earth; a rare spirit presses upon it so that the earth swells and is often split open where a stone of this kind is conceived. Thus it draws other small stones to itself just as a magnet draws iron. 

But this Herculean stone <term>Herculean stone: A common historical name for the magnet or loadstone, supposedly named after the city of Heraclea</term> pulls us even more vehemently to contemplate it at present. We see in the sailors' lookout the indicator of the pole: a balanced needle, touched at its tip by a magnet, which moves toward the Great Bear <note>The constellation Ursa Major, used to find the North Star.</note> because the magnet draws it there. This is because the virtue of the Great Bear prevails in this stone, and from here it is transferred into the iron, and it draws both toward the Bear. Such a virtue was both infused from the beginning and is continually refreshed by the rays of the Bear. Perhaps amber <note>original: "succinum"</note> relates in the same way to the other pole and to straws. But tell me meanwhile: why does the magnet attract iron everywhere? Not because they are similar. Otherwise, a magnet [would attract] a magnet...

<summary>This page describes the magical and astrological uses of Selenite and Helioselenite stones for spiritual refinement, as well as the legendary Pantaura stone and the "Herculean" magnet's relationship to the North Star.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the hidden properties of materials, specifically focusing on the relationship between magnets and iron, and how celestial hierarchies (the "series" of planets) govern human behavior and attraction.</meta>

...the magnet would attract much more: and iron [attracts] iron. This is not because it is superior in the general order of physical bodies; indeed, a metal is normally considered superior to a small stone. What is the reason then? Both, in fact, are included in the celestial order that follows the Bear <note>original: "ursam". This refers to the constellations Ursa Major or Ursa Minor, which signify the celestial poles. Renaissance thinkers believed magnetic force was a "virtue" or power flowing from the heavens, specifically the North Pole.</note>. But the magnet holds a superior rank in that very "Bear-like" property, while iron holds an inferior one. 

In the same interconnected context of things, a superior thing attracts that which is inferior and turns it toward itself; or it agitates or affects it in whatever way it pleases by means of a power <note>original: "virtute", referring to an inherent spiritual or natural essence rather than moral virtue.</note> previously infused into it. The inferior thing, in turn, by that same infusion, is turned toward the superior, or is otherwise agitated, or is entirely affected by it. 

In this way, within the <term>Solar series</term><note>A "series" or "chain" is a Neoplatonic concept where various things on Earth—plants, stones, animals, and people—are grouped under the influence of a specific planet.</note> an inferior human admires a superior one. In the Jovian <note>Related to the planet Jupiter, associated with law, authority, and priesthood.</note> order, the inferior venerates the superior. In the Martian order, he fears him. In the Venerean order, the inferior is swept toward the superior by the fire of love and abandons his own self. In the Mercurial order, the inferior is always learning from or being persuaded by the superior. In the Lunar movement, the one is frequently moved by the other. In the Saturnian order, he finds rest. 

Now, when I had explored these matters to this extent, quite...

<summary>This page explains that magnetic attraction is a result of celestial hierarchies rather than material density, and compares this physical attraction to the various ways humans are drawn to one another under the influence of different planets.</summary>
<keywords>magnetism, celestial influence, Ursa Major, planetary series, hierarchy of being, Neoplatonism, sympathy</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
From here it entwines the Great Bear; from there the Lesser. There also stands near the Scorpion the **Serpent-Bearer** <term>Serpentarius: The constellation now commonly known as Ophiuchus, the Serpent-Bearer.</term> like a man encircled by a serpent: holding the head of the serpent in his right hand and the tail in his left; with knees as if bent, and his head slightly tilted back.

Indeed, I have read that the Magi advised the Persian King to engrave this image upon an **amethyst** stone, which he should enclose in a gold ring—but in such a way that he inserted the root of a "serpent-herb" <note>original: "serpentis radicem"; likely referring to the plant Dracunculus or similar herbs believed to have power over snakes.</note> between the small stone and the gold. For by wearing this ring, he would be safe against poison and poisonous diseases. This is provided, of course, that the Moon was looking favorably upon the Serpent-Bearer. **Peter of Abano** <note>Petrus Aponensis (1250–1316), an influential Italian physician, astrologer, and professor of medicine.</note> confirmed the use of this image. 

But for my part, if that ring possesses such power, I believe it claims it from heaven not so much through the figure as through materials of this kind, composed in this manner and at such a time. Remember that small stones born within animals—without the animals becoming ill from them—such as in the dragon, the rooster, the swallow, and others, are always effective, just like stones born in the earth; and they are referred to the same stars to which these animals belong. 

Hence the **Alectorius** <term>Alectorius: A "cock-stone," a mythical stone said to be found in the gizzards of old roosters, believed to possess solar powers.</term> taken from the stomach of an old rooster is powerful with solar virtue; concerning which **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD) whose work was the primary source for medicinal substances for 1,500 years.</note> says it has been found that a man who carries this stone in his mouth fights unconquered. He also says: the red **Celidonium** <term>Celidonium: A "swallow-stone," believed to be found in the stomachs of young swallows.</term> taken from a swallow cures **melancholy** <term>Melancholia: A condition of "black bile," associated with depression and heavy spirits.</term> and makes one lovable and fit. This indeed it receives from Jupiter, through what we have said: namely, that things everywhere below the Moon are "stellar" in nature.

This confirms that very **Platonic** saying: that the parts of this machine of the world are so connected that even in the lowest material things <note>original: "in retis," likely referring to the "meshes" or lowest strata of the physical world.</note> there are celestial things in a terrestrial condition; and in heaven, in turn, there are terrestrial things in celestial dignity; and in the hidden life of the world and the Mind that is the Queen of the world, celestial things are present, yet with a vital and intellectual property and excellence. 

Furthermore, by these means some also confirm that magical principle: namely, that through lower things that correspond to higher things, celestial influences can be drawn down to men at opportune times; and through the celestial, the "super-celestial" <note>spiritual or divine forces above the physical stars.</note> can be reconciled to us, or perhaps even infused. But let them [the magicians] see to this last point. Truly, it seems probable enough (as we have said) that this can be done by a certain art, gathering many things together correctly and opportunely—both for the reasons we assigned above and because many such things, when found with a physician...

<summary>This page discusses the creation of astrological talismans, specifically a ring featuring the Serpent-Bearer (Ophiuchus) for protection against poison, and the magical properties of stones found in animals. It concludes by explaining the Neoplatonic philosophy of "as above, so below," where terrestrial objects mirror celestial powers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion from the previous section regarding how celestial influences are "captured" in physical objects. Ficino is specifically addressing the difference between medicinal compounds (concoctions) and astrological talismans (images).</meta>

...by the Astrologer <note>original: *Astrologum*; referring to the practitioner of celestial medicine</note> are gathered, crushed, mixed, and cooked under a specific star. By the very nature of the cooking <term>concoction: the process of ripening or "digesting" materials through heat to change their properties</term> and fermentation, they gradually take on a new form. They acquire this through a certain celestial nourishment, with the rays then acting from within: and for this reason, the result is called "celestial." 

However, when a metal or a small stone is engraved in a single moment, it does not seem to receive a new quality, but only a new shape. Nor does that process proceed through the proper stages of "digestion" <note>Ficino uses digestive metaphors to describe the slow, natural ripening of medicinal qualities</note> which natural alteration and generation usually observe. Indeed, since celestial nature—as the rule of our lower nature—usually proceeds with a certain natural rhythm and favors those things that proceed in the same way, many people deservedly doubt that images of this kind possess any celestial power. 

I also often doubt it; and unless Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism</note> and all the astrologers thought they had wonderful power, I would deny they have it. Yet, I would not deny it entirely. For I believe (unless someone asserts otherwise) that they have at least some power for supporting good health, especially by reason of the materials chosen for them. Nevertheless, I believe much greater power exists in medicines <note>original: *pharmacis*</note> and ointments blended under a favorable star. 

What I meant just now by "by reason of the materials chosen," I will declare in what follows. Furthermore, those things which can be alleged in favor of images from the opinions of the Magi or astrologers—in order to interpret Plotinus <note>The Neoplatonist philosopher whom Ficino is translating and commenting upon</note>—I will state briefly hereafter. But I must first warn you here: do not think I approve of the use of images, but rather that I am recounting it. For I use medicines tempered by the heavens, not images. This is the advice I give to others daily. 

But if you concede that God has implanted wonderful virtues in things below the Moon, you must concede even more wonderful ones to the celestial things. Furthermore, if you judge it lawful for a human to use inferior things <note>Earthly plants and minerals</note> for the sake of good health, you must judge it also lawful to use the superior things. And it is lawful to repair inferior things according to the pattern of superior things through the physician's skill, just as they were originally prepared by God from the beginning.

## Chapter 15. On the power of the heavens. On the power of rays, whence images can obtain power.

## Chapter 16.

### The immense magnitude of the celestial form, its virtue, and motion, create...
->ii<-

<summary>Ficino expresses skepticism regarding the efficacy of engraved astrological talismans compared to medicinal "concoctions," but argues that using celestial influences for health is a legitimate extension of God's natural laws. He emphasizes that he personally recommends celestial medicines over talismans while preparing to explain Plotinus's views on the subject.</summary>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding how celestial influences—the "rays" of stars—penetrate physical matter to imbue objects with specific powers.</meta>

So that all the rays of every star <note>original: "fyderum"</note> penetrate the mass of the earth—which is like a tiny point compared to the vastness of the heavens—instantly and with the greatest ease, reaching the very center of the earth’s roof; a fact that all astronomers acknowledge. There, as the followers of Pythagoras and Plato believe, these rays are at their strongest: both because they strike the center directly from every direction, and because they are all gathered together into such a narrow space. 

The intensity of these rays completely ignites the dry matter of the earth found there, far from any moisture. Once kindled, these rays are scattered and travel through every passage, breathing out into the deep channels of the earth as both heat and sulfur. But they believe this fire is deeply murky <note>original: "caliginosum"</note> and like a blaze that is <term>devoid of light</term> <note>original: "luminis expers"</note>; just as in the heavens there exists a light that is devoid of burning heat. Fire, however, which exists between the heavenly and the subterranean, joins light together with heat. 

They believe that the fire blowing from the center is the "Vestal" fire; for they once thought that Vesta was the very life of the earth. <note>Vesta was the Roman goddess of the hearth; the author is connecting the earth's molten core to the sacred eternal flame kept in her temples.</note> For this reason, the ancients used to build the Temple of Vesta in the center of their cities, placing a perpetual fire in the middle. 

But let us not wander <note>original: "pervagemur"</note> too far. Let us conclude now: if the rays of the stars penetrate the whole earth, it cannot easily be denied that they also penetrate metal and stone <note>original: "lapillum," referring to small stones or gems</note>. When they are struck by <insert>celestial patterns</insert>, the rays suddenly penetrate them and impress upon them certain gifts, or at least some kind of qualities. After all, they even generate the most precious things in the "bellies" or depths of the earth. 

Who could deny that the rays pass through these things? Even the air, along with its qualities and sound—which are far less powerful—passes through solid objects suddenly and affects them with its own nature. Indeed, if hardness could resist these penetrating rays, then light would pass through the air much faster than it passes through water. And it would pass through water much faster than glass, and through glass much faster than crystal. Yet, because light strikes through <note>original: "transverberet"</note> all solid things in the same moment it passes through liquids, it is clearly established that hardness in no way resists these rays. 

Therefore, one must say that it cannot be denied that metals receive the rays and influences of the heavens. They even preserve these influences for a time destined for them by the heavens. They preserve, I say, a certain "virtue" <note>In this context, "virtue" means an innate power or magical property.</note> created by the contact of these converging rays. Consider this: if a harder material seems to resist a powerful force, it actually exposes itself more to the "blows" of that force. Thus, a sword cuts through wood hidden under wool, yet the wool itself is not cut. Similarly, a flash of lightning sometimes melts the metal inside a leather pouch without damaging the leather itself. Since the nature of the heavens is incomparably finer than this fire of ours...

The author uses the example of lightning melting a sword inside a scabbard to prove that celestial forces can affect solid objects without harming the softer materials surrounding them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of celestial influences, specifically focusing on how the "rays" of stars transmit occult powers into physical objects and images. The author is Marsilio Ficino, a central figure of the Italian Renaissance, likely writing from his work "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens."</meta>

It should not be thought that the function of a celestial ray is only as more excellent as the work of a fire ray which we see clearly—namely, to illuminate, to heat, to dry, to penetrate, to thin out, and to dissolve—things which are very well known to our senses. Instead, they possess many more and far more wonderful powers and effects. Otherwise, inferior matter and fleeting sensation would be placed on a level completely equal with the divinity of heaven.

But who is so ignorant as to not know that the hidden virtues of things, which doctors call "special properties" <note>original: "speciales"; these are qualities that cannot be explained by the four elements (hot, cold, wet, dry) alone.</note>, are produced not by an elemental nature, but by a celestial one? Therefore, as they say, rays can impress hidden and wonderful powers—beyond those already known—into images <note>In this context, "images" refers to astrological talismans or figures carved under specific planetary alignments.</note>, just as they flow into all other things. 

For these rays are not inanimate, like the rays of a lamp, but are living and sensitive, flashing out just as they do through the eyes of living bodies. They carry wondrous gifts with them from the imaginations and minds of the celestial beings. They also possess a most powerful force derived from the strong affection of those beings and the very rapid motion of the heavenly bodies; they act properly and most especially upon the **spirit** <note>The "spirit" was believed to be a thin, vaporous substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note>, which is most similar to the celestial rays. 

Furthermore, they act even upon the hardest bodies; for all such things are very weak compared to the power of heaven. There are, however, various powers in various stars, and therefore their rays vary among themselves. Moreover, in the strikes of rays falling and reflecting in various ways, diverse virtues are born. Finally, in the mutual meeting of rays, in different ways—here or there, now or at another time—diverse effects are produced, much more greatly and more quickly than in other mixtures of elements, qualities, and elemental substances. 

This happens much more quickly even than in the tones and numbers of musicians sounding together in different ways. If you consider these things diligently, perhaps you will not doubt it; it is said that by a certain sudden casting of rays, powers are impressed upon images, and diverse powers arise from diverse castings. 

Is it then so quick? I marvel at fascinations <note>The "evil eye" or "fascination" was a standard Renaissance explanation for how one person could influence another through a look.</note> produced by a certain sudden gaze, and the most sharp loves kindled immediately by the rays of the eyes—which are themselves a kind of fascination, as we prove in our *Book on Love* <note>original: "libro de amore"; This refers to Ficino's famous "Commentary on Plato's Symposium."</note>. And I marvel how quickly a bloodshot eye infects others by its gaze, and how a menstruating woman infects a mirror by looking at it. 

Is it not also reported that certain families among the Illyrians and Triballians <note>original: "illincos & mballos"; classical tribes noted in ancient histories for having the power of the evil eye.</note>, when angry, killed men with a single look? And that certain women in Scythia were accustomed to do the same? And the Catoblepas and the Basilisk <note>original: "regulis"; literally "little king," the traditional name for the Basilisk serpent.</note>, serpents that kill by the rays from their eyes...

...with a strike, they kill men. Moreover, the tarantula <note>original: "Phalagia," a spider found in the Apulia region of Italy</note> in Apulia, with its strike—or even its hidden influence—suddenly transforms one's spirit and mind into a state of stupor. What does a rabid dog do, even when the bite is not visible? What about the butcher’s broom <term>scopa: likely *Ruscus aculeatus*, used in traditional medicine</term>, and the strawberry tree? Do they not stir up poison and madness with the lightest touch? Indeed, a pregnant woman, by a touch, immediately marks the limb of the child about to be born with a sign of the thing she desired. Or will you doubt that rays, touching in one way or another, produce different effects? Even you, when gathering the hellebore herb, whether you pull the leaf downward or upward, by this sudden touch you cause the hellebore to draw out humors either downward or upward <note>A common ancient belief that the direction a medicinal plant was harvested determined the direction of its effect on the body</note>. At the beginning of any thing being generated, do not the celestial influences—once the "cooking" <term>concoctione: the natural process of ripening or maturing matter through heat and time</term> and digestion of the matter is complete—bestow miraculous gifts <note>original: "dores," likely a typo for *dotes*, meaning gifts or qualities</note> in a single moment of time? Does it not happen that, when the face of heaven is favorable, countless frogs and similar living creatures often leap forth from the sands in an instant? So great is the preparing power of the heavens upon matter, and so great is its speed. Finally, if fire has the quality of doing in a very short time what other things do over a long time—chiefly because it is most similar to the heavens—why would you doubt that the heavens can accomplish great things in a single moment <note>original: "momensum," likely *momentum*</note>, even in matter that is less prepared, just as a larger flame is wont to do? "Why then do you doubt," they ask, "that the heavens act in almost the same way when an image <note>The author is referring to the creation of astrological talismans or "images" meant to capture celestial power</note> is being constructed?" You will say, I suppose—just as I used to say—that the natural steps of alteration are missing here. Certainly, this defect diminishes the celestial gift, yet it does not seem to take it away entirely. For the natural philosophers <term>Physici: those who study the laws of nature and the physical world</term> do not want an image to be fabricated from just any metal or stone, but from a specific one. In such a material, a celestial power has already naturally begun <note>original: "icohauerit," for *inchoaverit*</note> the very quality that is desired, and has almost perfected it—just as flame is potentially present in sulfur. It is this power which the heavens finally perfect when this matter is violently stirred by art under a similar celestial influence, and through that stirring, it grows warm. Therefore, art [receives]...

...an incipient virtue there: and while the craftsman reduces it to a form similar to a certain celestial figure of its own, he then fully exposes it to its own "Idea" <note>In Platonic philosophy, an "Idea" is the perfect, eternal archetype of a thing in the divine mind.</note> there. Serapio <note>Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th-century Arabic medical writer.</note> writes that a similar virtue was given to the stone *Albuge di* <note>original: "Albuge di," likely a reference to a stone mentioned in Arabic lapidaries, possibly a variety of agate or jasper.</note>, which is similar to a hyacinth <note>In historical texts, "hyacinth" usually refers to a blue or yellow-red zircon or sapphire, not the flower.</note>. However, that power is not so strong that it can be "scraped off" or transferred to other materials for use. But as soon as it receives the figure of that heavenly sign under the influence of the celestial Scorpio, it is thought to suddenly carry back a perfected power against scorpions. This power is such that it can then be communicated to mastic <note>A resin obtained from the mastic tree.</note> or incense. 

The same reasoning must be applied to the hyacinth, topaz, emerald, and others: the crafting of figures <note>referring to the engraving of astrological symbols or images onto gemstones.</note> has no efficacy unless the material agrees with the star and its effect—the very star from which the craftsman desires to receive power. And furthermore, this is only effective where the material is already, by its very nature, the kind of thing you intend to make it through the figure. Therefore, the authorities advise using no materials for these images except those which you know already possess nearly the same power you desire. 

For this reason, they command that the powers of small stones and metals be investigated most diligently. Meanwhile, remember that among the stones, the carbuncle <note>A historical term for deep red gems, usually garnets or rubies.</note> that flashes in the dark, and the *pantarbe* <note>original: "panrarum," likely referring to the mythical "pantarbe" stone said to attract other stones.</note> are subject especially to the Sun; the Sapphire to Jupiter; the Emerald to Venus; [mercury?] to Mercury; and [pearls?] to the Moon. Furthermore, metals besides gold and silver have hardly any virtue for these purposes. You will find a safer method in these matters if you relate pure gold to the Sun and to Jupiter. It relates to the Sun because of its color, and to Jupiter because of its balanced composition. For nothing is more balanced than Jupiter and gold. Pure silver, however, relates to the Moon; but gold mixed with silver belongs to Jupiter and Venus together. Furthermore, an image is more effective...

### What power figures have in heaven and under heaven. Chapter 17.

But lest you perhaps lose faith in figures, it will be helpful to remember that in this <term>elemental region under the moon</term> <note>The "sublunary" sphere, which in ancient cosmology refers to the Earth and its atmosphere, where everything is composed of the four elements and is subject to change</note>, the elements and their qualities carry the most weight in transformations—that is, when something is tending toward an elemental state. These qualities are heat, cold, moisture, and dryness.

However, those qualities that are less elemental or material—specifically lights (meaning colors), and similarly numbers and figures—are less capable of forming such physical things. Yet, they are thought to be extremely powerful concerning celestial gifts. For in the heavens, lights, numbers, and figures are the most powerful of all things, especially if there is no physical matter there, as most <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy, who taught that the heavens were made of a fifth element, or "aether," rather than the four heavy elements found on Earth</note> believe. 

Because of this, figures, numbers, and rays—since they are not supported by any other matter there—seem to possess an almost substantial essence. And since mathematical forms precede physical forms in the order of things (being simpler and less dependent), they rightly claim the highest authority in the preceding levels of the universe—that is, the celestial spheres. Thus, no less is accomplished there by number, figure, and light than by some elemental property. A sign of this power is found even below the Moon. For highly material qualities are common to many different species of things; even when these qualities are altered in some way, the species do not always change...

<meta>This page continues the argument for the efficacy of celestial influences, moving from the general properties of matter to the specific powers of mathematical proportions, light, and "celestial faces" (planetary configurations). ...protection. Moreover, the shapes and numbers of natural parts possess a property that is inseparable and peculiar to their species. This is because they were destined from the heavens <note>original: "çelirus," a likely OCR error for "caelitus," meaning from the sky or divinely</note> together with those species. Indeed, they have the greatest connection with the **Ideas** <note>In Platonic philosophy, Ideas are the eternal, perfect forms of which all earthly things are mere shadows.</note> in the queen-mind of the world. And since these species and numbers are themselves designated there by their own Ideas, they surely derive their own specific powers from that source. 

Therefore, the species of natural things are bound to certain shapes, and their motions, generations, and changes are bound to certain numbers. What shall I say of light? For light is the very act or image of the intellect. Colors, moreover, are a kind of light. Wherefore, when **Astrologers** say that lights (that is, colors), shapes, and numbers have great power in preparing our materials to receive celestial influence, you should not (as they say) rashly deny these things. 

You are not unaware that musical harmonies, through their numbers and proportions, have a wonderful power to steady, move, or affect the **spirit** <term>spiritus: a subtle, vaporous substance in Renaissance medicine that connects the physical body to the immaterial soul</term>, the soul, and the body. Proportions constituted from numbers are like certain shapes, as if they were constructed from points and lines—but in motion. Similarly, the **celestial figures** <note>The "figures" or configurations of the stars and planets in a horoscope.</note> behave in their own motion toward action. For these, through both their harmonic rays and their motions that penetrate all things, affect the spirit daily in secret, just as many other things are known to affect us openly. 

You know, moreover, how easily the figure of a mourner moves many people to pity. And how much the figure of a lovely person immediately affects and moves the eyes, the imagination, the spirit, and the **humors** <term>the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) believed to determine health and temperament</term>. The celestial figure is no less alive or effective. Does not the merciful and sweet <note>original: "demens & iſanis," likely an OCR error for "clemens et suavis"</note> face of a prince in the city gladden everyone? While a fierce or sad face suddenly terrifies? 

What then do you think the faces of the celestials—the masters of all earthly things—can do in comparison to these? Especially since even parents coming together to conceive offspring often tend to imprint upon the children to be born long afterward not only the faces they themselves wear at that time, but even the faces they imagine. By the same reasoning, celestial faces [bestow/contribute] to earthly materials...

...[the stars] infect things with their marks. If they seem to hide within these things for a long time, they emerge in their own proper time. 

Now, the "looks" <note>original: "vultus"</note> of the heaven are celestial figures. You can call the "faces" <note>original: "facies"; in astrology, these are 10-degree subdivisions of the zodiac</note> there the figures that are more stable than the rest; but "looks" are those figures that change more frequently. You can similarly call the aspects <term>aspects</term><note>The specific angular distances between planets, such as 60 or 90 degrees, believed to transmit different types of energy.</note> formed between the stars by their daily motion "looks" and "figures." Suppose someone says: "Let it be granted that celestial figures are very powerful in their effects. But what does this have to do with the figures of images made by human artifice?" 

The philosophers will respond: they do not claim that our man-made figures are powerful enough to act on their own, but rather that they are most prepared to receive the actions and powers of the celestial figures, insofar as they become opportune for those dominant celestial bodies and are modeled precisely <note>original: "examussim," meaning "by the mason's rule" or with extreme accuracy</note> after them. For that celestial figure demands an earthly figure. 

When a **lute** <note>original: "cithara"; a stringed instrument like a lyre or lute used here as a metaphor for resonance</note> sounds, does not another echo? This happens only if the second one has a similar shape and is placed within view, and if its strings are positioned and tuned <note>original: "intentæ"</note> in the same way. What else causes the lute to suddenly resonate with the other lute, if not some specific placement and a certain conforming shape? 

The shape of a smooth, concave, and shining mirror is congruent with the heaven. For this very reason, it receives such a great gift from above that it gathers the rays of **Phoebus** <note>The sun god, Apollo</note> within itself most fully. And it suddenly burns any solid object placed in its focus. Therefore, do not hesitate to say that a certain material chosen for making an image—which is otherwise very congruent with the heaven—might, through a celestial shape given to it by art, conceive a celestial power in reality and pass it on to someone nearby or to the person wearing it. 

Not only figure, however, but also a "pervious" or passable disposition—which they call **the Diaphanous** <term>Diaphanam</term><note>A term from Aristotelian physics referring to the quality of transparency that allows light to pass through a medium.</note>—is something ineffective and passive by its own nature. Nevertheless, since a pervious disposition in the heaven is the proper receptacle for light, wherever beneath the heaven this is either natural or...

->### What kind of celestial figures the ancients used to impress on images.<-

->### On the use of images<-
->### Chapter 18<-

Someone might ask which celestial figures are most commonly impressed upon these images. There are, in fact, forms in the heavens that are very conspicuous to the eye and recognized by many—such as the way Aries, Taurus, and similar figures of the zodiac are depicted, as well as those clearly visible outside the zodiac. Beyond these, there are many other forms there, not so much visible as they are imaginable, devised or at least observed by the Indians, Egyptians, and Chaldeans <note>The "Chaldeans" refers to ancient Mesopotamian observers famous for their astrological precision.</note> through the "faces" <term>faces|Also known as decans, these are 10-degree subdivisions of a zodiac sign (three per sign), each associated with a specific symbolic image.</term> of the signs. For example, in the first face of Virgo, there is a beautiful virgin sitting, holding two ears of grain in her hand and nursing a boy. There are others of this kind, some of them quite excellent, as described by Albumasar <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a Persian astrologer whose writings were the primary source for astrological theory in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>. Finally, there are certain symbols <note>original: "characteres"</note> of the signs and planets designated by the Egyptians. These practitioners intend for all these things to be engraved on the images. For instance, if someone expects a specific benefit from Mercury, they should place him in Virgo—or at least place the Moon there while it is in "aspect" <note>An "aspect" is a specific geometric angle between two planets that astrologers believe allows them to influence one another.</note> with Mercury. The image should then be made of tin or silver, and it should feature the entire sign of Virgo, along with its symbol and the symbol of Mercury. And if the first face of Virgo...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the construction of astrological images or talismans, specifically focusing on their physical shape and the transition from pagan astrological theory to Christian interpretation.</meta>

...of use; you should also add the figure which we said on the first surface <note>original: "prima facie"; this refers to the initial appearance or the first plane of a three-dimensional object</note> was observed. And similarly for the rest. Recent authors of images <note>In this context, "images" refers to <term>astrological talismans</term>, which were objects engraved with symbols to capture planetary power</note> indeed hold that their universal form should be round, in the likeness of the heavens. 

But the ancients—as we read in a certain college of the Arabs <note>original: "Atabum," likely a transcription error for "Arabum" (Arabs), who were historically regarded as authorities on talismanic magic</note>—placed the figure of the cross before all others. They did so because bodies act through their power upon a surface that is already spread out. Indeed, the first surface is described by a cross; for in this way, it primarily possesses both length and width. This is the first figure, the straightest of all, and the greatest, containing four right angles. 

The effects of the celestial bodies shine forth most greatly through the straightness of rays and angles. For the stars are most powerful when they occupy the four angles of the sky—or rather the <term>pivots</term> <note>original: "cardines"; the four most important points in an astrological chart: the East (Ascendant), West (Descendant), Mid-heaven, and the opposite point below</note>—namely, the East and the West, and the mid-points of the sky on either side. When disposed in this way, they cast their rays into each other so that they form a cross. 

Therefore, the ancients said that the cross was a figure formed both by the power <note>original: "fortitudine" (strength or power)</note> of the stars and as a receptacle for that same power. For this reason, it possesses the highest authority in talismans and receives the forces and spirits of the planets. 

This opinion was either introduced or greatly confirmed by the Egyptians. Among their sacred characters, the cross was a sign signifying "the life to come" according to their custom; they used to carve that figure on the breast of Serapis <note>Serapis was a Greco-Egyptian deity. The "cross" mentioned here is likely the <term>Ankh</term>, the Egyptian symbol for life, which early Christians and medieval scholars often identified with the Cross of Christ</note>. 

However, I believe that the excellence of the cross among the Egyptians before Christ was not so much a testimony to the gift of the stars as it was a prophecy of the power it was destined to receive from Christ. On the other hand, the astrologers who lived immediately after Christ—seeing the wonders performed by Christians through the cross, but being ignorant or unwilling to attribute such great things to Jesus—referred these powers back to the heavens. Yet they ought to have considered that miracles are not performed by the cross itself without the name of Jesus. Indeed, it is suitable for talismans because it reflects the power of the planets...

...and of all the stars it is perhaps probable. Yet, it does not possess great power for this reason alone. But let us return to relating the opinions of others, as we have started to do.

The ancients used to create an image of Saturn <note>Saturn was traditionally associated with old age, time, and endurance</note> for longevity in a stone of *Feyrizech* <note>original: "de Feyrizech"; likely a Persian term for turquoise, though the text here equates it with sapphire</note>—that is, a sapphire—during the hour of Saturn, while he was ascending and in a favorable astrological position. The form was: an old man sitting upon a high throne or a dragon; his head was covered with a certain linen cloth, and he raised his hands above his head, holding a sickle in his hand, or perhaps fish, and he was dressed in a dark garment.

For a long and happy life, they made an image of Jupiter <note>Jupiter, or Jove, was considered the "Great Benefic" planet, bringing growth and luck</note> in a clear or white stone. It was a man sitting upon an eagle or a dragon, crowned, created during the hour of Jupiter when he was fortunately ascending in his exaltation <note>An "exaltation" is the specific sign of the zodiac where a planet's influence is most powerful</note>, and he was dressed in a saffron-yellow garment.

To counter fearfulness, they crafted images during the hour of Mars <note>Mars governed courage, war, and vital energy</note> when the first face <note>A "face" or decan is a ten-degree subdivision of a zodiac sign</note> of Scorpio was rising. It depicted Mars armed and crowned.

To cure diseases, they fashioned an image of the Sun <note>The Sun was viewed as the source of vital heat and restorative health</note> in gold during the hour of the Sun, with the first face of Leo ascending with the Sun. It showed a king on a throne in a saffron garment, along with a crow and the shape of the Sun itself.

For joy and bodily strength, they made an image of Venus <note>Venus governed pleasure, beauty, and physical harmony</note> as a young girl holding apples and flowers in her hand, dressed in saffron and white, during the hour of Venus when the first face of Libra, Pisces, or Taurus was ascending with Venus.

They made an image of Mercury <note>Mercury ruled the intellect, memory, and communication</note> for wit and memory during the first face of Gemini. Likewise, Mercury was carved to ward off fevers: a man holding a spear in his hand, during the hour of Mercury while Mercury was rising. They carved this in red marble or purple marble; it was considered the best for the sick to take up. From this, they said all types of fevers could be cured.

An image of the Moon <note>The Moon governed growth, moisture, and cycles of "increase"</note> [was made] when the first face of Cancer was ascending for the purpose of increase.

He is winged, sometimes upon a peacock, holding a staff in his right hand, and wearing a garment of many colors.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological medicine, specifically the timing and creation of celestial images (talismans) for healing various ailments, following previous mentions of planetary influences on the body.</meta>

For kidney stones and pains of the genitals, and to stanch bleeding, they created an image in the hour of Saturn, when the third face <note>original: "facie"; a ten-degree division of a zodiac sign, also known as a decan</note> of Aquarius is rising along with Saturn. Likewise, they would engrave a lion in gold, rolling a stone in the shape of the Sun with its feet, during the hour of the Sun, with the first degree of the second face of Leo rising. They believed this would be beneficial for driving away diseases.

For kidney diseases, they made a similar image when the Sun in the "Heart of the Lion" <note>the star Regulus</note> occupied the Midheaven <note>original: "cęlum medium"; the highest point in the sky at a given time</note>. This was verified by Peter of Abano <note>original: "Petro Aponensi"; a famous 13th-century physician and astrologer</note> and confirmed by experience—but on this condition: that Jupiter or Venus aspects the Midheaven, while the harmful planets <note>Saturn and Mars</note> "fall" <note>occupy weak positions in the chart</note> and are made unfortunate. It is accepted from the distinguished physician Mengus that such an image, made with Jupiter joined there with the Sun, freed Giovanni Marliani <note>original: "Iohannem Marlianum"; a celebrated 15th-century mathematician and physician</note>—a singular mathematician of our age—from the terror he used to feel during thunder.

Furthermore, to strengthen health and avoid sorcery <note>original: "ueneficia"; can also mean poisoning or harmful magic</note>, they fashioned an image from silver in the hour of Venus, while the Moon occupied the "angles" <note>the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses, considered the strongest points of a chart</note> and viewed Venus favorably. This was provided that the lord of the <term>sixth house</term> <note>The house in astrology associated with illness and physical infirmity</note> aspects Venus, or that Jupiter aspects it with a trine <note>a harmonious 120-degree angle</note> or an opposition. Let Mercury not be "unfortunate" <note>weakened by sign or position</note>. They performed these tasks in the last hour of the day of the Sun <note>Sunday</note>, so that the lord of the hour held the tenth region of the sky <note>the Midheaven</note>.

Peter of Abano says that a physician can cure a sick person through an image, provided that in its manufacture he observes that the angles—the Ascendant, the Midheaven, and the Western angle—are fortunate, as well as the lord of the Ascendant. By the same reasoning, the second house should be fortunate, but the sixth house and its lord should be unfortunate <note>to weaken the power of the disease</note>. He also says that health will be firmer and more lasting if the image is made after examining the <term>nativity</term> <note>the birth chart of the patient</note>, in which these significators are placed fortunately: namely, the "Givers of Life" <note>original: "uirę datores"; specific points in a chart believed to determine lifespan</note>, both the signs and their lords, especially the Ascendant and its lord. Also the Midheaven, the position of the Sun, the Part of Fortune, and the lord of the conjunction or "prevention" <note>original: "pręuentionis"; the New or Full Moon occurring immediately before birth</note> that happened before the birth. Let the evil planets also fall and be unfortunate. He concludes that no astrologer doubts that such things contribute to the lengthening of life.

It would be tedious to describe which faces <note>decans</note> the ancients used through every sign, and which stations of the Moon <note>lunar mansions</note> they considered necessary in crafting images...

...they observed [these timings]. For in the station of the Moon from the seventeenth degree of Virgo <note>original: "uirginis"</note> to its end, they made images against diseases and hatreds, and for a successful journey. In the station from the beginning of Capricorn to the twelfth degree, [they made images] against diseases, discords, and imprisonment. In the station from the twelfth degree of Capricorn to the twenty-fifth degree, [they worked] against weakness and prison. And in other [stations] similarly for other purposes; they frequently contrived to consult vain things. 

Indeed, I have recounted only those which suggest the physician rather than the mage. For I suspect that medicine of this sort is for the most part vain. However, for other more legitimate medicinal preparations, I judge that such mansions of the Moon should be selected. 

And also [select] the sixth degree in Aries <note>original: "ariete"</note>; again the nineteenth degree and twenty-six minutes. Likewise, the tenth degree and fifty-one minutes in Gemini. The nineteenth degree and twenty-six minutes in Cancer. The sixth degree and thirty-four minutes in Libra. The nineteenth degree and twenty-six minutes in Capricorn. The second degree and seventeen minutes in Aquarius. In the same [sign], the fifteenth degree and eight minutes.

So that you may direct the Moon there, toward that very gift proper for receiving medicines. 

There, I say—that is, to the sign, and the <term>face</term> <note>A "face" or decan is a subdivision of a zodiac sign, each spanning ten degrees and ruled by a specific planet.</note>, and especially the degree—so that if you seek the benefits of Jupiter <note>original: "Iouis"</note>, you should raise the Moon directed toward these points, or joined with them, as long as the Sun illuminates a place of this sort where the <term>Jovian property</term> <note>Attributes associated with the planet Jupiter, such as growth, health, and benevolence.</note> flourishes. And similarly for the others. 

It would be curious to relate—and perhaps harmful—what images they fashioned, and in what manner, to join or separate souls from one another, in order to bring about happiness or inflict calamity, whether upon a single person, a house, or a city. For my part, I do not affirm that such things are possible. But the astrologers think they are possible and teach the methods; such things I do not dare to recount. Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist scholar and student of Plotinus.</note>, where he describes the life of his teacher Plotinus <note>The founder of Neoplatonism, who was said to possess spiritual powers.</note>, confirms that such things can happen. <meta>Continuing the discussion on the power of celestial influences and the legitimacy of astrological talismans, citing historical and theological authorities.</meta>

...that such things were attempted against Plotinus <note>original: "Plotinū"; referring to the Neoplatonist philosopher Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD)</note> in Rome: when someone tried to "star-strike" <note>to bewitch or affect someone through astrological magic</note> Plotinus using images or similar objects. But the attempt was turned back upon its author because of Plotinus’s lofty soul. Albert the Great <note>original: "Albertus Magnus"; a famous 13th-century polymath and theologian</note> also, a professor of both astrology and theology, says in his *Mirror* <note>original: "i ſpeculo"; referring to the *Speculum Astronomiae*, a medieval guide to lawful and unlawful books of astrology</note>—where he follows the path of discerning the lawful from the unlawful—that images properly constructed by astrologers acquire power and efficacy from the celestial figure <note>the specific arrangement of stars and planets at a given moment</note>. And immediately thereafter he recounts their wonderful effects, such as Thebit Ben-Thorat <note>Thabit ibn Qurra, a 9th-century mathematician and astronomer</note> and Ptolemy and other astrologers promise. He even describes images for bringing calamity or prosperity to someone, which I choose to omit for the sake of prudence. 

And meanwhile, he confirms that they can indeed have an effect. Although as a good man he condemns the abuse of the art, and as a legitimate theologian he detests the prayers and incense-burnings which certain impious people have used with the making of images to summon **demons**. Yet he does not reject the shapes, letters, and words impressed upon images for this purpose alone: that they may receive some gift from the celestial figure. That this can indeed be obtained through images, Peter of Abano <note>original: "Petrus Aponēſis"; a 13th-century physician and astrologer</note> confirmed. Indeed, he even affirmed that some region or other was destroyed by that image which Thebit relates was fashioned by the astrologer Phedix. 

But Thomas Aquinas <note>original: "Thommas Aqnas"</note>, our leader in theology, fears these things more and attributes less to images. For he thinks they acquire only so much power from heaven through their shapes as is conducive to those effects which the heavens usually produce through herbs and other natural things. This is not so much because such a shape exists in that material, but because such a composition is already placed in a certain artificial species <note>a specific arrangement or "square" in the astrological sense</note> that harmonizes with heaven. He says these things in the third book of *Against the Gentiles* <note>original: "contra gentiles"; Aquinas's *Summa contra Gentiles*</note>. In that work, he scoffs at the **characters** <term>sigils or magical symbols</term> and letters added to the shapes. But he does not scoff at the shapes themselves, unless they are added as certain signs to summon **demons**. In his books *On Fate* he also says: 

The constellations give the order of coming into being and of lasting, not only to natural things but also to artificial ones; therefore, images are fashioned under certain constellations. And as for that wonder which happens to us through them—beyond the usual course of natural effects—he casts that upon the **demons**, the seducers of men. This is clearly evident in the book *Against the Gentiles*. Especially...
