<meta>This page continues a discussion on the efficacy of astrological images and talismans, likely from a work by a Renaissance Neoplatonist such as Marsilio Ficino or a contemporary. It addresses the skepticism toward "figures" (carved images) in favor of the inherent "natural virtues" of substances like herbs and metals.</meta>

...indeed, in the little book *Concerning the Hidden Riches of Nature* <note>original: "de occultis naturæ opibus"</note>. There you see that he even thinks little of the images themselves, no matter how they are made. I too, following his lead, consider them of no value. However, to attribute certain miraculous effects of images to the deception of **demons** <term>demons: In this context, "daimones" refers to mediating spirits, which the author warns can be deceptive if approached without holy intent</term> is not foreign to the Platonists. For even **Iamblichus** <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Syrian Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy and rituals</note> says that those who, having neglected the highest religion and holiness, trust only in images and expect divine gifts from them, are very often deceived by evil spirits who encounter them under the pretext of being good deities.

Nevertheless, he does not deny that certain natural benefits may occur from images constructed by the legitimate reasoning of astrology. Finally, I judge it to be safer to commit oneself to medicines than to images. The reasons given by us regarding celestial power—assigned through images—can have more efficacy in medicines rather than in mere figures. For it is probable that if images have any force, they do not acquire it so much through a newly made shape <note>figure</note> as they possess it naturally through the material being so affected. 

And if anything is newly acquired while they are being engraved, it is obtained not so much through the shape as through the heating that arises from a certain friction <note>original: "confusione," here implying the heat generated during the process of carving or rubbing</note>. Indeed, this friction and heating—performed under a celestial harmony similar to the harmony that had infused the power <note>virtue</note> into certain materials—excites and strengthens that power itself, just as a breath of air fans a flame, making manifest what was previously hidden. 

Just as the heat of a fire brings into view letters written in onion juice that were previously hidden. And letters inscribed in fat, completely hidden upon a stone, emerge if the stone is submerged in vinegar, standing out as if they were engraved. Indeed, just as the touch of a broom or a shrub stirs up a sleeping frenzy, so perhaps a certain friction and heating alone brings forth the virtue latent in the material, provided it is done at the opportune time. It is certainly beneficial to use this celestial opportunity in preparing medicines; and if one wishes to treat metals and stones, it is better to strike and heat them alone rather than to shape them into figures. 

For besides the fact that I suspect figures are vain things, we should not rashly admit even a shadow of **idolatry**. Likewise, one should not rashly use stars—even those considered beneficial—to expel diseases very similar to them, for they often increase these ailments. Conversely, harmful stars sometimes diminish diseases unlike themselves; **Ptolemy** and **Haly** <note>Referring to Claudius Ptolemy and Haly Abenragel, two of the most influential authorities on astrology in the medieval and Renaissance periods</note> clearly teach these things.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological talismans and images, specifically focusing on the "Image of the World" (Figura Mundi). It transitions from general celestial influences to the specific timing and materials required for a universal talisman.</meta>

### On the Construction of the Figure of the Universe, Chapter 18

And why should we pass over the universal image—that is, the image of the universe itself? From this, indeed, they seem to hope for benefit from the cosmos. A follower of those <note>Likely referring to the Neoplatonists or Hermetic philosophers</note> will perhaps carve an archetypal form of the entire world, if he so chooses, in bronze. Then, at the opportune time, he may stamp it onto a gilded silver plate. 

But when is the best time to stamp it? When the Sun reaches the first minute of Aries. <note>The "first minute of Aries" marks the Spring Equinox, traditionally considered the moment of the world's creation in ancient and Renaissance astrology.</note> From here, astrologers—as if from the revolution of its own birthday—forecast the fortune of the world, at least for that impending year. In this very "birth" of the world, therefore, he will stamp the figure of the whole world. 

But do you see how beautifully the argument occurs to us that the world was once born? Since it is reborn every year: in a person's very horoscope <note>original: "genesi"</note>, do not astrologers measure first in which sign, at which degree, and at which minute the Sun was found? They lay the foundation of the whole figure there. And in every year thereafter, as soon as the Sun enters the same minute, they judge the person to be as if reborn, and from that, they predict the year's fortune. 

Just as they could not do this for a human unless he were as if reborn, and he could not be as if reborn unless he had once been born; so too it is possible to conclude that the world was once born—specifically when the Sun was placed at the first minute of Aries. Then, through that same point every year, a certain fate of the world, as if reborn, revolves. 

At that time, therefore, he shall construct the figure of the world. He will take care, however, not to carve or stamp the figure on Saturday, the day of Saturn. For on this day, God, the maker of the world <note>original: "faber"</note>, is said to have rested from his work. This work he had begun on the ideal day of the Sun. For as much as the Sun is suited for generation, so much is Saturn unsuitable <note>In Renaissance astrology, Saturn is the "Greater Malefic," associated with coldness, decay, and endings, whereas the Sun is the source of life.</note>. He completed the work on the day of Venus, which signifies the absolute beauty of the work. 

But regarding the reasons for the world's construction, I shall say nothing further; for our friend Giovanni Pico della Mirandola <note>A brilliant Italian Renaissance philosopher and contemporary of the author, Marsilio Ficino</note> has recently expressed the divine mysteries of Moses <note>original: "Mosos"</note> concerning the world's creation <note>original: "genesi mundi"; this refers to Pico's work, the *Heptaplus*, a seven-fold interpretation of the six days of Genesis.</note>. Therefore, to return to our purpose: he will not carve his "world" on the day or hour of Saturn, but rather on the day or hour of the Sun. He will stamp it on the birthday of the year...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial influences on health and the soul, specifically focusing on how to harmonize one's environment and character with the planets.</meta>

...especially if at 그that time Jupiter and <note>original: "Diana"</note> Diana [the Moon] approach favorably. They will consider it best to apply colors to the work in addition to the <term>lineaments</term> <note>The underlying outlines or structural proportions of a design.</note>. Truly, there are three colors of the world that are both universal and singular: green, golden, and sapphire-blue, dedicated to the three Graces of heaven. Green belongs both to Venus and the Moon. It is moist—belonging to moist things—and characteristic of things being born; it is also suited to mothers. No one doubts that golden is the color of the Sun; furthermore, it is not foreign to Jupiter and Venus. Finally, we dedicate sapphire-blue especially to Jupiter, to whom the sapphire stone itself is said to be consecrated. Whence also <term>lapis lazuli</term> [the azure stone], distinguished by this color, has a special reputation among physicians for its Jovian power against the <note>original: "bilem atram"</note> black bile <note>Melancholy, the "heavy" humor associated with the planet Saturn.</note> proceeding from Saturn. Since it is born with gold and marked with golden flecks, it is a companion of gold just as Jupiter is the companion of the Sun. The <term>Armenian stone</term> <note>Likely azurite, a blue mineral often found alongside green malachite.</note> has a similar power, possessing a color somewhat similar to green. Therefore, they will judge it advantageous to use these three colors most frequently to obtain the gifts of the celestial Graces.

And in the <term>model of the world</term> <note>original: "formula mundi"; a physical representation or "orrery" of the cosmos.</note> that you build, you should apply the sapphire color to the spheres of the world. They will think it worthwhile to add golden stars to the spheres in the very likeness of heaven, and to clothe Vesta herself—or Ceres (that is, the Earth)—in a green robe. A follower of these teachings will either carry such a model with him or gaze upon it placed before him. Indeed, it will be useful to observe a sphere endowed with its own motions, such as Archimedes once made, and recently our fellow Florentine, named <note>Lorenzo della Volpaia (1446–1512), a famous clockmaker who built a complex planetary clock for the Medici family.</note> Lorenzo. Nor should one only look at it, but also ponder it in the mind.

Therefore, he will construct for himself, in the innermost part of his house, a chamber with a vaulted ceiling decorated with figures and colors of this kind. There he may spend much of his time, both awake and asleep. And when leaving the house, he will not look at the spectacles of individual things with as much attention as he perceives the form and color of the universe. 

But let those fashioners of images look to these things. You, however, shall fashion yourself into a more excellent image. For since nothing is more orderly than the heavens, and nothing more temperate can be imagined than Jupiter, you will hope to obtain the benefits of heaven and Jupiter only if you present yourself as most orderly and most temperate in your thoughts, affections, actions, and way of life. But since we have mentioned celestial temperance, it will be opportune to remember that there is no excess of elemental quality in heaven—to speak in the <term>Peripatetic manner</term> <note>Referring to the followers of Aristotle and their specific physical theories.</note>—otherwise...

[if the universe] were composed <note>of conflicting elements</note>: it would have already been dissolved for so many ages; or even if it were simple, it would have destroyed all other things by its vast size, power, and motion. But indeed, as something most moderate, it moderates all things: and mixes diverse things into one. Furthermore, both by this very temperance <note>original: *temperantia*; the harmonious balancing of opposing qualities like hot, cold, moist, and dry</note> of its own and by the excellence of its form, it has deserved life through divine favor. 

For we see that even composite things only finally attain life when a perfect mixing of qualities has broken the prior conflict <note>original: *contrarietatem*; the natural opposition between elements like fire and water</note>, as in plants. There is a more perfect life thereafter in animals, insofar as there is in them a <term>complexion</term> <note>the specific physiological blend of qualities and humors that determines a creature's nature</note> further removed from conflict than in plants. In humans, by the same reasoning, life is most perfect and in a certain way already celestial. Indeed, the human complexion has already approached celestial temperance, especially in the spirit: which, beyond the subtlety of its substance and the balance of its quality—by which it agrees with heaven—[receives] celestial light also...

...and into the innermost parts of the motion, whether driven from within or manifested in an outward manner. If, therefore, life itself is this very thing, consider one to be insane <note>original: *mentecaptum*; literally "seized in mind"</note> who does not recognize that such a form exists in the heavens—that most excellent body—which, through its ever-revolving and perfect motion, brings life to all things. This is especially true for those things which, by their nature, have approached closer to its likeness or which daily expose themselves more aptly to its influences.

### **How much power they think images have over the spirit, and the spirit over them. And concerning the effect of the burning and the working agent.**

We have found it to be true: if someone uses hellebore <note>A plant used in ancient medicine, often to treat madness or to "purge" the mind of black bile.</note> correctly, and drinks it, through its properties and qualities, the nature of both spirit and body—and even the character of the mind—is changed; it is as if one becomes young again, so that he seems almost reborn. Thus they say that Medea and the mages were accustomed to restoring youth with certain herbs, a feat not quite matched by the myrobalan <note>original: *mirobalani*; a dried fruit used in medieval medicine for its preservative and life-extending qualities</note>, which merely preserves it. 

In a similar way, astrologers <note>Referring here to practitioners of astral magic and talismans.</note> believe that specific images possess a power through which, when they are worn, the nature and character of the wearer are in some way changed and restored for the better, so that he emerges as if he were another person, or at least they preserve him for a very long time in a state of robust health.

Truly, harmful images have the power of hellebore against the one who carries them, but one joined with a poisonous and pestilential force through malicious art. Against another person, however—for whose ruin they were crafted and intended—they possess the power of a bronze mirror <note>original: *aenei speculi*</note>. This is so thoroughly positioned that, when the reflected rays are gathered, it burns those nearby, or at least compels those at a distance to suffer blurred vision. From this arose the story, or perhaps the opinion, that by the "machines" of astrologers and the sorceries of mages, humans, beasts, and plants can be "star-struck" <note>original: *sideratum*; a condition of being blasted or paralyzed by planetary influence</note> and waste away.

I, however, do not sufficiently understand how images could have any power over a distant object. But I suspect they do have some power over the one wearing them; yet, I do not think it is of the sort that most people imagine. For this reason, I place more value on the material than the shape, and (as I have said) I prefer medicines to images by a wide margin. Nevertheless, the Arabs and Egyptians attribute so much to statues and images crafted by the astronomical and magical arts that they believe the spirits of the stars <note>original: *spiritus stellarum*</note> are enclosed within them. By "spirits of the stars," they mean...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial influences, specifically moving from the general power of the heavens to the practical application of capturing these powers in physical objects through the use of "spirit."</meta>

Some attribute these things to the wonderful powers of the heavens; others, however, believe they are <term>spirits</term><note>Latin: *spiritus*. In this context, "spirit" refers to a subtle, vaporous substance that Renaissance philosophers believed connected the physical body to the immaterial soul and the stars.</note> who act as the attendants of this or that star. They believe that these **stellar spirits**, whatever their nature may be, are drawn down into statues and images, in much the same way that spirits are sometimes known to occupy human bodies—speaking through them, moving them, and performing wonders. They believe the spirits of the stars perform similar feats through these images. They think that the worldly spirits, who dwell in fire, insinuate themselves into our bodies through fiery humors or ignited spirits and similar passions.

Through rays received at the opportune moment, and through <term>fumigations</term><note>original: *suffumigationes*. The ritual burning of incense or herbs to attract specific planetary energies.</note>, lights, and powerful sounds, they believe the spirit of the stars is brought into the likenesses [talismans] within suitable materials. These can then perform wonders upon the person wearing the object or even someone standing near it. We, for our part, believe these things can be done by <term>daemons</term><note>original: *demonas*. These are not necessarily "demons" in the modern evil sense, but intermediary spirits or beings that inhabit the air and heavens.</note>—who are not so much confined by a specific physical matter as they are delighted by the ritual worship. But more on this elsewhere with greater care.

The **Arabs**<note>Referring to the tradition of Arabic astrological magic, such as the *Ghayat al-Hakim* (known in Latin as the *Picatrix*).</note> report that when we duly fashion images, if our own spirit has been most attentive to the work and the stars through imagination and emotion, it is joined with the **spirit of the world** itself and with the rays of the stars, through which the world-spirit acts. Once joined in this way, our own spirit becomes a conduit, so that a certain stellar spirit—that is, a specific power—is poured from the world-spirits through the rays into the image. This occurs most effectively when the spirit of the person performing the rite is in harmony with the star.

Such a work is also assisted by fumigations directed toward the stars, as these temper the air for the task. These fumigations affect the rays in the air, the spirit of the maker, and the material of the image quite profoundly. Therefore, I believe that odors—which are very similar in nature to the spirit of the air and are formed with the air—being in harmony with the stellar rays (whether **Solar** or belonging to **Jupiter**), strongly affect the air and our spirit. This allows the gifts of the Sun or Jupiter, whoever is then ruling, to be caught opportunely under their rays. The spirit, thus affected and gifted, can then act with a more powerful influence, not only on its own body but on a nearby one—especially one conforming in nature but weaker—affecting it with a similar quality.

However, I believe that the harder material of the image can scarcely receive even the smallest influence from the odors and the imagination of the operator. Yet the spirit itself is so affected by the odor that from both, a single thing is made; which indeed from that...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the human spirit interacts with celestial influences, moving from the physical senses to the psychological powers of imagination and faith.</meta>

It is clear that an odor does not act further upon the sense of smell after it has already acted sufficiently. For the sense of smell—and indeed any other sense—is affected by nothing except itself or something very similar to it. <note>original: "Olfac tus.n. & q̃od uis aliud a ſe ipſo uel ſimillimo quopia nihil pa tiť." Ficino is invoking the Aristotelian principle that "like is known by like."</note> But more on these matters elsewhere.

Therefore, I believe that the intensity of the **imagination** has power not so much in the *fabricating* of images or medicines, but in the *applying* and *assuming* of them. For example, if someone wears an image (as they say) that has been properly made, or uses a medicine in a similar way, and intensely desires help from it—believing without a doubt and hoping most firmly—then a very great heap of assistance is surely added from this source. 

For when the power of the image (if there is any) touches the flesh and penetrates it as it is warmed, or at least the natural power within its selected material—or certainly the vigor of a medicine taken internally—glides into the veins and marrow, it carries with it a <term>Jovian property|A quality associated with the planet Jupiter, believed to bring health, justice, and temperate heat</term>. The <term>spirit|In Renaissance medicine, the 'spiritus' was a subtle vapor that linked the physical body to the soul</term> of the person is then transferred into a "spirit" of such a Jovian disposition—that is, through love. For the power of love is to transfer. 

Furthermore, faith and certain hope join the spirit of the person (now moved in this way) to the spirit of Jupiter itself. Now, if—as **Hippocrates** and **Galen** <note>The two most authoritative Greek physicians of antiquity, whose works formed the basis of Western medicine for centuries.</note> teach—the love and faith of a sick person toward a lower, external physician contribute greatly to health (indeed, **Avicenna** <note>An influential Persian polymath and physician (980–1037 AD) whose 'Canon of Medicine' was a standard textbook.</note> says this confidence accomplishes more than the medicine itself), how much more should we think that emotion and faith toward celestial influences lead us to celestial help? These influences are already implanted within us, acting internally and penetrating our very vitals. 

Indeed, love and faith toward a celestial gift are often the cause of celestial assistance; and conversely, love and faith sometimes perhaps proceed from this: that the mercy of heaven already favors us in this very matter.

# On the power of words and song for capturing celestial benefit.
## And on the seven steps leading to celestial things. ### Chapter XXI.

Furthermore, certain **words** pronounced with a sharper emotion are thought to have great power regarding images, specifically for directing their effect toward the goals that the emotions and words intend. Therefore, to reconcile two people with a most burning love, an image was fashioned under the **Moon** coming together with **Venus** in Pisces or Taurus <note>In astrology, Venus is 'exalted' in Pisces and 'rules' Taurus, making these her strongest signs for love magic.</note>:

<summary>Ficino argues that a patient's faith and imagination are essential for medicine and astrological talismans to work, comparing the influence of a physician to the much greater influence of the heavens. He begins a new chapter on the magical power of words and song when synchronized with astrological timing.</summary>

--- Page 157 ---
Meanwhile, many things having been observed in the interim quite curiously concerning the stars and words; it is not my intention to bring them forth here. For we are not teaching philosophy, but medicines.

It is more probable, however, that effects of this kind are brought about either through Venusian demons <note>original: "Venereos Demonas," spirits associated with the planet Venus and the domain of desire</note>—who delight in these works and words—or simply through seductive demons. For they say that even Apollonius of Tyana <note>a 1st-century Greek philosopher and reputed wonder-worker</note> often detected and exposed Lamias <note>monstrous beings or female demons in Greek mythology known for preying on young men</note>: these are certain lecherous and Venusian demons who imitate beautiful girls and entice handsome men. Just as a serpent might drain an elephant with its mouth, so these demons suck men dry through both mouth and womb, completely exhausting them.

But let Apollonius himself see to these matters. As for words, Origen <note>an early Christian scholar</note> asserts a certain and great power in his work *Against Celsus*, as do Synesius <note>a philosopher-bishop</note> and Al-Kindi <note>an Arab polymath</note> when discussing magic. Likewise Zoroaster <note>the ancient Persian prophet, often regarded as the father of magic in the Renaissance</note> warns against changing "barbarous" <note>original: "barbara uerba," referring to foreign or traditional ritual words that lose their power if translated</note> words. Iamblichus says the same. Similarly, the Pythagoreans <note>followers of Pythagoras</note> were accustomed to performing certain wonders through words, chants, and sounds in the manner of Phoebus <note>Apollo</note> and Orpheus <note>the legendary musician of myth</note>.

The ancient teachers of the Hebrews observed this above all others, and all the poets sing of wonders accomplished through songs. Even the most serious Cato <note>Cato the Elder, a Roman statesman known for his stern traditionalism</note>, in his work *On Agriculture*, occasionally used strange incantations for curing the diseases of animals.

But it is better to leave aside incantations. As for that harmony with which the young David redeemed Saul from madness <note>the biblical account of David playing the harp to soothe King Saul’s "evil spirit"</note>, unless the mystery were ordered to be attributed to divinity, someone might perhaps attribute it to nature.

Since indeed there are seven levels corresponding to the number of the seven planets—through which an attraction occurs from higher things to lower ones—voices hold the middle rank and are dedicated to Apollo <note>god of music and the Sun</note>.

The lowest rank is held by harder materials, such as stones and metals; these seem to relate to the Moon.

The second place in ascending order belongs to things composed of herbs, tree fruits, gums, and animal limbs; these correspond to Mercury, if we follow the Chaldean order of the heavens.

The third rank consists of very fine powders and their vapors chosen from the aforementioned items, as well as the scents of herbs, flowers, and ointments; these pertain to Venus.

The fourth includes words, chants, and sounds; all these are rightly dedicated to Apollo, the primary author of music.

The fifth consists of powerful concepts of the imagination, forms, movements, and emotions, which reflect the power of Mars...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of human faculties and how they relate to the planets, specifically moving into the use of music (tones) to capture celestial influence, comparing the process to the preparation of complex medicines.</meta>

...the sixth level concerns the discourses and deliberations of human reason <note>original: "rationis humanæ diſcurſiones deliberationeſq;"</note>, which are deliberately linked to Jupiter. The seventh concerns the more secret and simpler intelligences: as if already separated from movement and joined to the divine, destined for Saturn. The Hebrews rightly call this the Sabbath <note>original: "Sabath"</note> by the name for rest.

Why choose these? You must understand that just as a specific composition of herbs and vapors—prepared through both the medical and astronomical arts—results in a certain common form, like a kind of harmony endowed with the gifts of the stars; so too, a common form is created from musical tones. These tones are first chosen according to the rule of the stars and then composed in harmony with each other, and in that form, a certain celestial power <note>original: "uirtutẽ"</note> arises.

It is indeed very difficult to judge which specific tones suit which stars, or which compositions of tones agree best with particular stars and their planetary aspects <note>original: "aſpectibus"</note>. But partly through our own diligence and partly through a kind of divine luck, we can achieve this—just as <term>Andromachus</term> <note>A physician to the Emperor Nero, famous for his recipe for theriac.</note> was divinely exhausted while composing <term>theriac</term> <note>A complex medicinal "cure-all" or antivenom containing dozens of ingredients; it was believed to gain a "form" greater than the sum of its parts.</note> and finally, through his diligence and divine luck, attained its medicinal power. Both Galen and Avicenna <note>Two of the most influential physicians in history; Galen from the Roman era and Avicenna from the Islamic Golden Age.</note> confirm that this happened through divine grace. Indeed, Iamblichus and Apollonius of Tyana <note>Neoplatonist and Pythagorean philosophers associated with theurgy and mysticism.</note> testify that all medicine had its origin in prophecies. This is why medicine places Phoebus [Apollo] <note>The Greek god of both healing and prophecy.</note> the seer in charge.

We shall present three primary rules for this, though first we must warn you: do not think we are speaking here of worshipping the stars, but rather of imitating them and capturing their influence through that imitation. Nor should you believe we are discussing gifts that the stars give through conscious choice; rather, they come through natural influence. We study to accommodate ourselves to these manifold and hidden influences through exquisite methods, just as we daily accommodate ourselves to healthily receive the manifest light and heat of the Sun. Adapting oneself to the Sun's hidden and wonderful gifts is the duty of the wise man alone.

But let us now come to the rules for adapting songs to the stars. The first rule is to investigate what powers and effects any star, constellation, or aspect has within itself or produces; what they take away and what they bring. One should then weave these into the meanings of our words: detesting what they take away and approving what they bring. The second rule is to consider which star...

...to which place or person it <note>the specific star or planet</note> chiefly dominates. Third, pay attention to the daily positions and **aspects** <term>aspects: the specific geometric angles between planets that astrologers believe alter their power and influence</term> of the stars. Under these celestial configurations, explore what kinds of speech, songs, movements, dances, customs, and actions people are most inclined toward. You should then imitate these things as much as possible in your songs, thereby pleasing a specific part of the heavens and preparing to receive a similar **influence** <term>influence: literally an 'in-flowing' (influxus) of celestial power into the physical and spiritual world</term>.

Truly, remember that song is the most powerful imitator of all things. For it imitates the intentions and affections of the soul as well as words; it also represents the gestures and movements of the body, and the actions and customs of humanity. It imitates and performs all these things so vehemently that it suddenly provokes both the singer and the listeners to imitate or perform the same. By this same power, when song imitates celestial things, it wonderfully draws our **spirit** <term>spirit: in Renaissance medicine, 'spiritus' was a thin, vaporous substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul</term> toward the celestial influence on one hand, and draws that influence toward our spirit on the other. Indeed, the very material of harmony is exceedingly pure and similar to the heavens...

--- Page 160 ---
...then, by its conception and agitation within one’s own body, it gains the power to act, and then by its effusion <note>The outward flowing or pouring out of this "spirit."</note>: it repeatedly moves what is nearby and affects both its own [body] and another’s by a certain sidereal <note>Star-like or related to the influence of the planets.</note> property, which it has conceived partly from its own form and partly from a chosen opportunity of time. For this very reason, many Eastern and Southern peoples—especially the Indians—are said to possess a certain power in their words that must be acknowledged. This is because they are, for the most part, "Solar" <note>Influenced by the Sun.</note> and possess a force that I call not merely natural, but vital and animal—the most powerful of almost all forces. And this applies to whoever in other regions is most "Phoebeian" <note>original: "phębei"; meaning those related to Phoebus Apollo, god of the sun and music.</note>. 

Song, however, when conceived with this virtue, opportunity, and intention, is almost nothing other than another <term>spirit|The "spiritus" was considered a subtle substance in the body that linked the physical soul to the material body.</term> newly conceived within you alongside your own spirit—made Solar, and acting both within you and upon those nearby through Solar power. For if a certain vapor and spirit, sometimes emitted outward through the rays of the eyes or otherwise, is able to fascinate, infect, or otherwise affect a neighbor, how much more effective is that spirit which flows from the imagination of the heart! For that spirit flows out more abundantly and more fervently, and is more powerful in its motion. 

Thus, it is not at all surprising that certain diseases of the mind and body can sometimes be taken away or brought on in this manner. This is especially true because a musical spirit of this kind properly touches and acts upon the human <term>spiritus|the medium between the body and soul</term>, which thoroughly affects both body and soul with its own disposition. 

You will grant that there is a wonderful power in a spirit that is excited and singing, if you follow the excellent Pythagorean and Platonic teachings: that the heaven itself is a "spirit" disposing all things by its own characters and tones. Remember, indeed, that all music proceeds from Apollo. And Jove <note>Jupiter</note> is "musical" only insofar as he is in harmony with Apollo. Furthermore, Venus and Mercury derive their music from their proximity to Apollo. Likewise, true harmony <note>original: "concentus"</note> pertains only to these four. 

The remaining three planets, however, possess "voices" of the same kind, but not "songs." Now, we attribute slow, deep, hoarse, and complaining voices to Saturn. But to Mars, we attribute the opposite: swift, sharp, harsh, and threatening voices. We attribute middle voices to the Moon. However, we ascribe harmonies to Jupiter that are deep, earnest, sweet, and joyfully constant. On the contrary, to Venus, we ascribe songs characterized by playfulness, softness, and pleasure. Between these two, we attribute the middle styles to the Sun and Mercury. If they are venerable, simple, and earnest, together with grace and sweetness, they are "Apolline" <note>Belonging to Apollo.</note>.

...are judged. If, together with a sense of judgment, they are somewhat more relaxed, they nonetheless emerge as vigorous and versatile <term>Mercurials</term> <note>Those influenced by the planet Mercury, associated with wit, communication, and speed</note>. You, therefore, shall win over each of these four for yourself by means of their own songs, especially if you apply sounds that correspond to the songs; so much so that when you have called out by singing and playing opportunely in their manner, they will seem to respond at once: either like an Echo, or like a string on a lyre <note>original: "cithara"</note> that trembles whenever another is plucked, being restored in the same way.

And as Plotinus <note>OCR reads "Plorinus," referring to the Neoplatonist philosopher Plotinus</note> and Iamblichus wish, this will happen to you naturally from heaven, just as a resonance or tremor comes from a lyre, or as an echo is formed from an opposite wall. Indeed, whenever frequent use of a <term>Jovial</term> <note>pertaining to Jupiter</note>, <term>Mercurial</term>, or <term>Venereal</term> <note>pertaining to Venus</note> harmony makes it so—namely while these planets are reigning—and you sing most attentively to this very end, the one harmonically conformed emerges as Jovial, Mercurial, or Venereal. 

Meanwhile, he emerges as <term>Phoebian</term> <note>pertaining to Phoebus Apollo, the Sun</note>; for the music of Phoebus himself is that power which flourishes in every consonance. And in turn, from the Phoebian song and sound, you yourself will become Phoebian, meanwhile claiming for yourself the power of Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury. In turn, being so affected within by these, you similarly affect both the soul and the body. 

Remember, truly, that a speech appropriately and opportunely composed—full of emotion and meaning, and delivered with intensity—has a power similar to songs. It is not expedient to relate what great power Damis and Philostratus <note>Ancient biographers of the sage Apollonius</note> report certain Indian priests to have had, nor with what words they affirm that Apollonius <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a famous 1st-century philosopher and reputed miracle-worker</note> summoned the ghost of Achilles. For we shall not speak now of worshipping deities, but of a certain natural power of speech, song, and words. 

That there is indeed a medicinal <term>Phoebian</term> power in sound—and specifically in a certain kind—is evident from this: those in Apulia <note>A region in Southern Italy</note> who have been bitten by the tarantula <note>original: "phalangio"</note> are all stunned and lie half-dead until each hears a certain sound. Then, each dances to the appropriate sound, sweats, and therefrom recovers. And if ten years later they hear a similar sound, they are suddenly moved to dance. I conclude from these signs that this sound is of Phoebus and Jupiter.

### By what seven ways we can accommodate ourselves to the celestial beings; and in what ways Saturn is harmful and in what ways propitious; whom Jupiter defends from Saturn; and what part of heaven acts upon both body and soul. Chapter 22.

<summary>This page explains how music and rhythmic speech can be used as a "natural power" to align the human soul with planetary energies, citing the famous example of the tarantella dance in Italy as proof of music's medicinal effects.</summary>
<keywords>astrological music, planetary influence, Apollo, Jupiter, Mercury, Venus, tarantism, Marsilio Ficino, Plotinus, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how human beings can align themselves with the celestial bodies. It specifically focuses on the concept of 'spirit' as a bridge between the physical body and the higher soul, and how different mental faculties relate to specific planets like Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</meta>

Since the heavens are composed according to a **harmonic principle**, and move harmonically, and effect all things through harmonic ways and sounds, it is rightly through harmony alone that not only humans but all these lower <note>original: "iſte niora," a likely transcription error for "inferiora"</note> things are prepared as much as possible to receive celestial influences. We distribute seven degrees of harmony capable of receiving higher things among the higher ranks:

*   Through **images** (as it is believed) harmonically constituted;
*   Through **medicines** tempered by a certain consonance <note>meaning a balanced mixture of ingredients that "sound" together well</note> of their own;
*   Through **vapors and odors** prepared with a similar elegance;
*   Through **musical songs and sounds**, to whose order and power we wish to refer the gestures of the body, dances, and rhythmic movements;
*   Through the concepts of the **imagination** and harmonious habits;
*   Through the congruent discourses of **reason**;
*   Through the tranquil contemplations of the **mind**.

For just as we daily expose the body through its own harmony—that is, through its position, habit, and shape—at the right time to the light and heat of the sun, so also the <term>spirit</term> <note>In this context, 'spirit' is a subtle, airy substance that acts as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note> is opened <note>original: "compandiſ," likely "expanditur" or "patescit"</note> to the hidden powers of the stars. This is done through a certain similar harmony of its own, prepared (as is believed) by images, and certainly by medicines and odors harmonically composed. 

And finally, through the spirit thus prepared for the higher things, as we have often said, we expose the soul and the body to them. The soul, I say, insofar as it is inclined by affection toward the spirit, and the body likewise. In the soul, we now place imagination, reason, and mind. 

Our imagination can certainly be so disposed, composed, and conformed—either because of the quality and mode of the spirits, or through our own choice, or even through both—to **Mars** or the **Sun**, so that it becomes immediately a proper vessel for the influx of <note>original: "Phebet," referring to Phoebus Apollo, the Sun</note> **Phoebus** or **Mars**. Similarly, in various ways—either through the imagination and spirit together, or through deliberation, or through both—it can be opened by a certain imitation toward **Jupiter**, so that by its own dignity and property, it may receive Jupiter much more; and the gifts of Jupiter, which are received through the imagination and spirit, are at the same time much more celestial than objects and any lower matter. 

Finally, the **contemplative mind**—insofar as it withdraws itself not only from what we perceive with the senses, but even from those things we commonly imagine and argue about through human habits, and recalls itself by affection and the intention of life to separate things <note>referring to divine or abstract entities beyond the physical world</note>—exposes itself in a certain way to **Saturn**. To him alone is Saturn... <note>The text cuts off here mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>This page explains how humans can use music, medicine, and mental focus to align their 'spirit' and 'soul' with the harmonic vibrations of the planets, specifically discussing how to attract the influences of the Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</summary>
<keywords>astrological magic, harmony of the spheres, spiritus, Phoebus, Jupiter, Saturn, Mars, imagination, contemplative mind, celestial influence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the astrological and metaphysical influences of the planets, specifically focusing on the transition from the solar influence to the nature of Saturn and Jupiter.</meta>

...Lord. Just as the Sun is indeed an enemy to nocturnal animals but a friend to diurnal ones, so Saturn is a friend to men who live a common or public life. For even to those spirits dwelling high in the air, Saturn himself (to speak in the Platonic manner) is in the place of Jupiter; just as Jupiter is a "helping father" <note>original: "iuuans parere," a play on the traditional etymology of the name Jupiter from *Iuvans Pater*</note> to men leading a common life. 

Truly, no one is more hateful to Saturn than those men who merely pretend to lead a contemplative life without actually practicing it. For Saturn does not recognize these as his own, nor does Jupiter himself help them against the harshness <note>original: "tempesties," referring to the storms or difficult seasons of life</note> of Saturn—specifically those who flee the common laws, customs, and commerce of men. For Jupiter claimed these public matters for himself (as they say) after Saturn was bound. Saturn, however, governs things that are set apart.

Therefore, those "Lunar people" whom Socrates describes in the *Phaedo* <note>Referring to Plato's dialogue where Socrates describes a "true earth" located above the clouds</note>—who dwell upon the highest peak of things, higher than the clouds, living very soberly, content with the simple fruits of the earth, and dedicated to the study of secret wisdom and religion—taste the happiness of Saturn. They lead a life so prosperous and long-lived that they are considered not so much mortal men as immortal spirits <term>Daemones (Spirits)</term> <note>In this Neoplatonic context, "daemones" refers to semi-divine guardian spirits or mediators between gods and men, not evil entities</note>. Many call them "heroes," a golden race enjoying a certain Saturnian age and kingdom. 

I think perhaps this is what the Arabic astrologers intended to say. They commonly claimed that beyond the equinoctial line <note>The Equator</note> to the south, there are most refined <note>original: "lubriſſimos," here implying purity or transparency</note> inhabitants, certain spirits who are never seen to be born nor to die, and that Saturn holds power there. This indeed seems to be confirmed by Albumasar <note>Abu Ma'shar, a highly influential 9th-century Persian astrologer</note> in his book *Sadar*, where he says that certain regions of India are subject to Saturn. There, men are exceptionally long-lived, for the most part passing away only in extreme old age. He assigns a reason for this: that Saturn does not harm those of his own household, but only outsiders. 

Indeed, do not neglect the power of Saturn. For the Arabs say he is the most powerful of all. Certainly, planets take on the strength of those they approach; yet all planets approach him rather than the other way around. And the planets joined to him act according to his nature. For he himself is among...

<summary>This page discusses the dual nature of Saturn and Jupiter, noting that while Jupiter governs common social life, Saturn governs the sublime and contemplative, rewarding true wisdom with longevity and happiness.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Jupiter, Socrates, Phaedo, Albumasar, astrology, contemplative life, Lunar people, Arabic astrology, India</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
The planets are the head of a most vast sphere. Indeed, any planet is the head, the heart, and the eye of its own orb. Likewise, Saturn is closest to the innumerable stars <note>The "fixed stars" beyond the planetary spheres</note> and is most similar to the **First Mover** <note>original: "Primo mobili"; the outermost sphere of the heavens that carries all others</note>; it performs a long circuit. It is the highest of the planets. For this reason, those whom Saturn has favored happily call him "happy." And although men mostly fear him as something alien to common human life, they nonetheless believe he can be placated even for the needs of common life: if he should hold great power and dignity in the **ascendant** <note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at birth</note>, or if his own Jupiter looks upon him favorably, or receives him excellently within his own bounds.

Otherwise, his influence, when received unluckily in matter that is especially "thick" or "gross" <note>Referring to a person's physical or spiritual temperament being unrefined</note>, becomes like a poison—just as an egg becomes poisonous through rot or scorching. From this, there are born or turn out certain unclean, lazy, sad, and envious people, exposed to unclean demons. Flee far from their inventions. For Saturn’s poison lies dormant elsewhere, like sulfur kept far from a flame. In living bodies, however, it often blazes, and like ignited sulfur, it not only burns but also fills everything around it with a noxious vapor and infects those nearby.

Against Saturn's influence—which is generally foreign and somewhat discordant to men—Jupiter arms us, both by his own natural quality and certainly by his specific foods and medicines, and (as they believe) even by **images** <note>Astrological talismans or symbols used to channel planetary power</note>, as well as by habits, business, and studies, and things properly pertaining to Jupiter himself.

One escapes the harmful influence of Saturn and undergoes a favorable one not only by those who take refuge in Jupiter, but also by those who devote themselves with their whole mind to the **divine contemplation** signified by Saturn himself. For the **Chaldeans** <note>Ancient Mesopotamian astronomers and astrologers</note>, **Egyptians**, and **Platonists** believe that by this means, the malice of fate can be avoided. For since they do not wish the celestial bodies to be empty things, but rather divinely animated and directed by divine minds, they surely want many things pertaining to the body and spirit to proceed from there to men—but also many goods that overflow, in a sense, into the soul. These come not from the physical bodies of the planets into the soul, but from their indwelling souls. Rather, these and more of this kind flow from the higher minds in heaven. Among these matters, if you wish to assign all the reasons that led **Moses** to command the leisure of the **Sabbath** <note>original: "fabbati"; the day of rest associated with Saturn, the seventh planet</note> to the Hebrews: perhaps beyond the more sublime...

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion on planetary influences, this page moves from general celestial observations to specific methods for mitigating the "malefic" influences of Saturn and Mars through contemplation, sacrifice, and aligning one's life with celestial harmony.</meta>

You will find a more secret allegory in this: the day of Saturn is unfit for civil and warlike actions, but suitable for contemplations. On that day, the beneficial distinctions of divination provide protection for those who observe them. 

Abraham, Samuel, and many Hebrew astrologers agree that it is possible to escape the threats of Mars and Saturn through the elevation of the mind toward God and through frequent sacrifices. This is confirmed by that Chaldean <note>referring to the ancient wisdom of the Near East, often associated with the "Chaldean Oracles"</note> precept: if you keep your mind burning and extended toward the work of piety, you will also preserve your frail body. 

It is worth considering the thought of Iamblichus <note>a 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher who emphasized theurgy, or "divine work"</note>: that celestial and worldly divinities possess certain superior powers and some inferior ones. Through the inferior ones, we are bound by the effects of fate; but through the superior ones, in turn, we may free ourselves from fate. It is as if they hold the keys to both open and close, as Orpheus <note>the legendary poet and musician</note> says. Therefore, the divinity that is superior to the world redeems us from fatal necessity much more effectively. 

It is also most worthy of exploration, that Hebrew practice of sacrificing animals and dispersing our possessions; by these means, the evils threatening from heaven are deflected from us and onto our belongings. But we leave these matters to be explored by our Pico <note>original: "Pico noſtro"; referring to the philosopher Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, a friend and colleague of the author</note>.

Finally, whenever we say that the gifts of the celestials descend to us, you should understand it in several ways:
*   First, that the gifts of celestial bodies enter our own bodies through our properly prepared <term>spirit|In this context, "spiritus" is a subtle, airy substance that acts as a bridge connecting the physical body to the non-physical soul</term>.
*   Second, that these gifts have already flowed naturally into the spirit through their rays, or into anything exposed to them. 
*   Third, that the goods of celestial souls partly leap into the same spirit through rays and from there reflect back into our own souls; and partly arrive from their souls or from angels into the souls of men who are "exposed" to them. 

I say "exposed" if our nature is made open by some agreement, by the choice of free will, or by our affections. In short, consider those who—through prayer, study, life, morals, and beneficent action—imitate the order of the celestials. Think of them as being more like those highest beings and more fit to receive gifts from that place. Conversely, men who are artfully unlike or discordant with the celestial arrangement are secretly miserable and, in the end, turn out to be openly unhappy.

->To live and act prosperously: first of all, know your own nature <note>original: "ingenium"</note>, your star <note>original: "syduus"</note>, and your guiding spirit <note>original: "genium"</note>, and find a place suitable for them. Dwell here. Follow your natural profession.<-

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>The text discusses the Neo-Platonic and astrological concept of finding one's "natural work" through the guidance of a celestial guardian or star.</meta>

However much a person is born of sound mind and in control of their faculties, they are naturally destined by the heavens for some honorable task and way of life. Therefore, whoever wishes to have the heavens on their side should first of all undertake this specific work and calling, and pursue it with diligence. For heaven favors the beginnings it has inspired.

Indeed, you were made by nature for this very purpose: that which you first do, speak, imagine, desire, or imitate in your dreams from your earliest years; that which you attempt most often and accomplish with the greatest ease; that in which you make progress without effort and take the most delight. It is that which you are most reluctant to leave behind. This is surely that for which heaven and the Ruler of heaven brought you forth. Therefore, heaven will favor your undertakings and breathe life into your path only so long as you follow the guidance of your Creator.

This is especially true if that <term>Platonic doctrine</term> <note>Referring to the philosophy of Plato regarding the soul's purpose and the "daimon."</note>—with which all of antiquity agrees—is correct: that for every person being born, there is a certain <term>Daemon</term> <note>original: "Demonem"; here meaning a protective spirit or guiding "genius," not an evil entity.</note> appointed as a guardian of their life by their own particular star. This guardian will assist in the very duty to which the celestial powers assigned the soul at birth.

Therefore, whoever examines their own natural character <note>original: "ingenii"; referring to innate talent or disposition.</note> through the signs we have just mentioned and finds their natural work, will simultaneously discover their star and their guiding spirit. By following their lead, he will act prosperously and live happily. Otherwise, he will experience adverse fortune and feel the hostility of the heavens.

There are, therefore, two kinds of men who are more unfortunate than all others: first, those who pursue no calling and do nothing; second, those who take up a profession that is foreign to their natural character and contrary to their guiding spirit. The former waste away in idleness, even while heaven is constantly urging them toward action. The latter, while they perform tasks foreign to their celestial patrons, labor in vain because they are deprived of heavenly support. 

The first point is confirmed by an ancient proverb: "The gods help those who take action, but they are hostile to the idle." The second is confirmed by a similar saying: "Do nothing <term>against the grain</term>" <note>original: "invita Minerva"; literally "with Minerva being unwilling," a classical proverb meaning to act against one's own natural talent or inspiration.</note>. For this reason, I believe Jupiter is beseeched in the <term>Pythagorean verses</term> <note>likely referring to the "Golden Verses of Pythagoras," a collection of moral exhortations.</note> either to relieve the human race of its many evils himself, or to reveal if we are being led by a false spirit.

Therefore, it is well worth the effort to investigate which specific region your star and your spirit have designated for you to inhabit and cultivate from the beginning. For it is there that they offer the most inspiration. Indeed, that is the place where, upon arriving, your spirit is somehow refreshed and your senses become more vigorous...

...it remains: where the condition of the body is stronger; where most people favor you; and where your prayers find success. Therefore, try these things. Choose this region. Cultivate it happily. For clearly you would depart from here unhappy, unless you were to return or proceed to similar circumstances. But meanwhile, exercise yourself in the frequent customs of this region: and perform certain revolutions in imitation of the heavenly bodies. For by a motion and circuit of this kind, you—being born of such things—will be preserved by things similar to them. 

Furthermore, as far as it pertains to where you live, you will usefully remember this: just as the countryside supplies the necessary food for life to the city, but the city consumes it, so also life itself is greatly increased by frequent stays in the countryside—where the weariness of returning to work has the least hold on you—but it is worn down by both the leisure and the business of the city. 

As for what concerns both your habitation and your <term>profession</term><note>In this context, one's "profession" refers to their lifelong calling or daily work.</note> together, that teaching of the Eastern <term>astrologers</term><note>original: "astrologorum"; often referring to the ancient Persians or Chaldeans.</note> is not to be despised: namely, that by a change of name, profession, clothing, food, or place, the celestial influence upon us is changed, sometimes for the better and sometimes for the worse. The <term>Platonists</term><note>Followers of the philosopher Plato, particularly those in late antiquity like Plotinus or Porphyry.</note> will judge that either the <term>daemons</term><note>original: "Demones"; in the Platonic sense, these are not "demons" but intermediary guardian spirits or "guiding geniuses" that connect humans to the divine.</note> are changed, or that we relate to the same ones differently here than elsewhere.

Indeed, the astrologers, along with the Platonists, agree that the guardian spirit of each person can be twofold: one proper to one's birth, and the other to one's profession. And whenever the profession agrees with one's nature, the same daemon—or certainly one very similar to us—is present for both; and from this, our life will be more fertile, harmonious, and tranquil. But if the profession disagrees with one's natural talent, the spirit acquired by our chosen art will be discordant with our natural guardian; and then life is laborious and anxious. 

For those wishing to find what kind of daemon presides over each person from the moment of birth: <term>Porphyry</term><note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the soul and astrology.</note> investigates a rule based on the planet that is the master of the birth-chart. <term>Julius Firmicus</term><note>Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century Roman lawyer and astrologer.</note> says the master of the birth-chart is either the planet that has the most "dignity" (influence) there, or rather—according to the highest opinion—the one in whose house the Moon is about to enter after leaving the sign it held at the moment of birth. But he thinks the daemon should not be investigated by that same rule. Instead, following the opinion of the <term>Chaldeans</term><note>Ancient Babylonian astrologers renowned for their mathematical precision.</note>, he believes it is found from the Sun or the Moon. Specifically, from the Sun to the Moon in a daytime birth, and conversely from the Moon to the Sun in a nighttime birth. Once you have calculated the interval between these two, you traverse an equal distance descending from the degree of the <term>Ascendant</term><note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the exact moment of birth.</note>; and the point at which you arrive...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between a person's guiding spirit (Daimon) and the astrological houses of their birth chart. It focuses on how the placement of planets at birth determines the nature of one's fortune and character.</meta>

...defined against. For they judge the **Daimon** <note>original: *Dæmon*, here referring to a guiding spirit or guardian angel rather than a malevolent entity</note> to belong to the same star that governs that specific boundary <note>term: *Terminus* (Bound), in astrology, refers to specific sub-divisions of signs ruled by different planets</note>. In summary, the course of life and its fortunes usually depend simultaneously on the lord of the birth chart <note>term: *Genitura*, the nativity or birth horoscope</note> and the Daimon. I have added Fortune, since some calculate the **Part of Fortune** <note>term: *Pars Fortunae*, a specific mathematical point in a horoscope representing prosperity and physical well-being</note> by almost the same reasoning.

The ancients used to hope that their own Daimon had descended to them from one of the cardinal points of the heavens: namely from the East, the West, or the midheaven from either side. Or at least from the eleventh or fifth region. Indeed, the eleventh region follows the midheaven above our heads and is named the **Good Daimon** <note>The 11th house in traditional astrology, associated with friends and hopes</note>. And when a Daimon descends from any of the cardinal points of the sky: the fifth region, which follows the midheaven of the antipodes <note>the IC or "Bottom of the Sky," specifically the 5th house</note>, is called **Good Fortune**; it looks upon the ascending degree from a trine <note>term: *Trine*, a harmonious astrological angle of 120 degrees</note>.

In the third place, they hoped that if the Daimon came from a cadent <note>term: *Cadent*, houses that "fall away" from the cardinal angles, often considered weaker or less active</note> region, it would at least come from the ninth or the third. For the ninth is called **God**, and the third is called **Goddess**. The former looks upon the ascending degree from a trine, while the latter looks upon it from a sextile <note>term: *Sextile*, an astrological angle of 60 degrees, considered beneficial</note>.

But they dreaded the twelfth and sixth regions, which are cadent. They named the former the **Evil Daimon** and the latter **Bad Fortune**. However, as we consider it useless to wish for things already past, we advise that these same regions—which the ancients chose for Daimons and fortunes—should be observed when arranging planets and stars for the completion of a work: so that they might be in the angles, or in the two "succedent" <note>term: *Succedent*, houses that "follow" the angles (the 2nd, 5th, 8th, and 11th)</note> houses we mentioned, or at least in the two cadent ones we named earlier. For it is not without reason that they say the Sun rejoices in the ninth, the Moon in the third, Jupiter in the eleventh, and Venus in the fifth. For these planets behold the ascending degree.

But let us return to the point. Therefore, whether by the experience and diligence I spoke of above, or by this art I have just reviewed, let us first investigate the instinct of the Daimon’s nature. We judge that person to be unfortunate who performs no honorable duty. For such a person has no leader for their worldly profession, since they undertake nothing honorable; nor do they have a natural guide for life. For the duty of the star of the Daimons—or of the angelic leaders divinely appointed for guardianship—is to act always, excellently, and most extensively. 

Moreover, as we said before, that person is unfortunate who, through a profession contrary to their nature, undergoes a Daimon different from their own **Genius** <note>term: *Genius*, an individual's innate natural spirit, talent, or inclination</note>. Remember, however, that according to the dignity of professions, more noble Daimons or angels are received step-by-step; and those in public governance receive even more noble ones.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the guardian spirit (Daimon) and how its influence, combined with one's natural character, dictates the course of life. It is possible that the art and tenor of life are received from one's guardian spirit <note>original: *genio*</note> and natural talent <note>original: *ingenio*</note> in a way that is not contrary to them, nor far different; and if you proceed toward more excellent things, remember again to enter into familiarity with those whom celestial graces inspire. You can perceive this from the goods of the soul, the body, and fortune. For just as a scent comes from musk, so something of the good exhales from a good person onto their neighbor, and it often persists through association. Truly marvelous would be a gathering of three happy people, or even two, whose spirits wonderfully overflow toward each other. 

Finally, remember to flee far from the unrestrained, the shameless, the malicious, and the unhappy. For these are full of evil spirits or harmful rays; they are malevolent and, like lepers or the plague-stricken, they harm not only by touch, but even by proximity and sight. Indeed, the very proximity of living bodies is thought to be a form of touch, because of the effective exhalation of vapors flowing outward from the heat, the spirit, and the emotions. The familiarity of the wicked and the cruel will be especially pestilential, if it is true that after the vegetative life is infused into us in the month of Jupiter (that is, the second month of gestation), the sensitive soul—enslaved to disturbances and passions—is then infused in the month of Mars (that is, the third month). For thus those who are driven by passion, being full of Mars, infect those near them with a Martial contagion. 

By the contrary reasoning, frequent habit and close commerce with the happy and excellent is, as we have said, accustomed to be wonderfully beneficial. They say that Apollonius of Tyana <note>original: *Apolloniū rheaneū*; Ficino refers to the famous Neopythagorean philosopher and miracle-worker</note> discovered an old man in Ephesus under whose form a Demon <note>In this context, a malevolent entity or plague-spirit</note> lurked, who by his mere presence contaminated the entire city with plague. On the other hand, Xenophon and Plato testify how much Socrates benefited many by his presence alone.

## By what means men of letters may know their own natural talent, and how they should follow a way of life agreeable to their spirit.
### Chapter 24.

Since I am speaking to those devoted to letters, I want each one captured by the love of literature to remember that he is, first and foremost, **Mercurial**; furthermore, he is **Solar**, insofar as Mercury himself is Solar <note>Ficino views Mercury as being closely tied to the Sun, representing the light of the intellect</note>. And this condition is common to all of them. 

Specifically, however, beyond the Mercurial nature, whoever excels in the grace of eloquence, wit, dignity, and beauty should recognize **Apollo** and **Venus** within himself. He who is more inclined toward laws or natural and common philosophy should not be unaware that he has **Jupiter** as a patron. But he who is inwardly driven to investigate the most secret things with greater curiosity should know that he is not only Mercurial...

<sig>ii</sig>

<summary>Ficino explains how spiritual qualities and "vapors" are transmitted between people like a contagion, and begins advising scholars on how to identify which planetary deities (Mercury, Sun, Apollo, Venus, or Jupiter) govern their specific intellectual talents.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual contagion, Apollonius of Tyana, Socrates, planetary types, Mercurial, Solar, Jupiter, spiritual hygiene, astrology of character</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
...to be. But rather **Saturnian**. Under whose dominion are all those who are diligent in any study right until the end: especially those who are negligent in other matters. <note>original: "Saturnium" — People born under the influence of Saturn were traditionally thought to be prone to melancholy but also capable of deep, sustained intellectual focus.</note> Finally, if it is true what many physicians and astronomers teach—that the soul endowed with intellect descends into the human conception in the "month of the Sun," that is, the fourth month—then those who use the intellect most are, from their very beginning and in their daily lives, primarily **Solar** beings. The favor of these planets, therefore, will be most auspicious for such men. Their medicine should be prepared under their influence <note>original: "spiraculo" — literally 'breathing hole' or 'inspiration,' referring here to the astrological 'breath' or influence of the planets.</note>. One must live in their regions.

But I call upon you first, O **scholars**, <term>cultivators of the Muses</term>, and upon **Apollo**, the leader of those Muses: Whoever among you, most beloved brothers in the love of the Muses, is much more powerful in genius and for a longer time than you are in body, should know for certain that in your birth, **Phoebe** <note>original: "Phebe" — Refers to the Moon, representing the material and physical aspects of birth.</note> supplied very little material indeed. But the **Phoebean** spirit <note>original: "Phebeũ fpũm" — Referring to Phoebus Apollo, the Sun, representing the light of the mind and spirit.</note> was infused most abundantly. Indeed, every day the humors and nourishment in your body are, for the most part, resolved into spirit. Each of you, therefore, is almost entirely <term>spirit</term>. <note>In this context, 'spirit' (spiritus) is a technical term for a very thin, vaporous substance that Renaissance thinkers believed connected the physical body to the immortal soul.</note> You are, I say, a spiritual man "masked" by this tiny earthly body. You fatigue this spirit before others do by your constant labor; thus, more than others, your spirit must be continually refreshed. Furthermore, in old age—when the spirit commonly becomes thicker—it must be called back to its proper refinement and subtlety.

You know, of course, that the dense body is nourished by the four dense **elements** <note>The four traditional elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>. Know then that the spiritual body is nourished by its own four "thin" elements. For the spirit, **wine** stands in for the Earth. The **scent** of wine takes the place of Water. **Song** and sound act as the Air. **Light**, however, provides the element of Fire. By these four, then, the spirit is especially nourished: by wine, I say, and its scent, and likewise by song and light. 

But I know not how, having begun with Apollo, we have soon fallen upon **Bacchus**. <note>Apollo represents the sun, reason, and music; Bacchus (Dionysus) represents wine and divine frenzy. The author argues they are inseparable for the health of the scholar.</note> And rightly so, for from light we have arrived at the burning love of wine. Truly, Phoebus and Bacchus are brothers and inseparable companions. The former brings you two things above all: namely, **light** and the **lyre**. The latter likewise brings two: **wine** and its **scent**, to refresh the spirit. By the daily use of these, the spirit itself finally becomes Phoebean—solar—and free. Therefore, prepare yourselves daily to receive the light of the Sun in this way...

...so that, as far as possible, by avoiding a certain **distillation** <term>distillation: a thinning or trickling away of the body's vital fluids</term> and **drying out** <term>drying out: the loss of "radical moisture," which Renaissance doctors believed caused aging</term>, you may live as much as possible in the light. We have spoken enough now, brothers; we have drunk enough. Therefore, farewell.

### Astronomical care in the procreation of children; in the preparation of feasts; in buildings and habitation; in clothing; and when it is permitted to attend to such matters. Chapter 25.

But it is also helpful to speak for a little while with the stern priest of religion. "Tell me, Priest, what exactly do you condemn in the use of the stars?" "Whatever," you will say, "detracts from our free will, and whatever disparages the worship of the one God." I do not only agree with you; I strongly detest those things as well. Indeed, I know you curse—and I too am horrified by—certain people who, when they wish to entreat God, turn instead to **Jupiter** in the middle of the sky as it approaches that vast **Head of the Dragon** <note>original: "draconis caput"; the North Node of the Moon. Ficino is mocking those who use astrology as a substitute for prayer</note>. These wretched and foolish people flee for help to that very dragon which once fell from heaven, dragging a third of the stars with it, only to be swallowed up in the end. 

"But will you concede that it is permissible to choose favorable hours for contracts, marriages, conversations, journeys, and similar affairs?" I see you do not easily grant your assent to these things; I know not what your free will fears. Therefore, I—and even that great theologian **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a prominent Catholic saint and philosopher who argued that natural astrology was compatible with Christianity</note>—will grant them. And reason itself will perhaps dictate that the very act of choosing an hour belongs to the domain of free will...

<meta>This page appears to be a continuation of a philosophical and medical defense of astrology, likely from Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life." ...that celestial bodies are subjected to the use of our prudence in the same way that herbs serve a physician; however, I would rather trust your own feelings on this. And as for those things which you now grant with difficulty, I myself shall dismiss them just as easily. 

You have long ago (as I believe) permitted the observation of the Moon and the other stars for the purpose of curing diseases and for preparing remedies. It has also been granted by you, and furthermore approved, that when the Moon is waxing and its light is increasing, and it is not afflicted by any misfortune <note>original: *infortunata*, an astrological term for a planet being in a negative position or aspect</note>, one may sow fields with seeds and plant vines and olives. 

Why then should we not use the benefit of the Moon, the Sun, and Phoebus <note>A name for Apollo, often used interchangeably with the Sun</note> for the "planting" of a man (to speak bluntly)? <note>A euphemism for human procreation and conception</note> For we always use the service of Venus for these duties; but I should have said more correctly, always both: for to me, Venus is herself Diana <note>The author suggests that the influences of Venus (love/procreation) and the Moon (fertility/growth, associated with Diana) are inextricably linked</note>. What then shall we say of diet? Will it not be permitted and beneficial, under a lucky star, to prepare wine in the autumn, and daily to make bread and feasts? And if we cannot wait for the specific <term>aspects: the geometric angles between planets that astrologers believe determine their influence</term> of the stars in these preparations, it will at least be expedient to catch them when they are rising or otherwise in the <term>angular houses: the most powerful positions in an astrological chart (the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses)</term>, namely the Sun, Jupiter, Venus, and the Moon. In this way, all things we use, being happily influenced from the heavens, will happily influence us in turn. 

Thus far, I see you will concede without controversy, unless perhaps you say that in this way our life would be nothing but perpetual slavery. To this I would reply: mortals will serve in vain by accumulating money and honors, to which they are perpetually enslaved, unless in the meantime, through natural diligence <note>original: *physica*, referring here to the study of medicine and natural laws</note>, they accumulate more days for their own use. Therefore, let them either serve God alone, which is the most important thing, or if they must serve anything else, let them indulge in a strong and long life rather than in vain money and honors. 

We are therefore in agreement. But will they never build houses at random, or live in unlucky ones? For a certain contagious disaster within a building almost affects the inhabitant in such a way that a poisonous pestilence seems to live for two years, preserved within the walls. Just as a hidden plague from an epidemic lingers in a garment and later infects and destroys the unwary wearer. But since we have mentioned clothing here, will you forbid us, holy father, from diligently watching for the "breath of Venus" <note>A favorable astrological moment associated with the planet Venus</note> when making or first putting on a garment? So that the clothing, made as if by Venus, might similarly affect the body and spirit with a certain prosperous quality. Do not physicians forbid fox skins? And do they not approve of lambskins? Perhaps not otherwise...

### Chapter 26. How higher things are drawn down through lower things exposed to higher things; and especially how world-wide gifts are drawn through pre-mundane materials.

But lest we wander further from that which we established at the beginning while interpreting Plotinus, <note>Plotinus was a 3rd-century philosopher and the founder of Neoplatonism, whose works Ficino translated and commented upon.</note> briefly gather this: The world was made as good as it could possibly be made by the Good itself, as Plato teaches together with Timaeus the Pythagorean. <note>referring to the *Timaeus*, Plato's dialogue on the creation of the universe.</note> It is, therefore, not only corporeal, but also a participant in life and intelligence. Therefore, besides this body of the world which is familiarly manifest to our senses, there lies hidden within it a certain "body of spirit" <note>The <term>Spirit</term> (spiritus) is a core concept here: it is a fine, invisible substance that acts as a bridge or "glue" between the physical body and the non-physical soul.</note> exceeding the capacity of our frail senses. In the spirit, the soul flourishes; in the soul, the intelligence shines. 

And just as below the Moon air is not mixed with earth except through the medium of water, nor fire with water except through air; so in the entire universe there is a certain "bait" or "tinder" for coupling the soul to the body: it is that very thing which we call the Spirit. The soul also is a certain tinder in the spirit and body of the world for attaining intelligence from the divine. Just as a certain dryness in wood is prepared for the penetration of oil, and the oil soaked into this wood is fuel for the fire—specifically for the heat—and the heat itself is the vehicle for the light; and if this wood is of such a kind that it shines when fire is present but does not burn (such as we have sometimes seen), we shall see by this example how a human being, or something else under the Moon, through certain preparations—partly natural and partly sought through art—can receive vital and perhaps even certain intellectual gifts.
