<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural magic, specifically how a "mage" or wise philosopher acts like a spiritual farmer, grafting heavenly influences onto earthly matter to improve its nature.</meta>

...propagates the life of a plant and leads it toward another, better species. A doctor, a natural philosopher, and a surgeon perform similar tasks in our own bodies, both to nurture our health and to more fully realize the nature of the universe.

Likewise, the philosopher skilled in natural things and the stars—whom we are accustomed to properly call a <term>mage (magus)</term><note>In this context, a "mage" is not a sorcerer, but a "natural magician" who understands the hidden properties of plants, minerals, and stars to produce beneficial effects.</note>—brings celestial things down to earthly ones through certain attractions at just the right time. He does this just like a diligent farmer grafting a fresh shoot onto a tree trunk. 

**Ptolemy** <note>original: "Ptolemeus"; Claudius Ptolemy was a 2nd-century astronomer whose work was central to Renaissance science.</note> strongly approves of this, affirming that this kind of wisdom can assist the work of the stars, much like a farmer assists the fertility of the earth. The mage subjects earthly things to the celestial—indeed, he subjects the lower things everywhere to the higher—like wives to be made fruitful by their husbands. He sees iron as something to be drawn to a magnet, camphor as something that flees into hot air, crystal as something to be illuminated by the Sun, and sulfur or refined liquids to be ignited by flame. He sees an empty eggshell filled with dew as something to be lifted by the Sun—even an egg itself as something to be nurtured by a hen.

Furthermore, just as some people hatch eggs and bring life to them from the universe even without the presence of animals, they often prepare matter at the right time to create living creatures without eggs or visible seeds. They might produce a scorpion from basil, bees from a bull, or a bird similar to a blackbird from sage <note>original: "ex saluta"; likely referring to sage or willow. These examples refer to "spontaneous generation," a common historical belief that life could arise from decaying or prepared matter.</note>. 

By applying life to a mixture of specific materials at favorable times, the wise man acts. When he knows which materials (whether they are raw parts of nature or refined ones) are capable of receiving a specific heavenly influence when they are gathered together, he collects, prepares, and applies them while that celestial body is most dominant. Through these materials, he claims those heavenly powers for himself.

For whenever matter is refined and made ready for the higher powers—like a polished mirror reflecting your face, or a wall reflecting a voice—it suddenly receives an influence from above. This comes from a most powerful agent, a miraculous life and power present everywhere, and the matter gains virtue through this reception. It is no different than a mirror representing an image from a face, or a wall representing an echo from a voice.

**Plotinus** <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher who influenced Renaissance thought deeply.</note> uses these very examples when, following **Hermes** <note>original: "Mercurium"; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermetic philosophy.</note>, he says that the ancient priests or mages used to receive something divine and wonderful within statues and physical sacrifices. He means, along with **Trismegistus**, that through these material things...

<meta>This page concludes a discussion on the animation of statues and the metaphysical mechanism behind planetary magic, distinguishing between demonic deception and natural spiritual influence. It refers back to Plotinus and Hermes Trismegistus, and concludes by pointing toward the author's work on Christian theology.</meta>

If they have indeed produced speaking statues, it was not the simple influence of the stars themselves that formed the words there, but rather spirits <note>original: "dæmonas." In this Neoplatonic context, "daemons" are intermediary spirits, not necessarily the "demons" of modern hell.</note>. Furthermore, if it should happen that they enter such statues, I do not believe they were bound there by celestial influence; rather, they willingly obeyed their worshipers, and ultimately, they will deceive them. For a superior nature is indeed sometimes won over by an inferior one, but it cannot be constrained. And that arrangement of the stars described a little earlier may perhaps not coincide with the ritual. 

Even if spirits are not enclosed in statues by astronomical logic, yet when they are present through the worship offered to them, Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Plotinus.</note> says they gave oracles according to astronomical rules, and for that reason, they were frequently ambiguous. And rightly so, since Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist who defended the use of "theurgy" or ritual to connect with the divine.</note> proves that true and certain prophecy does not suit evil spirits, nor does it come from human arts or nature, but comes through divine inspiration to purified minds. 

But let us now return to Hermes <note>original: "Mercurium," referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage of the Hermetic tradition.</note>—or rather, to Plotinus. Hermes says that priests received a power from the nature of the world that was suitable to it, and mixed it. Following him, Plotinus thinks that all of this can be accomplished by winning over the Soul of the World <note>original: "aia mūdi" (anima mundi). The vital force that Neoplatonists believed connected the physical world to the divine.</note>. Insofar as she generates and moves the forms of natural things through certain **seminal reasons** <term>seminal reasons: "rationes seminales," the formative principles or "seeds" within the Soul of the World that organize matter into specific shapes and types.</term> implanted in her by the divine. He even calls these "reasons" gods, since they are never abandoned by the Ideas of the supreme mind. 

Therefore, through such "reasons," the Soul of the World easily applies itself to materials which it formed from the beginning through those same reasons, whenever a magician or priest has applied, at the proper times, the forms of things duly collected, which specifically relate to this or that reason; just as a magnet attracts iron, rhubarb treats bile, saffron the heart, agrimony and burnt ash the liver, and spikenard and musk the brain. 

It may truly happen sometimes that when "reasons" are thus applied to forms, even higher gifts descend; insofar as the reasons in the Soul of the World are joined to the stars—to the powers and forms of that same soul—and through them to the Ideas of the Divine Mind. Iamblichus also approves of this where he discusses sacrifices. On this matter, we shall debate others at a more opportune time. There it will also appear how impure the superstition of the pagan people was, and on the other hand, how pure the Gospel piety was; which we have already done for the most part in our book *On the Christian Religion*.

<meta>This page serves as the introductory "Apology" (defense) for Marsilio Ficino’s influential work "De vita libri tres" (Three Books on Life), published in 1489. Ficino is addressing potential criticisms regarding his roles as both a priest and a scholar of medicine and astrology.</meta>

-># A Certain Apology. In which concerning medicine, astrology,<-
->and the life of the world. Also concerning the Magi who<-
->greeted Christ immediately after his birth.<-

**MARSILIO Ficino** the Florentine to his most beloved brothers in long-standing study, the three **Pieros**—Nero, Guicciardini, and Soderini—three and four times greetings. Perhaps I should have just said "Triple-Peter" instead of the three Pieros. For just as where there is one palm, the several fingers on it do not create several hands, so your three bodies, my friends, seem to present no obstacle to your single will making you one Peter <note>A play on the Latin name "Petrus," which means "rock." Ficino is comparing his three friends, all named Piero/Peter, to a single solid foundation.</note>. That craftsman of the heavenly homeland, Christ, brought forth so great a rock <note>Original: "petram"</note> that this single rock was sufficient for founding the immense edifice of the Church. I also, by a certain divine fate, have obtained such great rocks that three now suffice for my own quite arduous building.

Now, my friends, I do not know whether the shield of **Pallas** <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom; her shield or "aegis" provided divine protection</note> might seem necessary to you, by which we might ward off the savage attack of those very giants <note>A metaphorical reference to the "Gigantomachy" or battle of the giants, used here to represent harsh critics or "monstrous" intellectual opponents</note> far from us. Therefore, within your citadel constructed of three Pieros, I have decided to fortify the life of my three books as they come to the aid of the public life.

You know (as I believe) that I have composed a book on life, divided into three small volumes. The first of these is *On Healthy Life* <note>Original: "de uita humana" (on human life), usually titled "De Vita Sana"</note>. The second: *On Long Life* <note>Original: "de uita longa," usually titled "De Vita Longa"</note>. The third: *On Obtaining Life from the Heavens* <note>Original: "de uita celitus," usually titled "De Vita Coelitus Comparanda"</note>. Therefore, since the title is so sweet, it will entice many to taste it; but in such a great number, there will be many who are ignorant (as I suppose) and not a few who are malicious. 

One man will therefore say: "Is Marsilio not a priest?" He is, indeed. "Then what business do priests have with medicine? What, again, have they to do with astrology?" Another will likewise ask: "What has a Christian to do with **magic** or with **images**?" <note>Ficino’s "natural magic" involved using astrological timing and physical objects like talismans/images to promote health, which was controversial to some church authorities.</note> Yet another, unworthy of life himself, will envy the life of an equal. Finally, all those so inclined will be far too ungrateful for our benefit toward them. And against our charity—by which we consult the prosperity of the public life according to the capacity of our intellect—they will not be ashamed to be cruel. This labor will therefore be shared with you; but let it be somewhat lighter, for there are three of you against...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino introduces his "Three Books on Life" by dedicating them to three friends named Piero, seeking their protection against critics who might question why a priest is writing about medicine, astrology, and natural magic.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Three Books on Life, medicine, astrology, natural magic, Pallas Athena, priesthood and science, Florentine humanism</keywords>

<meta>This page appears to be part of a defense or a treatise concerning the dual role of the priest and the physician, likely written by Marsilio Ficino to Francesco Neri. It continues an argument for why a spiritual leader should also understand the physical and astrological arts.</meta>

...the struggle was suddenly distributed among three enemies. You will not refute this invective with another invective (for I know your character), but rather you will overcome the bitterness of another’s bile with your own sweetness and the wonderful delight of your honey.

In the beginning, most brilliant Neri, I will respond to the first points: that in ancient times, the most venerable priests were simultaneously physicians and <term>astronomers</term> <note>original: "astronomos"; in this context, it refers to the study of celestial influences on health.</note>. The histories of the Chaldeans, Persians, and Egyptians certainly testify to this. Furthermore, the duties of singular charity belong to no one more than to a pious priest; indeed, these duties shine brightest in the greatest of all acts of service.

The most excellent duty is undoubtedly that which is also most necessary and especially desired by everyone: namely, to ensure that people possess <note>original: "mens sana in corpore sano"</note> **a sound mind in a sound body**. However, we can only provide this if we join medicine to the priesthood. Since medicine—as the famous Hippocrates and Galen admit, and as we ourselves have experienced—is often empty and even harmful without the favor of the heavenly bodies, it follows that astronomy belongs to that same priestly charity to which we said medicine pertains.

I believe the Holy Scriptures command that such a physician be honored, because the Most High created him for this necessity. And Christ himself, the giver of life—who commanded his disciples to heal the sick throughout the whole world—will also command priests that if they cannot heal with words (as they once did), they should at least heal with herbs and stones. If these are not sufficient on their own, he will command that they be combined by a favorable **influence of the heavens** <note>original: "afflatu caeli"; referring to the Renaissance belief that the timing of a cure must align with astrological movements.</note> and applied to the sick.

For He Himself, by that same heavenly influence, stirs animals everywhere to seek their own medicine; so abundantly does He provide for the life of all. Thus, by a divinely inspired heavenly instinct, serpents treat their eyes with <term>fennel</term> <note>original: "maratro"</note>, and swallows heal theirs with <term>celandine</term> <note>original: "celidonia"</note>. Eagles, when troubled by the pains of labor, have divinely discovered the <term>eagle-stone</term> <note>original: "echisem" (likely a variant of aetites); a stone believed in ancient lore to assist in successful egg-laying and childbirth.</note>, by which they might immediately and successfully bring forth their eggs. 

Therefore, God Himself, who through the heavens goads all animals toward medicines, certainly permits priests—not for profit, I say, but out of charity—to expel diseases through medicines confirmed by the heavens. To these points, you may then add more if it serves the sharp wit of your own mind.

<summary>This page argues for the historical and divine precedent of priests acting as physicians and astronomers, suggesting that spiritual and physical healing are naturally linked. The author uses examples from nature—such as eagles and serpents—to demonstrate how God provides medical knowledge through celestial instinct.</summary>

<meta>This page appears to be a defense of Marsilio Ficino's work on "natural magic," likely from a preface or apology regarding his book *On Obtaining Life from the Heavens* (De Vita Coelitus Comparanda). The author addresses a "Guicciardini," likely Luigi Guicciardini, a friend of Ficino.</meta>

Arise now, you too, passionate Guicciardini, and answer those inquisitive minds: Magic or the use of images <note>In this context, "images" refers to astrological talismans or symbols used to capture planetary influences.</note> are not actually "approved" by Marsilio <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), the influential Florentine philosopher who revived Neoplatonism.</note>; rather, they are merely described as he interprets Plotinus himself. His writings clearly state this, if they are read with an open mind. Not a single word is asserted here concerning "profane magic," which relies on the worship of demons. Instead, mention is made of *natural magic*, which seeks to capture celestial benefits through natural objects for the sake of the body's health.

Indeed, such a skill should be granted to those who use their minds legitimately, just as medicine and agriculture are rightly permitted. It is all the more worthy because it is more perfect, joining celestial powers to earthly things through diligent effort. It was from this very workshop that the Magi—the first of all people—worshipped Christ immediately after His birth. Why then, you fearful person, do you tremble at the name "Magus"? <note>original: "Magi nomẽ". The author is defending the term against its association with witchcraft.</note> It is a name honored by the Gospel. It does not signify an evildoer or a poisoner, but rather a wise man and a priest. 

What, then, does that Magus—the first to venerate Christ—actually profess? You may be reluctant to hear it: he is like a kind of farmer; he is certainly a "cultivator of the world" <note>original: "mundicola". A term coined to show the Magus cares for the world as a farmer cares for a field.</note>. Nor does he worship the world in this role, any more than a farmer worships the soil. But just as the farmer prepares his field to meet the air for the sake of human sustenance, so that wise man—that priest—prepares the lower things of the world to meet the higher things for the sake of human health. Just as a hen sits on her eggs, he opportunely places earthly things beneath the heavens to be nourished. 

God Himself does this constantly; by His actions, He teaches and encourages us to do the same, so that the lowest things may be generated, moved, and governed by the highest. Ultimately, there are two kinds of magic. One belongs to those who win over demons through specific rituals; relying on the works of these spirits, they often create illusions. This form, however, was utterly banished when the "prince of this world" <note>A biblical reference to the Devil (John 12:31), whose power was broken by Christ.</note> was cast out. 

The other kind belongs to those who skillfully subject natural materials to natural causes, to be shaped by a certain wondrous logic. This craft also has two types: one is merely "curious," and the other is "necessary." The curious type fashions useless wonders for the sake of show—for instance, when the Persian Magi take saliva putrefied under manure while the Sun and Moon are in the second face of Leo... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the "second face of Leo" is an astrological timing used for specific magical or alchemical operations.</note>

->m i<-

<vocab>Magic, Demons, Magi, farmer, Persians</vocab>

<summary>This text defends Marsilio Ficino's concept of 'natural magic,' distinguishing it from demonic sorcery and likening it to a sacred form of 'world-farming' that uses celestial influences for healing. It justifies the practice by citing the biblical Magi and the hierarchical order of nature established by God.</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, Marsilio Ficino, Magi, Plotinus, demons, astrology, agriculture, medicine</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the distinction between deceptive "glamour" magic and the legitimate study of the vital forces of the universe (Natural Magic). The author is addressing his contemporaries, likely within the circle of the Florentine Academy.</meta>

...they might occupy or hold the same degree there. Moreover, they produced a creature like a **blackbird** <note>original: "merale", likely "merulam" (blackbird). This refers to a specific "natural magic" trick mentioned in medieval grimoires.</note> with a serpent’s tail; when this was reduced to ashes and poured into a **lamp** <note>original: "lampadi"</note>, the house immediately appeared to be full of serpents. This practice, being as vain, false, and harmful as it is, must be utterly avoided. 

Nevertheless, a necessary form must be maintained, joining <term>medicine with astrology</term> <note>The author argues for a scientific fusion of healing and celestial influence, distinguishing it from deceptive sorcery.</note>. But if anyone should stubbornly persist further, deal with him in this way, **Guicciardini** <note>Francesco Guicciardini (1483–1540), the famous Italian historian and friend of the author.</note>: let him not read these writings of ours, nor understand them, nor remember them; such a man—if he is even a man—is entirely unworthy of so great a benefit in this age. Furthermore, there are many things which you, with your own intellect, will be able to bring forward against such ungrateful ignorance. 

And what about you, our active **Soderini** <note>Likely referring to a member of the prominent Florentine Soderini family, supporters of the Platonic Academy.</note>? Will you tolerate these future crimes of superstition—I know not whose—committed by those who see manifest life in animals, even the lowliest ones, and in the commonest herbs, yet they do not see it in the heavens, nor in the world at large? Now indeed, if these "little men" grant life to the smallest particles of the world, what madness is this? What envy? That they neither know nor wish to acknowledge that the whole universe lives—that world in which **"we live, and move, and have our being"** <note>original: "in quo uiuimus & mouemur & ſumus". This is a direct quote from the Apostle Paul in Acts 17:28.</note>. 

This is exactly what **Aratus** <note>A Greek poet (c. 315–240 BCE). His poem "Phaenomena" was frequently cited by Christians because St. Paul quoted it.</note> sings of, clearly using the name "Jupiter" to signify the common life of the worldly body. I ask: have these words of Aratus been reported opportunely? I seem to have stumbled upon them somehow. I remember that **Luke the Evangelist** and **Paul the Apostle** used these words willingly. In these words, those wise men do not shudder at the idea of a "world life" <note>The *Anima Mundi* or World Soul.</note>. 

Yet, some superstitious person will object to this; he is not easily convinced by such words that Paul was asserting the world has a soul, but rather that it is simply subject to God, and that we live in God himself. So be it, then. Let us not use the word "soul" in relation to the world since it is displeasing to you. Let the name "soul" be considered profane. But might we at least be allowed to speak of some kind of "life"? A life which God himself, the craftsman of the world, mercifully <note>The OCR reads "dementer" (madly), but the context and other editions suggest "clementer" (mercifully) or "divinitus" (divinely).</note> breathed into this work of His, so happily completed? Since He is not stingy toward even the lowliest living things, does He not most generously bestow life through the heavens and upon the many things within them? 

Tell me, I pray, do you not see oxen and asses—O you ox and ass!—who, through a certain touch, generate living things from themselves and are alive? If, therefore, these things are furthermore living from themselves...

<summary>The author (Marsilio Ficino) defends the concept of the World Soul (Anima Mundi) by citing St. Paul and the poet Aratus, arguing that if even low animals possess life, the entire cosmos must also be a living entity created by God.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Anima Mundi, Marsilio Ficino, Guicciardini, Soderini, Aratus, St. Paul, Natural Magic, Astrology, Medicine</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical defense of the animated nature of the universe, likely written by Marsilio Ficino to his circle of friends in the Platonic Academy. It transitions from abstract cosmology to a call for intellectual allies.</meta>

...if certain things generate even by a look, would you not judge these things to be much more alive, if you have any judgment or life in yourself? 
The Sky, the husband of the Earth, does not touch the Earth (as is the common opinion). He does not lie with his wife; instead, he illuminates her from every side with the rays of his stars as if they were eyes. By illuminating her, he makes her fertile and produces living things. <note>original: "lustrando fecundat / procreatoque uiuentia"</note> Does he, who bestows life simply by looking, possess no life of his own? And is that which gave or constructed life and a life-giving gaze somehow inferior because of this? If these arguments are brought forward and you are still not convinced, cast away that superstitious "half-living" thought—or rather, that non-living thought.

Therefore, so that we may plead our case with more advocates <term>patronis (patrons/advocates): Ficino uses legal language to describe his friends defending his philosophical ideas</term>, add our **Amphion**, the famous Cristoforo Landino, who is equally an orator and a poet. Our Amphion, with his wondrous sweetness, will quickly soften the stony hearts of our enemies. <note>In Greek myth, Amphion was a singer whose music was so powerful he could move stones to build the walls of Thebes.</note> 

But you, my dearest godfather **Guicciardini**, go now, go briskly! Summon the "Hercules" Poliziano. Once, when a more dangerous battle was to be fought, Hercules would call for Iolaus; now, you likewise call for Hercules. <note>original: "Politianum Herculem accerfito"</note> You surely know how many barbarian monsters now devastating Latium <note>Latium refers to the region around Rome, but here represents the Latin language and culture being "corrupted" by non-humanist scholars.</note> our "Hercules" Poliziano has attacked, torn apart, and slain, and how fiercely he fights them everywhere. He will immediately crush with his club and burn with flames even the hundred heads of the hydra <note>The Hydra was a multi-headed monster slain by Hercules; here it represents the many-headed arguments of Ficino's critics.</note> threatening our **children**. <note>Ficino frequently referred to his books and philosophical translations as his "children."</note>

Come, my sweetest **Soderini**! Rise and go; greet Pico, the servant of Phoebus Apollo. I often call him my Phoebus, and he in turn calls me Dionysus and Liber. Therefore, we are brothers. <note>Apollo (Phoebus) and Dionysus (Liber) were both sons of Zeus. Ficino is highlighting his deep intellectual bond with Giovanni Pico della Mirandola.</note> Tell my Phoebus that a poisonous python <note>In mythology, the Python was a dragon-monster slain by Apollo.</note> is even now emerging from the marsh against us. Let him draw his bow, I pray you. Let him shoot his arrows immediately. He will draw it at once, for he knows what I mean; he will kill all the poison at once with a single death.

Farewell now, my most beloved brothers—you who are worthy not only of good health, but of happiness itself. Take care of the health and happiness of my **children** <gloss>his books</gloss> who are now coming out into the light. 

15 September 1488. 
At the villa in Careggi. <note>Careggi was the location of the Medici villa where the Platonic Academy met.</note>

<summary>Marsilio Ficino uses vivid mythological metaphors to call upon his famous colleagues—Landino, Poliziano, and Pico della Mirandola—to defend his philosophical works (his "children") against critics. He argues for a living, fertile universe where the Heavens animate the Earth through light.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Cristoforo Landino, Angelo Poliziano, Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, Platonic Academy, Careggi, animation of the world, humanism, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory correspondence of Marsilio Ficino to his circle of Platonic friends. Following the previous page's recruitment of Landino, Guicciardini, and Poliziano, Ficino now addresses three more companions—Canigiani, Canaccio, and Corsini—using a playful but philosophically loaded metaphor of the "hunt for truth."</meta>

# Why security and
## tranquility of mind are necessary for life.

Marsilio Ficino sends greetings to his dearest brothers in the hunt for truth: Bernardo Canigiani, Giovanni Canaccio, and Amerigo Corsini. Since we had initially begun a sort of "hunt" in these words, perhaps we appropriately introduced hounds and runners right away. We quite fitly called those who practice philosophy "hunters," as they are always laboring in the breathless pursuit of truth. But was it also appropriate to call them "dogs"? Socrates says it is most appropriate in the *Republic*. <note>In Plato's Republic, Socrates compares the guardians of the state to well-bred dogs who are gentle to those they know and fierce toward strangers.</note>

For those who practice philosophy are either legitimate or false. Both are "dogs." The legitimate ones sagaciously track truth itself and, once found, hold onto it with a firm bite. The false ones, however, bark, bite, and tear for the sake of mere opinion. Indeed, dogs claim such a place among philosophers that they not only inserted themselves into a particular sect, but even established their own sect under the name of **Cynic** <note>original: "Cynicam" — derived from the Greek word for dog, 'kyon'. The Cynics were known for their biting wit and rejection of social norms.</note> at one time. Furthermore, the Academy <note>The "Academy" refers to Ficino's Platonic Academy in Florence.</note> has its own dogs. 

Therefore, I call upon you here, you sagacious dogs of the Academy; I call upon you, most swift runners. For there are three of you. I pray you, defend my three children <term>liberos (children): Ficino frequently used "children" as a metaphor for his books or newly written philosophical treatises</term>, who are still quite young and tender, and who (as I fear) will soon go out among the wolves. Run, I say, with alacrity! For I am entrusting you now with tasks you desire, not with burdensome cares. 

You know my dear **Giorgio Benigno Salviato**: he has long since sagaciously attained that truth whose tracks you are now hunting for everywhere. Like a sun, he—being greater—illuminates his smaller brothers. Therefore, if you hear any howling of wolves, report it to him. That most valiant Giorgio will easily put all the wolves to flight, for he once even pierced through a vast dragon. <note>Giorgio Benigno Salviato was a Franciscan theologian; the "dragon" likely refers to a specific theological opponent or a work of heresy he successfully refuted.</note> He will therefore relieve me of anxiety and you of care at the same time. Indeed, one of you is accustomed—and quite often now—to say: nothing more healthy is experienced in life than to "swallow down" <note>original: "deglutire" — a vivid metaphor for consuming or enduring time peacefully.</note> the times with great security. And the others immediately smile in agreement with the speaker.

<summary>Marsilio Ficino uses the Socratic metaphor of philosophers as "hunting dogs" to ask his friends to protect his latest writings from critics ("wolves"). He places his trust in the theologian Giorgio Benigno Salviato to defend his work.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Socrates, Republic, Cynics, Platonic Academy, Giorgio Benigno Salviato, metaphors of hunting, tranquility of mind</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the letter from Marsilio Ficino to Bernardo Canigiani, Giovanni Canaccio, and Amerigo Corsini. In the previous section, Ficino established a metaphor comparing philosophers to "hunting dogs" of the Academy. He now turns his attention specifically to Canaccio, addressing a favorite phrase of his regarding the consumption of time and the necessity of maintaining a "broad" and joyful soul.</meta>

But come now, Canaccio, what is this phrase of yours that is so often repeated? "Devour the times"? <note>original: "Tempora deuorate" — Ficino is playfully addressing a specific motto or habit of his friend Giovanni Canaccio.</note> What, finally, do you mean by it? You will say it is not to "be," but rather to drink; not to chew or grind away, but to swallow with full throats. This is because time itself is a certain fluid nature (so to speak) and slippery. 

Indeed, this is the condition of fluid things: if you confine them in a narrow space, you lose them suddenly. For when they are compressed, they flow apart and quickly escape. If you happen to squeeze water soaked into a sponge, you will press it out and immediately scatter it. If you hold it more loosely, you will retain it. This is even more true of air, fire, and the ether. Hence, among the poets, those struggle in vain who strive to grasp the vast images of the divine spirits <note>original: "diuorum manium" — referring to the ghosts or shades of the departed in classical poetry, who slip through the arms of those trying to embrace them, like Virgil's Creusa or Homer's Achilles and Patroclus.</note> with their arms. The broadest things must be received very broadly. Fluid and very vast things must be possessed in the most spacious way. 

Then, certainly, narrowness <term>angustia (a word meaning both a narrow place and mental distress)</term> presses upon us heavily when we reduce the soul itself and its motion—which is naturally vast—into a narrow space. Whoever weighs their studies and business with excessive precision, and always grinds down into every tiny detail with the most exacting scrutiny: meanwhile, alas, they miserably and secretly wear away their own life. 

Rightly, therefore, Pythagoras <note>Pythagoras was a Greek philosopher whose "Golden Verses" and symbolic sayings were highly influential in Ficino’s Neoplatonic circle.</note> seems to have commanded: "Take care lest you happen to be confined in a narrow space." Nothing is more vast than the heavens. Nothing is more full of life. In turn, the narrow earth has the least life in the world. Finally, if we live by the heavens and by time, the more broadly we absorb these, the more and the longer we live. 

Live, therefore, broadly <note>original: "lati" — Ficino is making a pun on the Latin words "latus" (wide/broad) and "laetus" (joyful). To live a "wide" life is to live a "happy" life.</note> and far from narrowness, O friends. Live joyfully. Your joy created the heaven for you. It declares this by its own sort of laughter—that is, by expansion, motion, and splendor—as if exulting. Your joy will preserve heaven for you. Therefore, live joyfully every day in the present. For anxiety about present things snatches the present from you and forestalls the future. Curiosity about future things quickly leads you back into the past. 

Again, therefore, I pray, and again: live joyfully. For the fates allow it as long as you live free from care. But so that you may truly live without care, do not take up even this one care: that you should ever worry anxiously by what particular diligence you might escape cares. For this one care, alas, burns the heart of wretched mortals with every worry. Therefore, neglect dili... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "diligence" or "diligent care" on the following page.</note>

<summary>Ficino argues that time and the soul are fluid and vast, requiring a "broad" and joyful disposition rather than narrow anxiety to be truly experienced. He warns his friends that excessive worry about the future or precision in business destroys the quality of life.</summary>
<keywords>time, joy (laetitia), narrowness (angustia), Pythagoras, soul, fluid nature, anxiety, present moment</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page concludes the letter from Marsilio Ficino to his friends, which began on the previous page with a discussion on avoiding the "narrowness" of soul. The first word on this page, "gence," completes the word "diligence" from the preceding text.</meta>

...gence. Indeed, you should love negligence, and love it negligently! As much as it is permitted to you, I say, and as much as is proper. I offer this advice to you not so much as a priest playing the part of a friend, but as a doctor. For without this one thing—which is, as it were, the very life of all medicines—all medicines applied to prolong life simply die. <note>Ficino is using a medical metaphor here. He suggests that over-exertion and "diligence" in study can be fatal to the health of scholars, and that a relaxed "negligence" or leisure is the essential ingredient that makes all other health remedies work.</note>

September 16, 1489. In the countryside at Careggi. <note>Careggi was the site of the Medici family villa where Ficino lived and lead the Platonic Academy.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the technical printing register (the "Registrum" on the previous page) to the formal Table of Contents for Marsilio Ficino’s seminal work, *Platonic Theology*. While the previous page helped the bookbinder organize the sheets of paper, this page introduces the intellectual structure of the treatise, which seeks to prove the immortality of the soul using Platonic philosophy and Christian doctrine.</meta>

# Chapters of the books of theology on the immortality of souls by Marsilio Ficino the Florentine, divided into eighteen books. The first book ascends all the way to God.

### Chapters of the first book.

Chapter 1. If the soul were not immortal, no animal would be more unhappy than man.
Chapter 2. The body, by its own nature, does nothing.
Chapter 3. Above the divided form in the body stands an individual form: that is, the soul. <note>original: "forma indiuidua .i. aīa"; Ficino argues that while bodies can be divided into parts, the soul is an indivisible unit.</note>
Chapter 4. The rational soul is immobile through its substance; it is mobile through its operation; through its power, it is partly immobile and partly mobile.
Chapter 5. Above the mobile soul is the immobile angel.
Chapter 6. Above the angel is God. Since the soul is a mobile multiplicity, the angel is an immobile multiplicity, and God is immobile unity. <note>Ficino uses these categories to create a logical "ladder" leading from the physical world up to the Divine.</note>

## THE SECOND Book disputes concerning God, who has now been found.

Chapter 1. Unity, truth, and goodness are the same; and above them, there is nothing.
Chapter 2. There are not multiple gods equal to one another.
Chapter 3. There are not multiple gods, one above another, without end.
Chapter 4. God’s power is infinite.
Chapter 5. God always is. <note>original: "Deus ſemper eſt"; meaning God exists eternally and outside of time.</note>
Chapter 6. God is everywhere.
Chapter 7. God acts upon and preserves all things; and in all things, He works all things.
Chapter 8. God does whatever He does through His own being.
Chapter 9. God understands Himself first, and also each individual thing.
Chapter 10. God understands infinite things.
Chapter 11. God has a will; and through it, He brings all things into being outside of Himself.
Chapter 12. The will of God is at once necessary and free, and it acts freely.
Chapter 13. God loves and provides. <note>original: "Deus amat & puidet"; referring to Divine Providence.</note>

## THE THIRD BOOK descends from God and compares the degrees of things in turn to the middle degree, and this middle degree to the others.

Chapter 1. The descent occurs through five degrees, through which the ascent was made; these degrees are fittingly compared to one another. <note>Ficino’s "Five Degrees of Being" are: Body, Quality, Soul, Angel, and God.</note>
Chapter 2. The soul is the middle degree of things and connects all degrees, both higher and lower, into one; for it both ascends to the higher things and descends to the lower things. <term>Copula Mundi<gloss>The bond or "knot" of the world</gloss></term>

<summary>This page lists the chapters for the first three books of Marsilio Ficino’s Platonic Theology, outlining a metaphysical hierarchy that places the human soul as the vital "middle degree" connecting the physical and divine realms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Table of Contents for Marsilio Ficino’s *Platonic Theology*. Having established the existence of God in the first three books, Ficino now turns his focus to the "Middle Degree" of the universe: the Soul. This section outlines the structure of the fourth, fifth, and sixth books, which categorize types of souls and provide a systematic series of logical proofs for the soul's immortality.</meta>

## THE FOURTH Book divides into its specific kinds the middle degree of things, that is, the soul.

<margin>Chapter 1.</margin> There are three degrees of rational souls. In the first is the soul of the world. In the second are the souls of the spheres. In the third are the souls of living beings that are contained within the individual spheres. <note>Ficino follows the Platonic idea that the universe itself is a living being with a soul (*anima mundi*), and that the celestial bodies (the "spheres") possess their own guiding intelligences.</note>

<margin>Chapter 2.</margin> The souls of the spheres move the spheres through the law of fate; and they move in a circle because they themselves are circles.

## THE FIFTH Book. Treats the immortality of the soul through common reasons.

<margin>Chapter 1.</margin> Every rational soul is immortal. The first reason is because it moves by itself and in a circle. <note>original: "p̄ se mouetur & in circulum"; In Platonic thought, "circular motion" refers to the soul's ability to return to its own source through self-reflection.</note>

<margin>Chapter 2.</margin> The second reason: because it stands through its own substance.

<margin>Chapter 3.</margin> The third reason: because it clings to divine things.

<margin>Chapter 4.</margin> The fourth reason: because it rules over matter.

<margin>Chapter 5.</margin> The fifth reason: because it is free from matter.

<margin>Chapter 6.</margin> The sixth reason: because it is indivisible.

<margin>Chapter 7.</margin> The seventh reason: because it has its being in its own essence.

<margin>Chapter 8.</margin> The eighth reason: because it has its own proper being and never departs from its form.

<margin>Chapter 9.</margin> The ninth reason: because being belongs to it through itself.

<margin>Chapter 10.</margin> The tenth reason: because it is referred to God through itself.

<margin>Chapter 11.</margin> The eleventh reason: because it is not composed of any potentiality into which it could be broken down.

<margin>Chapter 12.</margin> The twelfth reason: because it does not have in itself the potentiality for not being.

<margin>Chapter 13.</margin> The thirteenth reason: because it receives being from God without a medium. <note>Ficino argues that the soul is created directly by God, rather than being a byproduct of physical processes.</note>

<margin>Chapter 14.</margin> The fourteenth reason: because it is life through itself.

<margin>Chapter 15.</margin> The fifteenth reason: because life is more excellent than the body.

## THE SIXTH Book. Treats the soul through specific reasons showing that it is indivisible, even regarding the vegetative part. <note>The "vegetative part" (uegetatiua) refers to the soul's lowest functions, such as growth and nutrition, which Ficino argues are still connected to the soul's indivisible essence.</note>

<margin>Chapter 1.</margin> A reduction of opinions about the soul into five headings.

<margin>Chapter 2.</margin> Common philosophers, not persuaded by any reason but deceived by a corrupt habit, have posited that the soul is corporeal. <note>original: "Plebei philosophi... corporalem animam posuerunt"; Ficino refers to materialist thinkers (like the Epicureans) who believe the soul is made of matter and dies with the body.</note>

<summary>This page lists the chapters for Books 4 through 6 of Ficino's work, detailing the hierarchy of souls (from the world-soul to individual animals) and presenting fifteen logical arguments for the immortality of the rational soul.</summary>

<keywords>immortality of the soul, anima mundi, celestial spheres, rational soul, vegetative soul, metaphysics, Platonic philosophy, hierarchy of being</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Table of Contents for Marsilio Ficino’s *Platonic Theology*. The previous page concluded with the initial arguments for the immortality of the soul in Book 5. This page presents the final chapters of Book 6—which focuses on the soul’s distinction from the physical body—and the beginning of Book 7, which addresses the soul’s indivisibility in relation to sensory perception.</meta>

<margin>Chapter 3.</margin> The soul is not a body, nor a form divided within a body: as is demonstrated through the soul's natural power.

<margin>Chapter 4.</margin> First reason: because the soul is the principle of nourishment, and of operating in any other way whatsoever.

<margin>Chapter 5.</margin> Second reason: it is neither an animate body nor an inanimate one. <note>Ficino argues that if the soul were a body, we would need to ask if that "soul-body" was itself alive or dead, leading to an infinite loop.</note>

<margin>Chapter 6.</margin> Third reason: it is neither a dense body nor a subtle one.

<margin>Chapter 7.</margin> Fourth reason. A body is composed of matter and form.

<margin>Chapter 8.</margin> Fifth reason. A body is by its nature dispersed.

<margin>Chapter 9.</margin> Sixth reason. Two bodies are not in the same place.

<margin>Chapter 10.</margin> Seventh reason. A body is not whole in many parts at the same time.

<margin>Chapter 11.</margin> Eighth reason. The soul is life or the source of life.

<margin>Chapter 12.</margin> Ninth reason. A body does not move itself.

<margin>Chapter 13.</margin> Tenth reason. The soul does not grow according to the growth of this physical body.

---

### THE SEVENTH Book. Concerning the soul, that it is indivisible in regard to its sensitive part.

---

<margin>Chapter 1.</margin> The soul is not a body: nor a form divided within a body: nor some point of a radial form; but it is a form whole in any part of the body: as is shown through the power of sensing. <note>original: "ralalis [radialis] forme"; Ficino is arguing against the idea that the soul is just a single point—like the center of a circle—or a physical shape spread out like light rays.</note>

<margin>Chapter 2.</margin> First reason. A body does not receive images suited for sense perception. Second reason. It is necessary for the five senses to be led to a single center. <note>This refers to the "common sense" (*sensus communis*), the faculty that coordinates input from eyes, ears, etc., into a single experience.</note>

<margin>Chapter 3.</margin> Third reason. The more concentrated a sense is, the more perceptive it is.

<margin>Chapter 4.</margin> Fourth reason. If the sense were divisible, the sensible image within it would be divided.

<margin>Chapter 5.</margin> Fifth reason. The soul feels as a whole everywhere.

<margin>Chapter 6.</margin> Sixth reason. The soul does not necessarily suffer in the act of feeling. <note>original: "patitur"; In medieval philosophy, "suffering" means to be acted upon or changed by an external force. Ficino argues the soul remains its own master even when receiving sensory input.</note>

<margin>Chapter 7.</margin> Seventh reason. Specific reasons through the natural and sensory power of the soul showing that the soul is not a divisible form: because the work of a quality is one.

<margin>Chapter 8.</margin> Eighth reason. The temperament <term>complexio: the physical constitution or balance of the four humors</term> acts through the power of qualities.

<margin>Chapter 9.</margin> Ninth reason. The temperament is an accidental form. <note>An "accidental form" is a non-essential quality, such as hair color or health, as opposed to the "substantial form" which makes a human a human.</note>

<margin>Chapter 10.</margin> Tenth reason. The harmony of the temperament does nothing to the body.

<margin>Chapter 11.</margin> Eleventh reason. Harmony exists in every composite body.

<summary>This page lists the logical proofs from Books 6 and 7 of the Platonic Theology, focusing on why the soul cannot be a physical body and how it maintains unity across the five senses. Ficino argues that the soul must be an indivisible "form" because it can process complex sensory data into a single coherent experience.</summary>
<keywords>incorporeality of the soul, five senses, common sense, matter and form, temperament, harmony, indivisibility, sensory perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Table of Contents for Marsilio Ficino’s *Platonic Theology*. It concludes the arguments from Book 7—which posits that the soul cannot be a mere "harmony" or physical proportion of the body—and introduces the first thirteen arguments of Book 8, which focuses on the immortality of the soul's intellectual power.</meta>

<margin>Chapter 12.</margin> Twelfth reason. Harmony is understood either as the actual composition of the limbs, or as a certain ratio <note>or proportion</note> of that composition.

<margin>Chapter 13.</margin> Thirteenth reason. A harmony of humors <note>The "humors" refer to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—which ancient medicine believed governed health and temperament.</note> receives nothing without matter.

<margin>Chapter 14.</margin> Fourteenth reason. That which is more in accord is more of a harmony.

<margin>Chapter 15.</margin> Fifteenth reason. Harmony admits nothing dissonant.

### THE EIGHTH Book. Concerning the soul, specifically its intellectual part, since it is indivisible and therefore immortal.

<margin>Chapter 1.</margin> First reason. The soul is an indivisible form, whole in every part; it draws no origin from matter, and is therefore immortal, as is demonstrated through the power of understanding. The mind ascends through four stages to the spirit. <note>In Ficino’s Neoplatonism, "spirit" often refers to the subtle, intermediary vapor that links the physical body to the immaterial soul.</note>

<margin>Chapter 2.</margin> Second reason. The soul is nourished by truth.

<margin>Chapter 3.</margin> Third reason. Because the soul’s power is indivisible, it cannot be a quality of the body.

<margin>Chapter 4.</margin> Fourth reason. An intelligible form <note>original: "species"</note> or concept cannot be received by a body.

<margin>Chapter 5.</margin> Fifth reason. If the mind were a body, it would understand by touching or by running. <note>Ficino uses this absurd imagery to show that physical motion or contact cannot produce abstract thought.</note>

<margin>Chapter 6.</margin> Sixth reason. The intellect receives whatever it grasps according to its whole self; it would not do this if it were corporeal <note>physical</note>.

<margin>Chapter 7.</margin> Seventh reason. In receiving the forms of things, the intellect does not lose its own form. Bodies, by contrast, lose theirs. <note>For example, if water becomes hot, it loses its coldness; but the mind can think of "heat" without losing its own nature.</note>

<margin>Chapter 8.</margin> Eighth reason. Form exists in the mind as a universal. Such a thing cannot exist in a body. <note>A "universal" refers to a general concept—like the idea of "Humanity"—which applies to all people, whereas a body is always a specific, individual thing.</note>

<margin>Chapter 9.</margin> Ninth reason. Minds embrace one another. Bodies do not.

<margin>Chapter 10.</margin> Tenth reason. The mind operates through all bodies and above bodies.

<margin>Chapter 11.</margin> Eleventh reason. The mind is perfected by stillness <note>original: "statu"</note>. The body is perfected by motion.

<margin>Chapter 12.</margin> Twelfth reason. Things that are opposites in matter are not opposites in the mind. <note>For example, the mind can hold the concepts of "cold" and "heat" simultaneously to compare them, whereas a physical object cannot be both at once.</note>

<margin>Chapter 13.</margin> Thirteenth reason. The mind is not changed by receiving forms.

<summary>This page lists the concluding arguments of Book 7 against the soul as a physical harmony and the opening thirteen arguments of Book 8 proving the soul's immortality through its intellectual functions. Ficino emphasizes that the mind's ability to handle universals, opposites, and abstract truths proves it is not a physical entity.</summary>
<keywords>immortality of the soul, intellect, harmony, universals, indivisibility, humors, Neoplatonism, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Table of Contents for Marsilio Ficino’s *Platonic Theology*. It concludes the arguments for Book 8 and outlines the chapters for Books 9 and 10, which transition from the soul's indivisibility to its independence from the physical body and its role in the universal hierarchy.</meta>

<margin>Chapter 14.</margin> Fourteenth reason: the operation of the body is directed toward composite things <note>Things made of multiple parts or matter.</note>. That of the mind is directed toward simple things <note>Pure, indivisible concepts or essences.</note>.
<margin>Chapter 15.</margin> Fifteenth reason: the operation of the mind is fulfilled within the operation itself; the operation of the body is fulfilled in the resulting work <note>original: "opere," referring to a physical product or external result.</note>.
<margin>Chapter 16.</margin> Sixteenth reason: Physical form does not possess infinite power. The mind does.

## BOOK NINE. That the soul is immortal, shown more effectively not only
## by the fact that it is indivisible, but also by the fact that it does not
## depend on the body.

<margin>Chapter 1.</margin> Proven through the rational power. The soul is not only an individual form, but truly does not depend on the body, so that its immortality may be more clearly demonstrated. First rea-
<margin>Chapter 2.</margin> son: because the mind reflects upon itself.
Second reason: the more the mind is separated from the body, the better it fares.
<margin>Chapter 3.</margin> Third reason. The mind resists the body.
<margin>Chapter 4.</margin> Fourth reason. The soul operates freely.
<margin>Chapter 5.</margin> Fifth reason. The mind operates without the body.
<margin>Chapter 6.</margin> Sixth reason. The soul corresponds partly with divine things and partly with the brutes <note>The "brutes" refers to non-rational animals.</note>.
<margin>Chapter 7.</margin> An objection of the Epicureans <note>Followers of the philosopher Epicurus, who argued that the soul was made of atoms and died with the body.</note>: and a response concerning the trial of things.

## BOOK TEN. That the soul is immortal with respect to the
## order of things.

<margin>Chapter 1.</margin> First reason. Just as the last thing in the order of bodies <note>Likely referring to the celestial spheres or heavens, which ancient philosophy held to be physical yet changeless.</note> is incorruptible, so too is the last thing in the order of minds.
<margin>Chapter 2.</margin> An objection of Epicurus and a response regarding the series of things.
<margin>Chapter 3.</margin> Second reason: Just as in natural things there is a reduction to the first immortal matter <note>Ficino refers here to "prime matter," the underlying substance of the universe that survives all changes of form.</note>; so there is a reduction to the final immortal form.
<margin>Chapter 4.</margin> An objection of Epicurus and a response regarding forms most like God.
<margin>Chapter 5.</margin> A clearer response regarding the grades of forms.
<margin>Chapter 6.</margin> An objection of Lucretius <note>The Roman poet and author of *De Rerum Natura*, who defended the idea that the soul is mortal.</note>: and a response that the mind can operate without the body.

### **THE ELEVENTH BOOK.**
**That the soul is immortal: insofar as it is joined to eternal objects, and from that source receives absolute hopes.**

Chapter 10. The first reason. The mind is joined to an everlasting object, and it receives absolute hopes and eternal principles <note>original: "rationefq; fempiternas." This refers to the "Logoi" or the rational structures that underpin the universe.</note>.

Chapter 11. Objection and response concerning the union of the mind with absolute forms <term>species|In Platonic philosophy, these are the 'blueprints' or ideal versions of things that exist in the divine mind.</term> and eternal principles.

Chapter 5. <note>The numbering in the original text jumps here from 13 to 5.</note> A confirmation of the above through signs.

Chapter 6. The second reason. The mind is the subject of eternal truth.

Chapter 7. An objection from the Sceptics <note>Philosophers who argued that nothing could be known for certain.</note>: and the response that something certain can indeed be known.

Chapter 8. An objection from the Peripatetics <note>The followers of Aristotle, who often argued that knowledge must come through the physical senses.</note>: and the response that truths dwell familiarly within the soul.

### **THE TWELFTH BOOK.**
**That the soul is immortal: because it is formed by the divine mind.**

Chapter 1. Many reasons and signs: The human mind is formed by the act of understanding the divine mind.

Chapter 2. The first question concerning the ascent to God: that is, how the mind is raised up into the divine Idea.

Chapter 3. The second question. For what reason we do not notice at all that we are seeing God while in that state.

Chapter 4. The third question: In what manner God continually pours intelligence into us.

Chapter 5. A confirmation of the previous reason: the first through the sense of sight.

Chapter 6. The second confirmation through hearing, according to Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, the influential Christian theologian who blended Platonic philosophy with scripture.</note>.

Chapter 7. The third confirmation through the mind, according to Augustine.

### **THE THIRTEENTH BOOK.**
**That the soul is immortal through four signs: from prophetic frenzies <note>original: "phrafie affectibus," likely a typo for "phrenesi." This refers to divine inspiration or ecstasy.</note>, from reasons, from effects, and from miraculous arts.**

Chapter 17. Third, because whenever two people understand the same thing, completely absurd consequences follow if they share one mind.

Chapter 18. Fourth, because either the knowledge of many men would be identical, or there would be redundant qualities in the same subject.

Chapter 19. Fifth, because contradictory things would exist in the same subject.

### **THE SIXTEENTH BOOK. It resolves the questions of the Epicureans.**

Chapter 1. The second question follows: Why earthly souls are enclosed in bodies. First reason: so that they may know individual things.

Chapter 2. Second reason: So that individual forms may be reconciled with universal forms.

Chapter 3. Third reason: So that both the divine ray and its formulas <note>original: *formulae*; the specific blueprints or patterns of creation contained within the soul</note> may be reflected back to God.

Chapter 4. Fourth reason: So that the soul may become more blessed <note>Ficino argues that the struggle and experience of bodily life eventually leads to a greater state of joy in the afterlife</note>.

Chapter 5. Fifth reason: So that the lower powers of the soul may proceed toward their realization.

Chapter 6. Sixth reason: So that the world may be adorned and God may be worshipped.

-># CERTAIN ADDITIONS AND CORRECTIONS<-

<meta>This page serves as an "Errata" list—a common feature in early printed books where the author or printer provides a list of corrections for mistakes found after the pages were set. It lists the sheet number, the side of the page, the error, and the intended correction.</meta>

On the **second sheet**, **first side**, it is written *to consume*. Write *to complete*. <note>The Latin 'consumere' (to use up) was mistaken for 'consummare' (to finish or perfect).</note>
On the **second side** of the same sheet: *If by benefit*. So [it should be] *by benefit*.

**Sheet 4, side 2:** *Now what*. [Should be] *Whether*. <note>The Latin 'Nunc quid' was corrected to 'Nunquid', which introduces a question.</note>
**Sheet 5, side 1:** *affixed*. [Should be] *to the fixed*.
Also, *all and altogether*. [Should be] *all and more*.
Also, *by whose firmness*. [Should be] *by whose simplicity*.
Also, **side 2:** *where to follow*. [Should be] *whence*.
Also, *something otherwise*. [Should be] *certain persons*.

**Sheet 6, side 1:** *substantial to that*. [Should be] *substantial of fire*.
Also, **side 2:** *which properly*. [Should be] *proper things*.
Also, *at the same time and quantities*. [Should be] *and quantities*.
Also, *material things*. [Should be] *material things*. <note>Correction of a spelling error in the Latin 'materialia'.</note>

**Sheet 7, side 1:** *or otherwise*. [Should be] *hardly*.
Also, *truly he shows*. [Should be] *he extends*.
Also, **side 2:** *so that we may have*. [Should be] *that we may depart*.
Also, *ever*. [Should be] *never unless*.

**Sheet 8, side 1:** *he said of the body*. [Should be] *he said the adult of the body*.
Also, **side 2:** *the seal*. [Should be] *of any*.
Also, *let us return*. [Should be] *let us sweep back*.

**Sheet 9, side 2:** *to which*. [Should be] *add that which*.

**Sheet 10, side 1:** *otherwise in another way*. [Should be] *in another way*.
Also, **side 2:** *his own hope*. [Should be] *of his own soul, write "animal-like"*. <note>The Latin 'animalis' refers to the soul (anima) or the breath of life.</note>
Also, *he denies in the body*. [Should be] *incorporeal*.

**Sheet 11, side 2:** *dimension in things*. [Should be] *insert "it"*.

**Sheet 16, side 1:** *to those nature*. [Should be] *nature*.

**Sheet 18, side 2:** in Greek *genarcha*. [Should be] *genarcha*. <note>original: "γεναρχα" — This refers to the founder of a race or family; the correction likely addresses a typesetting error in the Greek characters.</note>

**Sheet 21, side 2:** *they had defined*. [Should be] *they had delineated*.
Also, in Greek *kraton*. [Should be] *kraton*. <note>original: "κρατων" — Ruler or governing power.</note>

**Sheet 23, side 2:** *beyond than*. [Should be] *than*.

**Sheet 24, side 1:** *of the worlds*. [Should be] *of the breaths*.
*objective*. Also, *abject*. [Should be] *objective*.
Also, *all things into the measured*. [Should be] *into the immense*.

**Sheet 25, side 1:** *Auetoi* <note>unclear name or term</note> *at the end*. [Should be] *faith*.
Also, *any cheaper things*. [Should be] *insert "and"*.
Also, in Greek *day* [hemera]. *Deta*. *See the other things*. *All the others*. *Loves*. <note>original: "ημερα. Δητα. Δεῖτε ματοσαλλο. παντοσαλλο. ερωσμοσι" — These appear to be fragmentary Greek references or corrections to a philosophical passage.</note>

**Sheet 26, side 1:** *whether western*. [Should be] *accidental*.

**Sheet 27, side 1:** where it is written *the nature of the sense*, the following words which I write below are missing: *if the absent intellect should seek the good, the present intellect embraces it. All of this is the nature of the sense.*

**Sheet 30, side 1:** *is to be*. [Should be] *insert "you yourself"*.

**Sheet 31, side 1:** *this therefore is to take away*. [Should be] *therefore*.
Also, *which he does not want*. [Should be] *which he truly wants*.

**Sheet 32, side 1:** *the end of God*. [Should be] *his own end*.
Also, *and the highest*. [Should be] *insert "itself"*.
Also, *from the divine*. [Should be] *from the divine*. <note>Correction of a subtle inflection or spelling.</note>
Also, **side 2:** *it might have within*. [Should be] *they might have*.
Also, *although*. [Should be] *whatever*.

**Sheet 33, side 2:** in Greek *sembropallugi* <note>original: "σεμβροπαλλυγι" — Likely a corrupted or highly specific technical term</note> *800*.
Also, **side 2:** *and how many*. [Should be] *how great*.
*Animals of which*. Also, *these things are better*. [Should be] *these*.

**Sheet 34, side 2:** *God not layered*. [Should be] *natured*. <note>The Latin 'naturatum' refers to 'natura naturata', or nature as a created thing.</note>

**Sheet 35, side 2:** *God himself*. [Should be] *itself*.

**Sheet 37, side 1:** in Greek *nor is he able*. [Should be] *insert "always"*. <note>original: "ουλε Δυνα.νει... ειει"</note>

**Sheet 38, side 1:** *moves moves*. Delete the second *moves*.
Also, **side 2:** *the angel those things*. [Should be] *these*.

**Sheet 41, side 1:** *before he moves*.

...and first. Also on face 2: "definito" should be "definition" <note>original: "definitio"</note>. Page 43, face 1: "ni" should be "unless" <note>original: "nisi"</note>. Also where it is written "as an instrument," many things are missing, which I transcribe here as follows: "But neither will he himself be generated by any means. For since nothing acts beyond its own proper degree <note>The author is discussing the limits of causality; a cause cannot produce an effect greater than its own nature</note>, an accident <term>accident: a non-essential quality of a thing, like its color or size</term> can in no way generate a substance; but can only dispose the matter through a certain accidental preparation." Then follows in the main text: "It is necessary..." etc.

Also on face 2: "so that in natural art," delete "so" <note>original: "ut"</note>. Page 43, face 1: where it says "it has in act," add the following: "Nor should it be thought to suffice if a universal and remote cause is so much more excellent. Otherwise, every most imperfect cause among us could, by the power of the most perfect celestial bodies, produce any perfect thing." Then follows: "All these..." etc. Also on face 2: "fecundiam" should be "eloquence" <note>original: "facundia"</note>. Also "infused and with corpuscles," remove "and." Page 43, face 1: "earth and waters" should be "water" <note>original: "aqua"</note>. Also "acto" should be "eight" <note>original: "octo"</note>. Page 45, face 1: "stars" should be "starry" <note>original: "siderea"</note>. Also face 2: "faplicem" should be "simple" <note>original: "simplicem"</note>. Also "xii" <note>the Roman numeral for 12</note> "prelint" should be "is smeared" <note>original: "prelitur"</note>. Also "denatis" should be "relating to twelve" <note>original: "duodenariis"</note>.

Page 46, face 1: "let us yield" should be "let us return" <note>original: "redeamus"</note>. Also "we complete" should be "let us proceed" <note>original: "pergamus"</note>. Page 47, face 2: "whence nature" should be "whence then?" <note>original: "unde nam?"</note>. Also face 2: "in the whole body" should be "in the universe" <note>original: "in universo"</note>. Also "one principle" should be "principle." Also "our Plato" should be "Plato seems" <note>original: "Plato videtur"</note>. 

Page 51, face 2: "in the world such" should be "web" <note>original: "tela"</note>. Also face 2, at the end: "to this" should be "lowest" <note>original: "infime"</note>. Page 53, face 1: where it is written "defines universal," add these words: "For if that which is moved by another is moved as long as it adheres to the mover, much more so does that which is moved by itself—because it is the same as its own mover—never cease to be moved, and never lets go of its motion." Then follows: "Furthermore..." etc. 

Also "spiritual" should be "spirituals" <note>original: "spiritales"</note>. Also for those same "spiritual" ones, insert "to make" <note>original: "facere"</note>. Also face 2: "The fourth reason because" should be "because" <note>original: "quia"</note>. Also "and humidity," insert "if" <note>original: "si"</note>. Page 54, face 2: "to the angel" should be "to acting" <note>original: "ad agendum"</note>. Also "from the middle" should be "from the middle" <note>original: "e medio"</note>. Also "to that place" should be "that thing" <note>original: "istud"</note>. Also "indeed the matter" should be "to lose matter" <note>original: "materia perdere"</note>. 

Page 55, face 1: "order of matter" should be "order" <note>original: "ordinem"</note>. Page 56, face 1: "from fury" should be "and fury" <note>original: "et furore"</note>. Page 57, face 1: "to non-being" should be "not from that," and "to non-being indeed from that." Page 58, face 1: "and because" should be "also because" <note>original: "etiam quia"</note>. Also face 2: "let it be proposed" should be "let it first be placed" <note>original: "primo ponatur"</note>. Page 60, face 1: "certainly in essence," remove "in" and insert "is" <note>original: "est"</note>. 

Page 62, face 1: "second but first" should be "and second but first." Page 63, face 2: "whether all" should be "before all" <note>original: "ante omnes"</note>. Page 64, face 2: "eternal of power" should be "power" <note>original: "potentia"</note>. Also "both consists" should be "they consist" <note>plural: "constant"</note>. Page 66, face 2: "to cling to matter" should be "they seem to cling to matter." Page 68, face 2: "certainly bodies" should be "corporeal" <note>original: "corporea"</note>. Page 68, face 2: "that first" should be "from that first." Page 71, face 2: "Critolaus and" <note>Critolaus was a Peripatetic philosopher</note>. Page 72, face 2: "would respond" <note>original: "responderet"</note>. Page 73, face 2: "to provide for the body" should be "to see through the body" <note>original: "pervidere"</note>. Also "they appear to look back" should be "to look at" <note>original: "aspicere"</note>.

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This is the opening page of the work; there is no previous page for continuity.</meta>

-># THE PREFACE OF MARSILIO FICINO THE FLORENTINE TO THE <term>PLATONIC THEOLOGY</term> ON THE IMMORTALITY OF SOULS, DEDICATED TO <term>LORENZO DE' MEDICI</term>, A MAGNANIMOUS MAN.<-

<note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was the leader of the Platonic Academy in Florence. His "Platonic Theology" was a monumental effort to harmonize ancient Greek philosophy with Christian doctrine. His patron, Lorenzo "the Magnificent" de' Medici, was the de facto ruler of the Florentine Republic.</note>

Of the philosophers, the Platonists <note>The OCR reads "LATO", likely missing the initial "P" from a decorative drop-cap for "Plato" or "Philosophorum."</note> are a group who maintain that the soul is immortal. Among these, moreover, there is a portion who affirm that the soul is not only immortal but also divine. Within this group, there are those who profess that the soul is not only immortal and divine but was also created by God. Furthermore, among these, there is a part who affirm that the soul is not only immortal, divine, and created by God, but also possesses free will <note>original: "liberam," meaning the soul is free in its choices and actions.</note>. Among all of these, there are those who contend that the soul is not only immortal, divine, created by God, and free, but is also rational. Finally, among all these, there are some who profess that the soul is not only immortal, divine, created by God, free, and rational, but is specifically the human soul.

At this point, it is appropriate for us to discuss the human soul itself: what all nations, theologians, and philosophers everywhere on earth have determined regarding the immortality of the soul; what the Platonists—who have excelled most in this matter—have thought; and finally, what we ourselves, who follow the Platonists, believe <note>The OCR ends with "sent," likely a truncation of "sentimus" (we feel/think).</note> concerning the human soul.

<summary>In this preface, Marsilio Ficino introduces his philosophical project, categorizing different beliefs about the soul's nature and declaring his intent to prove its immortality through the lens of Platonic thought.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Plato, Lorenzo de' Medici, immortality of the soul, Platonic Theology, Renaissance philosophy, divinity of the soul, free will</keywords>
